# Great Britain series: audit pack

This file contains the audit brief followed by all three courses. Read the brief first, then review the courses below it.

# Audit brief

You are reviewing a three-course English series before it is published. Be a strict, independent reviewer: a teacher-trainer, an editor and a fact-checker in one. Don't rewrite the courses. Find problems and explain them clearly.

### About the series

- **Learners:** adult Japanese learners in 1:1 online lessons with a British teacher. The slides sit beside the video call. Lessons are about 30–45 minutes.
- **Structure:** each course has 12 lessons in 4 units of 3. The last lesson of each unit has a progress check, and the last lesson of each course has a final check.
- **Levels:**

  | Course | Level | Story |
  |---|---|---|
  | Great Britain for Beginners | A2 | Haruka, 26, from Osaka, spends her first year in London: her flat, her landlady Gwen, her flatmate Tom, work with Priya, British food, manners and travel. |
  | Great Britain: Intermediate | B1 → B2 | Year two. A new colleague, Yuki, arrives. Practical life in the UK (renting, the NHS, the Post Office, travel problems, a visa). A light mystery: who is drawing the web comic *Milk Second* about Haruka's life? It turns out to be her mum. |
  | Great Britain: Advanced | B2 → C1 | Year three. Haruka is team lead. A new designer, Josh, from Sydney, clashes with her communication style. The team pitches to rebrand Hartley's Tea, decodes a very indirect British client, and solves a puzzle in the company archive (a crane on an old tin, and a 1920s friendship with a tea family in Uji). The series ends with Tom's wedding. |

- **Story timeline:** Beginner runs September 2025 – summer 2026, Intermediate runs September 2026 – July 2027, and Advanced runs September 2027 – July 2028.
- **What is invented:** the characters, Hartley's Tea, Edith Hartley, the Morita family, Crane Blend, the comic *Milk Second*, and all the companies and buildings in the story. Don't flag these as false. Do flag invented details that clash with real history or real places, for example a date, a law or a custom that couldn't be true.
- **What must be real:** every cultural, legal, historical and practical fact, including prices, laws, dates, customs, places and rules (NHS, renting, tax and National Insurance, visas, driving licences, Royal Mail, rail, weddings, etc.). Judge them as of 2026. Where a fact sits in a future story year (2027–28), flag it only if it's clearly wrong or likely to change.

### Deliberate choices (don't flag these as problems)

- Japanese characters only speak English to each other when a non-Japanese person is present.
- Mrs Hartley, the client, is deliberately indirect ("brave", "interesting", "I'll bear it in mind"). Decoding her is a running puzzle until Lesson 11.
- Josh's directness is his personality, not his nationality. Australian and British English are treated as essentially the same language.
- Teacher notes may use harder English than the student-facing slides.
- Grammar is not bolded in dialogues that come before that lesson's language focus slide. Vocabulary is bolded after the vocabulary slides.
- Each lesson has one role play, plus a progress check or final check where the unit ends.
- Practice slides that need a specific form start with a small pattern box (shown as *Pattern*).
- "British phrases" / "Useful phrases" slides come after the dialogues that use them.
- Slides marked "(flashback, black and white)" replay scenes the student has already seen.
- Lines like "[image: …]", "[video clip: …]" and "[audio: …]" are placeholders, and the description tells you what the student will see or hear. "(teacher only)" text is hidden from students.

### Already reviewed (don't re-raise unless you think the decision is wrong)

Earlier audits of each course have been applied. These points were considered and deliberately kept:

- Role plays give concrete details on the cards (times, prices, limits) so that teacher and student don't have to invent a situation. Freer speaking happens in the progress checks, the final checks and the discussion slides.
- Intermediate Lesson 11 (the reveal) is longer than other lessons and can be split into two sessions.
- Intermediate Lesson 2: Gwen's "You're supposed to see a flat before you pay" teaches *be supposed to* (what's expected), not a legal rule.
- The Intermediate mystery clue "American English from school" fits anyone who learned English in Japan (Mum, Kenta, Yuki). The suspect boards include "Someone else?" and "Someone in Japan?".
- Practical and legal information (renting, tax, pensions, licences) is deliberately simplified to what the student needs for the story situation, with fuller detail in teacher notes. Only flag it if it's wrong for that situation, not just incomplete.
- The series teaches speaking, listening and reading, not writing. Model emails and letters are for reading and phrase work.
- Practical facts were checked for 2026. Where the story is in 2027–28, the teacher notes say which year the figures are from.

This is the final audit of the **whole series**. Give extra weight to things a single-course review can't see: continuity between courses, progression of level from A2 to C1, characters' development, callbacks and repeated content across courses.

### What to check

#### 1. Effectiveness at the target level

- Does each lesson actually teach its objective? Do the dialogues, language focus, practice, role play and wrap-up line up?
- Is the student-facing language right for the course level (A2 / B1–B2 / B2–C1)? Flag anything clearly above or below, using the CEFR Companion Volume and the British Council/EAQUALS Core Inventory.
- Is grammar taught in a sensible order across each course and across the series? Flag anything taught too early, too late, or re-taught without reason.
- Are practice items clear and answerable, and are the answers in the teacher notes correct and complete?
- Is earlier language recycled? Are there gaps in coverage for the level?

#### 2. Story loopholes

- Does any character know something before the student has seen a scene where they learned it?
- Do events, dates, ages, places, relationships and objects stay consistent within each course and across all three?
- Is the mystery (Intermediate) and the archive puzzle (Advanced) fair play? Could a student solve them from what they've seen? Is any clue missing, contradicted or explained by something off-screen?
- Do the characters' reactions make sense? Is anything left unresolved that should have been resolved?

#### 3. Unnecessary filler or confusion

- Flag slides, lines or activities that add nothing, repeat an earlier slide, or could confuse the student or teacher.
- Flag jokes that don't land without explanation, unclear instructions, and role plays where either person would have to invent a lot.

#### 4. Cultural and historical accuracy

- List every factual claim you think is wrong, out of date or doubtful, and say why. Give a source if you can.
- Flag stereotypes (about British, Japanese, Australian, Welsh or Scottish people, or anyone else), even mild or friendly ones.
- Flag any British phrase that is dated, regional without explanation, American, or not really used today.
- Flag comparisons with Japan that are inaccurate or that tell the student what Japan is like instead of asking.

#### 5. Tone and level appropriateness

- Would a real person say this line in this situation? Flag anything stiff, unnatural or machine-sounding.
- Do Haruka and Yuki sound like believable Japanese speakers of English at that stage, improving visibly across the series without becoming different people?
- Is the humour kind? Is anything inappropriate for adult learners or a professional setting?

#### 6. Coherence across the series

- Do the three courses feel like one story, with consistent characters, running jokes, callbacks and progression?
- Does each course stand alone well enough for a new student, for example through "The story so far" slides?
- Does the ending of the series resolve the main threads?

#### 7. Logical teaching order within each lesson

- Vocabulary is pre-taught before the dialogue that needs it.
- The language focus comes after the dialogues that contain its examples, and its "From the story" examples come from dialogues already read.
- Culture explanations come straight after the scene that raises them.
- Role plays come after the language they practise.
- The listening clips fit the story timeline.

### How to report

1. A table with one row per issue:

   | Course | Lesson | Slide | Quote | Problem | Category (1–7) | Severity (must fix / should fix / nice to have) | Suggested fix |

   Keep quotes short. In "Suggested fix", give the actual replacement line where you can.

2. The **10 most important issues** across the series, in order.
3. A short **verdict per course**: is it right for its level, and what is its biggest weakness?
4. **What works especially well**, briefly.
5. **Confidence:** for every factual claim you flag, say "sure" or "unsure". Don't guess. Say "unsure" and explain what to check.


---

## Great Britain for Beginners (A2)

Follow Haruka from Osaka through her first year living in Britain. Learn everyday English for food, manners, weather, places, accents, music and travel, with real British culture.

Characters: Haruka, Gwen, Tom, Priya, Mum, Barman, Callum, Stranger, Sian, Passenger, Kenta, Linda, Waitress


## Unit: Settling in


### Lesson 1: New flat, new friends

**Objective:** Students can introduce themselves and another person in a short conversation, add one extra detail to each answer, ask a follow-up question, and make and reply to a simple offer (CEFR A2 conversation and information exchange).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 1 · Settling in · Lesson 1

**New flat, new friends**

Today’s goal: introduce yourself and a friend, keep the conversation going, and offer someone a cup of tea.

[image: Photo idea: a row of London terraced houses with colourful front doors]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Welcome the student to the course. Every lesson follows Haruka, a young woman from Osaka, through her first year living in Britain.

Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Timing: 21 slides, about 28 to 32 minutes depending on how much the student talks. If time is short, skip True or false and do Extra practice as homework.

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Let’s get started!

Talk with your teacher.

- Have you ever lived with flatmates or in a shared house?
- What do you say when you meet someone for the first time?
- Would you like to live in another country for a year? Where?

[image: Photo idea: a traveller with a suitcase arriving at an airport]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep this short and personal (~2 min). Write down any words the student needs but doesn’t know, and come back to them in the vocabulary slides.

</details>


#### Slide 3: Story · Meet the characters

> **Haruka · Osaka**
>
> A graphic designer on a one-year working holiday in London. 
> She wants to see everything,
> but is also a little nervous.
> She loves food 
> and taking photos.

> **Gwen · Cardiff (Wales)**
>
> Haruka’s landlady. She lives downstairs.
>  She is kind, she loves to talk, 
> and she is very serious 
> about tea.

> **Tom · Manchester**
>
> Haruka’s flatmate. 
> He is friendly and funny, 
> loves football 
> and talks a lot!

> **Priya · Birmingham**
>
> Haruka’s new colleague. 
> Her parents are from India. 
> She is hard working and
> loves making plans.

[image: ]

[image: ]

[image: ]

[image: ]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the four profiles. Ask: Who do you want to meet first? Who is most like you? Why? (~1.5 min)
Each card uses that character’s speech-bubble colour, so it changes if you change their colour.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: Haruka (26, Japanese) walks up to a London terraced house pulling a big suitcase, stops at the front door, looks up at the house, takes a deep breath and knocks. She pulls the suitcase up to the door, looks up, takes a breath and knocks. Slow and calm.]

- What is she doing in the video? 
- How is she feeling? 

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Welcome to London!

Haruka arrives in London and meets her landlady, Gwen.

**Gwen:** Hello! You must be Haruka. I’m Gwen. I live **downstairs**.  
**Haruka:** Hello! Yes, I’m Haruka. Nice to meet you.  
**Gwen:** Nice to meet you too. Wow, that’s a big **suitcase**!  
**Haruka:** I know! It’s really heavy.  
**Gwen:** Well, welcome to London, love! Was your **flight** OK?  
**Haruka:** Long! Over fourteen hours. But I’m really **excited**.  
**Gwen:** Come in, come in. I’ll put the **kettle** on. Would you like a cup of tea?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Haruka), then swap roles. (~2 min)

“love”: a friendly way to speak to someone, common with older people in the UK. It doesn’t mean romance here. It comes up on the British phrases slide.
“I’ll put the kettle on” means I’ll make some tea. Ask: what would a Japanese host offer a new guest?
Pronunciation: link “Nice to meet you” as one phrase, with the stress on MEET.

</details>


#### Slide 6: Story · Did you understand?

- Where does Gwen live?
- How long was Haruka’s flight?
- How does Haruka feel?
- What does Gwen offer Haruka?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Downstairs.  2. Over fourteen hours.  3. Excited.  4. A cup of tea.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. If the student can’t answer, go back to the dialogue together. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Moving in (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a flat | noun | マンション・アパートの部屋 | a home on one floor of a building | Haruka lives in a **flat** in London. |
| a flatmate | noun | ルームメイト、同居人 | a person you share a flat with | Tom is Haruka’s **flatmate**. |
| a landlady | noun | （女性の）大家さん | the woman who owns your home. You pay her every month. | Gwen is the **landlady**. |
| downstairs | adverb | 下の階に | on a lower floor | Gwen lives **downstairs**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Drill pronunciation: flat /flæt/, landlady /ˈlændleɪdi/, downstairs /ˌdaʊnˈsteəz/. (~1.5 min)
Note for Japanese learners: a UK “flat” is what Japanese calls マンション or アパート. In English, a “mansion” is a very large, expensive house.
Ask: Who is your landlord or landlady in Japan? Do you live upstairs or downstairs?

</details>


#### Slide 8: Vocabulary · Moving in (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a suitcase | noun | スーツケース | a big bag for travelling | My **suitcase** is very heavy. |
| a flight | noun | フライト、飛行機の便 | a journey by plane | The **flight** was over fourteen hours. |
| excited | adjective | わくわくしている | very happy about something new | I’m really **excited**! |
| a kettle | noun | やかん、電気ケトル | you make hot water in it, for tea or coffee | I’ll put the **kettle** on. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Drill pronunciation: suitcase /ˈsuːtkeɪs/, kettle /ˈketl/.
Ask the student to make one new sentence with two of the words. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 9: Story · Meet Tom

Upstairs in the **flat**, Gwen introduces Haruka to her flatmate, Tom.

**Gwen:** Haruka, this is Tom. He’s your **flatmate**.  
**Tom:** Alright? Nice to meet you, Haruka!  
**Haruka:** Nice to meet you too... “alright”? Am I OK?  
**Tom:** “Alright?” just means “Hi, how are you?”  
**Haruka:** Oh, I see! Where are you from, Tom?  
**Tom:** Manchester, in the north of England. And you?  
**Haruka:** I’m from Osaka. I’m a graphic designer.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

“Alright?” is a very common greeting, especially in the north of England. It means “Hi, how are you?” and doesn’t need a long answer. A natural reply is “Yeah, good thanks. You?” Practise the greeting a few times. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 10: Listening · Tom’s message

That evening, Haruka gets a voice message from Tom. Listen and answer.

[audio: Tom’s voice message]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Hi Haruka, it’s Tom. I’m at football tonight, so I’ll be back late. The Wi-Fi password is on the fridge. See you tomorrow!

- Where is Tom tonight?
- Where is the Wi-Fi password?
- When will Haruka see Tom?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. At football.
> 2. On the fridge.
> 3. Tomorrow.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Hi Haruka, it’s Tom. I’m at football tonight, so I’ll be back late. The Wi-Fi password is on the fridge. See you tomorrow!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice: once for the general idea, once for details. Don’t show the transcript until after the questions. (~2 min)
The clip is new: it isn’t in the dialogues, so this checks real listening.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Language focus · Keep the conversation going

> **Answer, add a detail, ask back**
>
> Where are you from? 
> I’m from Osaka. **It’s in the west of Japan. How about you?**
>
> What do you do? 
>  I’m a designer. **I work for a design company. And you?**

**From the story**
- Tom: Manchester, in the north of England. And you?
- Haruka: I’m from Osaka. I’m a graphic designer.
- Gwen: I’ll put the kettle on. Would you like a cup of tea?

To keep the conversation going, you should add more **details** and a **question**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Model the pattern: short answer, one extra detail, then a question back. Ask the student which lines in the story follow the pattern. (~1.5 min)
Students at this level often stop after the first sentence (“I’m from Osaka.”). Praise any extra detail.

</details>


#### Slide 12: Practice · Make the answer longer

- Where are you from? → I’m from Tokyo. ____
- What do you do? → I’m a nurse. ____
- Where do you live? → I live in Yokohama. ____
- Do you like London? → Yes! ____
- This is my friend, Ken. → ____
- Would you like a cup of tea? → ____

> **Possible answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. It’s the capital of Japan. And you?
> 2. I work in a big hospital. How about you?
> 3. It’s near Tokyo. Where do you live?
> 4. It’s big and exciting. Do you like it?
> 5. Nice to meet you, Ken!
> 6. Yes, please! / No, thanks. I’m fine.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Any sensible answer is fine. The answers here are examples. (~2 min)
Watch for: “I from Tokyo” (missing am), “He is Ken” when introducing someone (use “This is Ken”), and answering an offer with just “Yes.” (add “please”).

</details>


#### Slide 13: Pronunciation · Sounding natural

> **How it sounds**
>
> Nice to meet you → nice ta **MEE**-choo
>
> Where are you from? → wair-ra-ya **FROM**?
>
> How about you? → how a-bou-**CHOO**?

**Say it with your teacher**
- Nice to meet you, Tom.
- Nice to meet you **too**.
- Where are you **from**?
- How about **you**?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Say each line, the student repeats. (~1 min)
The spelling on the slide shows how the words join in natural speech: “to” becomes a weak “ta”, and “t” + “you” becomes “choo” (meet you → mee-choo, about you → a-bou-choo). In southern English accents the r in “where” is only heard when the next word starts with a vowel: wair-ra.
Capitals show the stressed word. In “Nice to meet you too”, the stress moves to TOO. In “How about you?”, stress YOU.
This is for listening and copying, not spelling. Remind the student to write it the normal way.

</details>


#### Slide 14: Story · First day at work

Monday: Haruka’s first day at a London design company. She meets Priya.

**Priya:** Hi! You must be the new designer. I’m Priya.  
**Haruka:** Hi Priya, I’m Haruka. Nice to meet you.  
**Priya:** Nice to meet you too. Where are you from?  
**Haruka:** I’m from Osaka. It’s in the west of Japan. How about you?  
**Priya:** I’m from Birmingham. My parents are from India.  
**Haruka:** Oh, nice! What do you do here?  
**Priya:** I’m a project manager. I make the plans, and the tea!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to find where Haruka adds a detail and asks back (line 4). (~1.5 min)
Background: Birmingham is the UK’s second-biggest city, about an hour and a half from London by train.

</details>


#### Slide 15: Role play · Meet your flatmate

> **Student**
>
> You are new in London. Meet your new flatmate.
>
> • Say your name and where you are from, and add one detail.
> • Say what you do.
> • Ask two questions back.

**Useful phrases**
- Nice to meet you.
- How about you?
- It’s in…
- I work for…
- This is…
- Would you like a cup of tea?
- Yes, please! / No, thanks.

> **Teacher**
>
> You are the flatmate. Choose a UK city you are from.
>
> • Greet with “Alright?”
> • Answer and ask questions.
> • Offer a cup of tea!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Do it twice. (~2.5 min)
Second time, the student introduces a friend or family member to you with “This is…” and adds one detail about them.
Note one or two errors to correct at the end, not during the role play.

</details>


#### Slide 16: Reading · Sharing a flat in the UK

[image: Photo idea: young flatmates chatting and cooking in a small shared kitchen]

Many young people in the UK live in a **flat** with **flatmates**. Rent in big cities like London is very expensive, so sharing is cheaper. Flatmates usually have their own bedroom, but they share the kitchen, the bathroom and the living room.

Flatmates are not always friends at the start. Often, they meet for the first time on moving day! Some flatmates cook and eat together. Others just say hello in the kitchen.

Some British people wear shoes inside the house, but many take them off at the door. And when someone new arrives, British people often offer them a cup of tea.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. Correct pronunciation gently at the end of each paragraph, not mid-sentence. (~2.5 min)
Check: Why do people share flats? What do flatmates share? What do British people often offer a new person?

</details>


#### Slide 17: Reading · True or false?

- Rent in London is cheap.
- Flatmates usually share a bedroom.
- Flatmates are always friends from the start.
- British people often offer a new person a cup of tea.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: rent in London is very expensive.  2. False: they usually have their own bedroom.  3. False: they often meet on moving day.  4. True.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences in their own words. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 18: Discussion · What do you think?

[image: Photo idea: a pair of shoes by a front door with a doormat]

- Is sharing a flat common in Japan? Who usually does it?
- Would you like to live with flatmates? Why / why not?
- Do you take your shoes off at home? What do you think about wearing shoes inside?
- What do you offer a new guest in your home?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Encourage longer answers with “Why?” and “Can you give an example?” Push for today’s pattern: answer, add a detail, ask back. (~3 min)

</details>


#### Slide 19: Story · Dinner plans

Friday evening. Tom knocks on Haruka’s door.

**Tom:** Haruka, are you hungry?  
**Haruka:** Yes, very! What do people eat for dinner here?  
**Tom:** Tonight? It's Friday, so fish and chips of course!  
**Haruka:** Fish and… chips? Like potato chips?  
**Tom:** No, no. You’ll see. 
On Sunday, Gwen wants to cook for us.  
**Haruka:** Really? That’s so kind. What will she cook?  
**Tom:** A **Sunday roast**. Trust me, you’re going to love it.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

In the UK, “chips” are hot, thick fries. Japanese “potato chips” are “crisps” in British English. (~1.5 min)
Build curiosity for the next lesson: what is a Sunday roast? Let the student guess.

</details>


#### Slide 20: Culture · Fish and chips on Friday

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| chips | noun (plural) | フライドポテト | hot fried potatoes | Fish and **chips**, please. |
| crisps | noun (plural) | ポテトチップス | thin potato snacks in a bag | A bag of **crisps**, please. |

> **Why Friday?**
>
> Lots of British people eat fish and chips on Friday. Why? 
> A long time ago, many Christians didn’t eat meat on Fridays, so they ate fish. Some people also say workers got paid on Friday, so they bought dinner at the chip shop.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Tom said “It’s Friday!” in the dialogue. This slide explains why. (~1.5 min)
Note: in American English, crisps are called “chips”, and chips are called “fries”. What do you call them in Japanese? Is there a food people in Japan often eat on a special day of the week? (For example, curry on Fridays in the Japanese navy.)
A fish and chip shop is often called a chippy. Fish and chips come back in Lesson 4.

</details>


#### Slide 21: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- met Haruka, Gwen, Tom and Priya
- learned 8 words for moving into a flat
- introduced yourself and someone else
- added a detail and asked a question back

[image: Photo idea: a cup of English tea with milk on a kitchen table]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Give one piece of positive feedback and one thing to practise. Preview the next lesson: Sunday lunch at Gwen’s. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 2: Sunday lunch at Gwen’s

**Objective:** Students can describe their family and talk about their weekly routines, using possessives and frequency phrases (CEFR A2: describing people, habits and routines).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 1 · Settling in · Lesson 2

**Sunday lunch at Gwen’s**

Today’s goal: talk about your family and say what you do every week.

[image: Photo idea: a Sunday roast on a family dining table]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Haruka’s first Sunday in London. Gwen cooks a Sunday roast for Haruka and Tom. Haruka brings a gift, shows her family photos and shares her “UK list”. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Haruka arrived in London, met Gwen (landlady), Tom (flatmate) and Priya (colleague). On Friday Tom said Gwen wants to cook a Sunday roast.

Timing: 23 slides, about 31 to 34 minutes. If time is short, skip True or false and do More practice as homework.

Teacher note on Haruka: she should feel like a real Japanese 26-year-old, not a stereotype. Let the student say what Japan is like. Haruka’s English: clear and correct, but like a real Japanese learner, not a native speaker. She uses simple sentences and the English words Japanese people already know (English breakfast, not “a full English”). British words come from the other characters.

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · What do you do on Sundays?

Talk with your teacher.

- What do you usually do on Sundays?
- Does your family eat a big meal together? When?
- Who in your family do you talk to the most?

[image: Photo idea: a family having lunch together at home]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep this short and personal. Ask follow-up questions and remind the student of Lesson 1’s pattern: answer, add a detail, ask back. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: Haruka stands at Gwen’s flat door holding a small paper gift bag. Gwen (sixties, Welsh) opens the door with a big smile. Gwen opens the door. Haruka gives a small bow and holds out the gift bag with both hands. Gwen looks surprised and happy.]

- What does Haruka give Gwen? How does she give it?
- What does Haruka do when Gwen opens the door?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Sunday lunch

Sunday, one o’clock. Haruka goes to **Gwen’s** flat. Tom is there too.

**Gwen:** Hello, love! Come in, come in.  
**Haruka:** Thank you, Mrs Davies. This is for you. It’s a small gift from Osaka.  
**Gwen:** Oh, how kind! But please, call me Gwen.  
**Haruka:** OK… Gwen! Do you cook a big lunch every Sunday?  
**Gwen:** Yes, every Sunday! It’s my favourite meal of the week.  
**Tom:** And I always help! Well… I sometimes help.  
**Gwen:** Tom never helps! He usually just eats.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Haruka), then swap roles. (~2 min)

Haruka brings a gift (手土産), as many people do in Japan when they visit someone’s home. In the UK people often bring something small too, like flowers, chocolates or wine, so Gwen is happy.
“Mrs Davies”: Haruka is polite with an older person. Gwen prefers her first name. In the UK many people use first names quickly, even with older people.
Point out the frequency words: every Sunday, always, sometimes, never, usually.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- What does Haruka give Gwen?
- What does Gwen want Haruka to call her?
- How often does Gwen cook a big lunch?
- Does Tom help?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. A small gift from Osaka.  2. Gwen.  3. Every Sunday.  4. Not really. He usually just eats!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. If the student can’t answer, go back to the dialogue together. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · People (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| grandchildren | noun (plural) | 孫 | the children of your son or daughter | Gwen visits her **grandchildren** in Cardiff. |
| a younger brother | noun | 弟 | a brother who is younger than you | Kenta is Haruka’s **younger brother**. |
| to look like | verb | 〜に似ている | have the same face or style as someone | I **look like** my mum. |
| an only child | noun | 一人っ子 | a person with no brothers or sisters | Priya isn’t an **only child**. She has two sisters. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Drill pronunciation: grandchildren /ˈɡræntʃɪldrən/ (the d is almost silent). (~1.5 min)
Note for Japanese learners: English often doesn’t show older or younger (brother, sister). Add “older” or “younger” when it matters, like 兄 / 弟.
Ask: Do you look like your mum or your dad? Are you an only child?

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · People (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| clever | adjective | 頭がいい | good at learning and thinking | My brother is very **clever**. |
| shy | adjective | 恥ずかしがり屋 | a little nervous with new people | He’s **shy**, but he’s very kind. |
| a bit | adverb | 少し | a little | Tom is **a bit** loud! |
| a fan (of) | noun | ファン | a person who loves a sport, band, film, etc. | Kenta is a big Harry Potter **fan**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Drill: clever /ˈklevə/, shy /ʃaɪ/. (~1.5 min)
Note: British English says “clever”. American English often says “smart”. In British English “smart” often means well dressed (it can also mean clever), and Japanese スマート (slim) is different again.
Ask: Describe someone in your family with two of these words.

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · Family photos

After lunch, Gwen shows Haruka and Tom her family photos.

**Gwen:** This is my daughter, Sian. She lives in Cardiff with her husband.  
**Haruka:** She looks like you! Are these her children?  
**Gwen:** Yes, my **grandchildren**. I visit them once a month.  
**Haruka:** They’re so cute! This is my family, at my parents’ house in Osaka.  
**Tom:** And who’s this in the Harry Potter T-shirt?  
**Haruka:** My **younger brother**, Kenta. He’s a student. He’s **clever**, but **a bit shy**.  
**Tom:** A Harry Potter **fan**? Then he needs to visit the UK!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. Then ask: Who looks like Gwen? What is Kenta like? (~2 min)
Find the possessives: **my parents’ house**, **Gwen’s** daughter. These come back in the Language focus.
Sian is a Welsh name, said “Shahn”.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Listening · A message from Kenta

After lunch, Haruka gets a voice message from Kenta. Listen and answer.

[audio: Kenta’s voice message]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Hi Haruka, it’s Kenta. How’s London? Are you eating well? Send me some photos, please. Especially Harry Potter things!

- What does Kenta ask about?
- What does he want?
- What does he especially want to see?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. London, and if she’s eating well.
> 2. Some photos.
> 3. Harry Potter things.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Hi Haruka, it’s Kenta. How’s London? Are you eating well? Send me some photos, please. Especially Harry Potter things!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice: once for the general idea, once for details. Don’t show the transcript until after the questions. (~2 min)
The clip is new: it isn’t in the dialogues, so this checks real listening.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Vocabulary · Sunday lunch words

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a roast | noun | ロースト（オーブンで焼いた肉料理） | meat cooked in the oven, with potatoes and vegetables | We have a **roast** on Sundays. |
| gravy | noun | グレイビー（肉汁のソース） | a hot brown sauce made from meat juice | Can I have more **gravy**, please? |
| a Yorkshire pudding | noun | ヨークシャー・プディング（甘くない） | made from flour, eggs and milk. It isn’t sweet! | I love **Yorkshire puddings**! |
| pudding | noun | デザート | (British English) dessert | What’s for **pudding**? |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Watch out: in Japan “pudding” (プリン) is a custard dessert. In British English a Yorkshire pudding is not sweet, and “pudding” can also mean any dessert: “What’s for pudding?” (~1.5 min)
Ask: Which one do you want to try?

</details>


#### Slide 11: Language focus · Whose is it?

Use ’s to say who something belongs to.

> **’s and s’**
>
> *One person*
>
> **Kenta’s** room · **Gwen’s** daughter
>
> *Plural with -s*
>
> my **parents’** house · my **grandparents’** dog
>
> *Other plurals*
>
> the **children’s** toys

**From the story**
- Haruka: This is my family, at **my parents’ house** in Osaka.
- Haruka goes downstairs to **Gwen’s** flat.
- Gwen: Yes, my grandchildren. (= **Sian’s** children)

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Explain: ’s works like の. 健太の部屋 = Kenta’s room. (~1.5 min)
Watch for: Japanese learners often drop the ’s (“Kenta room”), or say “the room of Kenta”. Both sound unnatural.

</details>


#### Slide 12: Practice · Say it with ’s

- Tom / bike → ____
- my parents / car → ____
- Gwen / grandchildren → ____
- the children / books → ____
- Haruka / list → ____
- my friends / names → ____

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Tom’s bike
> 2. my parents’ car
> 3. Gwen’s grandchildren
> 4. the children’s books
> 5. Haruka’s list
> 6. my friends’ names

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Student says the phrase, then makes a sentence with it, for example: “Tom’s bike is very fast.” (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Language focus · How often?

Use these words to say how often you do something.

> **Frequency words**
>
> *Frequency words*
>
> **always** · **usually** · **often** · **sometimes** · **never**
>
> *Where they go*
>
> I **usually** cook on Sunday. Tom is **always** late.
>
> *Phrases at the end*
>
> I visit them **every Sunday** / **once a month**.

**From the story**
- Tom: And I **always** help! Well… I **sometimes** help.
- Gwen: Tom **never** helps! He **usually** just eats.
- Gwen: I visit them **once a month**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Model with the student’s real life. Point out the word order: the frequency word goes before most verbs, but after “be”. Phrases like “every Sunday” go at the end. (~1.5 min)
Watch for: “I always am late” (should be “I’m always late”).

</details>


#### Slide 14: Practice · How often do you…?

- How often do you cook dinner?
- How often do you see your family?
- How often do you go to a café?
- How often do you take the train?
- How often do you eat out?
- How often do you drink tea?

> **Example answer** (teacher only)
>
> I usually cook dinner, but on Fridays I eat out with friends. How about you?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask each question. The student answers with a frequency word or phrase, adds a detail, and asks back. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Pronunciation · Sounding natural

> **Three sounds for ’s**
>
> Kate**’s** → **s**     Tom**’s**, Gwen**’s** → **z**     George**’s** → **iz**

**Say it with your teacher**
- It’s **Gwen’s** flat.
- That’s **Tom’s** bike.
- This is **Kate’s** sister.
- We’re at **George’s** house.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Say each name, the student repeats. Many learners drop the final ’s, so make it clear and a little long. (~1 min)
The rule: “s” after p, t, k, f sounds (Kate’s, Jack’s). “z” after vowels and most other sounds (Tom’s, Gwen’s, Haruka’s). “iz” after s, z, sh, ch, j sounds (George’s, Grace’s).
Don’t add an extra vowel: Tom’s is one beat, not “Tomuzu”.

</details>


#### Slide 16: Story · Milk first?

Gwen makes a pot of tea for Haruka and Tom.

**Gwen:** Tea, Haruka? How do you have it?  
**Haruka:** With milk, please. Can I put the milk in first?  
**Gwen:** Milk first?! Oh no. Tea first, then milk second. **Always**!  
**Tom:** Haha! Gwen’s tea rules! So, Haruka, what do you want to do in the UK?  
**Haruka:** I have a list! English breakfast, afternoon tea, Harry Potter spots…  
**Tom:** English breakfast? You mean a **full English**! I go to a great café **every Saturday**. Come with me!  
**Haruka:** Yes, please! That’s number one on my list.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)

Milk first or tea first is a real argument in the UK. George Orwell wrote in 1946 that you should put the tea in first. Ask the student what they think.
Haruka says “English breakfast”, the name most Japanese people know (イングリッシュブレックファスト). Tom teaches her the British name: a **full English**.
Haruka’s UK list starts here and grows as she discovers new things. Tom’s café is next: Lesson 4.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Role play · Family photos

> **Student**
>
> Show or describe 2 or 3 people in your family.
>
> • Who are they? (my sister, my dad’s brother…)
> • What are they like?
> • How often do you see them?

> **Teacher**
>
> You are a new friend.
>
> • Ask: Who’s this? What’s she like?
> • Ask: How often do you see him?
> • Say who they look like!

**Useful phrases**
- This is my…
- She looks like…
- He’s a bit…
- I see them once a…
- Who’s this?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Do it twice: student first, then swap. (~2.5 min)
If the student has a photo on their phone, use it! Note one or two errors with ’s or frequency words to correct at the end.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Reading · The Sunday roast

[image: Photo idea: Yorkshire puddings and roast potatoes with gravy]

On Sundays, many families in the UK eat a big lunch together. It’s called a **Sunday roast**. There is roast meat (usually chicken, beef, lamb or pork), roast potatoes, vegetables and **gravy**. Many people also have **Yorkshire puddings**. They aren’t sweet!

Some families have a roast every week. Others have one once a month, or only on special days. You can also have a Sunday roast in a lot of pubs.

Each meat often has its own sauce: mint sauce with lamb, apple sauce with pork, and horseradish sauce (hot and spicy) with beef. And after lunch? Many people have a **pudding**, or just a cup of tea!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. Correct pronunciation at the end of each paragraph. (~2.5 min)
Check: What’s in a Sunday roast? Where else can you have one? Which sauce goes with lamb?
Horseradish = 西洋わさび. Mint sauce surprises many visitors: it’s sour, made with mint and vinegar.

</details>


#### Slide 19: Reading · True or false?

- A Sunday roast is a big dinner on Saturday evening.
- Yorkshire puddings are sweet.
- You can have a Sunday roast in a pub.
- People usually have mint sauce with beef.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: it’s a big lunch on Sunday.  2. False: they aren’t sweet.  3. True.  4. False: mint sauce goes with lamb. Beef has horseradish.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences in their own words. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 20: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a small gift wrapped in a furoshiki cloth]

- Does your family have a special meal together? How often?
- What do you bring when you visit someone’s home? Why?
- What do you call older people you know? Is it the same in English?
- Milk first or tea first? How do you have your tea or coffee?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student explain Japan; don’t tell them. Encourage longer answers with “Why?” and “For example?” (~3 min)
Background: in the UK people often bring something small when they visit for a meal, such as flowers, chocolates or a bottle of wine. It isn’t a strict rule.

</details>


#### Slide 21: Story · Call home

That evening, Haruka video-calls her mum in Osaka.

**Mum:** Haruka! How are you? Are you eating well?  
**Haruka:** Yes, Mum! Today I had a Sunday roast at Gwen’s flat.  
**Mum:** Who’s Gwen?  
**Haruka:** My landlady. She’s really kind. She cooks **every Sunday**!  
**Mum:** That’s nice. Did you take photos of the food?  
**Haruka:** Of course! I take photos of everything.  
**Mum:** Good. Send them to me. I want to see your new life.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

In real life they speak Japanese, of course. The dialogue is in English for practice. (~1.5 min)
Ask: How does Haruka feel? Does she miss home?
“I’m going to the pub” is a future plan. Just understand it for now; it comes back in Lesson 6. Next lesson: the pub with Tom.

</details>


#### Slide 22: Story · Tea rules

A moment later. Haruka and her mum.

**Haruka:** Oh, and Gwen has very serious tea rules. Tea first, milk second. Never milk first!  
**Mum:** Oh, really? Gwen sounds fun. So, do you miss home?  
**Haruka:** **A bit**. But I’m OK! On Friday I’m going to the pub with Tom.  
**Mum:** The pub? Hmm… OK. But call me **every week**, and tell me everything!  
**Haruka:** **Every week**. I promise.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Haruka tells Mum about Gwen’s tea rules from earlier today (Milk first?).
Ask: How often do you call your family? What do you tell them?

</details>


#### Slide 23: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- described people in your family
- used ’s and s’ (Kenta’s, my parents’)
- talked about your week with always, usually, every…
- learned about the Sunday roast

[image: Photo idea: milk being poured into a cup of tea]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Give one piece of positive feedback and one thing to practise. Preview the next lesson: Haruka goes to the pub and meets Tom’s friend Callum. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 3: Sorry, sorry, sorry

**Objective:** Students can make and respond to polite requests and apologies in everyday situations, such as ordering at a pub bar, using can and could (CEFR A2: requests, apologies).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 1 · Settling in · Lesson 3

**Sorry, sorry, sorry**

Today’s goal: ask for things politely and say sorry, British style.

[image: Photo idea: people standing along the bar in a busy British pub]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Haruka’s first night at a British pub. She waits at the bar, meets Tom’s friend Callum, and learns about rounds. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Haruka had Sunday lunch at Gwen’s (Lesson 2) and told her mum she was going to the pub with Tom on Friday.

This is the last lesson of Unit 1, so it has a progress check (slide 18) and a self-check on the wrap-up.

Timing: 23 slides, about 34 to 36 minutes. If time is short, skip True or false and the second practice slide.

Haruka: a real Japanese 26-year-old, not a stereotype. Her English is clear and correct but simple, like a real learner. British words come from the other characters.

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Going out

Talk with your teacher.

- Where do you usually go with friends after work?
- How often do you go out in the evening?
- Have you ever been to a British pub?

[image: Photo idea: the outside of a British pub in the evening]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it short and personal. Push for longer answers: answer, add a detail, ask back. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: Haruka waits at a crowded wooden pub bar, holding money. A bartender is pulling pints. The bartender serves a man next to Haruka, then another person. Haruka lifts her hand a little, then puts it down and sighs.]

- What happens when Haruka waits? Who gets served?
- How does Haruka feel? Why?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · At the bar

Friday evening at the pub. Haruka is waiting at the bar. Tom arrives.

**Tom:** Alright, Haruka? Have you got the drinks?  
**Haruka:** No! I’m waiting, but where’s the queue?  
**Tom:** There isn’t really a queue at the bar. The staff know who’s next.  
**Haruka:** Really? But it’s ten minutes now!  
**Tom:** Just smile, look at the barman and say “**Excuse me**”.  
**Haruka:** **Excuse me**! Could I have a pint and a glass of white wine, please?  
**Barman:** Of course, love. Sorry for the wait!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Haruka), then swap roles. (~2 min)

In British pubs there usually isn’t a proper line at the bar, but there is an “invisible queue”: the staff try to serve people in the order they arrived. You wait at the bar and try to catch their eye. (Kate Fox, Passport to the Pub.)
Watch for: Japanese learners sometimes say “Please give me a beer.” It sounds like an order. “Could I have…, please?” is much more polite.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- Where is Haruka?
- What is Haruka looking for?
- What does Tom tell her to say?
- What does Haruka order?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. At the bar in a pub.  2. The queue.  3. “Excuse me.”  4. A pint and a glass of white wine.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. If the student can’t answer, go back to the dialogue together. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · At the pub (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the bar | noun | カウンター | the place in a pub where you order drinks | Order your drinks at the **bar**. |
| a pint | noun | パイント（約568ml） | a big glass of beer (568 ml) | Could I have a **pint**, please? |
| a soft drink | noun | ソフトドリンク | a cold drink with no alcohol | I’ll have a **soft drink**, thanks. |
| a round | noun | （全員分の）一杯ずつのおごり | drinks one person buys for the whole group | It’s my **round**! What would you like? |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Drill: pint /paɪnt/ (not “pinto”), round /raʊnd/. (~1.5 min)
A British pint is 568 ml. “A half” means half a pint.
Ask: In Japan, what do you order first at an izakaya?

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Being polite (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Excuse me | phrase | すみません（呼びかけ） | you say it to get attention | **Excuse me**, could I order, please? |
| Sorry? | phrase | え、何ですか？ | I didn’t hear. Please say it again. | **Sorry?** Could you say that again? |
| I’m so sorry | phrase | 本当にすみません | a strong way to say sorry | Oh, **I’m so sorry**! |
| No worries | phrase | 気にしないで | it’s OK, don’t worry | “Sorry!” “**No worries**!” |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These four phrases come back all lesson. (~1.5 min)
Note: “Sorry?” with a rising voice means “I didn’t hear. Please say it again.” “No worries” is a friendly way to say “That’s OK”.

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · Meet Callum

Tom’s friend Callum arrives and says hello to Haruka.

**Callum:** Alright? You must be Haruka. I’m Callum.  
**Haruka:** Nice to meet you, Callum. Are you from London?  
**Callum:** No, I’m from Edinburgh. I work here now.  
**Haruka:** Oh! So you’re English?  
**Callum:** No, I’m Scottish! Edinburgh’s in Scotland!  
**Haruka:** Oh, **I’m so sorry**!  
**Callum:** **No worries**! It’s my **round**. What can I get you?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap roles. (~1.5 min)
In Japan, イギリス usually means the whole UK, so Haruka’s mistake is easy to make. Callum isn’t angry; he just corrects her.
Callum is from Edinburgh. He comes back in Lessons 5, 9 and 12.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Listening · Last food orders!

Later, the barman makes an announcement. Listen and answer.

[audio: At the bar]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Last food orders, please! The kitchen’s closing in ten minutes!

- What does “last orders” mean?
- What is closing?
- When is it closing?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. The last chance to order.
> 2. The kitchen.
> 3. In ten minutes.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Last food orders, please! The kitchen’s closing in ten minutes!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice: once for the general idea, once for details. Don’t show the transcript until after the questions. (~2 min)
The clip is new: it isn’t in the dialogues, so this checks real listening.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Culture · Four nations, one country

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| England → English | noun → adjective | イングランド（人） | capital: London | Tom is **English**. |
| Scotland → Scottish | noun → adjective | スコットランド（人） | capital: Edinburgh | Callum is **Scottish**. |
| Wales → Welsh | noun → adjective | ウェールズ（人） | capital: Cardiff | Gwen is **Welsh**. |
| Northern Ireland → Northern Irish | noun → adjective | 北アイルランド（人） | capital: Belfast | Belfast is in **Northern Ireland**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

All four nations are part of the UK. People may describe themselves as British, English, Scottish, Welsh or Northern Irish (and in Northern Ireland, some people feel Irish). Many Scottish, Welsh and Northern Irish people don’t like being called English. (~1.5 min)
Remember: Gwen is from Cardiff (Wales), Tom is from Manchester (England), Priya is from Birmingham (England) and Callum is from Edinburgh (Scotland).
Ask: Which nation would you like to visit first? Why?
Great Britain vs the UK: strictly, Great Britain is the island with England, Scotland and Wales. The United Kingdom adds Northern Ireland. In everyday speech many people say “Britain” for the whole UK, as the course title does.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Language focus · Asking politely

Use can and could to ask for things politely.

> **Can and could**
>
> *Asking for something*
>
> **Can I** have a pint, please? · **Could I** have the menu, please?
>
> *Asking someone to do something*
>
> **Could you** say that again, please?
>
> *Answers*
>
> **Of course.** · **Sure.** · **Sorry, I can’t.**

**From the story**
- Haruka: **Could I have** a pint and a glass of white wine, please?
- Barman: **Of course**, love. Sorry for the wait!
- Compare an offer: Would you like a cup of tea? (Lesson 1)

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

“Can I…?” is friendly. “Could I…?” is a little more polite. Both are fine in a pub or shop. (~1.5 min)
Watch for: “Please give me…” (sounds like an order) and forgetting “please”. Compare with an offer from Lesson 1: Would you like…?

</details>


#### Slide 12: Practice · What do you say?

- You want the menu.
- You didn’t hear the barman.
- You want to sit at a free table.
- You want some water.
- You want your friend to wait.
- You want to pay.

> **Possible answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Could I have the menu, please?
> 2. Sorry? Could you say that again?
> 3. Excuse me, can I sit here?
> 4. Could I have some water, please?
> 5. Could you wait for me, please?
> 6. Can I pay, please?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student makes a polite request for each situation. Then you answer, and the student replies. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Language focus · Sorry or excuse me?

In Japanese, すみません does all of these jobs. English uses different words.

> **Which one?**
>
> *Before*
>
> **Excuse me** → to get attention or get past
>
> *After*
>
> **Sorry!** → you made a mistake · **Sorry?** → you didn’t hear
>
> *Reply*
>
> **No worries.** · **That’s OK.**

**From the story**
- Tom: Just smile, look at the barman and say “**Excuse me**”.
- Haruka: Oh, **I’m so sorry**!
- Callum: **No worries**!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Japan link: すみません can mean all of these. English uses different words. Ask the student when they say すみません. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 14: Practice · Sorry, excuse me, or no worries?

- You walk into someone.
- You want to get past someone.
- You didn’t hear a question.
- Someone says sorry to you.
- You want the waiter to come.
- You arrive late.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Sorry!
> 2. Excuse me.
> 3. Sorry?
> 4. No worries. / That’s OK.
> 5. Excuse me!
> 6. Sorry I’m late!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick fire: read the situation, the student says the phrase. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Pronunciation · Sounding polite

> **Your voice matters**
>
> Could I have the menu, please? ↘ (warm, goes down at the end)
>
> Sorry? ↗ = I didn’t hear
>
> Sorry. ↘ = I apologise

**Say it with your teacher**
- **Excuse me** ↗, could I order, please?
- Could I have a pint, please? ↘
- Sorry? ↗ Could you say that again?
- Oh, I’m so sorry. ↘

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Say each line, the student repeats. Exaggerate at first. (~1 min)
A flat, level voice can sound bored or even rude in English, even with polite words. Start a little higher and let the voice fall at the end of a request.
“Sorry?” with a rising voice means “please repeat”. “Sorry.” with a falling voice is an apology. Say one, and ask the student which it was.

</details>


#### Slide 16: Story · Haruka’s round

An hour later, it’s Haruka’s turn to buy drinks for Tom and Callum.

**Tom:** Haruka, it’s your **round** next!  
**Haruka:** My round? Oh, OK! What would you like, Tom?  
**Tom:** Another pint, please. Cheers!  
**Haruka:** And you, Callum?  
**Callum:** Just a wee lemonade for me, thanks.  
**Haruka:** **Sorry?** **Could you** say that again, please?  
**Callum:** Sorry! “Wee” means small in Scotland. A small lemonade, please!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
“Wee” means small in Scotland. Callum’s accent and words are new for Haruka. They come back in Lesson 9.
Point out: Haruka asks Callum to repeat with “Sorry? Could you say that again, please?” This is today’s language in action.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Role play · At the bar

> **Student**
>
> You’re at a busy bar with two friends.
>
> • Get the barman’s attention.
> • Order drinks for everyone.
> • Ask one more question (crisps? the toilets?).

> **Teacher**
>
> You are the barman.
>
> • Serve someone else first: “Sorry, one minute!”
> • Say “Sorry?” once.
> • Ask: “Pint or half?”

**Useful phrases**
- Excuse me
- Could I have…, please?
- Can I…?
- Sorry?
- A pint / a half
- Cheers!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Do it twice, then swap. (~2.5 min)
Make it real: serve another customer first, and say “Sorry?” once so the student has to repeat. Note one or two errors to correct at the end.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Progress check · New neighbour

> **Student**
>
> You meet your new neighbour.
>
> • Say hello and introduce yourself.
> • Talk about your family and your week.
> • Offer a drink.
> • Ask a favour: Could you…?

> **Teacher**
>
> You are the new neighbour.
>
> • Ask questions and ask back.
> • Accept the drink.
> • Ask a favour too: “Can I borrow…?”

**Unit 1 phrases**
- Nice to meet you
- This is my…
- I usually…
- Would you like…?
- Could you…?
- Sorry / Excuse me

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Unit 1 progress check. Let the student lead and don’t correct during the role play. Note what they can do well and one thing to practise. (~3 min)
Check the Unit 1 can-dos: introduce yourself and add a detail, describe family and routines, offer something, ask for things politely, say sorry or excuse me. The student self-checks on the next slide.

</details>


#### Slide 19: Reading · Sorry, sorry, sorry

[image: Photo idea: a friend bringing a round of drinks to a pub table]

British people say **sorry** a lot. They say it when they make a mistake, but not only then. If someone walks into you, you might both say sorry! People also say “Sorry?” when they didn’t hear something.

In a pub, you usually order and pay at the **bar**. There usually isn’t a proper queue, but the staff try to serve people in order. Don’t shout or wave at them. Just wait, and smile when they look at you.

Friends often buy drinks in **rounds**. One person buys drinks for everyone. Then the next person buys the next round. Always buy your round!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. Correct pronunciation at the end of each paragraph. (~2.5 min)
Check: When do British people say sorry? How do you order in a pub? What is a round?
Source for the pub customs: Kate Fox, Passport to the Pub (Brewers and Licensed Retailers Association).

</details>


#### Slide 20: Reading · True or false?

- British people only say sorry when they make a mistake.
- In a pub, people stand in a neat queue at the bar.
- It’s a good idea to wave at the bar staff.
- In a round, one person buys drinks for everyone.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: also when someone walks into them, or they didn’t hear.  2. False: there isn’t usually a proper queue, but staff serve people in order.  3. False: just wait and smile.  4. True.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences in their own words. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: friends making a toast in a Japanese izakaya]

- When do you say すみません? Is it the same as “sorry”?
- When you go out with friends, how do you pay? What do you think of rounds?
- How is a British pub different from an izakaya?
- Is it easy to ask for things politely in English? Why? Why not?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student explain Japan; don’t tell them. Encourage longer answers with “Why?” and “For example?” (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 22: Story · Walking home

Haruka and Tom are walking home. A stranger walks into Haruka.

**Stranger:** Oh! **Sorry**, sorry!  
**Haruka:** No, no, **I’m sorry**!  
**Tom:** Haha! He walked into you, and you both said sorry. Very British!  
**Haruka:** In Japan we say “sumimasen” all the time too!  
**Tom:** So, tomorrow morning, a full English? My café opens at eight.  
**Haruka:** Yes! **Can** we go at nine? I’m tired!  
**Tom:** **Sure**. Nine o’clock. Don’t worry, I’m never late!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Two people saying sorry when only one made the mistake is very common in the UK.
Next lesson: a full English breakfast at Tom’s café. Tom says he’s never late… let the student guess what happens.

</details>


#### Slide 23: Wrap-up · Unit 1 check

**I can…**
- introduce myself and add a detail
- describe my family and my week
- ask for things politely (can, could)
- say sorry and excuse me at the right time

[image: Photo idea: a round of drinks on a pub table]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Self-check: for each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give one piece of positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1.5 min)
Preview Unit 2: Everyday Britain. Next lesson: a full English breakfast.

</details>


## Unit: Everyday Britain


### Lesson 4: A full English

**Objective:** Students can order food and drink in a café and ask simple questions about dishes, using countable and uncountable nouns (CEFR A2: ordering, requests).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 2 · Everyday Britain · Lesson 4

**A full English**

Today’s goal: order food in a café and ask what’s in a dish.

[image: Photo idea: a full English breakfast in a café]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Haruka’s first full English. Tom is late (again), Haruka meets black pudding, and on Monday Priya shows her a meal deal. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: after the pub (Lesson 3), Tom promised breakfast at nine and said, “I’m never late!”

Timing: 23 slides, about 32 to 34 minutes. If time is short, skip True or false and the second practice slide.

Haruka: a real Japanese 26-year-old, not a stereotype. Her English is clear and correct but simple. British words come from the other characters.

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Breakfast time

Talk with your teacher.

- What did you have for breakfast today?
- Do you ever eat breakfast at a café? Where?
- What is your favourite breakfast?

[image: Photo idea: a busy British café in the morning]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it short and personal. Push for longer answers: answer, add a detail, ask back. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: A café table: a waitress puts down two full English breakfasts in front of Haruka and Tom. Haruka picks up her phone and takes a photo of her plate. Tom picks up his fork, waits, then looks at Haruka and laughs.]

- What does Haruka do before she eats?
- What does Tom do? Why?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Sorry I’m late!

Saturday morning. Haruka waits for Tom at his favourite café.

**Tom:** Morning, Haruka! Sorry I’m late!  
**Haruka:** You said, “I’m never late!” Twenty minutes, Tom!  
**Tom:** I know, I know. Breakfast is on me, OK?  
**Waitress:** Morning, both. What can I get you?  
**Tom:** Two full English breakfasts and two teas, please.  
**Waitress:** White or brown toast?  
**Haruka:** Brown, please. And could I have some orange juice too?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Haruka), then swap roles. (~2 min)

“Breakfast is on me” means “I’ll pay”. It comes up on the British phrases slide.
“Two teas” is natural in a café: it means two cups of tea. This comes back in the Language focus.
Tom’s “I’m never late!” was in Lesson 3. Ask: is Tom a good friend? Why?

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- How late is Tom?
- Who is paying for breakfast?
- What do they order?
- What else does Haruka ask for?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Twenty minutes.  2. Tom.  3. Two full English breakfasts and two teas.  4. Brown toast and some orange juice.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. If the student can’t answer, go back to the dialogue together. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · Breakfast (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| baked beans | noun (plural) | ベイクドビーンズ | beans in a sweet tomato sauce | I love **baked beans** on toast. |
| scrambled eggs | noun (plural) | スクランブルエッグ | eggs mixed and cooked in a pan | Could I have **scrambled eggs**, please? |
| brown sauce | noun | ブラウンソース | a dark, sweet and sour sauce | Tom puts **brown sauce** on everything! |
| a slice of | phrase | 〜一切れ | one thin piece of bread, cake, etc. | Can I have **a slice of** toast? |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Drill: scrambled /ˈskræmbld/, sauce /sɔːs/ (not “so-su”). (~1.5 min)
Note: “toast” and “bread” are uncountable, so we say “a slice of toast”, not “a toast”. This comes back in the Language focus.
Brown sauce is a British sauce, a bit like Japanese ソース. HP Sauce is the best-known brand.

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · At the table (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to come with | verb | 〜が付いてくる | be served together with | Does it **come with** toast? |
| instead of | preposition | 〜の代わりに | in the place of something | Can I have tomatoes **instead of** mushrooms? |
| delicious | adjective | とてもおいしい | very, very good to eat | This is **delicious**! |
| full | adjective | おなかいっぱい | you can’t eat any more | No more, thanks. I’m **full**! |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words help the student order and talk about food. (~1.5 min)
Ask: Make a sentence with “instead of” about a Japanese meal.

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · What’s this?

The breakfasts arrive. Haruka takes a photo before she eats. Tom waits.

**Tom:** Haruka, stop taking photos! It’s getting cold.  
**Haruka:** One more! OK… What’s this round black thing?  
**Tom:** Try it first. Then I’ll tell you.  
**Haruka:** Mmm, it’s **delicious**! What’s in it?  
**Tom:** It’s black pudding. It’s made with pork blood, fat and oats.  
**Haruka:** Blood?! Well… it’s still delicious.  
**Tom:** Good answer. Is there any brown sauce?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~2 min)
Black pudding is usually made with pork blood, fat and oats. Like Yorkshire pudding in Lesson 2, it isn’t sweet.
Haruka’s photo before eating is one of the course’s running jokes.
Ask: Would you try black pudding? Before or after you know what’s in it?

</details>


#### Slide 9: Listening · Anything else?

Later, the waitress comes back to the table. Listen and answer.

[audio: At the café]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Is everything OK here? Can I get you anything else? More tea? Just let me know when you want the bill.

- What does the waitress offer?
- What does she ask first?
- What should they tell her later?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. More tea (anything else).
> 2. If everything is OK.
> 3. When they want the bill.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Is everything OK here? Can I get you anything else? More tea? Just let me know when you want the bill.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice: once for the general idea, once for details. Don’t show the transcript until after the questions. (~2 min)
The clip is new: it isn’t in the dialogues, so this checks real listening.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Vocabulary · British phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| It’s on me | phrase | 私がおごるよ | I’ll pay for it | Lunch **is on me** today! |
| Morning! | greeting | おはよう | a short way to say “Good morning” | “**Morning**, both!” |
| a fry-up | noun | （英国式の）朝食プレート | another name for a full English | I love a **fry-up** on Saturdays. |
| a cuppa | noun | お茶一杯 | a cup of tea (informal) | Do you want **a cuppa**? |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

“It’s on me” and “Morning!” were in the first dialogue. “A cuppa” is how “a cup of” sounds in fast speech. (~1.5 min)
Ask: In Japan, who usually pays when friends eat together?

</details>


#### Slide 11: Language focus · Count it or not?

> **Countable or uncountable?**
>
> *Countable*
>
> **an** egg · two **sausages** · How **many** eggs?
>
> *Uncountable*
>
> **some** toast · **some** juice · How **much** tea?
>
> *Count it like this*
>
> **a slice of** toast · **a cup of** tea · **a glass of** juice

**From the story**
- Tom: Two full English breakfasts and **two teas**, please.
- Haruka: Could I have **some** orange juice too?
- Tom: It’s made with pork **blood**, **fat** and **oats**.

Some food you can count (eggs, sausages). Some you can’t (toast, juice, tea).

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Food words are a common problem: Japanese doesn’t mark plural or countable. Go slowly and use the student’s own breakfast as examples. (~1.5 min)
“Two teas” works because it means two cups of tea. In cafés people often count drinks this way.
Watch for: “a toast”, “breads”, “many milk”.

</details>


#### Slide 12: Practice · A, an or some?

- ____ egg
- ____ toast
- ____ sausage
- ____ orange juice
- ____ baked beans
- ____ apple

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. an egg
> 2. some toast
> 3. a sausage
> 4. some orange juice
> 5. some baked beans
> 6. an apple

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student says the word with a, an or some, then uses it in a sentence: “Could I have some toast, please?” (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Language focus · Some or any?

> **Some, any, much, many**
>
> *Some*
>
> I’d like **some** tea. Could I have **some** water?
>
> *Any*
>
> Is there **any** milk? There isn’t **any** sugar.
>
> *Much or many*
>
> How **much** tea? (uncountable) · How **many** eggs? (countable)

**From the story**
- Haruka: Could I have **some** orange juice too?
- Tom: **Is there any** brown sauce?
- Waitress: What can I get you?

Use some and any to ask for food and say what there is.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it simple at A2: some in normal sentences, requests and offers; any in most questions and negatives. (~1.5 min)
Point out that “Could I have some…?” uses some even though it’s a question, because it’s a request.

</details>


#### Slide 14: Practice · At the café

- Is there ____ milk?
- Could I have ____ more tea?
- How ____ eggs would you like?
- How ____ sugar do you take?
- Sorry, there isn’t ____ bread.
- I’d like ____ toast, please.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. any
> 2. some
> 3. many
> 4. much
> 5. any
> 6. some

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick fire. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Pronunciation · No extra vowels

> **Don’t add a vowel**
>
> toast → **TOHST** (not tōsuto) · milk → **MILK** (not miruku)
>
> beans → **BEENZ** (not bīnzu) · sausage → **SOS**-ij (not sōsēji)
>
> Stop on the last sound: toas**t**, mil**k**, bean**s**

**Say it with your teacher**
- Could I have some **toast**?
- A cup of tea with **milk**, please.
- **Beans** on toast!
- Two **sausages**, please.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Say each line, the student repeats. (~1 min)
Katakana adds a vowel after most consonants (トースト = to-o-su-to). English words often end on a consonant: stop the sound there. Clap the beats: toast is 1 beat, sausage is 2.
Food words are perfect for this because most of them are already familiar in katakana.

</details>


#### Slide 16: Story · Meal deal

Monday lunchtime. Priya takes Haruka to the supermarket near the office.

**Priya:** Let’s get a meal deal. It’s a sandwich, a snack and a drink for one price.  
**Haruka:** Great! What do you usually get?  
**Priya:** A chicken katsu wrap. Katsu is my favourite curry!  
**Haruka:** Oh! Katsu isn’t curry. It’s the fried chicken or pork.  
**Priya:** Really? So what’s the curry called?  
**Haruka:** Just curry. In Japanese, we say “karē”.  
**Priya:** Wow. I always thought katsu was the name of the curry!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
A meal deal is a sandwich, a snack and a drink for one price. Most supermarkets and some chemists (like Boots) sell them. Prices change, so don’t give one.
The surprise: in the UK, “katsu” is often used to mean the curry. In Japanese it means the fried cutlet, and the curry is カレー. Ask the student to explain katsu, katsu curry and Japanese curry. The culture slide at the end explains the mix-up.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Role play · At the café

> **Student**
>
> You’re at a café for breakfast.
>
> • Order food and a drink.
> • Ask about one dish: “What’s in…?”
> • Change one thing: “Can I have… instead of…?”

**Useful phrases**
- Could I have some…?
- What’s in the…?
- Does it come with…?
- Can I have… instead of…?
- Is there any…?
- That’s all, thanks.

> **Teacher**
>
> You work at the café.
>
> • Ask: “What can I get you?”
> • Answer questions about the food.
> • Say “Sorry, there isn’t any…” once.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Do it twice, then swap. (~2.5 min)
Make it real: say “Sorry, there isn’t any…” once, so the student has to choose something else. Note one or two errors with a/an/some/any to correct at the end.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Reading · The full English

[image: Photo idea: a full English seen from above]

A **full English**, or a **fry-up**, is a big, hot breakfast. It usually has eggs, bacon, sausages, baked beans, mushrooms, tomatoes and toast. Some cafés also add black pudding. It’s a sausage made with pork blood, fat and oats.

It’s a big meal, so many people have it at the weekend, not every day. People often drink a cup of tea with it, and add ketchup or brown sauce.

Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland each have their own version, with different breads and extras.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. Correct pronunciation at the end of each paragraph. (~2.5 min)
Check: What’s in a full English? When do many people eat one? Which nations have their own version?
Regional names: full Scottish, full Welsh and Ulster fry (Northern Ireland). Source: Wikipedia, “Full breakfast”.

</details>


#### Slide 19: Reading · True or false?

- A full English is a cold breakfast.
- Black pudding is sweet.
- Some people eat a full English with brown sauce.
- Only England has a fry-up.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: it’s hot.  2. False: it’s a sausage made with blood, fat and oats.  3. True.  4. False: Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland have their own versions.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences in their own words. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 20: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a Japanese breakfast tray]

- What do you usually have for breakfast? How much, how many?
- Is there a Japanese food that visitors find strange? Why?
- Would you try black pudding? Why or why not?
- Japanese food abroad is often a little different. Is that OK?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student explain Japan; don’t tell them. Encourage longer answers with “Why?” and “For example?” (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Story · Next on the list

That evening, Haruka shows Gwen and Tom her UK list.

**Haruka:** Look! “English breakfast”. Done!  
**Gwen:** Lovely! And did you have any black pudding?  
**Haruka:** Yes! I didn’t know what it was, but I liked it.  
**Tom:** She ate it all! So, what’s next on your list?  
**Haruka:** Afternoon tea, Harry Potter spots, Edinburgh…  
**Tom:** Add Bonfire Night. It’s on the fifth of November.  
**Haruka:** Bonfire… Night? OK! I’ll write it down.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Haruka’s list grows as she learns about Britain. Don’t explain Bonfire Night yet: it’s Lesson 5. Ask the student to guess what it is.

</details>


#### Slide 22: Culture · Katsu? Which katsu?

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| katsu (in Japan) | noun | カツ（とんかつなど） | meat or fish fried in breadcrumbs | Tonkatsu is a pork **katsu**. |
| katsu (in the UK) | noun | （英国で）カレー味 | often used to mean Japanese curry or curry sauce | Let’s get a **katsu** wrap. I love that curry! |

> **Why the mix-up?**
>
> In Japanese, katsu is a fried cutlet. In the UK, “katsu” is often used to mean Japanese curry, so you can even buy katsu crisps with no cutlet! It became popular after Wagamama, a restaurant, put katsu curry on its menu in 1992.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Priya called Japanese curry “katsu” in the meal deal dialogue. This slide explains why. (~1.5 min)
Japan link: ask the student to explain katsu, katsu curry and Japanese curry. Then ask: are there English words that mean something different in Japanese? (e.g. smart, mansion, consent)
Sources: JETRO, “The story of how katsu curry conquered the UK”; Tesco and Walkers katsu curry crisps.

</details>


#### Slide 23: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- ordered breakfast in a café
- asked what’s in a dish
- used a, an, some and any with food
- learned about the full English and what “katsu” means in the UK

[image: Photo idea: a meal deal on an office desk]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Give one piece of positive feedback and one thing to practise. Preview the next lesson: November weather and Bonfire Night. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 5: Lovely weather, isn’t it?

**Objective:** Students can make small talk about the weather and compare the UK with Japan, using comparatives and superlatives (CEFR A2: describing and comparing things).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 2 · Everyday Britain · Lesson 5

**Lovely weather, isn’t it?**

Today’s goal: make small talk about the weather and compare the UK with Japan.

[image: Photo idea: a bonfire in a London park at night]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

November in London: grey mornings, dark afternoons and Haruka’s first Bonfire Night. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Haruka had her first full English (Lesson 4), and Tom told her to add Bonfire Night to her UK list.

Timing: 22 slides, about 32 to 34 minutes. If time is short, skip True or false and the second practice slide.

Haruka: a real Japanese 26-year-old, not a stereotype. Her English is clear and correct but simple. British words come from the other characters.

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · How’s the weather?

Talk with your teacher.

- What’s the weather like today where you are?
- What’s your favourite season? Why?
- What do you talk about with neighbours or people at work?

[image: Photo idea: a rainy London street in November]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it short and personal. Push for longer answers: answer, add a detail, ask back. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: A big bonfire in a park at night with a stuffed figure of a man (a “guy”) on top. Haruka, Tom and Callum stand in coats and hats. Fireworks burst in the sky. Haruka looks up at the bonfire, sees the figure on top, and points at it with a shocked face. Tom laughs.]

- What does Haruka point at?
- How does she feel? What do you think she says?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Lovely weather

A grey November morning. Haruka meets Gwen at the front door.

**Gwen:** Morning, love! Lovely weather, isn’t it?  
**Haruka:** Lovely? But it’s raining!  
**Gwen:** I’m joking! It’s horrible, and it’s getting colder every day.  
**Haruka:** Yes, it’s much colder than Osaka.  
**Gwen:** And the clocks went back last week, so it’s dark before five.  
**Haruka:** It gets dark earlier than in Osaka.  
**Gwen:** Wait until December. It’s the darkest month!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Haruka), then swap roles. (~2 min)
