“Lovely weather, isn’t it?” on a rainy day is a joke. Check the student noticed that Gwen is joking. The next slide explains British sarcasm.
The clocks go back one hour on the last Sunday in October, so evenings get dark very early. In London the sun sets at about half past four in early November, and before four by the end of the month.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- Was the weather good or bad?
- How is London different from Osaka?
- Why is it dark so early?
- What is the darkest month?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. No, it’s raining. Gwen is joking.  2. It’s colder, and it gets dark earlier.  3. The clocks went back.  4. December.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. If the student can’t answer, go back to the dialogue together. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Culture · Why “lovely”?

[image: Photo idea: someone smiling and giving a thumbs-up in the rain]

Gwen said “Lovely weather, isn’t it?” on a rainy day. She didn’t mean it! This is **sarcasm**: saying the opposite of what you actually mean.

You’ll often hear people in Britain joke like this about small problems, like bad weather or a long queue. It’s friendly, not rude. The other person often joins in: “Oh yes, perfect weather for the beach!”

How can you tell? Look at the situation. If it’s raining and someone says “lovely”, they’re using sarcasm. Just smile, or make a joke back.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together right after the first dialogue, so the joke makes sense. (~2 min)
Check: Did Gwen really think the weather was lovely? What is sarcasm? How can you tell when someone is joking?
Background: Kate Fox (Watching the English) describes irony as a constant part of English conversation. Ask the student: do people in Japan joke like this? When?
Also: weather is a safe topic for small talk in Britain because it changes a lot and everyone can join in.

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Weather (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| freezing | adjective | とても寒い | very, very cold | It’s **freezing** today! |
| grey | adjective | どんよりした | with lots of cloud and no sun | London is often **grey** in November. |
| a shower | noun | にわか雨 | a short time of rain | There’ll be **showers** this afternoon. |
| humid | adjective | 蒸し暑い | hot, with wet air | Osaka is very **humid** in summer. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Drill: freezing /ˈfriːzɪŋ/, humid /ˈhjuːmɪd/ (not “hyumiddo”). (~1.5 min)
Note: “a shower” is also what you have in the bathroom. In a weather forecast it means short rain.
Ask: Which word describes Japan in August? And London in November?

</details>


#### Slide 8: Vocabulary · Bonfire Night (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a bonfire | noun | たき火 | a big fire outside | We stood near the **bonfire** to keep warm. |
| fireworks | noun (plural) | 花火 | colourful lights that explode in the sky | The **fireworks** start at seven. |
| a sparkler | noun | 手持ち花火 | a small firework you hold in your hand | The children are holding **sparklers**. |
| a toffee apple | noun | りんご飴 | an apple covered in hard, sweet toffee | I bought a **toffee apple** at the party. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue. (~1.5 min)
Japan link: a toffee apple is very like りんご飴 at a summer festival. Ask: What do you eat at a hanabi festival?

</details>


#### Slide 9: Story · Bonfire Night

5 November. Tom and Callum take Haruka to a Bonfire Night party in the park.

**Callum:** Look at the **bonfire**! It’s huge!  
**Haruka:** Wow! But… is that a person on top?!  
**Tom:** No, it’s a “guy”. It’s a model of Guy Fawkes.  
**Haruka:** Who’s Guy Fawkes?  
**Callum:** He was a man who tried to blow up the Houses of Parliament in 1605.  
**Haruka:** And you burn him every year? That’s a bit scary!  
**Tom:** It’s tradition! Come on, the best toffee apples are over there.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~2 min)
Guy Fawkes was one of a group who planned to blow up the Houses of Parliament on 5 November 1605. The plan failed. Not every bonfire has a “guy” today, but many still do.
Ask: Is it strange to burn a model of a person? Is there anything like this in Japan?

</details>


#### Slide 10: Listening · The weather forecast

Before the party, Gwen’s radio is on. Listen and answer.

[audio: Weather forecast]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Tonight will be cold and dry, down to three degrees. Tomorrow, rain in the morning, but it’ll brighten up later.

- Will it rain tonight?
- How cold will it be tonight?
- What does “brighten up” mean?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. No, it’ll be dry.
> 2. Three degrees.
> 3. The weather will get better.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Tonight will be cold and dry, down to three degrees. Tomorrow, rain in the morning, but it’ll brighten up later.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice: once for the general idea, once for details. Don’t show the transcript until after the questions. (~2 min)
The clip is new: it isn’t in the dialogues, so this checks real listening.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Language focus · Colder than Osaka

> **Comparatives**
>
> *Short words*
>
> cold → **colder than** · dark → **darker than** · hot → **hotter than**
>
> *Long words*
>
> **more** beautiful **than** · **more** expensive **than**
>
> *Irregular*
>
> good → **better** · bad → **worse**   A lot: **much** colder

**From the story**
- Haruka: Yes, it’s **much colder than** Osaka.
- Haruka: It gets dark **earlier than** in Osaka.
- Gwen: It’s getting **colder** every day.

Use comparatives to compare two things.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Use the student’s own city. Japanese learners often say “more cold” or “colder than” with the wrong order (“Osaka is colder London”). (~1.5 min)
Spelling: one-syllable words ending in one vowel + one consonant double it: hot → hotter, big → bigger. Words ending in -y: early → earlier.

</details>


#### Slide 12: Practice · London or Osaka?

- hot  → Osaka is ____
- humid → Osaka is ____
- grey  → London is ____
- wet → Osaka is ____
- big → London is ____
- good  → ____

> **Possible answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. hotter than London
> 2. more humid than London
> 3. greyer than Osaka
> 4. wetter than London
> 5. bigger than Osaka
> 6. Any answer: I think… is better because…

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student makes a sentence comparing the two cities, then adds a detail. Facts: Osaka gets more than twice as much rain as London in a year, and its summers are much hotter and more humid. London often has light drizzle, so it can feel wetter than it is. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Language focus · The darkest month

> **Superlatives**
>
> *Short words*
>
> dark → **the darkest** · big → **the biggest** · hot → **the hottest**
>
> *Long words*
>
> **the most** beautiful · **the most** famous
>
> *Irregular*
>
> good → **the best** · bad → **the worst**

**From the story**
- Gwen: Wait until December. It’s **the darkest** month!
- Tom: Come on, **the best** toffee apples are over there.
- Callum: Look at the bonfire! It’s huge!

Use superlatives to compare three or more things.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Superlatives compare one thing with all the others. Always use “the”. (~1.5 min)
Watch for: “most cold” and missing “the” (“It’s darkest month”).

</details>


#### Slide 14: Practice · How about you?

- What’s the hottest month where you live?
- What’s the best season in Japan?
- What’s the most beautiful place you know?
- What’s the worst weather for you?
- What’s the biggest festival in your town?
- What’s the coldest place you’ve visited?

> **Example answer** (teacher only)
>
> August is the hottest month in Osaka. It’s really humid, so I stay inside with the air conditioner!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask each question. The student answers with a superlative and adds a reason. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Pronunciation · The th sound

> **Tongue between your teeth**
>
> Soft **th**: **th**anks · **th**ink · **Th**ursday
>
> With your voice: **th**e · **th**an · wea**th**er
>
> Not s or z: **th**ink ≠ sink · **th**ank ≠ sank

**Say it with your teacher**
- **Th**ank you!
- It’s colder **th**an Osaka.
- **Th**e wea**th**er is terrible.
- I **th**ink it’ll rain on **Th**ursday.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Show the student your tongue between your teeth, then blow air out. (~1 min)
Japanese learners often replace th with s or z (“sank you”, “zey”) or with d. The soft th (think) has no voice; the th in the, than and weather has voice: touch your throat to feel it.
Comparatives from today use a lot of th: than, the, weather.

</details>


#### Slide 16: Story · Fireworks

The fireworks start. Haruka, Tom and Callum watch from the park.

**Callum:** So, Haruka, are Japanese fireworks **better than** this?  
**Haruka:** Hmm… Japanese fireworks are **bigger**. But this is fun too!  
**Tom:** Bigger? Wow. When do you have them?  
**Haruka:** In summer when it’s hot. And people wear yukata.  
**Callum:** Next year, come to Edinburgh. It’s even **colder**!  
**Haruka:** **Colder than** this? No thanks!  
**Tom:** Come on. It’ll rain soon. Let’s go home.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Large Japanese fireworks festivals use some of the biggest fireworks shells in the world, so Haruka has a point. Let the student describe a hanabi festival.
“It’ll rain soon” is will for a prediction.
Callum’s invitation to Edinburgh comes back at the end of the course.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Role play · Small talk with a neighbour

> **Student**
>
> You meet your British neighbour outside.
>
> • Answer the weather comment.
> • Compare the weather with Japan.
> • Ask a question back.

**Useful phrases**
- It’s freezing!
- It’s much colder than…
- In Japan, it’s…
- It’ll rain later.
- What about you?
- See you later!

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re a friendly British neighbour.
>
> • Start: “Lovely weather, isn’t it?” (it’s raining)
> • Ask about the weather in Japan.
> • Say: “It’ll brighten up later.”

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Do it twice, then swap. (~2.5 min)
Start with “Lovely weather, isn’t it?” on a rainy day and see if the student catches the joke. Note one or two errors with comparatives to correct at the end.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Reading · Bonfire Night

[image: Photo idea: fireworks over a park on Bonfire Night]

Every year on 5 November, people in Britain celebrate **Bonfire Night**. In 1605, a group of men, including Guy Fawkes, planned to blow up the Houses of Parliament. Their plan failed, and people lit bonfires to celebrate.

Today, families go to big **fireworks** displays in parks. Some people put a model of Guy Fawkes, called a “guy”, on top of the **bonfire**. People eat **toffee apples** and hold **sparklers**. There is a famous rhyme British people say for this holiday: “Remember, remember, the fifth of November.”

It’s usually cold, so everyone wears hats, scarves and gloves.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. Correct pronunciation at the end of each paragraph. (~2.5 min)
Check: What happened in 1605? What do people do on Bonfire Night? What is a “guy”?
Source: English Heritage, “The real story of Bonfire Night”.

</details>


#### Slide 19: Reading · True or false?

- Bonfire Night is in December.
- Guy Fawkes planned to blow up Parliament.
- The plan worked.
- Some people put a “guy” on the bonfire.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: it’s on 5 November.  2. True.  3. False: it failed.  4. True.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences in their own words. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 20: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a summer hanabi festival in Japan]

- How is Bonfire Night different from a hanabi festival?
- Do people in Japan talk about the weather? What do they say?
- What’s the best season in Japan? Why?
- Is British weather worse than Japanese weather? Why?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student explain Japan; don’t tell them. Encourage comparatives and superlatives. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Story · Party invitation

Late November. Priya finds Haruka at her desk.

**Priya:** Haruka, are you free on the fifteenth of December?  
**Haruka:** I think so. Why?  
**Priya:** It’s the office Christmas party! You have to come.  
**Haruka:** A Christmas party with everyone from work?  
**Priya:** Yes! Food, dancing and Christmas jumpers.  
**Haruka:** Christmas jumpers? What are they?  
**Priya:** You’ll see. I’ll help you find one!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Many British workplaces have a Christmas party in December, and people often wear Christmas jumpers (knitted sweaters with Christmas pictures). Don’t explain too much: it’s Lesson 6.
Ask: Does your company have a party at the end of the year? (忘年会)

</details>


#### Slide 22: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- made small talk about the weather
- compared the UK and Japan (colder than, the best)
- made a prediction with will
- learned about Bonfire Night

[image: Photo idea: a hand holding a sparkler]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Give one piece of positive feedback and one thing to practise. Preview the next lesson: Christmas plans and the office party. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 6: Christmas plans

**Objective:** Students can talk about future arrangements with the present continuous, and invite someone, accept or say no politely (CEFR A2: invitations and arrangements).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 2 · Everyday Britain · Lesson 6

**Christmas plans**

Today’s goal: talk about your plans, and invite someone or say no politely.

[image: Photo idea: a Christmas dinner table with crackers]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

December: Christmas plans, an office party and Haruka’s first British Christmas dinner. This is the last lesson of Unit 2, so it has a progress check (slide 18) and a self-check on the wrap-up. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Priya invited Haruka to the office Christmas party (Lesson 5) and promised to explain Christmas jumpers.

Timing: 23 slides, about 34 to 36 minutes. If time is short, skip True or false and the second practice slide.

Haruka: a real Japanese 26-year-old, not a stereotype. Her English is clear and correct but simple. British words come from the other characters.

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · End of the year

Talk with your teacher.

- What do you usually do at the end of the year?
- Do you celebrate Christmas? How?
- What’s your favourite holiday of the year? Why?

[image: Photo idea: Christmas lights on a London street]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it short and personal. Push for longer answers: answer, add a detail, ask back. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: Haruka at her office desk. Priya walks over holding up a bright, funny Christmas jumper with a reindeer on it. Priya holds the jumper against Haruka. Haruka looks at it, laughs and shakes her head. Priya nods: yes!]

- What does Priya show Haruka?
- Does Haruka like it? How do you know?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · What are you doing for Christmas?

Early December. Haruka and Tom are in the kitchen.

**Tom:** So, what are you doing for Christmas, Haruka?  
**Haruka:** Nothing special. In Japan, Christmas Eve is often for couples and friends.  
**Tom:** Really? Here it’s a family day. I’m going home to Manchester on the 23rd.  
**Haruka:** Oh. Is everyone going home?  
**Tom:** Most people, yes. London gets really quiet.  
**Haruka:** In Japan, New Year is our big family holiday.  
**Tom:** Then this year, you need to experience a British Christmas!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Haruka), then swap roles. (~2 min)

Japan link: in Japan, Christmas Eve is often a day for couples, and many families eat fried chicken (KFC has run a Christmas campaign in Japan since 1974) and Christmas cake. New Year (お正月) is the big family holiday. For many people in the UK, Christmas is the main family time. Let the student explain.
Point out “I’m going home on the 23rd”: present continuous for a plan with a date.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- What is Haruka doing for Christmas?
- Where is Tom going? When?
- What happens to London at Christmas?
- What is the big family holiday in Japan?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Nothing special.  2. Home to Manchester, on the 23rd.  3. It gets really quiet.  4. New Year.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. If the student can’t answer, go back to the dialogue together. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · Christmas (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a Christmas jumper | noun | クリスマスセーター | a warm top with Christmas pictures on it | Everyone wears a **Christmas jumper** at the party. |
| Christmas Eve | noun | クリスマスイブ | 24 December | The shops close early on **Christmas Eve**. |
| Boxing Day | noun | ボクシング・デー（12月26日） | 26 December, a public holiday in the UK | We watch football on **Boxing Day**. |
| a present | noun | プレゼント | something you give to someone | We open our **presents** in the morning. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Drill: jumper /ˈdʒʌmpə/. (~1.5 min)
Note: British English says “jumper”; American English says “sweater”. Boxing Day has nothing to do with the sport.
Ask: When do people give presents in Japan?

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Making plans (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| free | adjective | 空いている | not busy | **Are you free** on Friday? |
| to move | verb | （日時を）変更する | change the day or time of something | They **moved** the party to the 18th. |
| to cancel | verb | キャンセルする | say that something will not happen | Sorry, I need to **cancel** our lunch. |
| on your own | phrase | 一人で | alone, with nobody else | Are you spending Christmas **on your own**? |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogues. (~1.5 min)
Ask: Make a sentence with “free” about this week.

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · Party news

At work. Priya has news for Haruka about the office party.

**Priya:** Bad news. The Christmas party is moving to the 18th.  
**Haruka:** Oh no! I’m working late that day.  
**Priya:** Can you come after work? We’re starting at seven, but it finishes late.  
**Haruka:** Yes, I think so. Are you going to wear a Christmas jumper?  
**Priya:** Of course! Everyone is. **Are you free** at lunchtime tomorrow?  
**Haruka:** Yes. Why?  
**Priya:** We’re going shopping. You need a jumper too!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Count the plans with be + -ing (is moving, I’m working, we’re starting, are you wearing, we’re going). They all talk about the future.
Office Christmas parties are very common in the UK, and Christmas jumpers are a popular way to dress up for them.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Listening · In the shop

Priya and Haruka go shopping for Christmas jumpers. Listen and answer.

[audio: Shop announcement]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Good afternoon, shoppers! All Christmas jumpers are half price today. You can find them on the first floor, next to the lifts.

- What’s half price?
- Where are the jumpers?
- Is it a good day to buy one? Why?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Christmas jumpers.
> 2. On the first floor, next to the lifts.
> 3. Yes, they’re half price.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Good afternoon, shoppers! All Christmas jumpers are half price today. You can find them on the first floor, next to the lifts.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice: once for the general idea, once for details. Don’t show the transcript until after the questions. (~2 min)
The clip is new: it isn’t in the dialogues, so this checks real listening.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Vocabulary · British phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| What are you up to? | phrase | 何するの？ | What are you doing? (informal) | **What are you up to** this weekend? |
| a do | noun | パーティー | a party (informal) | Are you coming to the Christmas **do**? |
| Secret Santa | noun | シークレットサンタ（プレゼント交換） | everyone buys one small present for one person, chosen at random | I got Tom in **Secret Santa**! |
| Happy Christmas! | phrase | メリークリスマス！ | another way to say “Merry Christmas!” | Bye! **Happy Christmas!** |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

All four are very common at work and with friends in December. (~1.5 min)
Secret Santa is a popular office tradition. Ask: Is there a present exchange like this in Japan?

</details>


#### Slide 11: Language focus · Plans for the future

> **Present continuous for plans**
>
> *Plans*
>
> I**’m going** home on the 23rd. · We**’re starting** at seven.
>
> *Questions*
>
> What **are** you **doing** for Christmas? · **Are** you **wearing** a jumper?
>
> *Time words*
>
> **on** the 23rd · **on** Friday · **at** seven · **this** weekend

**From the story**
- Tom: **I’m going** home to Manchester on the 23rd.
- Priya: The Christmas party **is moving** to the 18th.
- Tom: So, **what are you doing** for Christmas?

Use be + -ing for plans you have already arranged.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Japanese learners often use “will” or the present simple for plans (“I go home tomorrow”). For arranged plans with a time or place, the present continuous sounds natural. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 12: Practice · What are you doing…?

- this evening
- on Saturday
- next weekend
- on New Year’s Day
- for your next holiday
- after this lesson

> **Example answer** (teacher only)
>
> I’m meeting a friend for dinner this evening. We’re going to a new ramen place. What about you?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask “What are you doing…?” for each time. The student answers with a real plan, adds a detail, and asks back. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Language focus · Inviting and saying no

> **Invitations**
>
> *Inviting*
>
> **Would you like to** come? · **Are you free** on Friday? · **How about** Saturday?
>
> *Saying yes*
>
> **I’d love to!** · **That sounds great.**
>
> *Saying no politely*
>
> **Sorry, I can’t.** I’m working late. · **Can we make it** another day?

**From the story**
- Priya: **Are you free** at lunchtime tomorrow?
- Priya: **Can you come** after work?
- Haruka: Oh no! I have a big deadline that day.

Use these phrases to invite people, and to say yes or no politely.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

In the UK a plain “No” can sound quite direct. People say sorry, give a short reason, and often suggest another time. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 14: Practice · Yes or no?

- Would you like to get a coffee after work?
- Are you free on Saturday evening?
- How about lunch tomorrow?
- Would you like to come to my party?
- Are you free this weekend?
- Do you want to watch a film tonight?

> **Example answers** (teacher only)
>
> I’d love to!
>
> Sorry, I can’t. I’m meeting my sister. Can we make it Sunday?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read each invitation. The student says yes, or says no politely with a reason (using be + -ing). Skip this slide if time is short. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Pronunciation · Thirteen or thirty?

> **Listen for the stress**
>
> 13 thir**TEEN** · 30 **THIR**ty
>
> 18 eigh**TEEN** · 80 **EIGH**ty
>
> Dates: the 18th → the eigh**TEENTH** · the 23rd → the twenty-**THIRD**

**Say it with your teacher**
- The party is on the **18th**.
- I’m going home on the **23rd**.
- It’s **thirty** pounds, not **thirteen**.
- We’re starting at **seven**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Say a number or a date; the student writes it down. Then swap. (~1 min)
-teen numbers stress the end and have a long “teen”. -ty numbers stress the start, and the t is soft. This matters for plans: times, dates and prices.
Dates: people say “the eighteenth of December” or “December the eighteenth”.

</details>


#### Slide 16: Story · An invitation

A week before Christmas. Gwen knocks on Haruka’s door.

**Gwen:** Haruka, what are you doing on Christmas Day?  
**Haruka:** Nothing. Tom’s going home, so I’m staying here.  
**Gwen:** **On your own**? No, no. **Would you like to** have Christmas dinner with me?  
**Haruka:** Really? **I’d love to!** Is anyone else coming?  
**Gwen:** My daughter Sian is coming from Cardiff. You saw her photo.  
**Haruka:** Yes! Can I bring something?  
**Gwen:** Just yourself, love. And maybe some of those Japanese sweets!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Gwen saw Sian’s photo with Haruka in Lesson 2, and Haruka brought Japanese sweets then too.
“Just yourself” is a friendly way to say “You don’t need to bring anything”.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Role play · Make a plan

> **Student**
>
> You want to meet your teacher this week.
>
> • Invite them: coffee, lunch or a film.
> • If they’re busy, suggest another day.
> • Agree a day, time and place.

**Useful phrases**
- Are you free on…?
- Would you like to…?
- How about…?
- Sorry, I can’t. I’m…
- I’d love to!
- See you on…!

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re a friend with a busy week.
>
> • Say no to the first day, with a reason.
> • Use be + -ing: “I’m working late.”
> • Say yes to the second idea.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Do it twice, then swap. (~2.5 min)
Make it real: say you’re busy on the first day the student suggests, so they have to suggest another day. Note one or two errors with be + -ing to correct at the end.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Progress check · Coffee with a friend

> **Student**
>
> You meet a friend at a café.
>
> • Order food and a drink. Ask what’s in one dish.
> • Make small talk about the weather.
> • Plan to meet again next week.

**Unit 2 phrases**
- Could I have some…?
- What’s in the…?
- It’s much colder than…
- Are you free on…?
- How about…?
- Sorry, I can’t. I’m…

> **Teacher**
>
> Play two people: the café worker, then the friend.
>
> • Say “Sorry, there isn’t any…” once.
> • Say “Lovely weather, isn’t it?”
> • Be busy on the first day they suggest.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Unit 2 progress check. Let the student lead and don’t correct during the role play. Note what they do well and one thing to practise. (~3 min)
Check the Unit 2 can-dos: order food and ask about a dish, make small talk about the weather, compare things, talk about plans, invite someone and say no politely. The student self-checks on the wrap-up slide.

</details>


#### Slide 19: Reading · Christmas in the UK

[image: Photo idea: two people pulling a Christmas cracker]

For many British families, Christmas Day (25 December) is the biggest family day of the year. Many people open **presents** in the morning. Then they have Christmas dinner: usually roast turkey with vegetables, and Christmas pudding after.

At the table, people pull Christmas crackers. Two people pull, and it goes BANG! Inside, there is a paper hat, a small toy and a joke. Many people also watch the King’s Christmas message on TV in the afternoon at 3 o'clock.

The next day, 26 December, is **Boxing Day**. It’s a public holiday, and people relax, watch football or go shopping in the sales.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. Correct pronunciation at the end of each paragraph. (~2.5 min)
Check: What do people eat for Christmas dinner? What’s inside a cracker? What is Boxing Day?
The monarch’s Christmas message has been broadcast at 3 pm on Christmas Day for many years; it is now the King’s Christmas message.

</details>


#### Slide 20: Reading · True or false?

- Christmas dinner is usually fish.
- There is a joke inside a cracker.
- The King’s message is on TV at 3 pm.
- Boxing Day is on 24 December.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: it’s usually roast turkey.  2. True.  3. True.  4. False: it’s on 26 December.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences in their own words. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a Japanese New Year osechi box]

- What do people in Japan do at Christmas?
- What does your family do at New Year?
- Which do you like more, Christmas or New Year? Why?
- Does your company have an end-of-year party? What’s it like?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student explain Japan; don’t tell them. Encourage longer answers with “Why?” and “For example?” (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 22: Story · My first cracker

Christmas Day at Gwen’s flat. Gwen, Sian and Haruka are at the table.

**Sian:** Haruka, pull this Christmas cracker with me. Ready?  
**Haruka:** Pull it? OK… Oh! What’s this?  
**Gwen:** A paper hat, a toy and a joke. Put your hat on!  
**Sian:** Now read the joke!  
**Haruka:** “What do snowmen eat for lunch? Iceburgers!” Hmm… I don’t get it.  
**Gwen:** No worries. Cracker jokes are always terrible!  
**Haruka:** My first cracker! I’m adding it to my list, and it’s done!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
The joke: “iceburgers” sounds like “icebergs” (big pieces of ice) and “burgers”. Cracker jokes are famous for being bad, and groaning at them is part of the fun.
Next lesson (Unit 3): it’s February, and Haruka tries to meet Tom in Camden.

</details>


#### Slide 23: Wrap-up · Unit 2 check

**I can…**
- order food and ask what’s in a dish
- make small talk and compare things
- talk about my plans (I’m going…)
- invite someone and say no politely

[image: Photo idea: a paper Christmas cracker crown]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Self-check: for each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give one piece of positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1.5 min)
Preview Unit 3: Exploring. Next lesson: getting around London.

</details>


## Unit: Exploring


### Lesson 7: Getting around London

**Objective:** Students can ask for and give simple directions and travel advice, using prepositions of movement and the first conditional (CEFR A2: directions, travel information).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 3 · Exploring · Lesson 7

**Getting around London**

Today’s goal: ask for and give directions, and give travel advice.

[image: Photo idea: a red telephone box on a London street]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

February. Haruka travels across London on her own to meet Tom in Camden. She takes the wrong train, asks for help, and finds a red phone box. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Unit 2 ended with Haruka’s first British Christmas. This is the first lesson of Unit 3, Exploring.

Timing: 24 slides, about 32 to 34 minutes. If time is short, skip True or false, the articles practice and the second practice slide.

Haruka: a real Japanese 26-year-old, not a stereotype. Her English is clear and correct but simple. British words come from the other characters.

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Getting around

Talk with your teacher.

- How do you usually get to work or school?
- Have you ever got lost in a city? What happened?
- What do you use to find your way: a map, an app, or people?

[image: Photo idea: a train arriving at a London Underground platform]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it short and personal. Push for longer answers: answer, add a detail, ask back. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: Haruka sits on a London Underground train, looking at the line map above the doors. No readable text. She looks at the map, then at the window, then back at the map. Her eyes go wide, she puts her hand on her head, and she stands up as the doors open.]

- What does Haruka realise?
- What does she do next?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · How do I get to Camden?

Friday evening. Haruka asks Tom how to get to Camden tomorrow.

**Haruka:** How do I get to Camden Market from here?  
**Tom:** Easy. Take the Northern line to Camden Town. Go northbound, not southbound!  
**Haruka:** OK. And from the station?  
**Tom:** Turn left and walk **past** the pub. The market’s next to the canal.  
**Haruka:** Which pub?  
**Tom:** Good question. If you get lost, call me.  
**Haruka:** OK. Meet at eleven? Don’t be late this time!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Haruka), then swap roles. (~2 min)

British people often give directions using pubs, but there are lots of pubs, which is Tom’s joke. Camden Market is next to Regent’s Canal, a short walk from Camden Town station.
Haruka’s “Don’t be late this time!” is the running joke: Tom was late in Lesson 4.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- Which line does Haruka need?
- Should she go northbound or southbound?
- Where is the market?
- What time are they meeting?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. The Northern line.  2. Northbound.  3. Next to the canal.  4. At eleven.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. If the student can’t answer, go back to the dialogue together. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · On the Tube (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a line | noun | （地下鉄の）路線 | one route on the Underground | Take the Northern **line**. |
| a platform | noun | ホーム | where you wait for the train | The train leaves from **platform** 2. |
| northbound | adjective | 北行きの | going north | This is the **northbound** train. |
| to get off | verb | 降りる | to leave a train or bus | **Get off** at the next stop. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Drill: platform /ˈplætfɔːm/ (not “hōmu”). (~1.5 min)
Note: in Japanese ホーム comes from “platform”. In English, “home” means your house, so check the student says “platform”.
Southbound, eastbound and westbound work the same way.

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Directions (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| opposite | preposition | 〜の向かいに | on the other side, facing it | The café is **opposite** the station. |
| past | preposition | 〜を通り過ぎて | go by something and continue | Walk **past** the pub. |
| along | preposition | 〜に沿って | from one end towards the other | Walk **along** the canal. |
| on the corner | phrase | 角に | where two streets meet | The shop is **on the corner**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words help with directions in the language focus. (~1.5 min)
Ask: Describe where your nearest station is, using two of these words.

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · Wrong way!

Saturday. Haruka’s phone is dead, so she asks a passenger on the Tube.

**Haruka:** Excuse me. Is this train going to Camden Town?  
**Passenger:** Sorry, no. This one’s going south.  
**Haruka:** Oh no! How do I get there?  
**Passenger:** **Get off** at the next stop and go to the other **platform**.  
**Haruka:** The other platform… **northbound**?  
**Passenger:** That’s right. If you take the next train, you’ll be there in fifteen minutes.  
**Haruka:** Thank you so much!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Point out how polite and short Haruka’s first question is: “Excuse me. Is this train going to…?”
Ask: What do you do in Japan if you take the wrong train?

</details>


#### Slide 9: Listening · On the train

Haruka hears an announcement on the Tube. Listen and answer.

[audio: Tube announcement]

*Transcript (teacher only):* This is a Northern line train to Morden. Please mind the gap between the train and the platform.

- Which line is Haruka on?
- Where is the train going?
- What does “mind the gap” mean?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. The Northern line.
> 2. To Morden (in south London, so the wrong way!).
> 3. Be careful of the space between the train and the platform.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> This is a Northern line train to Morden. Please mind the gap between the train and the platform.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice: once for the general idea, once for details. Don’t show the transcript until after the questions. (~2 min)
The clip is new: it isn’t in the dialogues, so this checks real listening.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Vocabulary · Tube words

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the Tube | noun | ロンドンの地下鉄 | the London Underground | I take **the Tube** to work. |
| Mind the gap | phrase | 足元にご注意ください | be careful of the space between the train and the platform | “**Mind the gap**,” says the announcement. |
| to touch in / out | verb | （カードを）タッチする | to touch your card on the reader at the start and end | Don’t forget to **touch out**! |
| Stand on the right | phrase | エスカレーターは右に立つ | the rule on Tube escalators | **Stand on the right**, walk on the left. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

All four are part of everyday London travel. “Mind the gap” is one of the most famous announcements on the Tube. (~1.5 min)
Japan link: in Osaka people also stand on the right on escalators; in Tokyo they usually stand on the left. Ask the student what they do.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Language focus · Giving directions

> **Directions**
>
> *Moving*
>
> **go along** the road · **go past** the pub · **turn left** / **turn right**
>
> *Where is it?*
>
> **next to** the canal · **opposite** the station · **on the corner**
>
> *Asking*
>
> **How do I get to** Camden? · **Is this the way to** the station?

**From the story**
- Haruka: **How do I get to** Camden Market from here?
- Tom: **Turn left** and walk **past** the pub.
- Tom: The market’s **next to** the canal.

Use these words to give directions and say where places are.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Use real places the student knows. Japanese learners often mix up “in front of” and “opposite”: opposite means facing, on the other side of the road. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 12: Practice · Where is it?

- café / opposite / station
- bank / next to / pub
- go / past / church
- go / along / river
- turn left / bridge
- shop / on the corner

> **Possible answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. The café is opposite the station.
> 2. The bank is next to the pub.
> 3. Go past the church.
> 4. Go along the river.
> 5. Turn left at the bridge.
> 6. The shop is on the corner.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student makes a full sentence from each prompt. Then ask about real places near the student’s home. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Practice · A, the or nothing?

**a, the or – (nothing)?**
- Is there ____ café near here?
- Turn left at ____ station.
- Take ____ Northern line.
- There’s ____ red phone box on the corner.
- ____ Tube is very busy today.
- I go to work by ____ train.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. a (one of many)
> 2. the (we both know which)
> 3. the (line names)
> 4. a (first time)
> 5. The (only one)
> 6. – (by train)

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student chooses a, the or nothing (–), and says why. Articles are hard for Japanese learners because Japanese doesn’t have them, so keep it short and come back to it when you hear mistakes. (~2 min)
Simple rules:
• a / an: one of many, or the first time you mention it (a café, a red phone box).
• the: we both know which one, or there’s only one (the station near here, the Tube). Tube and Underground lines use the (the Northern line).
• nothing: by train, by bus, at home, go to work.

</details>


#### Slide 14: Language focus · If you take the next train…

> **First conditional**
>
> *If + present*
>
> **If you take** the Northern line, …
>
> *will + verb*
>
> … **you’ll get** there in twenty minutes.
>
> *Careful*
>
> **If you get** lost, call me. (not “If you will get”)

**From the story**
- Passenger: **If you take** the next train, **you’ll be** there in fifteen minutes.
- Tom: **If you get** lost, call me.

Use if + present, then will + verb, to give travel advice.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it to real, possible situations (first conditional). Japanese learners often put will in both parts: “If you will take…”. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Practice · Travel advice

- If you take a taxi, you ____ there faster.
- If you walk along the canal, you ____ the market.
- If you miss this train, you ____ late.
- If you go at rush hour, it ____ very busy.
- If you stand on the left, people ____ angry!
- If you touch out, you ____ the right price.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. ’ll get
> 2. ’ll see
> 3. ’ll be
> 4. ’ll be
> 5. ’ll get
> 6. ’ll pay

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student completes each sentence with will + verb. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 16: Pronunciation · L and R

> **Two different sounds**
>
> **L**: your tongue touches behind your top teeth → **l**eft · **l**ine · p**l**atform
>
> **R**: your tongue doesn’t touch anything → **r**ight · **r**ed · t**r**ain
>
> **l**ight ≠ **r**ight · **l**ong ≠ w**r**ong

**Say it with your teacher**
- Turn **l**eft, then turn **r**ight.
- Take the Northern **l**ine.
- Go to p**l**atform two.
- Look, a **r**ed phone box!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student watch your mouth. Practise the pairs slowly, then fast. (~1 min)
Japanese ら行 sounds are between English l and r, so both can be hard. For l, the tongue touches behind the top teeth; for r, the lips round a little and the tongue touches nothing.
In many English accents (including London), an r at the end of a word isn’t pronounced: Northern = NOR-thn, car = KAH. Scottish accents do pronounce it, so listen to Callum! Directions use left and right all the time, so this matters today.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Story · Late again

Camden. Haruka waits by a red phone box. Tom arrives twenty minutes late.

**Tom:** Haruka! There you are. Sorry I’m late!  
**Haruka:** Again! I took the wrong train, and I was still here first!  
**Tom:** The wrong train? What happened?  
**Haruka:** I went south! A woman on the train helped me.  
**Tom:** Didn’t you check your phone?  
**Haruka:** My battery died. But look, a red phone box! Can you take my photo?  
**Tom:** Sure. Stand **in front of** it. Say cheese!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Haruka ticks “Take a photo in a red phone box” off her UK list.
Ask: Who is usually late, you or your friends? What do you say when someone is late?

</details>


#### Slide 18: Role play · Can you help me?

> **Student**
>
> A visitor stops you outside your nearest station. They want to go to a café or shop you know.
>
> • Give directions: past, along, opposite, next to.
> • Add one tip with if… will.
> • Then swap: ask the way to a place near your teacher.

**Useful phrases**
- Excuse me, how do I get to…?
- Is this the way to…?
- Go past…
- Turn left at…
- It’s opposite…
- If you…, you’ll…

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re the visitor. Ask the way to a real place near the student’s station.
>
> • Ask: “Is it far?”
> • Check: “So I go past…?”
> • Then swap: give directions to a real place near you.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Use real places, so nobody has to imagine a map. First the student gives directions in their own area. Then swap: the student asks the way to a place near you and checks with “So I go past…?”. Note one or two errors with prepositions to correct at the end. (~3 min)

</details>


#### Slide 19: Reading · The London Underground

[image: Photo idea: people standing on the right of Tube escalators]

The London Underground, or **the Tube**, opened in 1863. It was the first underground railway in the world. Today it has 11 lines, and each line has its own colour on the map.

To pay, you can use an Oyster card or a contactless bank card or phone. **Touch in** at the start of your journey and **touch out** at the end.

On the escalators, stand on the right and walk on the left. And when the train arrives, listen for a famous announcement: “Mind the gap!”

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. Correct pronunciation at the end of each paragraph. (~2.5 min)
Check: When did the Tube open? How can you pay? Where do you stand on the escalator?
Sources: Transport for London (TfL). The Tube has 11 lines; the Elizabeth line is run separately.

</details>


#### Slide 20: Reading · True or false?

- The Tube was the first underground railway in the world.
- You can only pay with cash.
- On escalators, you stand on the left.
- Each Tube line has its own colour.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. True.  2. False: you use an Oyster card or a contactless card or phone.  3. False: stand on the right.  4. True.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences in their own words. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a busy Japanese train platform]

- How is the Tube different from trains in your city?
- Which side do people stand on escalators where you live?
- What would you tell a visitor about trains in Japan?
- Have you ever taken the wrong train? What happened?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student explain Japan; don’t tell them. Encourage longer answers with “Why?” and “For example?” (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 22: Story · Weekend plans

Monday at work. Priya asks Haruka about Camden.

**Priya:** So, how was Camden?  
**Haruka:** Great! But I took the wrong train on the way there.  
**Priya:** Everyone does that! Listen, are you free one weekend in April?  
**Haruka:** I think so. Why?  
**Priya:** I’m planning a weekend in the Cotswolds. Do you want to come?  
**Haruka:** The Cotswolds? Are there any Harry Potter spots near there?  
**Priya:** Yes! **If we go** to Lacock, **you’ll see** one. I’ll make a plan!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Priya loves making plans (from her character card in Lesson 1). Lacock, a village near the Cotswolds, was used for scenes in the early Harry Potter films.
Next lesson: the Cotswolds weekend.

</details>


#### Slide 23: Culture · The red phone box

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a phone box | noun | 電話ボックス | a small box on the street with a public phone | There’s a red **phone box** on the corner. |
| a mobile | noun | 携帯電話 | (British English) a mobile phone | My **mobile** battery is dead. |

> **A famous design**
>
> The red phone box is one of the most famous British designs. Today most people have a mobile, so not many people use them for calls. Some are now tiny libraries or coffee shops!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Japan link: are there still public phones in Japan? Where? (~1.5 min)
The red phone box was designed by Giles Gilbert Scott; the most common version, the K6, dates from 1935. Some boxes now hold small book exchanges or defibrillators.

</details>


#### Slide 24: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- asked for and gave directions
- used next to, opposite, past and along
- gave travel advice with if… will
- learned about the Tube and red phone boxes

[image: Photo idea: Haruka in a red phone box]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Give one piece of positive feedback and one thing to practise. Preview the next lesson: Haruka’s weekend in the Cotswolds. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 8: A weekend in the Cotswolds

**Objective:** Students can describe a trip in order and ask about someone else’s, using the past simple and sequencers (CEFR A2: describing past experiences and places).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 3 · Exploring · Lesson 8

**A weekend in the Cotswolds**

Today’s goal: tell the story of a trip, and ask about someone else’s.

[image: Photo idea: a Cotswold village of stone cottages]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

April. Priya plans a perfect weekend in the Cotswolds: Harry Potter spots, a cream tea and a timetable for everything. Then the plan goes wrong. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: in Lesson 7 Priya invited Haruka to the Cotswolds and promised to make a plan.

The dialogues go in order: Saturday’s cream tea, Haruka’s call to her brother that evening (past simple), Sunday’s missed bus, and Monday with Tom.

Timing: 22 slides, about 32 to 34 minutes. If time is short, skip True or false and the second practice slide.

Haruka: a real Japanese 26-year-old, not a stereotype. Her English is clear and correct but simple. British words come from the other characters.

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Weekends away

Talk with your teacher.

- What did you do last weekend?
- Do you like trips to the countryside? Why?
- Do you plan your trips, or just go?

[image: Photo idea: English countryside in spring]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it short and personal. Push for longer answers: answer, add a detail, ask back. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: Priya and Haruka at a country bus stop in the evening, with stone houses behind them and an empty road. Priya checks a paper timetable, then her watch, then looks down the empty road. They look at each other, shrug, and start walking with their bags.]

- What are they waiting for?
- What do they decide to do?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · A cream tea

Saturday afternoon. Priya and Haruka are in a tea room in the Cotswolds.

**Haruka:** Is this afternoon tea? It’s on my list!  
**Priya:** Not quite. This is a cream tea: tea, **scones**, **clotted cream** and **jam**.  
**Haruka:** OK! Jam first, or cream first?  
**Priya:** Careful! In Devon, it’s cream first. In Cornwall, it’s jam first.  
**Haruka:** And in the Cotswolds?  
**Priya:** Here? Nobody minds. Just enjoy it!  
**Haruka:** Mmm! This is better than any cake in London!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Haruka), then swap roles. (~2 min)
A cream tea is a simple kind of afternoon tea. People in Devon and Cornwall really do argue about the order of cream and jam. Haruka counts it as “afternoon tea” on her UK list.
Recycles “better than” from Lesson 5. Ask: Which would you put first, and why?
This is Saturday afternoon. That evening Haruka tells her brother about the day.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- What is in a cream tea?
- Is a cream tea the same as afternoon tea?
- Where do people put the cream first?
- Does Haruka like it?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Tea, scones, clotted cream and jam.  2. Not quite: it’s a simple kind.  3. In Devon.  4. Yes: it’s better than any cake in London!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. If the student can’t answer, go back to the dialogue together. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · Tea time (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a scone | noun | スコーン | a small, round, plain cake, often with cream and jam | I’d like a **scone** with jam. |
| clotted cream | noun | クロテッドクリーム | very thick cream from the south-west of England | Put **clotted cream** on your scone. |
| jam | noun | ジャム | sweet fruit you put on bread or scones | Strawberry **jam**, please. |
| a tea room | noun | ティールーム | a small café that serves tea and cakes | We had a cream tea in a **tea room**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words were in the first dialogue. (~1.5 min)
Scone has two British pronunciations, “skon” and “skohn”. Both are correct; the culture slide at the end is about it.

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · The countryside (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the countryside | noun | 田舎 | land outside towns, with fields and villages | I love walking in **the countryside**. |
| a village | noun | 村 | a very small town | Lacock is a small **village**. |
| an abbey | noun | 修道院 | an old church building where monks or nuns lived | We visited the **abbey** first. |
| a timetable | noun | 時刻表 | a list of times for buses or trains | Check the bus **timetable**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Drill: village /ˈvɪlɪdʒ/ (not “bireji”), abbey /ˈæbi/. These words come in the next dialogues. (~1.5 min)
Ask: Is there a village you like in Japan? What’s it like?

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · Calling Kenta

Saturday evening. Haruka video-calls her brother Kenta from the hotel.

**Kenta:** Haruka! How was Lacock?  
**Haruka:** Amazing! We went there this morning.  
**Kenta:** What did you do?  
**Haruka:** First, we visited the abbey. Then I took lots of photos for you.  
**Kenta:** Send them now! What did you eat?  
**Haruka:** We had a cream tea. Scones with cream and jam!  
**Kenta:** I’m so jealous!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together (student = Haruka), then swap. (~2 min)

In real life they speak Japanese; the dialogue is in English for practice. Kenta is Haruka’s brother, the Harry Potter fan from Lesson 2. Lacock Abbey (National Trust) appears in early Harry Potter films.
Point out the past simple verbs: went, visited, took, had. These come next in the language focus.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Listening · Welcome to Lacock Abbey

Earlier, at Lacock Abbey, they joined a short tour. Listen and answer.

[audio: Tour guide]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Welcome to Lacock Abbey. Parts of this building are nearly eight hundred years old, and you might know these rooms from the Harry Potter films!

- Where are they?
- How old are parts of the building?
- Why might people know these rooms?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. At Lacock Abbey.
> 2. Nearly 800 years old.
> 3. They’re in the Harry Potter films.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Welcome to Lacock Abbey. Parts of this building are nearly eight hundred years old, and you might know these rooms from the Harry Potter films!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice: once for the general idea, once for details. Don’t show the transcript until after the questions. (~2 min)
The clip is new: it isn’t in the dialogues, so this checks real listening.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Language focus · What happened?

> **Past simple**
>
> *Regular (-ed)*
>
> visit → **visited** · walk → **walked** · miss → **missed**
>
> *Irregular*
>
> go → **went** · take → **took** · catch → **caught** · be → **was / were**
>
> *Negative*
>
> We **didn’t catch** the bus. (not “didn’t caught”)

**From the story**
- Haruka: We **went** there this morning.
- Haruka: First, we **visited** the abbey.
- Haruka: Then I **took** lots of photos for you.

Use the past simple for finished events, like last weekend.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Japanese learners often add -ed to irregular verbs (“goed”) or keep the past form after didn’t (“didn’t went”). (~1.5 min)
Pronunciation of -ed comes on the pronunciation slide.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Practice · Tell the story

- On Saturday we ____ (go) to Lacock.
- We ____ (visit) the abbey.
- Then we ____ (have) a cream tea.
- The scones ____ (be) delicious.
- On Sunday we ____ (miss) the bus.
- We ____ (not catch) it.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. went
> 2. visited
> 3. had
> 4. were
> 5. missed
> 6. didn’t catch

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student completes each sentence with the past simple, then retells Haruka’s Saturday without looking. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 12: Language focus · Asking and telling

> **Past questions and sequencers**
>
> *Questions*
>
> Where **did** you **go**? · What **did** you **do**? · How **did** you **get** home?
>
> *Telling a story*
>
> **First**, … · **Then** … · **After that**, … · **In the end**, …
>
> *Answers*
>
> We went to Lacock. (not “We did go”)

**From the story**
- Kenta: What **did** you **do**?
- Kenta: What **did** you **eat**?
- Haruka: **First**, we visited the abbey. **Then**…

Use did to ask about the past, and first, then, after that to tell a story in order.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Questions in the past use did + the base verb. Sequencers make a story easy to follow. (~1.5 min)
Watch for: “Where you went?” (no did) and “What did you went?”.

</details>


#### Slide 13: Practice · Your last weekend

- Where did you go?
- Who did you go with?
- What did you do first?
- What did you eat?
- How did you get home?
- Did you enjoy it? Why?

> **Example answer** (teacher only)
>
> First, I met my friend in Umeda. Then we had lunch. After that, we went shopping. In the end, I got home at nine!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask each question. The student answers in the past simple, and uses first, then, after that in at least one answer. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 14: Pronunciation · Sounding natural

> **How it sounds**
>
> walked → **WOKT**   stayed → **STAYD**   visited → **VIZ**-it-id
> What did you do? → wot dija **DOO**?
> Where did you go? → wair dija **GO**?

**Say it with your teacher**
- We **walked** to the next village.
- We **stayed** in a small hotel.
- We **visited** the abbey.
- What did you **do** at the weekend?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Say each word, the student repeats. (~1 min)
The -ed ending has three sounds: “t” after p, k, s, sh, ch (walked, missed); “d” after most other sounds (stayed, played); “id” after t and d (visited, wanted). Don’t add a vowel: walked is one beat, not “wokudo”.
“Did you” joins into “dija”.

</details>


#### Slide 15: Story · The last bus

Sunday, quarter past six. Priya and Haruka are at a bus stop in a small village.

**Priya:** OK, the bus leaves at half past six. We’re early!  
**Haruka:** Priya… this **timetable** says the last bus on Sunday was at six.  
**Priya:** What? My plan says half past six!  
**Haruka:** I think that’s the Saturday time.  
**Priya:** Oh no. I planned everything!  
**Haruka:** How far is the next village?  
**Priya:** About three miles. There’s a train station there. Let’s walk!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Buses in the English countryside can be infrequent, and Sunday timetables are often different. Priya, the person who plans everything, used the Saturday times.
Three miles is about five kilometres. The UK uses miles for distance.

</details>


#### Slide 16: Role play · Tell me about your trip

> **Student**
>
> Tell your teacher about a real trip.
>
> • Where did you go? Who with?
> • Tell the story in order: first, then, after that.
> • Did anything go wrong?

**Useful phrases**
- First, we…
- Then…
- After that…
- In the end…
- It was amazing!
- What happened next?

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re a curious friend.
>
> • Ask: Where…? What…? How…?
> • React: “Really? What happened next?”
> • Ask: “Would you go again?”

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Do it twice, then swap. (~2.5 min)
Ask follow-up questions with did. Note one or two errors with irregular verbs to correct at the end.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Reading · The Cotswolds

[image: Photo idea: a street in Lacock village]

The Cotswolds is an area of **countryside** in England, about two hours west of London. It’s famous for its small **villages** and old houses made of honey-coloured stone.

Many Harry Potter fans visit the area. Lacock, a village near the Cotswolds, appears in the early Harry Potter films, and Gloucester Cathedral was used for the corridors of Hogwarts.

After a long walk, visitors often stop at a **tea room** for a cream tea: tea with **scones**, **clotted cream** and **jam**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. Correct pronunciation at the end of each paragraph. (~2.5 min)
Check: What are the houses made of? Which village is in the Harry Potter films? What’s in a cream tea?
Sources: Cotswolds National Landscape; Wikipedia, “Lacock”; Gloucester Cathedral (Hogwarts corridors).

</details>


#### Slide 18: Reading · True or false?

- The Cotswolds is a big city.
- Many houses there are made of honey-coloured stone.
- Lacock appears in Harry Potter films.
- A cream tea has sandwiches and cake.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: it’s an area of countryside with small villages.  2. True.  3. True.  4. False: it has scones, clotted cream and jam.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences in their own words. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 19: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a village in the Japanese countryside]

- Do you prefer the city or the countryside? Why?
- Do you plan every minute of a trip, like Priya?
- What’s the most beautiful village or town you’ve visited?
- How do people get around the countryside in Japan?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student explain Japan; don’t tell them. Encourage past simple and comparatives. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 20: Story · In the end

Monday morning. Tom asks Haruka about her weekend with Priya.

**Tom:** Morning! How was the Cotswolds?  
**Haruka:** Beautiful! But we **missed** the last bus on Sunday.  
**Tom:** Oh no! How **did** you **get** home?  
**Haruka:** We **walked** to the next village and **caught** a train. **In the end**, it was fun!  
**Tom:** Priya and her plans! By the way, it’s my birthday next month.  
**Haruka:** Really? Are you having a party?  
**Tom:** A barbecue in Gwen’s garden. My mum’s coming from Manchester, and Callum's coming too!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Haruka now tells the whole weekend in the past simple. Recycles present continuous for plans from Lesson 6 (Are you having…? My mum’s coming…).
Next lesson: Tom’s birthday barbecue, where Haruka meets Tom’s mum and hears two very different accents.

</details>


#### Slide 21: Culture · Skon or skohn?

> **A very British argument**
>
> British people can’t agree how to say **scone**. Some say “skon”, like “gone”. Others say “skohn”, like “bone”. Both are correct!
>
> And people in Devon and Cornwall argue about what goes first on a scone: cream or jam. Which team are you on?

[image: image of scone with jam on top]

[image: image of scone with cream on top]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

A light, fun slide. Ask the student to say “scone” both ways. (~1 min)
Both pronunciations are in British dictionaries. The cream-or-jam argument is between Devon (cream first) and Cornwall (jam first).

</details>


#### Slide 22: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- told the story of a trip in order
- used the past simple (went, visited, took)
- asked about someone’s trip with did
- learned about the Cotswolds and cream teas

[image: Photo idea: a cream tea with scones, cream and jam]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Give one piece of positive feedback and one thing to practise. Preview the next lesson: Tom’s birthday barbecue. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 9: Sorry, what did you say?

**Objective:** Students can ask people to repeat, slow down or explain a word, and describe what they were doing using the past continuous (CEFR A2: asking for repetition, describing past events).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 3 · Exploring · Lesson 9

**Sorry, what did you say?**

Today’s goal: ask people to repeat or slow down, and say what you were doing.

[image: Photo idea: friends at a garden barbecue]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

May. Tom’s birthday barbecue in Gwen’s garden. Haruka meets Tom’s mum from Manchester, chats with Callum, and says yes to something she didn’t understand. This is the last lesson of Unit 3, so it has a progress check (slide 18) and a self-check on the wrap-up. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: in Lesson 8 Tom invited Haruka to his birthday barbecue.

Timing: 23 slides, about 34 to 36 minutes. If time is short, skip the second practice slide.

Accents: you can read Linda’s and Callum’s lines with a Manchester or Scottish accent if you’re comfortable, but keep it clear. Don’t rank accents or make fun of them.

Haruka: a real Japanese 26-year-old, not a stereotype. Her English is clear and correct but simple. British words come from the other characters.

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Understanding people

Talk with your teacher.

- Is it easy for you to understand English speakers? Why?
- Which English accents do you know?
- What do you do when you don’t understand someone?

[image: Photo idea: a group conversation at a party]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it short and personal. Push for longer answers: answer, add a detail, ask back. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: A garden barbecue. Linda (sixty, cheerful) talks to Haruka very fast with big hand movements. Tom stands behind them. Linda talks and laughs, waving her hands. Haruka smiles and nods again and again, looking a little lost. Tom watches and laughs.]

- Does Haruka understand Linda? How do you know?
- What would you do?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Meet Tom’s mum

Tom’s birthday barbecue in Gwen’s garden. Tom introduces Haruka to his mum, Linda.

**Tom:** Mum, this is Haruka, my flatmate.  
**Linda:** Alright, love! Our Tom never stops talking about you.  
**Haruka:** Sorry, could you say that again?  
**Linda:** Oh, sorry, I talk too fast. Tom talks about you all the time! Do you want a brew?  
**Haruka:** Sorry… what does “brew” **mean**?  
**Tom:** A cup of tea! Mum’s from Manchester.  
**Linda:** You’ll hear lots of new words today!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Haruka), then swap roles. (~2 min)

“Our Tom” is a northern English way to talk about someone in your family. “A brew” (a cup of tea) is common in the north of England.
Point out that Haruka asks politely instead of pretending to understand. That’s today’s main skill.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- Who is Linda?
- Why does Haruka ask her to say it again?
- What is a “brew”?
- Where is Linda from?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Tom’s mum.  2. Linda talks too fast.  3. A cup of tea.  4. Manchester.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. If the student can’t answer, go back to the dialogue together. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · Party words (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a barbecue | noun | バーベキュー | a party where you cook food outside | Tom’s having a **barbecue** for his birthday. |
| a toast | noun | 乾杯のあいさつ | a few words before everyone drinks together | Linda wants Haruka to make a **toast**. |
| to move house | verb | 引っ越す | to go and live in a new home | Callum’s friend **moved house** today. |
| starving | adjective | お腹ペコペコ | very, very hungry (informal) | I’m **starving**! Is the food ready? |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Drill: barbecue /ˈbɑːbɪkjuː/. (~1.5 min)
Note: “a toast” is also bread. At a party, it means a short speech before everyone drinks. The second meaning comes later in the story.

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Understanding people (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| an accent | noun | なまり | the way people from a place say words | Linda has a Manchester **accent**. |
| to slow down | verb | ゆっくりにする | to speak or move less fast | Could you **slow down** a bit? |
| to speak up | verb | 大きな声で話す | to speak more loudly | Sorry, could you **speak up**? |
| to mean | verb | 意味する | to have as its meaning | What does “brew” **mean**? |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These are today’s most useful phrases. (~1.5 min)
Ask: Have you ever asked someone to slow down? In English or Japanese?

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · Callum arrives

Callum arrives at the barbecue and says hello to Haruka.

**Callum:** Haruka! Sorry I’m late. I was helping a friend move house.  
**Haruka:** No problem! How are you?  
**Callum:** Aye, tired. We were carrying boxes all morning.  
**Haruka:** Sorry, “aye”?  
**Callum:** It means “yes” in Scotland. Am I speaking too fast again?  
**Haruka:** A little. Can you **slow down** a bit?  
**Callum:** Sure. I’ll try!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Callum is from Edinburgh (Lesson 3). “Aye” for yes is common in Scotland and parts of the north of England.
“I was helping”, “we were carrying”: past continuous for something in progress earlier today. This comes in the language focus.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Listening · Food’s ready!

Linda calls everyone at the barbecue. Listen and answer.

[audio: Linda at the barbecue]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Right, everyone! The burgers are ready! And there are veggie sausages on the left for anyone who wants them.

- What does Linda mean by “Right, everyone!”?
- What’s ready?
- Where are the veggie sausages?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Listen, everyone! (to get attention)
> 2. The burgers.
> 3. On the left.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Right, everyone! The burgers are ready! And there are veggie sausages on the left for anyone who wants them.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice: once for the general idea, once for details. Don’t show the transcript until after the questions. (~2 min)
The clip is new: it isn’t in the dialogues, so this checks real listening.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Vocabulary · Words around the UK

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a brew | noun | お茶 | a cup of tea (north of England) | Do you want a **brew**? |
| buzzing | adjective | わくわくしている | very happy and excited (informal) | I’m **buzzing** for the party! |
| aye | adverb | うん、はい | yes (Scotland and the north) | “Are you hungry?” “**Aye**!” |
| wee | adjective | 小さい | small (Scotland) | Just a **wee** bit, thanks. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

All four are used today. The student only needs to understand them, not use them. (~1.5 min)
Callum said “wee” in Lesson 3. Ask: Are there words in your Japanese dialect that people from Tokyo don’t know?

</details>


#### Slide 11: Language focus · Sorry, could you…?

> **Asking for help**
>
> *You didn’t hear*
>
> **Sorry?** · **Sorry, could you say that again?**
>
> *Too fast or too quiet*
>
> **Could you slow down a bit?** · **Could you speak up?**
>
> *A new word*
>
> **What does “brew” mean?** · **How do you spell that?**

**From the story**
- Haruka: Sorry, could you **say that again**?
- Haruka: Sorry… what does “brew” **mean**?
- Haruka: Can you **slow down** a bit?

Use these phrases when you don’t understand someone.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

A2 learners aren’t expected to understand everything. The skill is to ask quickly and politely instead of staying silent or nodding. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 12: Practice · What do you say?

- Someone speaks very fast.
- You didn’t hear the question.
- Someone speaks very quietly.
- You don’t know the word “skint”.
- You can’t write a name.
- You still don’t understand.

> **Possible answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Could you slow down a bit?
> 2. Sorry, could you say that again?
> 3. Could you speak up?
> 4. What does “skint” mean?
> 5. How do you spell that?
> 6. Sorry, I don’t understand. Could you explain?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read each situation. The student says the best phrase. Then say a real sentence too fast or too quietly and let them use it! (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Language focus · What were you doing?

> **Past continuous**
>
> *In progress*
>
> I **was working** all morning. · We **were carrying** boxes.
>
> *With the past simple*
>
> I **was nodding** when she **asked** me. · **While** we **were talking**, Tom **opened** his presents.
>
> *Questions*
>
> What **were** you **doing** at ten? · **Were** you **listening**?

**From the story**
- Callum: I **was helping** a friend move house.
- Callum: We **were carrying** boxes all morning.

Use was / were + -ing for something in progress in the past.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Past continuous = an action in progress in the past. With the past simple, it’s the background, and the past simple is the short action. (~1.5 min)
Watch for: “I was understand” (understand isn’t usually used with -ing).

</details>


#### Slide 14: Practice · What were you doing…?

- at 7 o’clock this morning?
- at 9 o’clock last night?
- at this time yesterday?
- when your phone last rang?
- before this lesson?
- on Saturday afternoon?

> **Example answer** (teacher only)
>
> At nine last night I was watching a drama on Netflix. I was eating ice cream too!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask each question. The student answers with was + -ing and adds a detail. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Pronunciation · Stress to correct

> **Stress the new information**
>
> Linda’s from Liverpool? → No, from **MAN**chester.
>
> A cup of coffee? → No, a cup of **TEA**.
>
> At half past six? → No, at **SIX**.

**Say it with your teacher**
- No, I said **TEA**, not coffee.
- Not **BREAD**, a **SPEECH**!
- No, Callum’s from **ED**inburgh.
- The bus was at **SIX**, not half past.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Say something wrong (“Tom’s from London?”); the student corrects you with stress. (~1 min)
To correct someone in English, make the important word louder, longer and higher. Japanese often uses words like 〜じゃなくて; English uses stress as well.
This helps when someone misunderstands you, like Haruka and the toast.

</details>


#### Slide 16: Story · A few words

Later, at cake time. Linda finds Haruka. Callum and Tom are nearby.

**Linda:** Haruka, will you make a toast for our Tom?  
**Haruka:** Toast? Now? Um… OK.  
**Linda:** Brilliant! Everyone! Haruka’s going to say a few words!  
**Haruka:** Huh? A few words? But I’m making toast…  
**Callum:** Not bread, Haruka! A **toast** is a short speech before everyone drinks.  
**Haruka:** Oh no… OK. Happy birthday, Tom! Thank you for… everything!  
**Tom:** Haha! Best toast ever. Cheers, everyone!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Haruka thinks “make a toast” means making bread in a toaster. In English, “a toast” is also a short speech before everyone drinks. Ask: What did Haruka think Linda wanted? What could she ask instead? (Sorry, what do you mean? / What does “toast” mean here?)

</details>


#### Slide 17: Role play · Sorry, could you…?

> **Student**
>
> Your teacher is telling you about their weekend.
>
> • Ask them to repeat, slow down or speak up.
> • Ask what new words mean.
> • Ask one follow-up question.

**Useful phrases**
- Sorry?
- Could you say that again?
- Could you slow down a bit?
- Could you speak up?
- What does… mean?
- Oh, I see!

> **Teacher**
>
> Tell the student about your weekend.
>
> • Speak fast at first.
> • Use two British words.
> • Speak very quietly once.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Talk about your weekend, but speak fast, use one or two British words (brew, buzzing, starving) and speak quietly once. The student must stop you each time. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 18: Progress check · A day out

> **Student**
>
> Tell your teacher about a real day out.
>
> • Tell the story in order (first, then…).
> • Say what you were doing when something happened.
> • Give directions to one place you visited.

**Unit 3 phrases**
- How do I get to…?
- It’s next to…
- If you…, you’ll…
- First, we…
- I was…ing when…
- Could you slow down?

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re a friend who wants to go there too.
>
> • Ask questions with did.
> • Speak fast once.
> • Ask: “How do I get there?”

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Unit 3 progress check. Let the student lead and don’t correct during the role play. Note what they do well and one thing to practise. (~3 min)
Check the Unit 3 can-dos: give directions, give travel advice, tell the story of a trip, ask people to repeat or slow down. The student self-checks on the wrap-up slide.

</details>


#### Slide 19: Reading · Accents and words

[image: Photo idea: people chatting on a street in a northern English city]

The UK is a small country, but it has lots of different **accents**. People from Manchester, Liverpool, Glasgow and London can sound very different, even when they use the same words.

Some words change too. In the north of England, a cup of tea is often a “brew”. In Scotland, people say “aye” for yes and “wee” for small. And lots of British people say “cheers” for thank you.

Don’t worry if you don’t understand everything. British people often ask each other to repeat things too!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. Correct pronunciation at the end of each paragraph. (~2.5 min)
Check: Do all British people sound the same? What does “aye” mean? What should you do if you don’t understand?
Keep this about words and sounds. Don’t rank accents or call any of them “correct”.

</details>


#### Slide 20: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a busy street in Osaka]

- Do you speak a dialect, like 関西弁? When do you use it?
- Which Japanese dialect is hard for you to understand?
- What do you do when you don’t understand someone?
- Have you ever said yes without understanding? What happened?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student explain Japan; don’t tell them. Encourage past simple and past continuous. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Story · The morning after

The next morning. Haruka and Tom are cleaning up Gwen’s garden.

**Tom:** Thanks for the toast yesterday. Everyone loved it!  
**Haruka:** I thought your mum asked me to make toast! I **was looking** for the toaster.  
**Tom:** So what **were** you **thinking** when she said “a few words”?  
**Haruka:** **I was like**, “Please, no!”  
**Tom:** Well, to say thank you, I’ve got two tickets for a gig next month. Want to come?  
**Haruka:** A gig? A concert? Yes, please!  
**Tom:** Great. And this time, you understood the question!
