<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Past continuous again: was nodding, were you thinking, was thinking.
Next lesson (Unit 4): the gig.

</details>


#### Slide 22: Useful phrase · I was like…

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| I was like, “…” | phrase | 「…」って感じだった | what you thought or felt at that moment (informal) | **I was like**, “Please, no!” |
| She was like, “…” | phrase | 彼女は「…」って感じだった | what someone said or how they reacted (informal) | Linda **was like**, “Brilliant!” |

> **How to use it**
>
> Use **be like** to tell people what you thought, said or felt in a story.
>
> “My train was cancelled, and **I was like**, ‘Oh no!’”
>
> It’s very common when chatting with friends, but don’t use it in writing at work.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Haruka used this in the last dialogue. It’s very common in everyday British (and American) speech, especially when telling a funny story. (~1.5 min)
It isn’t the past continuous: “was like” here means “thought” or “said”. Keep it for chatting with friends, not for writing or formal situations.
Practice: ask about a surprising moment (“What did you think when…?”) and let the student answer with “I was like, …”.

</details>


#### Slide 23: Wrap-up · Unit 3 check

**I can…**
- ask for and give directions
- tell the story of a trip (went, visited…)
- ask people to repeat or slow down
- say what I was doing (I was working…)

[image: Photo idea: gig tickets on a garden table]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Self-check: for each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give one piece of positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1.5 min)
Preview Unit 4: The big trip. Next lesson: Haruka and Tom go to a gig.

</details>


## Unit: The big trip


### Lesson 10: Going to a gig

**Objective:** Students can talk about music they like and their experiences of live music, using the present perfect for experience (CEFR A2: likes and dislikes, describing experiences).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 4 · The big trip · Lesson 10

**Going to a gig**

Today’s goal: talk about music you like and things you’ve done.

[image: Photo idea: a crowd at a small gig]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

June. Tom takes Haruka to a gig in a small London venue to say thank you for the toast. First lesson of Unit 4, The big trip. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: in Lesson 9 Tom invited Haruka to a gig next month.

Timing: 22 slides, about 32 to 34 minutes. If time is short, skip True or false and the second practice slide.

Haruka: a real Japanese 26-year-old, not a stereotype. Her English is clear and correct but simple. British words come from the other characters.

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Music and you

Talk with your teacher.

- What kind of music do you like?
- Do you prefer live music or listening at home? Why?
- Who is your favourite singer or band?

[image: Photo idea: a small music venue before a show]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it short and personal. Push for longer answers: answer, add a detail, ask back. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: A queue of young people outside a small London music venue in the rain. Haruka and Tom are in the middle of the queue. It starts to rain harder. Tom takes off his jacket and holds it over Haruka’s head. The doors open and everyone moves inside.]

- What’s the weather like? What does Tom do?
- What happens at the end?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · In the queue

June. Haruka and Tom are queuing outside a small music venue in London.

**Tom:** So, have you ever been to a gig in the UK?  
**Haruka:** No, I haven’t. But I’ve been to lots of concerts in Japan.  
**Tom:** Really? Who have you seen?  
**Haruka:** I’ve seen lots of Japanese bands. And one British band in Osaka!  
**Tom:** Nice! Tonight’s band is from Manchester, like me.  
**Haruka:** Everyone’s queuing so early! I don’t mind waiting, but it’s raining.  
**Tom:** There are no seats and no numbers. If you come early, you get the best place!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Haruka), then swap roles. (~2 min)

Many small UK venues are standing-only, and you go in in the order you arrive. Japanese live houses often give each ticket a number (整理番号) instead. The culture slide at the end compares them.
Point out the present perfect for experience: have you ever been, I’ve been, I’ve seen.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- Has Haruka been to a gig in the UK?
- Where has she been to concerts?
- Where is tonight’s band from?
- Why is everyone queuing early?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. No, she hasn’t.  2. In Japan.  3. Manchester.  4. There are no seats or numbers, so early people get the best place.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. If the student can’t answer, go back to the dialogue together. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · Live music (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a gig | noun | ライブ | a live music concert (informal) | We’re going to a **gig** tonight. |
| a venue | noun | 会場 | a place where events happen | It’s a small **venue** in London. |
| a stage | noun | ステージ | where the band plays | We stood near the **stage**. |
| a crowd | noun | 人混み、観客 | a big group of people | I lost Tom in the **crowd**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Drill: venue /ˈvenjuː/, crowd /kraʊd/. (~1.5 min)
Note: Japanese ライブ comes from “live”, but in English we say “a gig” or “a concert”, not “a live”.

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Music talk (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to queue | verb | 並ぶ | to wait in a line (British English) | We **queued** for an hour. |
| to sing along | verb | 一緒に歌う | to sing with the band | Everyone **sang along** to the last song. |
| the support act | noun | 前座 | a band or singer who plays before the main band | The **support act** was really good. |
| loud | adjective | うるさい、大きな音の | making a lot of noise | The music was so **loud**! |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogues and the reading. (~1.5 min)
Ask: Have you ever sung along at a concert? Which song?

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · Where are you?

Inside the venue. Tom went to get drinks ten minutes ago. Haruka calls him.

**Haruka:** Tom? Where are you?  
**Tom:** Sorry? I can’t hear you! The music’s too **loud**!  
**Haruka:** WHERE. ARE. YOU?  
**Tom:** At the bar! It’s so busy. Where are you?  
**Haruka:** Near the **stage**, on the left. Next to a tall man with a hat.  
**Tom:** OK! Don’t move. I’m coming!  
**Haruka:** Hurry up! I hate missing the start!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. Have fun with “WHERE. ARE. YOU?” (~1.5 min)
Recycles “Sorry?” for not hearing (Lessons 3 and 9) and directions (on the left, next to) from Lesson 7.
Point out “I hate missing the start”: hate + -ing comes in the second language focus.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Listening · Hello, London!

The band comes on stage. Listen and answer.

[audio: The singer]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Hello, London! Thanks for coming out in the rain! This first song is for anyone from Manchester!

- What was the weather like?
- What does “Thanks for coming out” mean?
- Who is the first song for?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. It was raining.
> 2. Thank you for coming to the gig.
> 3. Anyone from Manchester.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Hello, London! Thanks for coming out in the rain! This first song is for anyone from Manchester!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice: once for the general idea, once for details. Don’t show the transcript until after the questions. (~2 min)
The clip is new: it isn’t in the dialogues, so this checks real listening.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Language focus · Have you ever…?

> **Present perfect for experience**
>
> *Questions*
>
> **Have** you **ever been** to a gig? · **Have** you **seen** Oasis?
>
> *Answers*
>
> Yes, I **have**. I**’ve been** to lots. · No, I **haven’t**. I**’ve never seen** them.
>
> *Past participles*
>
> be → **been** · see → **seen** · hear → **heard** · eat → **eaten**

**From the story**
- Tom: So, **have** you **ever been** to a gig in the UK?
- Haruka: No, I **haven’t**. But **I’ve been** to lots of concerts.
- Haruka: **I’ve seen** lots of Japanese bands.

Use the present perfect to talk about experiences in your life.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Present perfect here = experiences in your life, with no time. When you add a time (last year, in 2025), use the past simple. Keep it simple at A2. (~1.5 min)
Watch for: “Have you ever went…?” and “I have seen him yesterday.”

</details>


#### Slide 11: Practice · Your experiences

- been to a music festival?
- seen a famous band live?
- sung karaoke in English?
- eaten black pudding?
- been to the UK?
- met a famous person?

> **Example answer** (teacher only)
>
> Yes, I have! I went to Summer Sonic in Osaka three years ago. It was so hot!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask each question. The student answers Yes, I have / No, I haven’t, then adds one detail. If they add when, help them switch to the past simple: “Yes! I went last year.” (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 12: Language focus · I love dancing

> **Likes and dislikes**
>
> *Likes*
>
> I **love dancing**. · I **enjoy going** to gigs. · I **like listening** to rock.
>
> *It’s OK*
>
> I **don’t mind waiting**.
>
> *Dislikes*
>
> I **hate missing** the start. · I **can’t stand sitting** down at concerts.

**From the story**
- Haruka: I **don’t mind waiting**, but it’s raining.
- Haruka: Hurry up! I **hate missing** the start!

Use these verbs + -ing to say what you like and don’t like.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

After love, like, enjoy, don’t mind, hate and can’t stand, use verb + -ing. “Can’t stand” is stronger than “don’t like”. (~1.5 min)
Watch for: “I enjoy to dance” (should be “I enjoy dancing”).

</details>


#### Slide 13: Practice · Your music

- Do you enjoy dancing?
- Do you mind queuing for concerts?
- What do you love listening to?
- What do you hate doing at concerts?
- Do you like singing karaoke?
- What can’t you stand?

> **Example answer** (teacher only)
>
> I love singing karaoke, but I can’t stand singing in English. It’s too difficult!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask each question. The student answers with love / enjoy / don’t mind / hate / can’t stand + -ing, and adds a reason. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 14: Pronunciation · V and B

> **Bite your lip for v**
>
> **v**: top teeth on your bottom lip → **v**ery · **v**enue · lo**v**e · li**v**e
>
> **b**: both lips together → **b**and · **b**est · **b**ig
>
> **v**ery ≠ **b**erry · **v**est ≠ **b**est

**Say it with your teacher**
- I lo**v**e this **b**and!
- It’s a **v**ery small **v**enue.
- The **b**est gig I’ve e**v**er seen!
- Ha**v**e you e**v**er seen them li**v**e?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Use a mirror or the camera so the student can see their lip. (~1 min)
Japanese ヴ is often said as b, so “love” becomes “lobu”. For v, the top teeth touch the bottom lip and the sound continues; for b, the lips close and open.
Related: f is also teeth on lip (fun, fan), not h (“hun”).

</details>


#### Slide 15: Story · After the gig

After the show. Haruka and Tom walk to the station.

**Tom:** So, did you enjoy it?  
**Haruka:** I loved it! I really **enjoy dancing** at gigs.  
**Tom:** Me too. I **can’t stand sitting** down at concerts.  
**Haruka:** **Have** you **ever seen** Oasis? They’re from Manchester too.  
**Tom:** Yes! I saw them in Manchester in 2025. Best night of my life!  
**Haruka:** Wow! **I’ve never seen** a really famous British band.  
**Tom:** Then we need to find you one!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Tom answers a present perfect question with the past simple because he adds a time (in 2025). Point this out lightly: it’s exactly how native speakers do it.
Oasis played reunion concerts in 2025, including at Heaton Park in Manchester.

</details>


#### Slide 16: Role play · Find out about your teacher

> **Student**
>
> Interview your teacher.
>
> • Ask five “Have you ever…?” questions.
> • If they say yes, ask: When? Where? What was it like?
> • Tell them one thing you’ve done too.

**Useful phrases**
- Have you ever…?
- Yes, I have.
- No, I haven’t.
- When was that?
- What was it like?
- I’ve never…

> **Teacher**
>
> Answer the student’s questions.
>
> • Say “Yes, I have” to at least three.
> • Give details in the past simple.
> • Ask one question back.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student interviews you. Answer honestly or invent answers. (~2.5 min)
Push for a follow-up question in the past simple after each “Yes, I have”.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Reading · Gigs in the UK

[image: Photo idea: a band playing in a small pub]

A **gig** is an informal word for a live music concert. In the UK, many gigs are in small **venues**, like pubs and clubs. Often there are no seats. Everyone stands, and the people who arrive first get the best places near the **stage**.

Many famous bands started in small venues. The Beatles came from Liverpool, and Oasis came from Manchester. In 2025, Oasis played together again for the first time in 16 years, and millions of fans tried to get tickets.

Before the main band, there is often a **support act**. In 2026 the Japanese band 'Otoboke Beaver' were a support act for the Foo Fighters in Liverpool's Anfield Stadium!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. Correct pronunciation at the end of each paragraph. (~2.5 min)
Check: What is a gig? Where do many gigs happen? What is a support act?
Oasis split up in 2009 and played their first reunion concerts in 2025. Otoboke Beaver, a punk band from Kyoto, supported the Foo Fighters at Anfield on 25 June 2026 (with Inhaler). Ask: Do you know them?

</details>


#### Slide 18: Reading · True or false?

- A gig is a sports match.
- At many small gigs, there are no seats.
- Oasis come from Liverpool.
- The support act plays before the main band.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: it’s a live music concert.  2. True.  3. False: they come from Manchester. The Beatles came from Liverpool.  4. True.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences in their own words. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 19: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a crowded live house in Japan]

- Have you ever been to a live house? What was it like?
- How are concerts in Japan different from UK gigs?
- What music do you enjoy listening to? Why?
- Which band or singer would you love to see live?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student explain Japan; don’t tell them. Encourage the present perfect and -ing forms. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 20: Story · Big news

The next evening. Haruka’s brother Kenta video-calls her from Osaka.

**Kenta:** Haruka! Guess what? I’ve booked my flight to London!  
**Haruka:** Really? When are you coming?  
**Kenta:** In August! I’m staying for ten days.  
**Haruka:** Amazing! What do you want to do?  
**Kenta:** Harry Potter spots, of course! And can we go to Scotland?  
**Haruka:** Scotland? Callum’s from Edinburgh. Let’s ask him!  
**Kenta:** Yes! **I’ve never been** to the UK. I can’t wait!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. In real life they speak Japanese; the dialogue is in English for practice. (~1.5 min)
Next lesson: planning Kenta’s visit and the trip to Edinburgh.

</details>


#### Slide 21: Culture · Numbers or no numbers?

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| first come, first served | phrase | 早い者勝ち | the people who arrive first get the best places | No seats at the gig: it’s **first come, first served**. |
| a standing ticket | noun | スタンディングチケット | a ticket with no seat | We bought **standing tickets**. |

> **Gigs in Japan and the UK**
>
> At many small live houses in Japan, tickets have numbers, and you go in in that order. You often pay for a drink at the door too. At many small UK gigs with standing tickets, it’s first come, first served, so people queue early for a place near the stage!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Japan link: let the student explain 整理番号 and drink tickets (ドリンク代) at live houses. Which system do they prefer? (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 22: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- talked about experiences (Have you ever…? I’ve never…)
- talked about music you like (love, enjoy, can’t stand + -ing)
- told the story of a night out
- learned about gigs in the UK

[image: Photo idea: a wristband and a guitar pick]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Give one piece of positive feedback and one thing to practise. Preview the next lesson: planning Kenta’s trip. (~1 min)

</details>

**Objective:** Students can make suggestions and give advice about travel plans, using should and have to (CEFR A2: suggestions, advice, obligation).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 4 · The big trip · Lesson 11

**Planning the trip**

Today’s goal: give advice and talk about what you have to do for a trip.

[image: Photo idea: planning a trip with a map and laptop]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

July. Kenta is coming in August, and Haruka plans a trip to Edinburgh with help from Priya and Callum. Then Priya has some big news. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: in Lesson 10 Kenta booked his flight to London and asked to go to Scotland.

Timing: 21 slides, about 31 to 33 minutes. If time is short, skip True or false and the second practice slide.

Haruka: a real Japanese 26-year-old, not a stereotype. Her English is clear and correct but simple. British words come from the other characters.

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Trips and travel

Talk with your teacher.

- How do you usually plan a trip?
- Do you book things early or at the last minute?
- Where would you like to go next?

[image: Photo idea: a half-packed suitcase with a passport]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it short and personal. Push for longer answers: answer, add a detail, ask back. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: Haruka and Priya at an office table with a laptop and a notebook. No readable text on the screen. Priya types and points at the screen. Haruka writes in her notebook, then looks up and gives a big thumbs up.]

- What are Haruka and Priya doing?
- Where do you think Haruka is going?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Lunchtime plans

July. At lunch, Haruka tells Priya about Kenta’s visit.

**Haruka:** My brother’s coming in August! We want to go to Edinburgh.  
**Priya:** In August? The Fringe festival is on. You should **book** a hotel now!  
**Haruka:** Now? Is it very busy?  
**Priya:** Very! And you should **book** your train tickets early too. They’re cheaper.  
**Haruka:** How do we get there? By plane?  
**Priya:** You don’t have to fly. The train from King’s Cross takes about four and a half hours.  
**Haruka:** OK! I’m going to book everything tonight.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Haruka), then swap roles. (~2 min)

The Edinburgh Festival Fringe happens every August, and the city gets very busy. Priya loves making plans, so of course she has advice.
Point out should for advice and don’t have to for “not necessary”.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- When is Kenta coming?
- Why should Haruka book a hotel now?
- Do they have to fly to Edinburgh?
- How long does the train take?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. In August.  2. The Fringe festival is on, so it’s very busy.  3. No, they can take the train.  4. About four and a half hours.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. If the student can’t answer, go back to the dialogue together. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · Planning (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to book | verb | 予約する | to arrange a hotel, ticket or table before you go | You should **book** a hotel now. |
| in advance | phrase | 前もって | before the day | Train tickets are cheaper **in advance**. |
| a return ticket | noun | 往復切符 | a ticket there and back | Two **return tickets** to Edinburgh, please. |
| a railcard | noun | 鉄道割引カード | a card that gives you cheaper train tickets | With a **railcard**, you save a third. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Drill: advance /ədˈvɑːns/. (~1.5 min)
Note: British English says “a return”; American English says “a round trip”. “A single” is one way.

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Before you fly (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| an ETA | noun | 電子渡航認証（ETA） | online permission to travel to the UK for a short visit | Kenta needs an **ETA**. |
| a visa | noun | ビザ | official permission to live, work or study in a country | Haruka has a working holiday **visa**. |
| to apply (for) | verb | 申請する | to ask officially for something | You can **apply for** an ETA on the app. |
| a fee | noun | 料金、手数料 | money you pay for a service | The ETA **fee** is £20. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue. (~1.5 min)
Haruka has a visa (Youth Mobility Scheme). Kenta is just visiting, so he needs an ETA instead.

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · Have you got an ETA?

That evening. Haruka video-calls Kenta about the trip.

**Kenta:** I’ve got my passport and my plane ticket. I’m ready!  
**Haruka:** Have you got an **ETA**?  
**Kenta:** A what?  
**Haruka:** An ETA. Visitors from Japan have to get one before they fly to the UK.  
**Kenta:** Really? I didn’t know! How much is it?  
**Haruka:** Twenty pounds. You have to **apply** online. You should do it today!  
**Kenta:** OK, OK! I’m doing it now.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. In real life they speak Japanese; the dialogue is in English for practice. (~1.5 min)
Since 2025, visitors from Japan need an ETA to travel to the UK. In April 2026 the fee went up to £20. The Home Office recommends applying at least three working days before you travel. Check GOV.UK for the current fee before teaching.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Listening · At King’s Cross

At King’s Cross, Haruka hears an announcement. Listen and answer.

[audio: Station announcement]

*Transcript (teacher only):* The train at platform one is the ten thirty to Edinburgh, calling at York and Newcastle.

- Where is the train going?
- Which platform is it?
- Where does it stop on the way?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Edinburgh.
> 2. Platform one.
> 3. York and Newcastle.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> The train at platform one is the ten thirty to Edinburgh, calling at York and Newcastle.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice: once for the general idea, once for details. Don’t show the transcript until after the questions. (~2 min)
The clip is new: it isn’t in the dialogues, so this checks real listening.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Language focus · You should…

> **Should for advice**
>
> *Advice*
>
> You **should book** a hotel now. · You **should wear** good shoes.
>
> *Negative*
>
> You **shouldn’t leave** it too late.
>
> *Asking*
>
> What **should** we **do**? · **Should** I **take** the train?

**From the story**
- Priya: You **should book** a hotel now!
- Priya: You **should book** your train tickets early too.
- Haruka: You **should do** it today!

Use should and shouldn’t to give advice.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Should = it’s a good idea. It’s softer than have to. Japanese learners sometimes use “must” for advice, which sounds too strong. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 11: Practice · Give some advice

- “I’m always tired.”
- “My English isn’t improving.”
- “I want to save money on trains.”
- “I’m going to Edinburgh in August.”
- “I don’t know what to do this weekend.”
- “My phone battery always dies.”

> **Possible answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. You should go to bed earlier.
> 2. You should practise every day.
> 3. You should get a railcard.
> 4. You should book a hotel now.
> 5. You should go to Camden!
> 6. You should carry a charger.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read each problem. The student gives advice with should or shouldn’t, then you swap. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 12: Language focus · Have to or don’t have to?

> **Have to**
>
> *It’s necessary*
>
> Visitors **have to get** an ETA. · You **have to apply** online or on the app.
>
> *It isn’t necessary*
>
> You **don’t have to fly**. (You can take the train.)
>
> *Questions*
>
> **Do** we **have to pay**? · What **do** I **have to do**?

**From the story**
- Haruka: Visitors from Japan **have to get** one.
- Haruka: You **have to apply** online.
- Priya: You **don’t have to** fly.

Use have to for rules, and don’t have to when something isn’t necessary.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Have to = it’s necessary (a rule). Don’t have to = it isn’t necessary, you can choose. Japanese learners often mix up “don’t have to” and “mustn’t”; keep the focus on necessary vs not necessary. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Practice · Rules for visitors

- Visitors from Japan ____ get an ETA.
- You ____ buy Tube tickets in advance.
- You ____ take a plane to Edinburgh.
- You ____ touch out on the Tube.
- You ____ pay to climb Arthur’s Seat.
- You ____ tip at the bar in a pub.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. have to
> 2. don’t have to
> 3. don’t have to
> 4. have to
> 5. don’t have to
> 6. don’t have to

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student completes each sentence with have to or don’t have to, and explains why. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 14: Pronunciation · Tricky place names

> **Not how they look!**
>
> Edinburgh → **ED**-in-bruh
>
> Gloucester → **GLOS**-ter 
>
> Leicester → **LES**-ter
>
> Greenwich → **GREN**-itch

**Say it with your teacher**
- We’re going to **Edinburgh**.
- **Gloucester** Cathedral is in Harry Potter.
- My friend lives in **Leicester**.
- Let’s visit **Greenwich**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Say each name, the student repeats. Have fun with it: visitors often get these wrong. (~1 min)
Many British place names drop letters: -cester sounds like “ster”, and -burgh in Edinburgh sounds like “bruh” or “burra”. The w in Greenwich is silent.
Gloucester Cathedral was in Lesson 8.

</details>


#### Slide 15: Story · Ask Callum

Haruka meets Callum for a coffee and asks about Edinburgh.

**Haruka:** Callum, what **should** we **do** in Edinburgh?  
**Callum:** You **should climb** Arthur’s Seat. It’s a wee hill in the city, and the view’s amazing.  
**Haruka:** **Do** we **have to pay**?  
**Callum:** No, it’s free! And walk along the Royal Mile. It’s full of street performers in August.  
**Haruka:** Sounds great! Where **should** we **stay**?  
**Callum:** Book soon! Actually, I’m going home that week. I can show you around.  
**Haruka:** Really? Kenta will be so happy!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Arthur’s Seat is an old volcano in Edinburgh, 251 metres high. Callum calls it “a wee hill”: a typical understatement. During the Fringe, the Royal Mile is full of street performers.
Recycles “wee” (Lessons 3 and 9) and present continuous for plans (I’m going home).

</details>


#### Slide 16: Role play · Visiting Japan

> **Student**
>
> A British friend is visiting Japan.
>
> • Give advice: what should they see and eat?
> • Tell them what they have to do (money, trains, shoes…).
> • Tell them one thing they don’t have to worry about.

**Useful phrases**
- You should…
- You shouldn’t…
- You have to…
- You don’t have to…
- If you…, you’ll…
- Do I have to…?

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re visiting Japan for the first time.
>
> • Ask: “What should I see?”
> • Ask: “Do I have to…?”
> • Ask about trains and money.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Do it twice, then swap. (~2.5 min)
Play a British friend who’s visiting Japan for the first time. Note one or two errors with should / have to to correct at the end.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Reading · Getting to Edinburgh

[image: Photo idea: a train crossing a stone bridge by the sea]

Visitors from Japan need an **ETA** before they travel to the UK. You **apply** on the UK ETA app. In 2026 the **fee** is £20, and you should apply at least three working days before you travel.

The train from London King’s Cross to Edinburgh takes about four and a half hours. Tickets are usually cheaper if you **book in advance**, and people aged 16–25 or 26–30 can get a **railcard** for a third off most fares.

In August, Edinburgh is very busy because of the Fringe, the world’s biggest arts festival. There are thousands of comedy, theatre and music shows, so you should book a hotel early.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. Correct pronunciation at the end of each paragraph. (~2.5 min)
Check: What do visitors from Japan need? How long is the train? Why should you book a hotel early in August?
Sources: Home Office ETA factsheet (April 2026, fee £20); LNER journey times; Railcard (16–25 and 26–30 railcards give a third off most fares). Check the ETA fee on GOV.UK before teaching.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Reading · True or false?

- Visitors from Japan don’t have to get an ETA.
- In 2026 the ETA costs £20.
- Train tickets are usually cheaper if you book early.
- Edinburgh is very quiet in August.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: they have to get one.  2. True.  3. True.  4. False: it’s very busy because of the Fringe.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences in their own words. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 19: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: travellers on a bullet train platform]

- How do you plan a trip? Do you book early?
- What should visitors to Japan know before they go?
- What do visitors have to do when they arrive in Japan?
- A friend is visiting your city. Where should they go?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student explain Japan; don’t tell them. Encourage should and have to. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 20: Story · Some news

Friday afternoon. Priya comes to Haruka’s desk.

**Priya:** Haruka, have you got a minute?  
**Haruka:** Sure. Is everything OK?  
**Priya:** Everything’s great. The boss loves your work.  
**Haruka:** Really? Thank you!  
**Priya:** You’re going back to Japan in September, right?  
**Haruka:** That’s the plan. Why?  
**Priya:** She wants you to stay another year. You **should think** about it!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Haruka’s Youth Mobility Scheme visa lets her stay up to two years, but she only planned one. Ask: What should Haruka do? Why?
Next lesson: Edinburgh with Kenta and Callum, and Haruka’s big decision.

</details>


#### Slide 21: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- gave advice with should and shouldn’t
- talked about rules with have to and don’t have to
- planned a trip with going to
- learned how to travel to Edinburgh

[image: Photo idea: a passport and train tickets on a table]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Give one piece of positive feedback and one thing to practise. Preview the last lesson: Edinburgh, and a big decision. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 12: Edinburgh, and a big decision

**Objective:** Students can talk about their experiences and look back on the course, using the language from all 12 lessons (CEFR A2: describing experiences).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 4 · The big trip · Lesson 12

**Edinburgh, and a big decision**

Today’s goal: talk about your experiences and look back on what you’ve learned.

[image: Photo idea: Edinburgh Castle above the city]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

August. Haruka, Kenta and Callum in Edinburgh during the Fringe. Haruka looks back on her year and makes her big decision. This is the last lesson: a review with no new grammar, a final check (slide 15) and a course self-check on the wrap-up. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Kenta is visiting (Lesson 10), Callum offered to show them around, and Haruka’s boss wants her to stay another year (Lesson 11).

Timing: 21 slides, about 32 to 34 minutes. If time is short, skip True or false.

Haruka: a real Japanese 26-year-old, not a stereotype. Her English is clear and correct but simple. British words come from the other characters.

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Looking back

Talk with your teacher.

- What’s the best thing you’ve done this year?
- What’s the most useful English you’ve learned in this course?
- Would you like to live in another country? Where?

[image: Photo idea: travel photos on a phone]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it short and personal. Push for longer answers: answer, add a detail, ask back. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: Edinburgh’s Royal Mile in August, busy with people. A street performer juggles. Haruka, Kenta (20, glasses) and Callum watch. The performer juggles three balls. Kenta takes a photo. Callum points up the street towards the castle and they all start walking.]

- What is the street performer doing?
- Where is Callum pointing? What do you think they do next?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Welcome to Edinburgh!

August. Callum meets Haruka and Kenta at Edinburgh Waverley station.

**Callum:** Welcome to Edinburgh! You must be Kenta.  
**Kenta:** Hi, Callum! Nice to meet you. Wow, it’s so busy!  
**Callum:** Aye, it’s the Fringe. Have you ever been to a festival like this?  
**Kenta:** No, never! What should we see first?  
**Callum:** The castle. It’s on an old volcano, right in the middle of the city.  
**Haruka:** A volcano? Is it safe?  
**Callum:** Don’t worry. It stopped millions of years ago!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Haruka or Kenta), then swap roles. (~2 min)

Edinburgh Castle stands on Castle Rock, the remains of a volcano that was active around 350 million years ago.
This dialogue recycles “You must be…” (Lesson 1), “Aye” (Lesson 9), the present perfect (Lesson 10) and should (Lesson 11).

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- Where does Callum meet them?
- Why is Edinburgh so busy?
- What should they see first?
- What is the castle on?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. At Edinburgh Waverley station.  2. It’s the Fringe.  3. The castle.  4. An old volcano.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. If the student can’t answer, go back to the dialogue together. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · Edinburgh (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a castle | noun | 城 | a big, strong old building where kings and queens lived | Edinburgh **Castle** is on a hill. |
| a volcano | noun | 火山 | a mountain that can send out fire and hot rock | Arthur’s Seat is an old **volcano**. |
| a street performer | noun | 大道芸人 | someone who sings, dances or does tricks in the street | There are **street performers** everywhere. |
| a view | noun | 眺め | what you can see from a place | What a **view**! |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Drill: castle /ˈkɑːsl/ (the t is silent), volcano /vɒlˈkeɪnəʊ/. (~1.5 min)
Ask: Is there a famous castle or view in your town?

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Looking back (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to decide | verb | 決める | to choose after thinking | I’ve **decided** to stay. |
| to miss | verb | （人が）いなくて寂しい | to feel sad because someone isn’t with you | I **miss** my family. |
| an experience | noun | 経験 | something that happens to you | It was an amazing **experience**. |
| to celebrate | verb | 祝う | to do something fun for a special reason | Let’s **celebrate**! |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogues. (~1.5 min)
Note: “miss” also means not catching something (miss the bus, Lesson 8). Here it’s about people.

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · On Arthur’s Seat

The next morning. Haruka and Kenta are at the top of Arthur’s Seat.

**Kenta:** What a **view**! So, how was your year in London?  
**Haruka:** Amazing! I’ve done everything on my list.  
**Kenta:** Everything? Have you had a full English?  
**Haruka:** Yes, and I’ve eaten black pudding! I’ve been to Bonfire Night and pulled a Christmas cracker.  
**Kenta:** And now you’ve climbed a volcano! Are you sad to go home next month?  
**Haruka:** Actually… my boss asked me to stay another year.  
**Kenta:** Really? What are you going to do?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~2 min)
Haruka looks back on the whole course: full English (Lesson 4), black pudding, Bonfire Night (Lesson 5), Christmas cracker (Lesson 6). Ask the student which of these they would like to try.
Kenta’s question is the big question of the course. Ask the student: What do you think Haruka will do?

</details>


#### Slide 9: Listening · Roll up!

On the Royal Mile, a street performer shouts to the crowd. Listen and answer.

[audio: Street performer]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Roll up, roll up! The show starts in five minutes. It’s free, but tips are very welcome!

- What does “Roll up!” mean?
- When does the show start?
- Do you have to pay?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Come here and watch!
> 2. In five minutes.
> 3. No, it’s free, but you can give a tip.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Roll up, roll up! The show starts in five minutes. It’s free, but tips are very welcome!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice: once for the general idea, once for details. Don’t show the transcript until after the questions. (~2 min)
The clip is new: it isn’t in the dialogues, so this checks real listening.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Language focus · Looking back

> **Present perfect or past simple?**
>
> *Your life so far*
>
> I**’ve done** everything on my list. · I**’ve eaten** black pudding.
>
> *A finished time*
>
> I **ate** black pudding **in October**. · We **climbed** it **this morning**.
>
> *Telling your news*
>
> I**’ve decided to** learn more English! · I**’ve got** some news.

**From the story**
- Haruka: **I’ve done** everything on my list.
- Haruka: Yes, and **I’ve eaten** black pudding!
- Kenta: And now **you’ve climbed** a volcano!

Use the present perfect for your life so far, and the past simple for a finished time.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Review, not new grammar. Keep it to the simple rule: life so far → present perfect; a finished time → past simple. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 11: Practice · Course review

- How often ____ you see your family?
- Could I have ____ water, please?
- London is much ____ (cold) than Osaka.
- I ____ (meet) Tom at eleven tomorrow.
- While we ____ (wait), it started to rain.
- Visitors from Japan ____ get an ETA.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. do (L2)
> 2. some (L4)
> 3. colder (L5)
> 4. ’m meeting (L6)
> 5. were waiting (L9)
> 6. have to (L11)

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

A quick review of grammar from every unit. The student completes each sentence, then makes their own example. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 12: Pronunciation · Sentence rhythm

> **Strong words, quick words**
>
> Put stress on the important words: I’ve **DONE EV**erything on my **LIST**.
>
> Say the small words quickly without stress: I’ve, on, my, to, a, the.
>
> This makes your English sound natural and clear.

**Say it with your teacher**
- I’ve **DONE** **EV**erything on my **LIST**.
- I’ve de**CID**ed to **STAY**!
- We **CLIMBED** an **OLD** vol**CA**no.
- I’ll **COME** **HOME** for ** NEW YEAR**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Clap the stressed words while you say each line; the small words fit between the claps. (~1 min)
English has a regular beat: the important words are strong, and the small words are quick and weak. Japanese gives each syllable a similar length, so English can sound flat without this.
Review: ask the student which pronunciation point from the course was the hardest, and practise it once more.

</details>


#### Slide 13: Story · Calling home

That evening. Haruka video-calls her mum in Osaka.

**Haruka:** Mum, I’ve got some news. My company wants me to stay another year.  
**Mum:** Another year? But we **miss** you!  
**Haruka:** I miss you too. But I love my job, and my English is much better.  
**Mum:** It sounds great! So, what do you want to do?  
**Haruka:** I want to stay. But I wanted to talk to you first.  
**Mum:** Haruka, it’s your life. If you’re happy, we’re happy.  
**Haruka:** Thank you, Mum. I’ll come home for New Year!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. In real life they speak Japanese; the dialogue is in English for practice. (~1.5 min)
New Year is the big family holiday in Japan (Lesson 6), so coming home for New Year matters.
Ask: Would you talk to your family first, like Haruka? Why?

</details>


#### Slide 14: Role play · Advice for Haruka

> **Student**
>
> You’re Haruka’s friend.
>
> • Ask about her year: What have you done?
> • Give advice: Should she stay or go home?
> • Use if… will: “If you stay, you’ll…”

**Useful phrases**
- What have you done this year?
- You should…
- If you stay, you’ll…
- I miss…
- I’ve decided to…

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re Haruka. Use the story.
>
> • Talk about your year: work, Tom, Gwen.
> • Say what you miss about Japan.
> • Ask: “Should I stay or go home?”

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Do it once. The teacher is Haruka and uses what happened in the course (her job, Tom, Gwen, tea). The student is her friend and gives advice. Encourage should, if… will, and the present perfect. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Final check · My year in English

> **Student**
>
> Talk to your teacher about your year.
>
> • What have you done? Who have you met?
> • Tell one story: first, then, in the end.
> • What are you going to do next?

**Course phrases**
- I’ve been to…
- Last month I…
- First…, then…
- I’m going to…
- I was like…
- You should…

> **Teacher**
>
> Ask about the student’s year.
>
> • Ask “Have you ever…?” and “What happened?”
> • Ask about their plans.
> • Give one piece of advice.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Final check for the whole course. Let the student lead and don’t correct during the conversation. Note what they do well and one thing to practise. (~3 min)
Look for: introductions and asking back, polite requests, comparing, plans with be + -ing, telling a story in the past, the present perfect for experiences, should and have to. The student self-checks on the wrap-up slide.

</details>


#### Slide 16: Reading · Edinburgh

[image: Photo idea: the Royal Mile during the Fringe]

Edinburgh is the capital of Scotland. Its famous **castle** stands on a rock that was once a **volcano**. Arthur’s Seat, another old volcano in the city, is 251 metres high, and the **view** from the top is amazing.

The Royal Mile is the old street between the castle and the Palace of Holyroodhouse.

Every August, the city has the Edinburgh Festival Fringe, the world’s biggest arts festival. Thousands of shows happen all over the city, and the Royal Mile is full of **street performers**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. Correct pronunciation at the end of each paragraph. (~2.5 min)
Check: What is the castle built on? How high is Arthur’s Seat? When is the Fringe?
Sources: Edinburgh Castle (Castle Rock is a volcanic plug); Arthur’s Seat 251 m; Edinburgh Festival Fringe (since 1947, every August).

</details>


#### Slide 17: Reading · True or false?

- Edinburgh is the capital of Scotland.
- Arthur’s Seat is a modern building.
- The Royal Mile goes from the castle to the Palace of Holyroodhouse.
- The Fringe happens every December.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. True.  2. False: it’s an old volcano.  3. True.  4. False: it happens every August.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences in their own words. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 18: Discussion · Living abroad

[image: Photo idea: a young woman looking out at a new city]

- Would you like to live abroad for a year? Where?
- What would be the hardest thing? Why?
- Would you make the same decision as Haruka?
- What have you learned from Haruka’s year?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student explain their own view; don’t tell them. Encourage the present perfect and should. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 19: Story · Some things never change

Back in London. Haruka has a cup of tea with Gwen and Tom.

**Gwen:** So, how was Edinburgh, love?  
**Haruka:** Wonderful! And I’ve got some news. **I’ve decided** to stay another year!  
**Tom:** Yes! So I don’t need a new flatmate!  
**Gwen:** Oh, that’s lovely. Let’s **celebrate**. I’ll put the kettle on!  
**Haruka:** Can I put the milk in first?  
**Gwen:** Absolutely not!  
**Tom:** Haha! Some things never change.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. Enjoy it: this is the end of the course story. (~1.5 min)
Callbacks: “I’ll put the kettle on” (Lesson 1) and Gwen’s tea rules, milk first or tea first (Lesson 2).
“Some things never change” means some things always stay the same.

</details>


#### Slide 20: What’s next · To be continued…

> **Haruka’s second year**
>
> Haruka is staying in the UK for another year, and her story continues in the intermediate course. New places, new people, and more British surprises!

[image: an image of haruka and her housemates celebrating with tea]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Tell the student Haruka’s story continues in the B1 course, if they’d like to carry on. (~0.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Wrap-up · Course check

**I can…**
- introduce myself, talk about my family and ask politely
- order food, make small talk and make plans
- give directions and tell stories about the past
- talk about my experiences and give advice

[image: Photo idea: Haruka’s UK list, all ticked]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Self-check for the whole course: for each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to keep practising. Congratulate the student on finishing the course! (~1.5 min)

</details>



---

## Great Britain: Intermediate (B1→B2)

Haruka’s second year in London. Learn the English you need to live in Britain: work, renting, health, travel problems and paperwork, with British culture, banter and a mystery running through the year.

Characters: Haruka, Gwen, Tom, Priya, Yuki, Mum, Kenta, Callum, Sian, Linda, Dan, Pharmacist, Clerk, Agent, Waiter, Staff, Receptionist, Manager, Steward, Host, Artist


## Unit: Year two


### Lesson 1: The new girl

**Objective:** Students can start and keep up a conversation with a new colleague, say how long they have been doing things, and give practical advice (CEFR B1 conversation: start up a conversation and help keep it going; give advice).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 1 · Year two · Lesson 1

**The new girl**

Today’s goal: meet someone new, keep the conversation going and give useful advice.

[image: Photo idea: Haruka at an office window in London]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

September, one year after the beginner course. Haruka starts her second year in London, and a new illustrator, Yuki, joins her company. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Level: B1, moving towards B2 later in the course. Main points today: the present perfect continuous (how long) and giving advice.

Timing: 27 slides, about 46 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip The story so far (if the student did the beginner course), Advice for Yuki, True or false and What do they really mean? (saves about 7 minutes).

In year two Haruka is confident and uses some British words herself. Yuki is new, so now Haruka is the one explaining Britain.

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · First days

Talk with your teacher.

- Think about a first day at a new job or school. How did you feel?
- What helped you settle in?
- What advice would you give someone new?

[image: Photo idea: a new employee’s desk on the first day]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal and push for longer answers: answer, add a detail, ask back. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Story · The story so far

> **Haruka’s first year**
>
> Last year, Haruka came to London from Osaka on a working holiday. She lived in Gwen’s house with her flatmate Tom, worked with Priya, and did everything on her UK list, from a full English breakfast to Bonfire Night and a trip to Edinburgh.
>
> Then her company asked her to stay another year, and she said yes.
>
> Now it’s September again, and someone new is starting at work…

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

For students who didn’t do the beginner course, this is all they need to know. For students who did, ask: What do you remember? What was your favourite part? (~1.5 min)
Visa note (teacher only): the UK Youth Mobility Scheme lets Japanese nationals stay up to two years, so this is Haruka’s last year on that visa. This comes back later in the course.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Meet the characters

> **Haruka · Osaka**
>
> Year two in London!
> Now she knows the UK well,
> and she loves helping
> new people.

> **Yuki · Tokyo (new!)**
>
> An illustrator. She has just
> joined Haruka’s company.
> She’s always drawing, and
> she often works late.

> **Tom · Manchester**
>
> Haruka’s flatmate and
> good friend. Funny,
> always late, and he loves
> teasing Haruka.

> **Priya · Birmingham**
>
> Haruka’s colleague
> and friend. A project
> manager who loves
> a good plan.

[image: Haruka]

[image: Yuki]

[image: Tom]

[image: Priya]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the profiles. Ask: Who is new? What do you think Yuki and Haruka will talk about? (~1.5 min)
Gwen, Haruka’s Welsh landlady, is back too: she appears in the last dialogue.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: A London office kitchen. Haruka (26) is making tea in five mugs. Yuki (29, glasses, olive shirt) watches with a sketchbook. Haruka makes tea in all five mugs and counts on her fingers. Yuki looks surprised and writes in her sketchbook.]

- What is Haruka doing? How many mugs are there?
- What is Yuki doing? Why, do you think?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.
Don’t explain the tea round yet: it comes in the dialogues.

</details>


#### Slide 6: Story · The office tour

Monday morning. Priya shows Yuki around the office, and Haruka joins them.

**Priya:** And this is the kitchen. The most important room in the office!  
**Yuki:** Can I use any of these mugs?  
**Priya:** Careful! Most of them belong to someone. Dev’s been using that one for ten years.  
**Yuki:** Ten years? How long have you been working here, Priya?  
**Priya:** Six years. And Haruka’s been here about a year.  
**Haruka:** That’s right. Oh, Yuki, I’ve been saving this mug for you!  
**Yuki:** For me? That’s so kind. Thank you!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Yuki), then swap roles. (~2 min)
In many British offices people have their own mug, and using someone else’s favourite mug can cause (mostly joking) complaints.
Priya is British, so the conversation is in English. Ask: Do people have their own cups at work in Japan?

</details>


#### Slide 7: Story · Did you understand?

- Which room does Priya say is the most important?
- Why should Yuki be careful with the mugs?
- How long has Priya been working there?
- What has Haruka been saving for Yuki?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. The kitchen.  2. Most of them belong to someone.  3. For six years.  4. A mug.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. If the student can’t answer, go back to the dialogue together. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 8: Vocabulary · At the office (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Fancy…? | phrase (informal) | 〜はどう？（くだけた誘い方） | Would you like…? | **Fancy** a coffee? |
| a tea round | noun | みんなの分のお茶を入れること | when one person makes tea for everyone | It’s my turn to do the **tea round**. |
| a turn | noun | 番、順番 | the time when it’s your job to do something | Next time, it’s your **turn**. |
| to take (tea / coffee) | verb | （紅茶などを）〜で飲む | to have tea or coffee in a certain way | How do you **take** your tea? Milk, no sugar. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue. (~1.5 min)
“Fancy…?” is a friendly, informal offer. With people you don’t know, “Would you like…?” is safer.
“How do you take it?” is the normal question about milk and sugar.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Vocabulary · Getting to know people (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a sausage roll | noun | ソーセージロール（パイ） | sausage meat inside crispy pastry | Can you get me a **sausage roll**? |
| to run late | phrase | 遅れている | to be later than you planned | Sorry, I’m **running late**! |
| to tease | verb | からかう | to make jokes about someone in a friendly way | Tom loves **teasing** Haruka. |
| to get used to | verb | 〜に慣れる | to start to feel normal about something new | You’ll **get used to** it. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come in the listening clip and dialogue 3. (~1.5 min)
Check “get used to + noun / -ing” (I’m getting used to the weather / to working here). Don’t confuse it with “used to” for past habits, which comes later in the course.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Story · The tea round

Later that morning, in the office kitchen. Haruka, Yuki and Priya.

**Haruka:** It’s my **turn** to do the **tea round**. **Fancy** a brew?  
**Yuki:** A brew? Tea round? Sorry, what’s that?  
**Haruka:** A brew is a cup of tea. In a tea round, one person makes tea for everyone.  
**Yuki:** Everyone? Do I have to do it too?  
**Haruka:** You don’t have to. But if I were you, I’d learn how everyone **takes** it.  
**Priya:** Ooh, tea round? Builder’s tea for me, please!  
**Yuki:** Builder’s tea? OK… I ought to write this down.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Priya, Haruka’s colleague from the beginner course, joins in.
Tea rounds are common in many British offices, but not everywhere. It’s polite to offer when it’s your turn.
“Fancy a…?” is an informal offer: Fancy a brew? = Would you like a cup of tea?
Builder’s tea is explained on the British phrases slide.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Listening · Tom’s message

Lunchtime. Haruka gets a voice message from Tom. Listen and answer.

[audio: Tom’s voice message]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Hiya, it’s Tom. I’m running a bit late. Again, I know! Can you get me a sausage roll? I owe you one. See you at Greggs in ten!

- Why is Tom calling Haruka?
- What does he want her to do?
- What does Tom mean by “Again, I know!”?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. He’s running late.
> 2. Buy him a sausage roll.
> 3. He’s often late, and he’s joking about it.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Hiya, it’s Tom. I’m running a bit late. Again, I know! Can you get me a sausage roll? I owe you one. See you at Greggs in ten!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice: once for the general idea, once for details. Don’t show the transcript until after the questions. (~2 min)
“Again, I know!” is Tom teasing himself. It prepares the student for the teasing in the next dialogue.

</details>


#### Slide 12: Vocabulary · British phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a brew | noun (informal) | （くだけた言い方）紅茶 | a cup of tea | Fancy a **brew**? |
| builder’s tea | noun | ミルク入りの濃い紅茶 | strong tea with milk and often sugar | **Builder’s tea** for me, please! |
| Hiya | phrase (informal) | やあ、こんにちは | hello | **Hiya**, it’s Tom. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

All three are common in everyday British English today. (~1.5 min)
Builder’s tea is named after the strong, sweet tea people imagine builders drinking on their break.
“Hiya” is friendly and informal, common in the north of England and Wales.

</details>


#### Slide 13: Language focus · How long have you been…?

> **have / has been + -ing**
>
> *Still happening now*
>
> I**’ve been living** in London for a year. · She**’s been drawing** all day.
>
> *Asking how long*
>
> How long **have** you **been working** here? · **For** a year. / **Since** September.
>
> *Not with some verbs*
>
> I**’ve known** Tom for a year. (not “I’ve been knowing”)

**From the story**
- Priya: Dev**’s been using** that one for ten years.
- Yuki: How long **have** you **been working** here?
- Haruka: I**’ve been saving** this mug for you!

Use the present perfect continuous for something that started in the past and is still happening now.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Elicit the form from the examples first. Japanese learners often say “How long are you working here?” Correct it to “How long have you been working here?” (~2 min)
for + a period (for a year), since + a starting point (since September).
Verbs like know, like and have (= own) don’t usually use the continuous: “I’ve known Tom for a year.”

</details>


#### Slide 14: Practice · How long?

- Yuki ____ (draw) all morning.
- How long ____ you ____ (learn) English?
- Haruka ____ (wait) for Tom for twenty minutes.
- I’ve lived here ____ March.
- I ____ (know) Priya for a year.
- Your turn: How long have you been…?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. has been drawing
> 2. have … been learning
> 3. has been waiting
> 4. since
> 5. have known
> 6. Student’s own answer

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student completes each sentence, then answers number 6 about their own life. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Language focus · Giving advice

> **Advice**
>
> *Advice*
>
> You **should** learn everyone’s names. · You **ought to** try Greggs.
>
> *Softer and more personal*
>
> **If I were you, I’d** bring a notebook.
>
> *It isn’t necessary*
>
> You **don’t have to** make tea for everyone.

**From the story**
- Haruka: You **don’t have to**.
- Haruka: **If I were you, I’d** learn how everyone takes it.
- Yuki: OK… I **ought to** write this down.

Use these to give advice. Some are softer than others.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Should and ought to mean almost the same. Ought to is a little more formal and less common in questions. “If I were you, I’d…” is softer and more personal: learn it as a fixed phrase for now. (~2 min)
Recycles “don’t have to” from the beginner course.

</details>


#### Slide 16: Practice · Advice for Yuki

- Yuki used someone else’s mug by mistake.
- She’s always tired because she works late.
- She wants to make friends in London.
- She thinks she has to make tea for everyone.
- She doesn’t know what to have for lunch.

> **Possible answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. You should say sorry and wash it.
> 2. If I were you, I’d go to bed earlier.
> 3. You ought to come to after-work drinks.
> 4. You don’t have to, but it’s nice to offer.
> 5. You should try Greggs!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student gives advice for each problem. Push for a reason: “You should…, because…”. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 17: Pronunciation · Short and quick

> **How it sounds**
>
> I’ve been living here → ive-bin **LIV**-ing here
> How long have you been here? → how **LONG** av-ya-bin here?
> You ought to ask → ya **AW**-ta **ASK**

**Say it with your teacher**
- I’ve been **working** here for a **year**.
- How **long** have you been in **London**?
- You ought to **try** it.
- She’s been **drawing** all **day**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

“Been” is usually weak in these sentences: /bɪn/, not /biːn/. “Ought to” sounds like /ˈɔːtə/. Say each line; the student repeats. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 18: Story · Are you two fighting?

Lunchtime outside Greggs. Haruka and Yuki are waiting when Tom arrives.

**Tom:** Sorry I’m late! **Have** you **been waiting** long?  
**Haruka:** Only twenty minutes. That’s early for you, Tom!  
**Tom:** Very funny. You must be Yuki. I’m Tom. I live with this one.  
**Yuki:** Nice to meet you… Sorry, are you two fighting?  
**Tom:** Fighting? No! If I didn’t like her, I’d be really polite.  
**Haruka:** It’s true. Friends here **tease** each other. Like in Osaka!  
**Tom:** You’ll **get used to** it. Anyway, thanks for the **sausage roll**!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
“I live with this one” is a friendly, informal way to point at someone.
Tom’s joke (“If I didn’t like her, I’d be really polite”) is the key idea of the next slide.
Haruka’s “Like in Osaka!” is her own view. Ask the student about it on the discussion slide; don’t present it as a fact about Japan.

</details>


#### Slide 19: Culture · Why is Tom so rude?

> **Teasing means we’re friends**
>
> In Britain, close friends often **tease** each other. They make jokes about each other, like Tom being late, and nobody gets angry.
>
> With people they don’t know well, many British people are very polite. So when a British friend starts teasing you, it can be a good sign: it often means “we’re close now”. People are different, though, and it’s fine to say if teasing feels too much.
>
> Many visitors find this strange at first, and some British people do too. You don’t have to tease back. “Very funny!” is a fine answer.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

This explains the joke in the last dialogue straight away. (~1.5 min)
Ask: Was Tom being rude? How would you feel if a friend teased you like that?
Teasing between friends is often called “banter”. The course comes back to banter later, including where the line is.

</details>


#### Slide 20: Role play · Showing someone around

> **Student**
>
> Someone new starts at your real workplace (or school) today. Show them around.
>
> • The kitchen, the toilets, where to sit…
> • Say how long you’ve been working or studying there.
> • Give two tips: ought to, If I were you…

**Useful phrases**
- This is the kitchen.
- I’ve been working here for…
- You ought to…
- If I were you, I’d…
- You don’t have to…
- Careful! That’s Dev’s mug.

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re new. It’s your first day.
>
> • Ask: How long have you been…?
> • Ask: Do I have to…?
> • Make one mistake: use someone’s mug!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student shows you around their real workplace or school, so there’s nothing to invent. You’re the new person. No swap needed. (~2.5 min)
Listen for the present perfect continuous and advice.

</details>


#### Slide 21: Reading · Greggs

[image: Photo idea: a queue outside a bakery on a British high street]

Greggs is a bakery chain from Newcastle, in the north-east of England. John Gregg started the business in 1939, and the first shop opened in 1951. Today there are more than 2,500 Greggs shops, on high streets and in train stations all over the UK.

People go there for cheap, warm food **on the go**, especially the **sausage roll**. In January 2019, Greggs started selling a **vegan** sausage roll, and it sold so well that the company had record sales that year.

In 2026, Greggs even had more coffee shops than Costa.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. Correct pronunciation at the end of each paragraph. (~2.5 min)
Check: Where is Greggs from? What happened in 2019? What happened in 2026?
Sources: Greggs (Wikipedia, company results): founded by John Gregg in 1939; first shop on Gosforth High Street in 1951; about 2,500 shops; vegan sausage roll launched January 2019 and record sales that year; in September 2026 Greggs had 2,737 coffee outlets to Costa’s 2,707.

</details>


#### Slide 22: Reading · True or false?

- Greggs started in London.
- The first Greggs shop opened in 1951.
- The vegan sausage roll came out in 2019.
- Greggs is a good place for an expensive dinner.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: it started in Newcastle.  2. True.  3. True.  4. False: it sells cheap food on the go.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences in their own words. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 23: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: colleagues laughing in an office kitchen]

- Is there anything like a tea round where you work or study?
- Where do people in Japan get a cheap lunch on the go?
- Haruka says friends in Osaka tease each other too. Is that true where you live?
- How do you feel when a friend teases you?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Encourage the present perfect continuous and advice. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 24: Story · A new friend

That evening. Haruka and Tom are having tea with Gwen.

**Gwen:** So, how was work today, love?  
**Haruka:** Good! A new illustrator started today, Yuki. She**’s been drawing** everyone all day.  
**Tom:** She drew me eating a sausage roll. Very flattering.  
**Gwen:** And where’s she living?  
**Haruka:** In a short-term room. But she has to leave next month.  
**Gwen:** Next month? Finding a flat in London isn’t easy.  
**Haruka:** I know. I said I’d help her look this weekend.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
“Very flattering” is sarcasm: Tom means the drawing didn’t make him look good. Ask the student how they know.
Hook: next lesson Haruka helps Yuki find a flat. Ask: What do you need to check before you rent a flat?
Remember that Yuki is always drawing. It matters later.

</details>


#### Slide 25: Story · Mum’s news

A moment later. Gwen, Tom and Haruka.

**Gwen:** And how’s your mum? Still worrying about you?  
**Haruka:** Always! But she’s busy now. She’s started an English class.  
**Tom:** Good for her!  
**Haruka:** Her teacher says she has to keep a diary in English all year. So she asks me a hundred questions about my day!  
**Gwen:** So you’re her homework now!  
**Haruka:** Basically, yes.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Teacher only: this is the first real clue to the Milk Second mystery, hidden in a joke. Mum’s English diary becomes the comic. Don’t draw attention to it.

</details>


#### Slide 26: Culture · What do they really mean?

> **Polite office English**
>
> At work, many people in Britain say things in a soft, indirect way. You might hear:
>
> “**Not bad**” = often means good!
> “**Quite good**” = can mean just OK.
> “**I’ll bear it in mind**” = often maybe… or maybe not.
> “**With respect…**” = often I don’t agree.
>
> Not sure what someone means? It’s fine to ask: “Sorry, do you mean…?”

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Office English that new people often misunderstand. (~1.5 min)
These are possible meanings, not fixed codes: tone and context matter. “Quite” can mean “fairly” (quite good = OK) or “very” (quite amazing), and American speakers use the “very” meaning more often. Yuki learned American English at school, so she might hear “quite good” as a big compliment.
Ask: Are there phrases in Japanese that mean something different from the words?

</details>


#### Slide 27: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- started a conversation with someone new and kept it going
- said how long you’ve been doing things
- gave advice with should, ought to and If I were you
- learned about tea rounds, Greggs and British teasing

[image: Photo idea: tea, a bakery bag and a sketchbook on a desk]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

For each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 2: Flat hunting

**Objective:** Students can ask detailed questions about renting a flat, understand the answers, and talk about rules and what is or isn’t necessary (CEFR B1: find out and pass on straightforward factual information; cope with less routine situations).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 1 · Year two · Lesson 2

**Flat hunting**

Today’s goal: ask detailed questions about a flat, understand the answers and talk about rules.

[image: Photo idea: London terraced houses in autumn]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

October. Yuki needs a flat, so Haruka helps her look. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Yuki joined Haruka’s company in Lesson 1. Her short-term room ends next month, and Haruka said she’d help her look this weekend.

Main points: need to / don’t need to / have to for rules, and be supposed to. Practical topic: renting a flat in England.

Timing: 24 slides, about 41 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip What are you supposed to do?, True or false and Useful phrases (saves about 4.5 minutes).

Renting rules differ a little in Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland. Everything here is for England, where the story happens.

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Home sweet home

Talk with your teacher.

- Have you ever moved house? What was the hardest part?
- What’s most important for you in a flat or house?
- Would you rather live alone or share? Why?

[image: Photo idea: moving boxes in an empty flat]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. Push for reasons and details. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: A small, empty London flat. Dan, a letting agent, shows Yuki and Haruka around. Yuki opens a tiny kitchen cupboard and laughs. Haruka checks the tap and opens the window. Dan points at the view.]

- What are Haruka and Yuki checking?
- What does Yuki think of the kitchen? How do you know?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Too good to be true

Saturday morning. Yuki and Haruka look at flat adverts with Gwen.

**Yuki:** Look at this one. A big flat in Camden, only £900 a month!  
**Gwen:** £900? In Camden? That’s too good to be true, love.  
**Haruka:** The landlord is abroad. It says you need to send the deposit first, and he’ll post the keys.  
**Gwen:** Don’t send a penny! You’re supposed to see a flat before you pay anything.  
**Yuki:** But he says lots of people want it.  
**Gwen:** Of course he does. It’s a scam. Delete it, and let’s find a real letting agent.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Yuki), then swap. (~2 min)
Gwen is right: a cheap flat, a landlord who is “abroad” and a deposit before a viewing are classic signs of a rental scam (Action Fraud and Which? give the same warnings).
“Don’t send a penny!” means don’t send any money at all.
Ask: Have you ever seen an advert that was too good to be true?

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- How much is the flat in the advert?
- What does the landlord want Yuki to do?
- Why does Gwen think it’s a scam?
- What does Gwen tell Yuki to do?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. £900 a month.  2. Send the deposit first.  3. It’s too cheap for Camden, and you should see a flat before you pay.  4. Delete it and find a real letting agent.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · Renting (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a letting agent | noun | 不動産仲介業者 | a company that rents out flats for landlords | Let’s find a real **letting agent**. |
| a viewing | noun | 内見 | a visit to see a flat before you rent it | The **viewing** is at ten o’clock. |
| a deposit | noun | 敷金、保証金 | money you pay at the start and get back at the end | The **deposit** is five weeks’ rent. |
| a scam | noun | 詐欺 | a dishonest plan to take your money | Don’t pay! It’s a **scam**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the first two dialogues. (~1.5 min)
Katakana false friend: マンション is usually just “a flat” in British English. A “mansion” is a very big, expensive house.

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Renting (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| bills | noun (plural) | 光熱費などの請求 | money you pay for water, electricity, gas, Wi-Fi | Are the **bills** included? |
| included | adjective | 含まれている | part of the price | Wi-Fi is **included** in the rent. |
| council tax | noun | 地方税（住民税に近い） | money you pay to the local council every year | You pay the **council tax** yourself. |
| bin day | noun | ごみの収集日 | the day the council collects rubbish | When’s **bin day**? |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come in the viewing. (~1.5 min)
Council tax pays for local services like rubbish collection and libraries. It’s paid by the people who live in a home, not by the landlord, unless the rent says “bills included”.

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · The viewing

Monday evening. Dan, a letting agent, shows Yuki and Haruka a flat.

**Dan:** Thanks for coming to the **viewing**. This is the living room. It’s small, but it gets lots of light.  
**Yuki:** It’s nice! Are the **bills included**?  
**Dan:** Water and Wi-Fi are. You need to pay the **council tax** yourself.  
**Haruka:** And the **deposit**? Is it protected?  
**Dan:** Yes, it’s five weeks’ rent, in a government scheme. You don’t need to pay any fees.  
**Yuki:** Great. Oh, and when’s **bin day**?  
**Dan:** Tuesday. You’re supposed to put the bins out on Monday night. Nobody remembers!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
All true in England: deposits are capped at five weeks’ rent (if the rent is under £50,000 a year) and must go into a government-approved deposit scheme within 30 days. Since the Tenant Fees Act 2019, landlords and letting agents can’t charge most fees (for example admin or referencing fees). Rent, a deposit and a holding deposit are still allowed, plus a few others, such as a fee for a lost key.
Haruka asks the expert question (“Is it protected?”) because she’s rented here before.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Listening · A message from Dan

Two days later, at work. Yuki plays Haruka a voice message from Dan. Listen and answer.

[audio: Dan’s voice message]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Hi Yuki, it’s Dan from Parkside Lettings. Good news: the landlord said yes! You’re supposed to send your share code by Friday, so call me back.

- What’s the good news?
- What is Yuki supposed to send?
- When does she need to send it?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. The landlord said yes: she can rent the flat.
> 2. Her share code.
> 3. By Friday.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Hi Yuki, it’s Dan from Parkside Lettings. Good news: the landlord said yes! You’re supposed to send your share code by Friday, so call me back.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~2 min)
A share code is a code from the Home Office website that lets a landlord check your right to rent. Landlords in England must check that tenants have the “right to rent”, and people with a digital visa (eVisa) give a share code.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Vocabulary · Useful phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| too good to be true | phrase | うますぎる話 | so good that it probably isn’t real | £900 in Camden? That’s **too good to be true**. |
| (not) a penny | phrase | 1ペニーも（払わない） | no money at all (in negative sentences) | Don’t send **a penny**! |
| a bargain | noun | 掘り出し物、お買い得 | something good that’s very cheap | This chair was only £15. What a **bargain**! |
| to move in | verb | 入居する | to start living in a new home | I **move in** next week. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

All common in everyday British English. (~1.5 min)
“Bargain!” on its own is a common reaction when something is cheap. Tom uses it sarcastically at the end of the lesson.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Language focus · Need to, don’t need to

> **Rules and necessity**
>
> *It’s necessary*
>
> You **need to** pay council tax. · You **have to** show your visa.
>
> *It isn’t necessary*
>
> You **don’t need to** pay any fees. · You **don’t have to** buy furniture.
>
> *Don’t do it!*
>
> You **mustn’t** pay before a viewing.

**From the story**
- Haruka: It says you **need to** send the deposit first.
- Dan: You **need to** pay the council tax yourself.
- Dan: You **don’t need to** pay any fees.

Use these to talk about what is necessary and what isn’t.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Need to and have to are very close for rules and necessary things. “Don’t need to” = “don’t have to”: it isn’t necessary. “Mustn’t” is different: don’t do it! Japanese learners often mix up “don’t have to” and “mustn’t”. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 12: Practice · Renting rules

- In England, tenants ____ pay letting agent fees.
- You ____ give your share code to the landlord.
- You ____ pay a deposit before you see the flat.
- If you live alone, you ____ pay full council tax.
- The landlord ____ protect your deposit.
- Your turn: a rule in your home.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. don’t need to
> 2. need to
> 3. mustn’t
> 4. don’t need to (you get 25% off)
> 5. needs to / has to
> 6. Student’s own answer

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student completes each sentence with need to, don’t need to or mustn’t, then gives a rule from their own home. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Language focus · Be supposed to

> **be supposed to + verb**
>
> *A rule*
>
> You**’re supposed to** put the bins out on Monday night.
>
> *But it doesn’t happen*
>
> We**’re supposed to** recycle glass, but Tom never does.
>
> *A plan that went wrong*
>
> I **was supposed to** call Dan yesterday, but I forgot.
