**From the story**
- Gwen: You**’re supposed to** see a flat before you pay.
- Dan: You**’re supposed to** put the bins out on Monday night.
- Dan: You**’re supposed to** send your share code by Friday.

Use be supposed to for rules and plans, especially when people don’t follow them.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

“Be supposed to” = it’s the rule, or what people expect. It often suggests the rule isn’t always followed. Japanese learners often drop the d: “suppose to”. In speech the d is almost silent, but it’s always there in writing. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 14: Practice · What are you supposed to do?

- …on bin day?
- …when it’s your turn for the tea round?
- …before you rent a flat?
- …when you get on a bus in Japan?
- Something you’re supposed to do, but don’t.

> **Possible answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. You’re supposed to put the bins out the night before.
> 2. You’re supposed to make tea for everyone.
> 3. You’re supposed to see it first.
> 4. Student’s own answer
> 5. I’m supposed to exercise every day, but I don’t!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student answers with be supposed to. For number 5, push for “…but I don’t”. Skip this slide if time is short. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Pronunciation · Two-word nouns

> **Stress the first word**
>
> **COUN**-cil tax · **BIN** day · **LET**-ting agent
> **CHAR**-i-ty shop · **TEA** round · **BUS** stop

**Say it with your teacher**
- When’s **bin** day?
- Is **coun**cil tax included?
- Who’s doing the **tea** round?
- You’re sup**posed** to put the **bins** out.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

In most two-word nouns the stress is on the first word: COUNcil tax, BIN day. Many learners stress both words equally. “Supposed to” sounds like s’POHS-ta, with a very short first syllable. Say each line; the student repeats. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 16: Role play · At the viewing

> **Student**
>
> You’re looking at a flat in London.
>
> • Ask about bills, the deposit and council tax.
> • Ask what you’re supposed to do on bin day.
> • Decide: will you take it?

**Useful phrases**
- Are the bills included?
- How much is the deposit?
- Is it protected?
- Do I need to…?
- When can I move in?
- I’ll think about it.

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re the letting agent. The flat:
>
> • £1,450 a month. Bills aren’t included.
> • Deposit: five weeks’ rent. Council tax: about £150 a month.
> • Bins: recycling on Tuesday, rubbish on Friday.
> • Good: near the station. Bad: a tiny kitchen.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

All the flat details are on the teacher’s card, so you only need to answer. The student is the renter; no swap needed. At the end, ask for a share code. (~2.5 min)
Listen for need to / don’t need to and be supposed to.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Reading · Renting in England

[image: Photo idea: new keys in front of a flat door]

Looking for a flat in England? Here are some things to check.

First, see the flat before you pay anything. Rental **scams** are common, and a very cheap flat with a landlord who is “abroad” is a warning sign.

Your **deposit** can be up to five weeks’ rent, and the landlord has to put it in a government scheme within 30 days. Letting agents can’t charge most fees, like admin fees.

The landlord will also check that you’re allowed to rent in England. If you have a visa, you give them a **share code**.

Finally, ask about **bills** and **council tax**. If you live alone, you get 25% off your council tax.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: What should you never do? How much can a deposit be? What’s a share code for?
Extra (teacher only): since 1 May 2026, under the Renters’ Rights Act, landlords in England can ask for no more than one month’s rent in advance, can’t accept bids above the advertised rent, and can’t use “no-fault” evictions.
Sources: GOV.UK and council guides on tenancy deposits (5-week cap, 30 days to protect), the Tenant Fees Act 2019, Right to Rent guidance (share codes), Action Fraud and Which? on rental scams, council guidance on the 25% single person discount.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Reading · True or false?

- It’s fine to pay a deposit before you see the flat.
- A deposit can be up to five weeks’ rent.
- Letting agents can charge tenants fees.
- People who live alone get 25% off council tax.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: see it first.  2. True.  3. False: they can’t.  4. True.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 19: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a cosy rented room]

- How do people find a flat in Japan? What do they need to pay?
- What surprised you about renting in the UK?
- Would you rent a flat without seeing it first? Why or why not?
- What are you supposed to do with rubbish where you live?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Many will mention key money (礼金), a guarantor, or the fact that many flats in Japan come without lights or curtains. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 20: Story · Milk Second

The next Monday, at lunch. Priya, Haruka and Yuki.

**Priya:** So, did you get the flat, Yuki?  
**Yuki:** Yes! I move in on Saturday. Haruka and Tom are helping me.  
**Priya:** Lucky you. Hey, have you two seen this web comic? It’s called Milk Second.  
**Yuki:** No. What’s it about?  
**Priya:** A Japanese girl in London. Haruka, she looks just like you!  
**Haruka:** Let me see… A girl with a huge suitcase… Wait. That’s my first day in London!  
**Yuki:** Really? Someone’s drawing your life?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~2 min)
The mystery starts here. Students who did the beginner course will remember Haruka’s huge suitcase on her first day (beginner Lesson 1).
Don’t give any answers. Just ask: Who could know about Haruka’s first day?

</details>


#### Slide 21: Mystery · Milk Second, episode 1

[image: Milk Second, episode 1: Hana arrives with a huge suitcase]

- What can you see? What’s the same as Haruka’s first day?
- How do you think Haruka feels about the comic?
- Who could know about her first day?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Look at the panel together. Let the student describe it and guess freely. Keep a note of their guesses: the course comes back to them. (~1.5 min)
Teacher only: the artist is Haruka’s mum, but don’t hint at it yet.

</details>


#### Slide 22: Story · Moving day

The next Saturday. Tom and Haruka help Yuki carry an armchair from a charity shop to her new flat.

**Tom:** Right, last thing. Who chose an armchair this heavy?  
**Yuki:** Sorry! It was only fifteen pounds in the charity shop.  
**Tom:** Fifteen pounds and my back. What a **bargain**.  
**Haruka:** Can we stop for a minute? I don’t feel very well.  
**Yuki:** You**’ve been coughing** all day. Are you OK?  
**Haruka:** I think I’m getting a cold. My head really hurts.  
**Tom:** Sit in the new armchair, then. That’s what it’s for!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
A charity shop is explained on the next slide.
“What a bargain” is sarcastic here: Tom means his back hurts, so it wasn’t cheap for him. Ask the student how they know.
Recycles the present perfect continuous from Lesson 1 (“You’ve been coughing all day”).
Hook: next lesson Haruka is ill. Ask: What do you do when you get ill in Japan? Where do you go?

</details>


#### Slide 23: Culture · Charity shops

> **Second-hand, for a good cause**
>
> A **charity shop** sells second-hand things that people give away: clothes, books, furniture and things for the kitchen. The money goes to a charity, like a heart charity or a children’s charity.
>
> Most British high streets have a few. People love them for bargains, and they’re very popular with students and people who have just moved.
>
> You can give things to a charity shop too. Just take them in.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Yuki bought her armchair in a charity shop. (~1.5 min)
Big charity shop chains include the British Heart Foundation, Oxfam and Barnardo’s. Some, like the BHF, have furniture shops.
Ask: Have you ever bought something second-hand? Are there shops like this in Japan? (Many students will mention recycle shops or second-hand apps. Let them explain the difference.)

</details>


#### Slide 24: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- asked detailed questions about a flat and understood the answers
- talked about rules with need to, don’t need to and be supposed to
- spotted a rental scam
- learned about deposits, council tax and charity shops

[image: Photo idea: a second-hand armchair in a new flat]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

For each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 3: Under the weather

**Objective:** Students can describe symptoms, say how long they have had a problem and when it started, and understand simple advice from a pharmacist or health service (CEFR B1: explain why something is a problem; cope with less routine situations).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 1 · Year two · Lesson 3

**Under the weather**

Today’s goal: describe symptoms, say how long you’ve had them and get help when you’re ill.

[image: Photo idea: tea, tissues and a blanket on a sofa]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

November. Haruka gets ill and has to find out how healthcare works in the UK. This is the last lesson of Unit 1, with a progress check. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Haruka helped Yuki find a flat (Lesson 2). On moving day she started to feel ill. A web comic called Milk Second seems to show Haruka’s life.

Main point: present perfect or past simple. Practical topic: what to do when you’re ill in the UK (England).

Timing: 24 slides, about 43 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip When did it start?, True or false and British phrases (saves about 4.5 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Feeling ill

Talk with your teacher.

- When were you last ill? What did you do?
- What’s your best cure for a cold?
- In Japan, where do you go first when you feel ill?

[image: Photo idea: rain on a window]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it light and personal. Encourage the past simple for the story and the present perfect for experience. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: Haruka on the sofa with a cold. Gwen brings her a mug of tea. Haruka sneezes. Gwen puts the tea down. Haruka takes a photo of the tea, and Gwen shakes her head and laughs.]

- What’s wrong with Haruka? How do you know?
- What does Haruka do with the tea? Why do you think she does that?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.
Callback: Haruka photographs every meal. Even when she’s ill!

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Calling in sick

Monday morning. Haruka phones Priya before work.

**Priya:** Hi Haruka. You sound terrible. Have you come down with something?  
**Haruka:** I think so. I’ve had a cold since Saturday, and my throat is really sore.  
**Priya:** Oh no. Have you taken anything for it?  
**Haruka:** I took some paracetamol last night, but it didn’t help much.  
**Priya:** Stay at home and rest. You can be off sick for a few days without a doctor’s note.  
**Haruka:** Thanks, Priya. Sorry about the meeting.  
**Priya:** Don’t worry about it. Just get better!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Haruka), then swap. (~2 min)
Priya is right: in the UK you can self-certify for the first seven days off sick, so you don’t need a doctor’s note. The next slide explains it.
Paracetamol is the usual everyday painkiller in the UK, sold in supermarkets and pharmacies.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- How long has Haruka had a cold?
- What did she take last night?
- Does she need a doctor’s note?
- What does Priya tell her to do?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Since Saturday.  2. Paracetamol.  3. No, not for the first few days.  4. Stay at home and rest.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Culture · Off sick

> **Calling in sick in the UK**
>
> If you’re ill and can’t work, you usually call or message your manager before work starts.
>
> For the first seven days, you don’t need a note from a doctor. You just tell your employer that you were ill.
>
> If you’re off for more than seven days, you need a **fit note** from a doctor or another health professional. Most people still call it a “sick note”.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Practical information for anyone working in the UK. (~1.5 min)
Source: GOV.UK “Taking sick leave”: employees can self-certify for the first seven days (including weekends), then need a fit note. Since 6 April 2026, Statutory Sick Pay is paid from the first day off sick (Employment Rights Act 2025). Fit notes can come from doctors, nurses, pharmacists, physiotherapists and occupational therapists (teacher information only).
Ask: What do you need to do in Japan when you’re ill and can’t work?

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Symptoms (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a sore throat | noun | 喉の痛み | when your throat hurts | I’ve had a **sore throat** since Saturday. |
| a temperature | noun | 熱 | when your body is hotter than normal | Have you got a **temperature**? |
| a cough | noun | 咳 | when you push air out of your throat, often with a noise | My **cough** is getting worse. |
| to feel sick | phrase | 吐き気がする | to feel like you might vomit | I **feel sick**. I can’t eat anything. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue. (~1.5 min)
Careful: in British English “I feel sick” usually means you might vomit. For general illness, say “I feel ill” or “I don’t feel well”. Japanese learners often use “sick” for everything.
“Have a temperature” is the usual British way to say “have a fever”.

</details>


#### Slide 8: Vocabulary · Getting help (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a GP | noun | かかりつけ医 | a family doctor at a local surgery | I need to register with a **GP**. |
| a pharmacist | noun | 薬剤師 | a person who prepares and sells medicine | The **pharmacist** can help with a sore throat. |
| a prescription | noun | 処方箋 | an official note from a doctor or nurse that lets you get medicine | You need a **prescription** for antibiotics. |
| to register | verb | 登録する | to put your name on an official list | Have you **registered** with a GP? |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come in dialogues 2 and 3. (~1.5 min)
GP = general practitioner. A GP’s office is called a surgery.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Story · Gwen’s cure

Tuesday evening. Gwen and Tom check on Haruka.

**Gwen:** How are you feeling, love? Still poorly?  
**Haruka:** Worse. I’ve had a **temperature** all day, and I **feel sick**.  
**Gwen:** I’ll make you a nice cup of tea. With honey.  
**Tom:** Gwen’s answer to everything. Have you seen a doctor?  
**Haruka:** No. I’ve never **registered** with a **GP** here. I’ve never needed to!  
**Gwen:** Then call 111. They’ll tell you where to go.  
**Tom:** Or try the pharmacy on the corner. They can treat sore throats now.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Tom teases Gwen (“Gwen’s answer to everything”), not Haruka this time. Friendly teasing, as in Lesson 1.
Facts: NHS 111 is free, open 24 hours and tells you which service to use. Since January 2024, pharmacies in England can treat some common problems, including sore throats, without a GP appointment (Pharmacy First).
Not registering with a GP for a year is quite common among young, healthy people from abroad.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Listening · Calling 111

Wednesday. Haruka calls 111. Listen and answer.

[audio: NHS 111 message]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Welcome to NHS 111. If this is a life-threatening emergency, hang up and call 999. Please stay on the line. A health adviser will speak to you soon.

- When should you call 999?
- What should Haruka do now?
- Who will speak to her?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. In a life-threatening emergency.
> 2. Stay on the line (wait).
> 3. A health adviser.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Welcome to NHS 111. If this is a life-threatening emergency, hang up and call 999. Please stay on the line. A health adviser will speak to you soon.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~2 min)
Automated messages are hard because you can’t ask them to repeat. “Stay on the line” = don’t hang up, wait.
The real 111 service asks questions and then tells you where to go. For Haruka, that’s the pharmacy (next dialogue).

</details>


#### Slide 11: Vocabulary · Useful phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| under the weather | phrase | 体調が悪い | a bit ill | I’m feeling a bit **under the weather**. |
| to come down with something | phrase | （病気に）かかりかけている | to start to get ill | I think I’m **coming down with something**. |
| off sick | phrase | 病欠で | not at work because you’re ill | Haruka is **off sick** today. |
| poorly | adjective (informal) | 具合が悪い | ill | Oh no, you look **poorly**! |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

All common in everyday English. Some, like “off sick”, “poorly”, “It should do”, “Fair enough” and “Rather you than me”, are especially British. “Poorly” is especially common in the north of England and when talking to children. (~1.5 min)
Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>


#### Slide 12: Language focus · Present perfect or past simple?

> **Up to now or finished?**
>
> *Present Perfect*
>
> I**’ve had** a cold since Saturday. · I**’ve never needed** a GP.
>
> *Past Simple*
>
> I **took** paracetamol last night. · It **started** on Saturday.
>
> *Question and answer*
>
> **Have** you **seen** a doctor? · Yes, I **saw** one yesterday.

**From the story**
- Haruka: I**’ve had** a cold since Saturday.
- Haruka: I **took** some paracetamol last night.
- Tom: **Have** you **seen** a doctor?

Use the present perfect up to now. Use the past simple for a finished time.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The key question: is the time finished? “Since Saturday” connects to now, so present perfect. “Last night” is finished, so past simple. Japanese learners often say “I have taken paracetamol last night”. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Practice · Which tense?

- I ____ (be) ill since Friday.
- I ____ (go) to the pharmacy yesterday.
- ____ you ever ____ (break) a bone?
- When ____ it ____ (start)?
- She ____ (not / sleep) well this week.
- Your turn: the last time you were ill.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. have been
> 2. went
> 3. Have … broken
> 4. did … start
> 5. hasn’t slept
> 6. Student’s own answer

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student completes each sentence, then answers number 6. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 14: Practice · When did it start?

- How long / have / it?
- When / it / start?
- you / take / anything?
- you / ever / have / this before?
- you / see / a doctor?

> **Questions** (teacher only)
>
> 1. How long have you had it?
> 2. When did it start?
> 3. Have you taken / Did you take anything?
> 4. Have you ever had this before?
> 5. Have you seen a doctor?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Role reversal: the teacher is ill. The student asks questions using the prompts and both tenses. The teacher answers with a real or invented illness. Skip this slide if time is short. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Pronunciation · Ill or eel?

> **Short or long?**
>
> Short: s**i**ck · f**i**ll · st**i**ll · s**i**t
> Long: s**ee**k · f**ee**l · st**ea**l · s**ea**t

**Say it with your teacher**
- I **feel** really **ill**.
- Can I **sit** in this **seat**?
- Please **fill** in this form.
- I **still** **feel** tired.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

/ɪ/ (sick, ill) is short and relaxed. /iː/ (seek, feel) is long, with a small smile. The Japanese い sound is between the two, so learners often say both the same way. The difference is the sound itself, not only the length. Say each pair; the student repeats, then says one word for you to guess. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 16: Story · At the pharmacy

Thursday. Haruka goes to the pharmacy and talks to the pharmacist.

**Pharmacist:** Hi there. What can I do for you?  
**Haruka:** I**’ve had** a really **sore throat** since Saturday. 111 told me to come here.  
**Pharmacist:** OK. **Have** you **had** a **temperature**, or a **cough**?  
**Haruka:** I **had** a temperature on Tuesday. And a cough, but it’s better now.  
**Pharmacist:** Let me have a look… It’s probably a virus, so you don’t need antibiotics. Rest, and see a GP if it’s not better in a week.  
**Haruka:** Thank you. And how do I **register** with a GP?  
**Pharmacist:** It’s easy. You can do it online, or on the NHS App. You don’t need any ID.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
True for England: you can register with a GP online, and you don’t need ID or proof of address (NHS guidance).
Antibiotics don’t help with viruses, and British pharmacists and GPs often say so. Ask the student: Is it easy to get antibiotics in Japan?

</details>


#### Slide 17: Role play · At the pharmacy

> **Student**
>
> You don’t feel well.
>
> • You’ve had a sore throat and a temperature since Monday.
> • You took paracetamol at 8 this morning.
> • Explain, then ask what you should do.

**Useful phrases**
- I’ve had… since…
- It started on…
- I took… last night.
- I feel ill.
- Have you had a temperature?
- Should I see a GP?

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re the pharmacist.
>
> • Ask: How long…? When did it start?
> • Ask: Have you taken anything?
> • Advise rest and fluids. If it isn’t better in a week, see a GP.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The symptoms are on the student’s card, so they don’t need to invent them. The student is the patient; no swap needed. (~2.5 min)
Listen for present perfect (I’ve had…) and past simple (It started on Monday) together.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Progress check · Unit 1 check

> **Student**
>
> You meet a new colleague from abroad.
>
> • Ask how long they’ve been in the UK.
> • Give advice on renting a flat.
> • Tell them what to do if they get ill.

**Unit 1 phrases**
- How long have you been…?
- If I were you, I’d…
- You don’t need to…
- You’re supposed to…
- Have you registered with a GP?
- Call 111.

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re new in the UK.
>
> • Answer with for or since.
> • Ask: Do I need to…? What am I supposed to…?
> • Ask about one real problem.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Progress check for Unit 1. Let the student lead and don’t correct during the conversation. Note what they do well and one thing to practise. (~3 min)
Look for: the present perfect continuous with for/since, advice (should, ought to, If I were you), need to / don’t need to / be supposed to, present perfect vs past simple, and asking follow-up questions.

</details>


#### Slide 19: Reading · Getting ill in the UK

[image: Photo idea: a pharmacist helping a customer]

In England, you can register with a **GP** (a family doctor) for free. You don’t need ID or proof of address, and you can do it online.

Not sure where to go? Call **111** or use 111 online. It’s free and open 24 hours a day, and they’ll tell you who can help.

For small problems like a sore throat or earache, try a **pharmacy** first. Pharmacists can give advice and, for some problems, medicine you usually need a doctor for.

In England, a **prescription** costs £9.90. In Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland, it’s free.

In a real emergency, call **999**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: Do you need ID to register with a GP? When can you call 111? How much is a prescription in England?
Sources: NHS “How to register with a GP surgery” (no ID or proof of address needed); NHS 111 (free, 24/7, phone, online or NHS App); NHS England Pharmacy First (from January 2024, seven common conditions including sore throat and earache); prescription charge £9.90 per item in England for 2026/27; prescriptions are free in Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland.

</details>


#### Slide 20: Reading · True or false?

- You need ID to register with a GP in England.
- 111 is open 24 hours a day.
- Pharmacists can help with a sore throat.
- Prescriptions cost £9.90 everywhere in the UK.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: you don’t.  2. True.  3. True.  4. False: they’re free in Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a calm clinic waiting room]

- In Japan, can you go straight to a doctor without an appointment?
- Do people in Japan go to work when they’re ill?
- Which system do you prefer, the UK’s or Japan’s? Why?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Many will mention going straight to a clinic or a specialist without an appointment. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 22: Story · Back at work

The next Monday at the office. Priya, Haruka and Yuki.

**Priya:** You’re back! Still under the weather?  
**Haruka:** No, much better, thanks. Have you seen the new Milk Second?  
**Priya:** Yes! Hana loves brown sauce, and then she gets a cold. Isn’t that you?  
**Haruka:** The brown sauce was my first English breakfast! And the cold was last week.  
**Yuki:** Maybe they saw your photos. Is your account public?  
**Haruka:** Not any more. I made it private after I saw the comic. But my soup photo from last week…  
**Priya:** …is in the comic! So it must be someone who can see your private photos.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
The first real clue: Haruka made her photos private after Lesson 2, but the comic still shows the soup she photographed while she was ill. So the artist must be someone who can see her private photos.
Callbacks: brown sauce and Haruka’s first English breakfast (beginner Lesson 4), and Haruka photographing every meal (even when she’s ill).
“It must be…” is a preview of deduction, which comes in Unit 3. Treat it as a phrase for now.
Don’t solve it. Ask: Who can see Haruka’s photos? Make a list together and keep it for later lessons.

</details>


#### Slide 23: Mystery · Milk Second, episode 3

[image: Milk Second, episode 3: Hana and the brown sauce]

- What happens in this episode?
- Who do you think is drawing the comic? Why?
- Would you be happy if someone drew a comic about your life?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe the panel and guess. Start a list of suspects together (people who can see Haruka’s private photos: Tom, Priya, Yuki, Gwen, Kenta, her family…). Keep it for later lessons. (~1.5 min)
Teacher only: the artist is Haruka’s mum. Don’t hint at it.

</details>


#### Slide 24: Wrap-up · Unit 1 check

**In Unit 1, I can…**
- start and keep up a conversation with someone new
- ask detailed questions about a flat and talk about rules
- describe symptoms and say how long I’ve had them
- get help when I’m ill in the UK

[image: Photo idea: a walk in a London park in autumn]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Self-check for Unit 1: for each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1.5 min)

</details>


## Unit: Home and away


### Lesson 4: The Christmas queue

**Objective:** Students can ask for detailed information politely with indirect questions, check information with question tags, and send a parcel abroad (CEFR B1: find out and pass on straightforward factual information; check understanding; cope with less routine situations).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 2 · Home and away · Lesson 4

**The Christmas queue**

Today’s goal: ask for information politely, check what you’ve heard and send a parcel abroad.

[image: Photo idea: a high street with Christmas lights]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

December. Yuki isn’t going home this Christmas, so she sends presents to Japan, and Gwen takes everyone to a panto. First lesson of Unit 2. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Haruka was ill and found out how the NHS works (Lesson 3). Someone is drawing her life in a web comic, Milk Second, and it must be someone who can see her private photos.

Main points: indirect questions (Could you tell me how long it takes?) and question tags for checking (It’s for Christmas, isn’t it?). Practical topic: sending a parcel abroad.

Timing: 23 slides, about 40 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip British phrases and True or false (saves about 2.5 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Sending things

Talk with your teacher.

- When did you last send a parcel? What was inside?
- Do you send cards or presents at the end of the year? Who to?
- What would you send to Japan from the UK?

[image: Photo idea: a parcel and Christmas cards]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. Push for details: what, who for, why. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: A busy post office in December. Gwen and Yuki in a long queue. Yuki has a big parcel. The queue moves. Yuki moves the heavy parcel, checks her phone and sighs. Gwen points to the counters.]

- Where are they? How do you know it’s December?
- How does Yuki feel? Why, do you think?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · In the queue

Mid-December. Gwen takes Yuki to the post office to help her send presents home.

**Gwen:** Look at this queue! It’s the same every December.  
**Yuki:** Do you know if it’s too late to send presents to Japan?  
**Gwen:** I’m not sure. They’re for Christmas, aren’t they?  
**Yuki:** For New Year, really. That’s the big family holiday in Japan.  
**Gwen:** Then you’ve got more time. You’ve checked what’s inside, haven’t you? Some things aren’t allowed.  
**Yuki:** Really? Like what?  
**Gwen:** Perfume, for one. I learned that the hard way!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Yuki), then swap. (~2 min)
Yuki is new at work, so she isn’t going home this year.
True: Royal Mail can’t send perfume and aftershave abroad, because they’re flammable liquids.
Ask: Is New Year or Christmas more important for families in Japan? Let the student explain.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- Why are Gwen and Yuki at the post office?
- Who are the presents for?
- Why does Gwen think there’s more time?
- What can’t you send abroad?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Yuki is sending presents to Japan.  2. Her family, for New Year.  3. Because the presents are for New Year, not Christmas.  4. Perfume.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · At the post office (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a parcel | noun | 小包 | a box or package that you send by post | I’d like to send this **parcel** to Japan. |
| fragile | adjective | 壊れやすい | easy to break | It’s not **fragile**, is it? |
| tracked | adjective | 追跡できる | you can follow where it is online | Send it **tracked**, just in case. |
| a customs form | noun | 税関申告書 | a form that says what’s inside a parcel going abroad | Do I need a **customs form**? |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue. (~1.5 min)
For small gifts abroad, the customs form is a CN22. At the post office counter, staff usually fill it in on the computer for you.

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · At the post office (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the scales | noun (plural) | はかり | a machine that tells you how heavy something is | Put it on the **scales**, please. |
| to weigh | verb | 重さを量る | to find out how heavy something is | I need to **weigh** your parcel. |
| to pop | verb (informal) | さっと置く・入れる | to put something somewhere quickly | **Pop** it on the scales for me. |
| to post | verb | 郵送する、投函する | to send something by post | It’s too late to **post** things for Christmas. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come in the next dialogue too. (~1.5 min)
“Pop” is very common in friendly British English: pop it here, pop in later, I’ll pop to the shop.
British people post letters and parcels; Americans mail them. The post / the mail works the same way.

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · At the counter

A few minutes later. Yuki is at the counter with a post office clerk.

**Clerk:** Hiya, what are you sending today?  
**Yuki:** This parcel to Japan, please. Could you tell me how long it’ll take?  
**Clerk:** About a week if you send it **tracked**. It’s not **fragile**, is it?  
**Yuki:** No, it’s just clothes and some chocolate. Do I need a **customs form**?  
**Clerk:** Yes, but I’ll do it here. **Pop** it on the **scales** so I can **weigh** it.  
**Yuki:** OK. And can you tell me if it’ll arrive before New Year?  
**Clerk:** It should do. It’s too late to **post** things for Christmas, but you’ve got time for New Year.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
“It should do” is very British: “do” replaces the verb (= It should arrive).
Royal Mail’s Christmas last posting dates for Asia are usually in early December, so Gwen was right that Yuki is too late for Christmas.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Listening · A message from Mum

That evening, Haruka gets a voice message from her mum in Osaka.

[audio: Mum’s voice message]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Haruka, it’s Mum. What time does your flight arrive on the 29th? Dad will meet you at the airport. I’m practising my English, so… see you soon!

- What does Mum want to know?
- Who will meet Haruka at the airport?
- Why does Mum speak in English?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. What time Haruka’s flight arrives (on the 29th).
> 2. Her dad.
> 3. She’s practising her English.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Haruka, it’s Mum. What time does your flight arrive on the 29th? Dad will meet you at the airport. I’m practising my English, so… see you soon!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~2 min)
Haruka is going home to Osaka for New Year (next lesson).
Teacher only: Mum practising English is a small clue for the mystery. Don’t draw attention to it.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Vocabulary · Useful phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| for one | phrase | 例えば（一つ挙げると） | as one example | Perfume, **for one**. |
| to learn the hard way | phrase | 痛い目にあって学ぶ | to learn something because you made a mistake | I **learned that the hard way**! |
| It should do. | phrase | たぶんそうなるはず | it probably will (do = the verb before) | Will it arrive in time? **It should do.** |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

All common in everyday English. Some, like “off sick”, “poorly”, “It should do”, “Fair enough” and “Rather you than me”, are especially British. (~1.5 min)
“It should do”, “I might do”, “I will do” are typically British. In American English people usually just say “It should.” Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Language focus · Could you tell me…?

> **Indirect questions**
>
> *Wh- questions*
>
> How long does it take? → Could you tell me **how long it takes**?
>
> *Yes / no questions*
>
> Is it too late? → Do you know **if it’s** too late?
>
> *No do, does or did*
>
> When does it start? → Do you know **when it starts**?

**From the story**
- Yuki: Do you know **if it’s** too late?
- Yuki: Could you tell me **how long it’ll take**?
- Yuki: Can you tell me **if it’ll** arrive before New Year?

Indirect questions sound more polite. Use them with people you don’t know.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

After “Could you tell me / Do you know”, use normal sentence order: no do/does/did, and the verb after the subject. For yes/no questions, use if. Japanese learners often say “Could you tell me how long does it take?” (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 12: Practice · Make it polite

- How much is it?
- Where’s the nearest post office?
- Does it need a customs form?
- When will it arrive?
- What time does the panto start?
- Is the parcel too heavy?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Could you tell me how much it is?
> 2. Do you know where the nearest post office is?
> 3. Do you know if it needs a customs form?
> 4. Could you tell me when it’ll arrive?
> 5. Do you know what time the panto starts?
> 6. Could you tell me if the parcel is too heavy?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student makes each question polite with Could you tell me… / Do you know… (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Language focus · …, isn’t it?

> **Question tags**
>
> *Positive → negative tag*
>
> It’s for Christmas, **isn’t it**? · You’ve checked it, **haven’t you**?
>
> *Negative → positive tag*
>
> It isn’t fragile, **is it**? · You don’t live here, **do you**?
>
> *Past simple*
>
> You sent it, **didn’t you**?

**From the story**
- Gwen: They’re for Christmas, **aren’t they**?
- Gwen: You’ve checked what’s inside, **haven’t you**?
- Clerk: It’s not fragile, **is it**?

Use question tags to check something you think is true.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Question tags: positive sentence → negative tag; negative sentence → positive tag. Use the same auxiliary (is, have, do, can…). Japanese learners often use “right?” for everything. That’s fine in speech, but tags are very common in British English. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 14: Practice · Add the tag

- You’re from Osaka, ____?
- It isn’t fragile, ____?
- You’ve sent it, ____?
- Tom’s always late, ____?
- They didn’t come, ____?
- You can come to the panto, ____?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. aren’t you
> 2. is it
> 3. haven’t you
> 4. isn’t he
> 5. did they
> 6. can’t you

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student adds the tag, then says the whole sentence with falling intonation (checking). (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Pronunciation · Checking or asking?

> **Listen to the tag**
>
> It’s for Christmas, isn’t it? ↘ = I’m sure. Just checking.
> It’s for Christmas, isn’t it? ↗ = I’m not sure. Please tell me.

**Say it with your teacher**
- It’s **cold** today, **isn’t** it? ↘
- You’ve **been** to Japan, **haven’t** you? ↗
- Tom’s **late** again, **isn’t** he? ↘
- It **isn’t** fragile, **is** it? ↗

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Falling intonation on the tag = I’m sure, I’m just checking. Rising = I really don’t know. Most everyday tags fall. Say each pair; the student repeats, then chooses one for you to guess. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 16: Role play · Sending a parcel

> **Student**
>
> Send a birthday present to a friend in Osaka.
>
> • Inside: a mug, some chocolates and a small bottle of perfume.
> • Ask how long it takes and how much it is.
> • Check one thing with a tag.

**Useful phrases**
- I’d like to send this to…
- Could you tell me how long it takes?
- Do you know if…?
- It’s tracked, isn’t it?
- Do I need a customs form?
- Pop it on the scales.

> **Teacher**
>
> You work at the post office.
>
> • Ask what’s inside and if it’s fragile.
> • Perfume can’t go by airmail. They have to take it out.
> • Offer tracked (more expensive) or untracked.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student is the customer; no swap needed. Make up simple prices. (~2.5 min)
Listen for indirect questions (Could you tell me how long…?) and question tags.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Story · Oh no it isn’t!

The Saturday before Christmas. Gwen takes Haruka, Yuki and Tom to a panto.

**Haruka:** Why is that man wearing a dress?  
**Gwen:** That’s the dame. It’s always a man in a big, silly dress. Tradition!  
**Yuki:** And why is everyone shouting?  
**Tom:** You have to shout back. When the dame says “Oh no it isn’t!”, you shout “Oh yes it is!”  
**Yuki:** That’s so strange. I love it!  
**Gwen:** Wait for “He’s behind you!” That’s the best bit.  
**Tom:** You’ve been coming every year since you were a girl, **haven’t you**, Gwen?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. Have fun with the shouting. (~2 min)
The next slide explains panto. Ask first: What do you think a panto is?

</details>


#### Slide 18: Reading · Panto

[image: Photo idea: a panto dame on stage]

A **pantomime**, or **panto**, is a funny show for the whole family. Many British theatres put one on every winter, from late November to January.

Pantos tell famous children’s stories like Cinderella or Aladdin. The stories are simple, and the fun comes from the jokes. The **dame**, a funny woman in a crazy dress, is traditionally played by a man.

The audience is part of the show. People **boo** the villain, shout “Oh yes it is!” and warn the hero: “He’s behind you!”

Panto has roots in an Italian kind of theatre from the 1500s, mixed with British traditions. For many British children, it’s their first trip to the theatre.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: When can you see a panto? Who plays the dame? What do people shout?
Sources: History.co.uk and the University of Warwick on panto dames; Historic UK on pantomime; NPR (December 2025) on panto in Britain. Panto grew from commedia dell’arte, Italian theatre from the 1500s.

</details>


#### Slide 19: Reading · True or false?

- Pantos are usually on in summer.
- The dame is traditionally played by a man.
- The audience has to be quiet.
- Panto has Italian roots.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: in winter (late November to January).  2. True.  3. False: people shout and boo.  4. True.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 20: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a laughing theatre audience]

- Do people in Japan send presents at the end of the year? What?
- Is there anything like the panto dame in Japanese theatre?
- Would you enjoy shouting in the theatre? Why or why not?
- What’s the best show you’ve ever seen?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Some may mention oseibo (end-of-year gifts) or nengajō. For question 2, kabuki onnagata (men playing women’s roles) is a good comparison, but let the student make it. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Story · Gone viral

The last day before the Christmas break. Priya, Haruka and Yuki.

**Priya:** Have you seen? Milk Second has gone viral. Two hundred thousand followers!  
**Haruka:** I know. There’s even a landlady in it now, with all of Gwen’s tea rules.  
**Priya:** Does Gwen know?  
**Haruka:** Yes, and she loves it. She’s shown everyone at the corner shop.  
**Priya:** It’s funny: new episodes always go up at 3 a.m. Who’s awake then?  
**Yuki:** Me! I’m always up then. I work for clients in Tokyo.  
**Haruka:** …Really?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
The clue: new episodes go up at 3 a.m. UK time. In December, that’s 12 noon in Japan.
Yuki is the obvious suspect now. That’s the idea: let the student suspect her.
Don’t solve it. Add the clue to the suspects list from Lesson 3.

</details>


#### Slide 22: Mystery · Milk Second: tea rules

[image: Milk Second: Gwen’s tea rule, milk second]

- Why do you think the comic is called Milk Second?
- Do you know what time is 3 a.m. London time in Japan?
- Who could be awake at 3 a.m. to post it?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe the panel and guess. Work out the time together: 3 a.m. in London is 12 noon in Japan in winter (9 hours ahead). Add Yuki to the suspects list if the student suggests her. (~1.5 min)
This episode explains the comic’s name: Gwen’s rule from the beginner course, tea first, milk second.
Teacher only: the artist is Haruka’s mum. Don’t hint at it.

</details>


#### Slide 23: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- asked for information politely with indirect questions
- checked information with question tags
- sent a parcel abroad at the post office
- learned about panto

[image: Photo idea: panto tickets on a theatre seat]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

For each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 5: Lost in arrivals

**Objective:** Students can report a problem and tell the story of what happened in the right order, using narrative tenses and the past perfect (CEFR B1: give detailed accounts of experiences; explain why something is a problem).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 2 · Home and away · Lesson 5

**Lost in arrivals**

Today’s goal: report a problem and tell the story of what happened, in the right order.

[image: Photo idea: a suitcase by an empty carousel]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

January. Haruka spends New Year at home in Osaka, then flies back to London… and her suitcase doesn’t. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Milk Second has gone viral, and new episodes go up at 3 a.m. UK time. Yuki says she’s always awake then (Lesson 4).

Main point: narrative tenses and the past perfect. Practical topic: reporting lost luggage.

Timing: 24 slides, about 41 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip British phrases and True or false (saves about 2.5 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Travel problems

Talk with your teacher.

- Have you ever had a problem when you were travelling? What happened?
- What do you always pack in your hand luggage?
- What does your family do at New Year?

[image: Photo idea: an airport at night]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. Let the student tell a short story; don’t correct tenses yet. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: Heathrow baggage reclaim, late at night. Haruka waits alone at a carousel. The belt stops. Haruka looks at the empty belt, checks her boarding pass and puts a hand on her head.]

- Where is Haruka? What’s happening?
- How does she feel? What do you think she does next?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Packing

28 December. Haruka is packing. Tom is reading Milk Second.

**Tom:** Hang on. This guy in Milk Second is always late. That’s me!  
**Haruka:** Sorry, Tom. You’re in it too. Whoever draws it knows everything about us.  
**Tom:** Is it someone at work?  
**Haruka:** Maybe. Only my friends and family can see my photos now.  
**Tom:** Family? What about Kenta? You send him photos all the time.  
**Haruka:** Kenta? He can’t even draw a cat!  
**Tom:** Fair enough. But ask him when you’re home!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
This is how Tom finds out about the comic, and why he suspects Kenta (Haruka sends Kenta photos, as he asked in the beginner course).
Recap the suspects list with the student: who can see Haruka’s private photos?

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Happy New Year!

1 January. Tom video-calls Haruka and Kenta in Osaka.

**Tom:** Happy New Year from London! I watched the fireworks on the Thames last night.  
**Haruka:** Happy New Year! We’re eating osechi, the special New Year food.  
**Tom:** Nice. Right, Kenta. Are you the Milk Second artist? Be honest!  
**Kenta:** Me? I can’t draw, and I’ve got exams this month. I’m studying all the time!  
**Haruka:** See? I told you. Mum, come and say hello to Tom!  
**Tom:** Happy New Year, Haruka’s mum! … Oh, she’s gone.  
**Haruka:** Sorry. She’s been on her tablet all holiday.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Haruka or Kenta), then swap. (~2 min)
Tom speaks to Kenta in English, so this call is in English.
Kenta is cleared: he can’t draw and he’s busy with exams.
Teacher only: Mum walks away when Tom says hello, and she’s always on her tablet. Small clues. Don’t point them out.
Ask: What do you know about osechi? What does each dish mean? Let the student explain.

</details>


#### Slide 6: Story · Did you understand?

- Why does Tom think he’s in Milk Second?
- Who does Tom think could be the artist? Why?
- What did Tom do on New Year’s Eve?
- Why does Kenta say he isn’t the artist?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. The guy in the comic is always late, like him.  2. Kenta, because Haruka sends him photos.  3. He watched the fireworks on the Thames.  4. He can’t draw, and he’s busy studying for exams.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check on both dialogues. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 7: Culture · New Year in the UK

> **A night out, then a day off**
>
> In the UK, many people spend New Year’s Eve (31 December) at a party or a night out with friends.
>
> In London, crowds watch Big Ben strike midnight, and then there’s a big fireworks show over the River Thames. Millions more watch it on TV.
>
> At midnight, many people hold hands and sing **Auld Lang Syne**, an old Scottish song by Robert Burns. 1 January is a bank holiday, and then most people go back to work.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Tom mentioned the fireworks. This slide explains the British New Year. (~1.5 min)
In Scotland, New Year is called Hogmanay, and 2 January is a bank holiday too.
Robert Burns comes back in the next lesson (Burns Night).
Sources: BBC and press coverage of Big Ben’s New Year bongs (broadcast since 1923) and the London fireworks; GOV.UK bank holidays.
Ask: How is this different from New Year in Japan?

</details>


#### Slide 8: Vocabulary · At the airport (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| baggage reclaim | noun | 手荷物受取所 | the place where you collect your bags after a flight | My bag never came out at **baggage reclaim**. |
| a belt | noun | ベルトコンベア | the moving belt that brings the bags | Your bags are on **belt** 5. |
| to check in (a bag) | verb | （荷物を）預ける | to give your bag to the airline before a flight | I **checked** my suitcase **in** at Kansai. |
| missing | adjective | 行方不明の | not where it should be, and nobody knows where it is | My suitcase is **missing**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue. (~1.5 min)
A belt is also called a carousel.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Vocabulary · At the airport (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a reference number | noun | 照会番号 | a number that helps a company find your case | Here’s your **reference number**. |
| to deliver | verb | 配達する | to bring something to someone’s home | We’ll **deliver** it to your home. |
| a sticker | noun | ステッカー | a small picture or label that sticks to something | It’s got a cat **sticker** on it. |
| a claim | noun | （補償などの）請求 | an official request for money | You can make a **claim** after 21 days. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come in the dialogue and the reading. (~1.5 min)
Katakana false friend: クレーム means a complaint. In English, a claim is an official request for money, for example from an airline or insurance company. A complaint is a complaint.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Story · At the baggage desk

3 January, late at night. Haruka goes to the baggage desk at Heathrow.

**Agent:** Hello. How can I help?  
**Haruka:** My suitcase is **missing**. I was waiting at **belt** 5, but it never came out.  
**Agent:** I’m sorry about that. Where did you fly from?  
**Haruka:** Osaka, via Tokyo. I**’d checked** it **in** all the way to London.  
**Agent:** Can you describe it?  
**Haruka:** It’s a huge navy suitcase with a cat **sticker**. By the time the belt stopped, everyone had gone!  
**Agent:** OK, let’s fill in a report. You’ll get a **reference number**, and we’ll **deliver** it to your home.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
This is the right thing to do: report a missing bag at the baggage desk before you leave the hall. The report is called a Property Irregularity Report (PIR).
Callback: it’s the same huge suitcase from Haruka’s first day in London (beginner Lesson 1).

</details>


#### Slide 11: Listening · Good news

The next morning, Haruka gets a voice message.

[audio: Baggage services message]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Hello, this is baggage services at Heathrow. Good news: we’ve found your suitcase. It had gone to Helsinki by mistake! We’ll deliver it tomorrow, between nine and one.

- Where had the suitcase gone?
- When will they deliver it?
- How do you think Haruka feels?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. To Helsinki, by mistake.
> 2. Tomorrow, between nine and one.
> 3. Happy, relieved (any good answer).

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Hello, this is baggage services at Heathrow. Good news: we’ve found your suitcase. It had gone to Helsinki by mistake! We’ll deliver it tomorrow, between nine and one.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~2 min)
Most delayed bags arrive in a few days. In 2024, about three in four mishandled bags were delayed, not lost (SITA Baggage IT Insights).

</details>


#### Slide 12: Language focus · It had gone to Helsinki

> **Telling a story**
>
> *Background: past continuous*
>
> I **was waiting** at belt 5.
>
> *Main events: past simple*
>
> The belt **stopped**. I **went** to the desk.
>
> *Earlier: past perfect (had + past participle)*
>
> By the time the belt stopped, everyone **had gone**. · It **had gone** to Helsinki.

**From the story**
- Haruka: I**’d checked** it in all the way to London.
- Haruka: By the time the belt stopped, everyone **had gone**!
- Message: It **had gone** to Helsinki by mistake!

Tell a story with three past tenses. Use the past perfect for what happened before.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Draw a timeline: had checked in (first) → waited → belt stopped → everyone had gone. The past perfect shows which past action happened first. Don’t overuse it: if the order is clear, the past simple is fine. Japanese learners often avoid it completely, or use it for everything in the past. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Practice · Which happened first?

- When I got to the gate, the plane ____ (already / leave).
- Haruka ____ (fly) to Osaka on 29 December.
- I ____ (never / see) snow before I went to Hokkaido.
- When we arrived, the fireworks ____ (start).
- She ____ (lose) her phone, so she ____ (call) from my phone.
- Your turn: something that had already happened when you arrived.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. had already left
> 2. flew
> 3. had never seen
> 4. had started
> 5. had lost / called
> 6. Student’s own answer

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student completes each sentence with the past simple or past perfect. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 14: Practice · What had happened?

- When Haruka got home, the door was open.
- When Tom woke up, his phone was dead.
- When Yuki got to the office, everyone was laughing.
- When Gwen looked in the kitchen, there was no milk.
- When I got to the party, nobody was there.

> **Possible answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Someone had forgotten to lock it.
> 2. He had forgotten to charge it.
> 3. Someone had told a joke.
> 4. Tom had used it all.
> 5. Everyone had left. / I’d got the wrong day.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student guesses what had happened before each moment, with the past perfect. Accept any logical answer. Push for two ideas each. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Pronunciation · Hidden had

> **How it sounds**
>
> I’d checked it in → ide-**CHEKT** it in
> Everyone had gone → **EV**-ree-wun-ad-**GON**
> It had gone to Helsinki → it-ad-**GON** ta **HEL**-sin-kee

**Say it with your teacher**
- I’d **checked** it in at **Kansai**.
- Everyone had **gone**!
- It had **gone** to **Helsinki**.
- She’d **left** before I **arrived**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

In fast speech, had is very weak: ’d or just /əd/. Learners often miss it when listening, so the story sounds like past simple. Say each line; the student repeats. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 16: Role play · Reporting a missing bag

> **Student**
>
> You flew from Osaka to Heathrow. Your bag didn’t arrive.
>
> • Say what happened: you were waiting at the belt, then it stopped.
> • Describe it: a large black suitcase with a yellow strap.
> • Ask what happens next.

**Useful phrases**
- My bag is missing.
- I was waiting at belt…
- By the time…, everyone had…
- It’s a… suitcase with…
- What happens now?
- Here’s your reference number.

> **Teacher**
>
> You work at the baggage desk.
>
> • Ask for the flight number.
> • Ask them to describe the bag.
> • Give a reference number: LHR-48210. Say you’ll deliver it when it’s found.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Everything the student needs is on their card. The student is the passenger; no swap needed. (~2.5 min)
Listen for narrative tenses: was waiting, stopped, had gone.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Reading · My bag is missing!

[image: Photo idea: a luggage receipt and a suitcase handle]

Your bag doesn’t come out at **baggage reclaim**? Don’t leave the hall. Go to the airline’s baggage desk first.

They’ll fill in a report with you and give you a **reference number**. Describe your bag carefully: the colour, the size and any **stickers**. Most missing bags turn up in a few days, and the airline delivers them to you.

If you’re away from home, keep the receipts for things you need to buy, like a toothbrush. The airline may pay you back.

If your bag still hasn’t arrived after 21 days, it counts as lost, and you can make a **claim**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: Where should you go first? Why keep receipts? What happens after 21 days?
Sources: Which? and the Aviation ADR guide on lost and delayed luggage (report it and get a PIR; keep receipts for essentials; claim in writing; 21 days); Montreal Convention (a bag is treated as lost after 21 days); SITA Baggage IT Insights 2025 (most mishandled bags are delayed, not lost).
Note: airlines may pay less if your bag is delayed on the way home, because you have things at home.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Reading · True or false?

- You should leave the airport and call the airline later.
- You get a reference number for your missing bag.
- Most missing bags are never found.
- After 21 days, a missing bag counts as lost.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: go to the baggage desk first.  2. True.  3. False: most turn up in a few days.  4. True.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 19: Story · Welcome back

4 January, evening. Haruka is home with Gwen and Tom.

**Gwen:** Welcome back, love! Where’s your suitcase?  
**Haruka:** Don’t ask. It **had gone** to Helsinki! It’s coming tomorrow.  
**Tom:** Helsinki? Did it go on holiday without you?  
**Haruka:** Very funny. By the time I**’d filled** in the form, it was nearly midnight.  
**Gwen:** You poor thing. Come on, show us your photos from home.  
**Haruka:** OK. This is New Year’s Day. That’s Kenta, and that’s Mum with her tablet.  
**Tom:** Your mum’s always on that tablet, isn’t she?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Tom’s Helsinki joke is friendly teasing, and “Very funny” is Haruka’s reply (Lesson 1).
Recycles the question tag from Lesson 4 (“isn’t she?”). Tom saw on the video call that Haruka’s mum is always on her tablet.
The next slide is Haruka’s photo.

</details>


#### Slide 20: Mystery · Haruka’s New Year photo

[image: Haruka’s New Year photo with Kenta and Mum]

- Who can you see? What are they doing?
- What can you see on the table?
- Is there anything interesting in the photo?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe the photo in detail: who, where, what’s on the table. Don’t comment on anything they notice. (~1.5 min)
Teacher only, dramatic irony: Mum looks tired, she has a tablet with a drawing pen, and there’s an English textbook on the table. She’s the artist, but Haruka hasn’t noticed. Some students may notice. If they do, just say “Interesting!” and move on.

</details>


#### Slide 21: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a shrine on New Year’s Eve]

- What does your family usually do at New Year?
- Which sounds more fun to you: New Year in Japan or in the UK? Why?
- Have you ever lost something important? What had happened?
- What would you do if your bag went missing abroad?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Encourage narrative tenses for question 3. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 22: Story · Burns Night

Mid-January. Callum calls Tom and Haruka.

**Callum:** Right, you two. Burns Night is on the 25th, and I’ve booked a Scottish restaurant.  
**Tom:** Will there be haggis?  
**Callum:** Of course there’ll be haggis! It’s Burns Night.  
**Haruka:** Sorry, what’s haggis?  
**Tom:** Trust me, you don’t want to know.  
**Haruka:** Can I bring my friend Yuki? She’s new in London.  
**Callum:** Course you can! The more the merrier.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Callum hasn’t met Yuki yet, so Haruka asks if she can bring her. “The more the merrier” = more people make it more fun.
Hook: next lesson is Burns Night. Robert Burns is the poet from Auld Lang Syne (the New Year slide).
Ask: What do you think haggis is? Don’t tell the student yet.

</details>


#### Slide 23: Vocabulary · Useful phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Fair enough. | phrase | まあ、それもそうだね | OK, I understand and accept that | I can’t come, I’m working. Fair enough! |
| Don’t ask. | phrase | 聞かないで（ひどい話なんだ） | it’s a long, bad story | How was your flight? **Don’t ask.** |
| You poor thing. | phrase | かわいそうに | I feel sorry for you | You lost your bag? **You poor thing.** |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

All very common in everyday English. “Fair enough” is especially British. (~1.5 min)
Tom says “Fair enough” when Haruka packs. “Don’t ask” and “You poor thing” come in the scene at home. Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>


#### Slide 24: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- reported a problem at the airport
- told a story in order with the past perfect
- compared New Year in Japan and the UK
- learned what to do when your bag goes missing

[image: Photo idea: the suitcase is home]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

For each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 6: Who’s paying?

**Objective:** Students can explain how something is made or done using the passive, and deal with the bill in a group, including service charges and tips (CEFR B1: describe events and customs; cope with less routine situations in restaurants).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 2 · Home and away · Lesson 6

**Who’s paying?**

Today’s goal: explain how something is made or done, and sort out the bill with friends.

[image: Photo idea: a Burns Night dinner table]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

25 January. Callum takes everyone to a Burns Night dinner at a Scottish restaurant. Last lesson of Unit 2, with a progress check. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Haruka’s suitcase went to Helsinki (Lesson 5). Kenta isn’t the Milk Second artist. Yuki is awake at 3 a.m., when new episodes go up.

Main point: the passive (present and past). Practical topic: paying the bill, service charge and tips. Culture: Burns Night, Tunnock’s and banter.

Timing: 24 slides, about 42 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip British phrases, True or false and British or American? (saves about 4 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Eating out

Talk with your teacher.

- When you eat out with friends in Japan, who pays?
- Have you ever left a tip? Where?
- What’s the strangest food you’ve ever tried?

[image: Photo idea: friends and a restaurant bill]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. Many students will mention warikan (splitting equally). Let them explain. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: A Scottish restaurant on Burns Night. A bagpiper brings in the haggis. Haruka, Yuki, Callum and Tom are at the table. The piper walks in, followed by the haggis. Haruka and Yuki stare; one covers her ears and laughs. Callum claps, and Tom films it.]

- What’s happening? Who comes into the room?
- How do Haruka and Yuki react? How about Callum and Tom?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Here comes the haggis

25 January, a Scottish restaurant. Callum, Haruka, Yuki and Tom.

**Callum:** Here it comes! The haggis is usually brought in with bagpipes.  
**Yuki:** Wow. So what is haggis, exactly?  
**Callum:** It’s made from sheep’s heart, liver and lungs, mixed with oats and onions.  
**Haruka:** Lungs?! OK… I’ve eaten black pudding. I’ll try it.  
**Tom:** Rather you than me.  
**Callum:** Says the man who was scared of my gran’s cat.  
**Tom:** It was a very big cat!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together, then swap. (~2 min)
Callback: Haruka ate black pudding in the beginner course (Lesson 4).
Callum and Tom tease each other like old friends. It’s banter about a real, silly moment, not about where they’re from.
Facts: haggis is made from sheep’s heart, liver and lungs, with oatmeal, onion, suet and spices. At a Burns supper it’s usually piped in on a silver plate.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- How is the haggis brought in?
- What is haggis made from?
- Why is Haruka brave enough to try it?
- Why does Callum tease Tom?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. With bagpipes.  2. Sheep’s heart, liver and lungs, with oats and onions.  3. She’s eaten black pudding before.  4. Tom said “Rather you than me”, and Callum remembers he was scared of a cat.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Culture · Burns Night

> **A poet’s birthday dinner**
>
> Robert Burns is Scotland’s most famous poet. He was born on 25 January 1759, so every year on that night many people have a **Burns supper**.
>
> The haggis is carried in with bagpipes, and someone reads Burns’s poem “Address to a Haggis” before they cut it. It’s eaten with **neeps and tatties** (mashed turnip and potatoes).
>
> At the end, everyone sings **Auld Lang Syne**, the song from New Year.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

This explains the dinner. (~1.5 min)
A full Burns supper also has the Selkirk Grace, a speech about Burns called the Immortal Memory, and a funny toast to the lassies (the women) and a reply.
Fun fact (teacher only): real haggis can’t be sold in the USA, because a 1971 rule bans lungs in food.
Sources: Burns supper and Robert Burns (Wikipedia, Scottish tourism and history sites); The Scotsman on haggis.

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · The bill (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the bill | noun | 会計、勘定 | the paper that shows how much you have to pay | Could we have the **bill**, please? |
| to split (the bill) | verb | 割り勘にする | to share the cost | Shall we **split** it four ways? |
| a service charge | noun | サービス料 | extra money for the staff, added to the bill | A 12.5% **service charge** is included. |
| a tip | noun | チップ | extra money you give for good service | Do you leave a **tip** in Japan? |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue. (~1.5 min)
In the UK people say “the bill”. “The check” is American English. Remember this: it matters later in the lesson.

</details>


#### Slide 8: Vocabulary · The bill (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to treat (someone) | verb | おごる | to pay for someone else | Put your money away. I’ll **treat** you. |
| It’s on me. | phrase | 私のおごりだよ | I’ll pay | Don’t worry, **it’s on me**. |
| separately | adverb | 別々に | each person on their own | Can we pay **separately**? |
| a card machine | noun | カード決済端末 | the small machine you pay by card with | I’ll bring the **card machine** over. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come in the next dialogue too. (~1.5 min)
In the UK, waiters usually bring the card machine to the table.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Story · Who’s paying?

Later. The waiter brings the bill to the table.

**Waiter:** Here’s your **bill**. A 12.5% **service charge** is included.  
**Yuki:** So the **tip** is included? In Japan we don’t tip at all.  
**Callum:** Yes, it’s included. So, shall we **split** it four ways?  
**Tom:** But I only had soup! Callum had three whiskies.  
**Callum:** Fine. Let’s each pay for what we had. You can **treat** us next time, Tom.  
**Haruka:** Is it OK to pay **separately**?  
**Waiter:** Of course. I’ll bring the **card machine** over.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Facts: many London restaurants add a service charge, often 12.5% (it varies). It’s discretionary (you can ask to remove it if service was bad), and since October 2024 staff must receive all of it. In pubs you usually don’t tip at the bar (beginner Lesson 3).
Splitting the bill equally is common with friends, but people often pay for what they had when the amounts are very different.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Listening · A message from Callum

The next morning, Haruka gets a voice message from Callum.

[audio: Callum’s voice message]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Morning! Thanks for coming last night. Tell Tom he owes me eight pounds for the haggis he didn’t eat. And Haruka, send me your photos!

- Why is Callum calling?
- What does Tom owe Callum?
- What does Callum want Haruka to do?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. To say thanks for coming.
> 2. Eight pounds, for the haggis.
> 3. Send him her photos.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Morning! Thanks for coming last night. Tell Tom he owes me eight pounds for the haggis he didn’t eat. And Haruka, send me your photos!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~2 min)
Callum is joking about the haggis: it’s more banter.
Teacher only: Haruka took photos, as always. They matter for the comic later in the lesson.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Language focus · It’s made from…

> **be + past participle**
>
> *Present simple passive*
>
> Haggis **is made** from sheep’s heart. · Service **is included**.
>
> *Past simple passive*
>
> The poem **was written** in 1786. · Burns **was born** in 1759.
>
> *Who did it? Use by*
>
> The haggis **was brought** in **by** a piper.

**From the story**
- Callum: The haggis **is** usually **brought** in with bagpipes.
- Callum: It**’s made** from sheep’s heart, liver and lungs.
- Waiter: A 12.5% service charge **is included**.

Use the passive to explain how something is made or done.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Use the passive when the action matters more than who did it, or when we don’t know or don’t care who did it. Form: be + past participle. Japanese learners often forget “be”: “Haggis made from…”. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 12: Practice · Made in Britain

- Tunnock’s teacakes ____ (make) in Scotland.
- The first teacake ____ (make) in 1956.
- Auld Lang Syne ____ (sing) at New Year.
- Greggs ____ (start) in Newcastle in 1939.
- In Japan, tips ____ (not / usually / give).
- Your turn: something that is made or was invented in Japan.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. are made
> 2. was made
> 3. is sung
> 4. was started
> 5. aren’t usually given
> 6. Student’s own answer

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student completes each sentence with the passive. All facts are true. (~2 min)
Tunnock’s is a Scottish bakery in Uddingston, near Glasgow. Its teacake was first made in 1956.

</details>


#### Slide 13: Pronunciation · Brilliant… or brilliant?

> **Sincere or sarcastic?**
>
> Sincere: bright, the voice goes up and down → “**Brilliant!**” (I’m really happy)
> Sarcastic: flat, low and slow → “Brilliant.” (This is bad)

**Say it both ways**
- Oh, **brilliant**.
- **Great**.
- **Lovely** weather.
- Thanks a **lot**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Sarcasm often sounds flat and low, and a bit slow. A real compliment goes up and down and sounds bright. Say each word both ways; the student guesses which one. Then swap. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 14: Role play · Splitting the bill

> **Student**
>
> You’re at a restaurant with a friend. You had a salad and a glass of water.
>
> • Ask if service is included.
> • Your friend had much more than you.
> • Suggest a fair way to pay.

**Useful phrases**
- Is service included?
- Shall we split it?
- Let’s pay for what we had.
- It’s on me.
- Can we pay separately?
- I owe you!

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re the friend.
>
> • You had three courses and wine.
> • First, suggest splitting it 50/50.
> • Agree in the end, or offer to treat them.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The teacher plays a friend who ordered a lot more. No swap needed. (~2.5 min)
Listen for the passive (Is service included?) and polite suggestions.

</details>


#### Slide 15: Progress check · Unit 2 check

> **Student**
>
> A British friend is visiting Japan.
>
> • Explain a Japanese food or tradition (it’s made / it’s eaten…).
> • Tell them about a time something went wrong on a trip.
> • Check one thing with a tag.

**Unit 2 phrases**
- It’s made from…
- It’s usually eaten…
- By the time…, I’d…
- Could you tell me…?
- …, isn’t it?
- Do you know if…?

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re the British friend.
>
> • Ask polite questions: Could you tell me…? Do you know if…?
> • Ask what had happened.
> • Ask one question about tipping.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Progress check for Unit 2. Let the student lead and don’t correct during the conversation. Note what they do well and one thing to practise. (~3 min)
Look for: indirect questions, question tags, narrative tenses with the past perfect, and the passive.

</details>


#### Slide 16: Reading · Banter

[image: Photo idea: friends laughing and teasing each other]

British friends often tease each other. It’s called **banter**: friendly jokes about small things, like always being late or being scared of a cat. Many British people see it as a sign of a close friendship.

For visitors, banter can be confusing. It can sound rude, and it’s hard to know when someone is joking. Some British people don’t like it either.

There is a line. Banter is usually between people who know each other well, and it’s about small, silly things, not someone’s body, family or background. If the other person isn’t laughing, it isn’t banter any more.

At work, be careful: unkind jokes can be bullying, even if someone says “It was just banter”.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: Why do friends tease each other? Why do visitors find banter hard? Where is the line?
Sources: Acas guidance lists unwanted jokes or banter among behaviours that can be bullying, and under the Equality Act 2010 offensive “banter” can be harassment. The rest describes a common British habit, using soft language (often, many).
Connects to Lesson 1 (teasing means we’re friends) and Callum and Tom in this lesson.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Reading · True or false?

- Banter is usually between good friends.
- Jokes about someone’s family are fine.
- All British people love banter.
- Unkind jokes at work can be bullying.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. True.  2. False: banter is about small, silly things.  3. False: some don’t like it.  4. True.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 18: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: friends laughing after dinner]

- Would you try haggis? Why or why not?
- Do friends in Japan tease each other? About what?
- Which do you prefer: splitting equally, or paying for what you had?
- Should restaurants in Japan add a service charge? Why or why not?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Some may mention tsukkomi in Kansai humour, or say teasing is rare with colleagues. All answers are fine. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 19: Story · The check

The next day at the office. Priya, Haruka and Yuki.

**Priya:** The Burns Night episode is up! Look, Hana tries haggis.  
**Haruka:** They’ve drawn Tom as really mean. He was only joking!  
**Priya:** And look at this: “Can we get the check?” and “color” with no u.  
**Haruka:** So?  
**Priya:** That’s American English. A British person would say “the bill”.  
**Yuki:** We learn American English at school in Japan.  
**Priya:** Hmm. Interesting…  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Two clues: the artist writes American English, and draws Tom’s banter as mean. So it’s probably not a British person (Priya and Callum are out).
Yuki learned American English at school, so she looks even more suspicious. That’s the idea.
Teacher only: Haruka’s mum also learned English at school in Japan. Don’t say it.

</details>


#### Slide 20: Mystery · Milk Second: Burns Night

[image: Milk Second: Hana tries haggis]

**In the comic**
- Hana: “What a pretty color!”
- Tim: “Nice pants, Hana!”
- Hana: “Can we get the check?”
- Who do you think draws it now? Why?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the speech bubbles from the episode. The student finds the American English and says the British version. Then update the suspects list. (~1.5 min)
Answers: check → bill; color → colour; pants → trousers (in British English, pants = underwear!).
Teacher only: the artist is Haruka’s mum. Don’t hint at it.

</details>
