#### Slide 21: Practice · British or American?

- the check
- pants
- a cookie
- an apartment
- a line (of people)
- fall (the season)

> **British English** (teacher only)
>
> 1. the bill
> 2. trousers
> 3. a biscuit
> 4. a flat
> 5. a queue
> 6. autumn

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student gives the British word. Useful on its own, and for spotting the comic’s mistakes. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 22: Story · An invitation

Early February. Gwen has some news for Haruka and Tom.

**Gwen:** Sian’s invited us to Cardiff for St David’s Day. Will you come, Haruka?  
**Haruka:** I’d love to! When is it?  
**Gwen:** The first of March. I’ll book the train tickets.  
**Tom:** Book them early. And good luck with the trains.  
**Gwen:** Says the man who’s never on time!  
**Haruka:** What happens on St David’s Day?  
**Gwen:** You’ll see! Daffodils, Welsh cakes… and a lot of singing.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Gwen uses “Says the man who…” from this lesson’s British phrases.
Hook: next lesson is about trains (and Pancake Day). Ask: What can go wrong with trains in Japan? What happens if they’re late?

</details>


#### Slide 23: Vocabulary · Useful phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Rather you than me. | phrase | 自分はやりたくないな | I wouldn’t want to do that! | You’re eating lungs? **Rather you than me.** |
| Says the man who… | phrase | 〜した人が言う？ | a joke: you’re no better than me | **Says the man who** was scared of a cat! |
| I owe you. | phrase | 借りがある | I need to pay you back | You paid for me? Thanks, **I owe you.** |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Common in friendly conversation. “Rather you than me” is especially British. (~1.5 min)
“Says the man / woman / one who…” is a typical banter reply. Use it only with friends. Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>


#### Slide 24: Wrap-up · Unit 2 check

**In Unit 2, I can…**
- ask for information politely and check what I’ve heard
- report a problem and tell a story in the right order
- explain how something is made or done
- sort out the bill with friends

[image: Photo idea: a chocolate teacake and a cup of tea]

## Unit: Getting closer


### Lesson 7: Delays on the line

**Objective:** Students can speculate about situations from evidence with must, might, could and can’t, talk about problems with too and enough, and deal with a cancelled train (CEFR B1: speculate about causes; cope with less routine situations in travel).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 3 · Getting closer · Lesson 7

**Delays on the line**

Today’s goal: make guesses from evidence and deal with a cancelled train.

[image: Photo idea: a busy London station]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

February. Pancake Day at work, then Haruka and Gwen travel to Cardiff for St David’s Day… and the train is cancelled. First lesson of Unit 3. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Gwen invited Haruka to Cardiff (Lesson 6). The Milk Second artist seems to write American English and doesn’t understand banter, and posts at 3 a.m.

Main points: modals of deduction (must, might, could, can’t) and too / enough. Practical topic: train problems, tickets and Delay Repay. Mystery: the clue board.

Timing: 25 slides, about 44 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip Too slow, not enough time, True or false and British phrases (saves about 4.5 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Getting there

Talk with your teacher.

- Have you ever missed a train or a plane? What happened?
- How do you usually travel long distances?
- What do you like on pancakes?

[image: Photo idea: pancakes with lemon and sugar]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. Let the student tell a short story. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: London Paddington. Gwen and Haruka with weekend bags look at the departure board. The board changes; people groan. Gwen throws her hands up, Haruka checks her phone, and a worker points to the buses.]

- What’s happening at the station?
- How do Gwen and Haruka feel? What do you think they’ll do?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Pancake Day

Shrove Tuesday at the office. Priya, Haruka and Yuki.

**Priya:** It’s Pancake Day! We’re having a pancake race at lunch.  
**Yuki:** A race? With pancakes?  
**Priya:** You run with a frying pan and flip the pancake. If you drop it, you go back.  
**Haruka:** I can’t run in these shoes. They’re too high.  
**Yuki:** And I’m not fast enough. I’ll just watch!  
**Priya:** It’s just for fun. Then we eat the pancakes with lemon and sugar.  
**Yuki:** Lemon? On a pancake? That must be a British thing.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Yuki), then swap. (~2 min)
British pancakes are thin, more like crêpes than Japanese hotcakes. Lemon and sugar is a classic topping.
The next slide explains Pancake Day.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- What’s happening at lunch?
- How do you do a pancake race?
- Why can’t Haruka run?
- What do they eat on the pancakes?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. A pancake race.  2. Run with a frying pan and flip the pancake.  3. Her shoes are too high.  4. Lemon and sugar.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Culture · Pancake Day

> **Shrove Tuesday**
>
> **Pancake Day** (Shrove Tuesday) is the day before Lent, a time when many Christians traditionally gave up rich food. People used up their eggs, milk and butter by making pancakes.
>
> British pancakes are thin, and many people eat them with lemon and sugar.
>
> Some towns have **pancake races**. In Olney, a small town in England, women run through the streets with a frying pan, flipping a pancake. The story says the race started in 1445.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

This explains the first dialogue. (~1.5 min)
Facts: the Olney race is said to date from 1445; it stopped during the Second World War and was restarted in 1948. Runners must flip the pancake at the start and the finish. The date of Pancake Day changes every year, because it depends on Easter (February or early March).
Sources: English Folk Dance and Song Society, Olney Pancake Race history.
Ask: Are pancakes in Japan the same?

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Train trouble (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| cancelled | adjective | 運休の、中止の | not happening any more | Our train is **cancelled**! |
| the departure board | noun | 発車案内板 | the screen that shows the trains leaving | Look at the **departure board**. |
| a rail replacement bus | noun | 代行バス | a bus that goes instead of a train | Take the **rail replacement bus** to Reading. |
| valid | adjective | 有効な | you can still use it | Your ticket is still **valid**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue. (~1.5 min)
Rail replacement buses are common in the UK at weekends, when lines are being repaired, and when there are problems.

</details>


#### Slide 8: Vocabulary · Train trouble (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a delay | noun | 遅延 | when something is later than planned | Sorry for the **delay**. |
| an Advance ticket | noun | 事前購入の割引乗車券 | a cheap ticket for one train, bought early | **Advance tickets** are cheaper, but only for one train. |
| to be stuck | verb | 足止めされる | to be in a place and unable to move or leave | We’re **stuck** on a bus! |
| compensation | noun | 補償（金） | money you get because something went wrong | You can claim **compensation** for the delay. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come in the next dialogues and the reading. (~1.5 min)
In the UK people write “cancelled” with two l’s. In American English it’s usually “canceled”.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Story · Cancelled!

Paddington station. Gwen and Haruka talk to a member of staff.

**Haruka:** Gwen, look at the **departure board**. Our train says “**Cancelled**”!  
**Gwen:** Oh, you’re joking. There must be a problem on the line.  
**Staff:** Sorry about this. There’s a signal problem near Reading. Please take the **rail replacement bus** to Reading.  
**Gwen:** A bus? That can’t be right. A bus is far too slow!  
**Haruka:** We’ve got **Advance tickets** for this train. Can we still use them?  
**Staff:** Yes, they’re still **valid**. And you might be able to claim Delay Repay.  
**Haruka:** Delay Repay? What’s that?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
True to life: when a train is cancelled, your ticket is usually valid on the replacement bus or the next train, even an Advance ticket.
The staff member explains Delay Repay on the next slide.
Paddington to Cardiff is normally about two hours by train.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Story · Delay Repay

A minute later. Gwen and Haruka are still talking to the member of staff.

**Staff:** It’s money back when your train is late. The later you are, the more you get.  
**Haruka:** Really? How do we claim it?  
**Staff:** Online, on the train company’s website. Just keep your tickets.  
**Gwen:** How late do we need to be?  
**Staff:** Fifteen or thirty minutes, depending on the company. Two hours late, and you get the full price back.  
**Gwen:** Two hours? With our luck, we’ll get it all back!  
**Haruka:** So for once, being late is good news!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
True: Delay Repay depends on the train company (15- or 30-minute schemes), and a delay of two hours or more gets 100% of a single fare back. Claims are made online within 28 days.
Gwen is half joking, but she’s right: they arrive in Cardiff two hours late at the end of the lesson.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Listening · On the bus

An hour later. The bus driver speaks to the passengers.

[audio: Driver’s announcement]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Hello, everyone. This bus is going to Reading station only. We should be there in about forty minutes. Please keep your train tickets with you.

- Where is the bus going?
- How long will it take?
- What should passengers keep?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. To Reading station only.
> 2. About forty minutes.
> 3. Their train tickets.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Hello, everyone. This bus is going to Reading station only. We should be there in about forty minutes. Please keep your train tickets with you.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~2 min)
“Should be there in…” is a typical way to give a time you expect, but can’t promise.

</details>


#### Slide 12: Language focus · It must be…

> **How sure are you?**
>
> *I’m almost sure it’s true*
>
> There **must be** a problem on the line.
>
> *Maybe*
>
> It **might be** the signals. · It **could be** a strike.
>
> *I’m almost sure it isn’t true*
>
> That **can’t be** right. (not “mustn’t be”)

**From the story**
- Yuki: That **must be** a British thing.
- Gwen: There **must be** a problem on the line.
- Gwen: That **can’t be** right.

Use these to make a guess from evidence.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Show the scale of certainty: must (I’m almost sure it’s true) → might / could (maybe) → can’t (I’m almost sure it’s not true). Japanese learners often use “must not” for this. Correct it: “It can’t be Kenta”, not “It mustn’t be Kenta”. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Practice · What’s going on?

- Tom isn’t answering his phone.
- Priya has three coffees on her desk.
- Gwen is singing in the kitchen.
- Yuki’s light is on at 3 a.m.
- The station is completely empty.
- Your teacher is smiling a lot today.

> **Possible answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. He might be asleep.
> 2. She must be tired.
> 3. She must be happy.
> 4. She could be working.
> 5. There can’t be any trains today.
> 6. They must have good news!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student makes a guess for each with must, might, could or can’t, and gives a reason. Accept any logical answer. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 14: Language focus · Too slow, not enough time

> **too and enough**
>
> *too + adjective*
>
> The bus is **too** slow. · My shoes are **too** high.
>
> *not + adjective + enough*
>
> I’m not **fast enough**.
>
> *enough + noun*
>
> We haven’t got **enough time**.

**From the story**
- Haruka: They’re **too** high.
- Yuki: I’m not **fast enough**.
- Gwen: A bus is far **too** slow!

Use too and enough to say there’s a problem.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Too + adjective = more than you want (a problem). Not + adjective + enough = less than you need. Enough comes before a noun but after an adjective. Japanese learners often say “too much slow” or “enough fast”. Skip the practice slide after this if time is short. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Practice · Too or enough?

- This flat is ____ small for two people.
- I can’t run. My shoes aren’t comfortable ____.
- We haven’t got ____ time for lunch.
- This bus is ____ slow!
- Is your tea hot ____?
- Your turn: something that’s too… or not… enough.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. too
> 2. enough
> 3. enough
> 4. too
> 5. enough
> 6. Student’s own answer

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student completes each sentence with too or enough, then answers number 6. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 16: Pronunciation · Must be, can’t be

> **How it sounds**
>
> must be → **MUS**-bee
> can’t be → **KAHN**-bee
> might be → **MY**-bee

**Say it with your teacher**
- It **must** be **Yuki**.
- It **can’t** be **Tom**.
- It **might** be the **signals**.
- There **must** be a **problem**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

In fast speech the t in must and can’t almost disappears before be. In many British accents can’t has a long “ah” sound (kahnt), which helps you hear the difference from can. Say each line; the student repeats. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 17: Story · Stuck on a bus

On the bus. Haruka and Gwen call Tom on speakerphone.

**Tom:** So you’re **stuck** on a bus? Brilliant.  
**Haruka:** Very funny. Tom, I’ve been thinking about Milk Second.  
**Tom:** Go on, then. Who is it?  
**Haruka:** It **can’t be** Priya. She’d never write “color”.  
**Gwen:** And it **can’t be** me. I can’t even send a photo!  
**Haruka:** It **must be** someone who’s awake at 3 a.m. and learned American English…  
**Tom:** …and who draws all day. It **must be** Yuki!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~2 min)
Tom’s “Brilliant” is sarcastic (Lesson 6).
Everything points to Yuki. That’s the plan. The next slide is the clue board.
Teacher only: every clue also fits Haruka’s mum.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Mystery · The clue board

> **Clues**
>
> • Can see Haruka’s private photos
> • Posts at 3 a.m. UK time
> • Writes American English
> • Draws Tom’s banter as mean
> • Knows about Haruka’s first day

**Suspects**
- Tom
- Priya
- Yuki
- Kenta
- Callum
- Gwen
- Someone else?

> **Ask**
>
> Who must it be?
> Who might it be?
> Who can’t it be?
> Why?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Go through each clue and each suspect with the student, using must, might, could and can’t. Let the student decide who it is and why. Most will say Yuki. Don’t correct them. (~2.5 min)
Teacher only: every clue also fits Haruka’s mum (private photos, 3 a.m. UK = noon in Japan, American English from school, doesn’t know British banter, loved manga as a girl). Don’t say so.

</details>


#### Slide 19: Role play · At the station

> **Student**
>
> Your 18:05 train to Cardiff is cancelled. You go to the help desk.
>
> • Ask what’s happened.
> • Ask if your ticket is still valid.
> • Ask how to get to Cardiff tonight.

**Useful phrases**
- What’s happened?
- Is my ticket still valid?
- Could you tell me how to get to…?
- How long will the bus take?
- There must be…
- Can I claim compensation?

> **Teacher**
>
> You work at the station.
>
> • The reason: a signal failure.
> • A rail replacement bus leaves at 18:30 from the front of the station. Tickets are valid.
> • Mention Delay Repay.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student is the passenger; no swap needed. (~2.5 min)
Listen for deduction (There must be…) and polite questions from Lesson 4.

</details>


#### Slide 20: Reading · Late again?

[image: Photo idea: a rail replacement bus at a station]

British train tickets can be confusing. **Advance** tickets are the cheapest, but you can only use them on the train you booked. **Off-Peak** tickets are for quieter times of day, and **Anytime** tickets cost the most.

If your train is cancelled, your ticket is usually still valid on the next train or on a **rail replacement bus**.

And if you arrive late, you can often get money back. It’s called **Delay Repay**. With some train companies you can claim after 15 minutes; with others, after 30. The longer the delay, the more you get. After an hour, you usually get the full price of a single ticket back, and after two hours, the full price of a return.

Claim online within 28 days, and keep your ticket.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: Which ticket is cheapest? Is your ticket valid on a replacement bus? How long do you have to claim Delay Repay?
Sources: Office of Rail and Road and Trainline guides to ticket types (Advance, Off-Peak, Anytime); National Rail and train company Delay Repay pages (claim within 28 days; 15- or 30-minute schemes depending on the company; 100% of a single fare after 60 minutes, 100% of a return after 120 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 21: Reading · True or false?

- Advance tickets are the most expensive.
- You can use an Advance ticket on any train.
- You have 28 days to claim Delay Repay.
- After a two-hour delay, you get the full price back.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: they’re the cheapest.  2. False: only on the train you booked (unless it’s cancelled).  3. True.  4. True (for that journey).

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 22: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a train arriving at a platform]

- What happens in Japan when a train is late?
- Have you ever been stuck somewhere because of transport? What happened?
- Are pancakes in Japan different from British pancakes? How?
- Would you rather wait for the next train or take a slow bus? Why?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Some may mention delay certificates (遅延証明書) for work or school. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 23: Story · Welcome to Cardiff

Two hours late, at Cardiff Central. Sian meets Gwen and Haruka.

**Sian:** There you are! I’ve been waiting for ages!  
**Gwen:** Don’t ask. Signals, a bus, then another train.  
**Sian:** Well, you’re here now. Haruka, welcome to Cardiff!  
**Haruka:** Thank you! Oh, sorry. I’ve just got a message from Yuki.  
**Sian:** Everything OK?  
**Haruka:** She says, “Can we talk on Monday? I’ve got something to tell you.”  
**Gwen:** Ooh. That sounds serious.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
They’re two hours late, so they can claim the full price back with Delay Repay. Gwen’s joke came true.
Sian is Gwen’s daughter from the beginner course. She lives in Cardiff.
Hook: what does Yuki want to tell Haruka? Ask the student: What do you think? Could it be about Milk Second?

</details>


#### Slide 24: Vocabulary · British phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| You’re joking! | phrase | うそでしょ！ | I’m surprised (often by bad news) | The train’s cancelled? **You’re joking!** |
| Go on, then. | phrase | じゃあ、どうぞ（言ってみて） | OK, tell me / do it | **Go on, then.** Who is it? |
| There you are! | phrase | あ、来た来た！ | I’ve been looking for you! | **There you are!** I’ve been waiting for ages! |
| for ages | phrase | 長い間 | for a very long time | I’ve been waiting **for ages**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

All common in everyday British English. They all came up in today’s dialogues. (~1.5 min)
Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>


#### Slide 25: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- made guesses from evidence with must, might and can’t
- used too and enough to talk about problems
- dealt with a cancelled train
- learned about Pancake Day and Delay Repay

[image: Photo idea: Cardiff Castle and daffodils]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

For each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 8: St David’s Day

**Objective:** Students can make guesses about the past with must have, might have and can’t have, talk about regrets with should have, and apologise properly to make up with someone (CEFR B1: express feelings; speculate about causes; manage a difficult conversation).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 3 · Getting closer · Lesson 8

**St David’s Day**

Today’s goal: guess what happened, talk about regrets and say sorry properly.

[image: Photo idea: Cardiff on St David’s Day]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

St David’s Day weekend in Cardiff, then back to London. Haruka is sure Yuki is the Milk Second artist… and she’s wrong. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: the trip to Cardiff was a disaster (Lesson 7). On the bus, Haruka and Tom decided it must be Yuki. Then Yuki sent a message: “Can we talk on Monday? I’ve got something to tell you.”

Main points: must have / might have / can’t have for guesses about the past, and should have / shouldn’t have for regrets. Practical topic: apologising properly and making up. Culture: St David’s Day and Wales.

Timing: 25 slides, about 44 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip What should they have done? and True or false (saves about 3 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Getting it wrong

Talk with your teacher.

- Is it easy for you to say sorry? Why or why not?
- Have you ever been completely wrong about someone?
- What special days does your country or region have?

[image: Photo idea: daffodils in spring]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it light and personal. Don’t push for details if the student prefers not to share. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: Cardiff on St David’s Day. Sian, Gwen and Haruka in the street with daffodils and flags. Sian pins a daffodil on Haruka. Gwen waves a flag. Haruka looks at her phone and her smile fades.]

- What are people celebrating? What can you see?
- How does Haruka feel at the end? Why, do you think?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Happy St David’s Day!

Sunday in Cardiff, the day before St David’s Day. Sian, Gwen and Haruka.

**Sian:** Happy St David’s Day! Well, nearly. Here, have a daffodil.  
**Haruka:** Thank you! It’s lovely. But I keep thinking about Yuki’s message.  
**Gwen:** Still? She might have just got some good news.  
**Haruka:** Or she must have realised that I know. Maybe I should have asked her already.  
**Sian:** Know what?  
**Haruka:** That she draws Milk Second. Everything points to her.  
**Sian:** Hmm. Be careful. You can’t be sure until you talk to her.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Haruka), then swap. (~2 min)
Ask: Do you agree with Sian or with Haruka?
St David’s Day is 1 March. Many towns hold parades and events on the nearest weekend.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- What does Sian give Haruka?
- What is Haruka thinking about?
- Who does Haruka think draws Milk Second?
- What is Sian’s advice?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. A daffodil.  2. Yuki’s message.  3. Yuki.  4. Be careful: talk to her before she’s sure.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Culture · St David’s Day

> **Wales’s national day**
>
> St David is the patron saint of Wales, and his day is **1 March**. Many Welsh people wear a **daffodil** or a **leek**, the two symbols of Wales.
>
> Some children go to school in traditional Welsh dress, and there are parades in Cardiff and other towns. People eat **Welsh cakes** (small, flat cakes with dried fruit) and **cawl**, a lamb and vegetable stew.
>
> It isn’t a bank holiday, so most people still go to work.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

This explains the first dialogue. (~1.5 min)
Legend says St David told Welsh soldiers to wear leeks in a battle, so they could recognise each other. The daffodil is the national flower and blooms around 1 March.
Sources: Visit Wales and other guides to St David’s Day (1 March; daffodils and leeks; Welsh cakes and cawl; children in traditional dress; parades). St David’s Day is not a public holiday in Wales.
Ask: Does Japan have a day like this for a region or a city?

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Getting it wrong (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a suspect | noun | 容疑者 | a person who people think did something wrong | Yuki is the main **suspect**. |
| to accuse | verb | 責める、非難する | to say someone did something wrong | Don’t **accuse** her in front of everyone! |
| a portfolio | noun | 作品集、ポートフォリオ | a collection of your best work | This is my **portfolio**. |
| upset | adjective | 動揺した、傷ついた | sad or worried because something bad happened | Yuki was really **upset**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue. (~1.5 min)
“Accuse someone of + -ing”: She accused me of lying.

</details>


#### Slide 8: Vocabulary · Making up (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to apologise | verb | 謝る | to say sorry | I want to **apologise**. |
| an excuse | noun | 言い訳 | a reason you give to explain something bad you did | No **excuses**. It was my fault. |
| properly | adverb | ちゃんと、きちんと | in the right way | Say sorry **properly**. |
| to make up | verb | 仲直りする | to become friends again after an argument | Have you two **made up**? |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come in the dialogues later in the lesson. (~1.5 min)
British spelling: apologise. American spelling: apologize.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Story · A few questions

Monday morning at the office. Haruka, Yuki and Priya.

**Yuki:** Haruka, there’s something I want to show you.  
**Haruka:** Wait. First, can I ask you a few questions?  
**Yuki:** Er… OK. What kind of questions?  
**Haruka:** What were you doing at 3 a.m. last night?  
**Yuki:** Drawing, like always. Why?  
**Haruka:** And when you write in English, is it “color” or “colour”?  
**Yuki:** “Color”, I think. We learned American English at school. Why do I feel like a **suspect**?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Haruka asks about two clues from the clue board: the 3 a.m. posts and American English. Both answers fit, so Yuki looks even more suspicious.
Ask: What do you think Yuki wants to show Haruka?

</details>


#### Slide 10: Story · Are you the artist?

A moment later. Haruka, Yuki and Priya.

**Haruka:** Yuki, be honest. Are you the Milk Second artist?  
**Yuki:** What? No! Look, this is my **portfolio**. Robots and space stations!  
**Priya:** She’s right. It’s a completely different style.  
**Yuki:** And how could I know about your first day in London? I only met you in September!  
**Haruka:** Oh no. It can’t have been you.  
**Priya:** So what’s the portfolio for?  
**Yuki:** MCM Comic Con. I’ve applied for a table. That was my news.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Yuki proves it can’t be her: her drawing style is completely different, and she couldn’t know about Haruka’s first day.
Her 3 a.m. drawing is her portfolio for the Artist Alley at MCM Comic Con London (a big comic and pop culture show, twice a year). Artists apply with a portfolio months before.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Story · I just need a minute

A moment later. Haruka, Yuki and Priya.

**Haruka:** Yuki, I’m so sorry. I shouldn’t have asked you like that.  
**Yuki:** I understand why you thought it. But you should have asked me on my own, not in front of Priya.  
**Priya:** I’ll… go and make some tea.  
**Yuki:** I just need a minute, Haruka.  
**Haruka:** Of course. I really am sorry.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Yuki isn’t angry, but she’s hurt, mostly because it happened in front of a colleague.
Priya leaves to give them space: a very British way to escape an awkward moment.
Ask: How do you think Yuki feels? What should Haruka do now?

</details>


#### Slide 12: Story · Did you understand?

- Which two clues does Haruka ask about?
- How does Yuki show it wasn’t her?
- What is her portfolio for?
- Why is Yuki hurt?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Drawing at 3 a.m. and American English.  2. Her style is completely different, and she couldn’t know about Haruka’s first day.  3. A table at MCM Comic Con.  4. Haruka asked her in front of Priya.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check on the three dialogues. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Listening · A message from Sian

Haruka plays a message Sian left on Sunday. Listen and answer.

[audio: Sian’s voice message]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Hiya Haruka, it’s Sian. Thanks for coming to Cardiff! Good luck with your friend on Monday. Talk to her properly, OK? Bye!

- Why is Sian calling?
- What advice does she give?
- Did Haruka follow her advice?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. To thank Haruka for coming to Cardiff.
> 2. Talk to her friend properly.
> 3. No: she asked Yuki in front of Priya.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Hiya Haruka, it’s Sian. Thanks for coming to Cardiff! Good luck with your friend on Monday. Talk to her properly, OK? Bye!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~2 min)
Sian left this on Sunday, before Monday’s conversation. Haruka didn’t follow her advice. Ask: What should Haruka have done?

</details>


#### Slide 14: Language focus · She must have…

> **Guessing about the past**
>
> *I’m almost sure it happened*
>
> She **must have realised**. · That **must have hurt**.
>
> *Maybe*
>
> She **might have got** good news. · She **could have been** busy.
>
> *I’m almost sure it didn’t happen*
>
> It **can’t have been** Yuki.

**From the story**
- Gwen: She **might have** just **got** some good news.
- Haruka: She **must have realised** that I know.
- Haruka: It **can’t have been** you.

Use these to guess what happened in the past.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Same scale as Lesson 7, now for the past: must have / might have / could have / can’t have + past participle. Japanese learners often say “must had” or “must be realised”. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Practice · What must have happened?

- Yuki didn’t come to lunch.
- Tom came home with flour all over his hoodie.
- Gwen’s daffodils are dead.
- Haruka has twenty missed calls.
- The office kitchen smells of pancakes.
- Your turn: something strange that happened to you.

> **Possible answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. She must have been upset.
> 2. He must have been baking.
> 3. She can’t have watered them.
> 4. Something might have happened at home.
> 5. Someone must have made pancakes.
> 6. Student’s own answer

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student makes a guess about the past for each one, and gives a reason. Accept any logical answer. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 16: Language focus · I should have…

> **Regrets**
>
> *A good idea that didn’t happen*
>
> You **should have asked** her. · I **should have checked** the trains.
>
> *A bad idea that happened*
>
> I **shouldn’t have asked** like that.

**From the story**
- Haruka: Maybe I **should have asked** her already.
- Haruka: I **shouldn’t have asked** you like that.
- Yuki: You **should have asked** me on my own.

Use should have and shouldn’t have for regrets.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Should have + past participle = it was a good idea, but it didn’t happen (regret or criticism). Shouldn’t have = it happened, but it was a bad idea. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 17: Practice · What should they have done?

- Haruka accused Yuki in front of Priya.
- Gwen and Haruka didn’t check the trains.
- Tom ate all the Welsh cakes.
- Yuki didn’t tell anyone about Comic Con.
- Your turn: something you regret (a small thing!).

> **Possible answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. She shouldn’t have accused her. She should have talked to her alone.
> 2. They should have checked the trains.
> 3. He shouldn’t have eaten them all.
> 4. She should have told someone.
> 5. Student’s own answer

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student says what each person should or shouldn’t have done. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 18: Pronunciation · Should’ve, must’ve

> **How it sounds**
>
> should have → **SHUD**-uv
> shouldn’t have → **SHUD**-n-uv
> must have → **MUST**-uv
> might have → **MY**-tuv

**Say it with your teacher**
- I should have **asked** her.
- I shouldn’t have **said** that.
- That must have **hurt**.
- She might have been **busy**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Have is almost always weak here: /əv/, often written should’ve. It sounds like “of”, which is why some native speakers wrongly write “should of”. Say each line; the student repeats. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 19: Story · You look terrible

Monday evening at home. Tom, Gwen and Haruka.

**Tom:** You look terrible. What happened?  
**Haruka:** I asked Yuki if she was the artist. She wasn’t. She **must have felt** awful.  
**Gwen:** Oh, love. You **should have listened** to Sian. She told you to talk to her properly.  
**Tom:** Teasing your friends is fine. Accusing them isn’t.  
**Haruka:** I know. And I **shouldn’t have listened** to you, Tom!  
**Tom:** Fair point. Sorry.  
**Gwen:** **Apologise** **properly** tomorrow. Say what you got wrong, and don’t make **excuses**.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Tom’s line connects the banter thread: teasing is fine between friends, but accusing someone is serious.
Gwen’s advice is a good guide to a British apology: say what you did wrong, don’t make excuses, and offer to make it right.
Tom said “It must be Yuki!” in Lesson 7, so Haruka blames him a little. He accepts it.

</details>


#### Slide 20: Reading · Wales

[image: Photo idea: a Welsh village with a dragon flag]

Wales is one of the four nations of the UK. Its capital is Cardiff, and its flag has a red **dragon** on it.

Wales has its own language, Welsh, which is very different from English. In the 2021 census, about 18% of people in Wales said they could speak it. Almost everyone in Wales speaks English too.

You’ll see both languages on road signs and hear both in train announcements, so don’t worry if a sign looks strange!

Many Welsh people are proud of their **identity**, so it’s best not to call them English.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: What’s the capital of Wales? Do people in Wales speak English? What might happen if you call a Welsh person English?
Sources: 2021 Census (Welsh Government / ONS): 17.8% of people aged three and over could speak Welsh. Bilingual signs and announcements are standard in Wales.
Connects to beginner Lesson 3 (Four nations, one country).

</details>


#### Slide 21: Reading · True or false?

- Cardiff is the capital of Wales.
- Most people in Wales can’t speak English.
- Road signs in Wales are in two languages.
- Welsh people are happy to be called English.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. True.  2. False: everyone speaks English, and about 18% speak Welsh too.  3. True.  4. False: they might correct you.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 22: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: two friends making up over coffee]

- How do friends in Japan make up after an argument?
- Are there strong regional accents or dialects in Japan? Which ones?
- Is it OK to draw a comic about real people without asking them?
- What should Haruka do to make up with Yuki?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Some may mention Kansai-ben or other dialects. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 23: Story · Friends again?

Tuesday, in the office kitchen. Priya is making tea nearby.

**Haruka:** Yuki, I want to **apologise**. I **shouldn’t have asked** you like that, in front of everyone.  
**Yuki:** Thanks, Haruka. I was really upset. But the comic is strange. I understand.  
**Haruka:** No **excuses**. It was my fault. So… can I see your **portfolio** **properly**?  
**Yuki:** Really? OK, here. Be honest!  
**Haruka:** Yuki, these are amazing. You **must have worked** so hard.  
**Priya:** Tea round! Oh good, have you two **made up**?  
**Yuki:** Yes. And now we’re going to find the real artist. Together.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Haruka follows Gwen’s advice: she says what she did wrong, makes no excuses, and shows real interest.
Yuki is now Haruka’s ally in the mystery.

</details>


#### Slide 24: Mystery · The clue board

> **It can’t have been…**
>
> • Priya: she writes British English.
> • Kenta: he can’t draw.
> • Gwen: she can’t even send a photo!
> • Yuki: she was drawing her portfolio.

**Clues**
- Private photos
- 3 a.m. UK time
- American English
- Doesn’t get banter
- Knows her first day

> **So…**
>
> Who’s left?
> Who else can see Haruka’s private photos?
> Who might it be?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Update the clue board from Lesson 7. Go through who it can’t have been, and why. Then ask who is left. Let the student think. If they suggest someone in Haruka’s family, just say “Interesting…”. (~2 min)
Teacher only: Tom and Callum are British and understand banter, so they don’t fit either. The artist is Haruka’s mum.

</details>


#### Slide 25: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- guessed what happened with must have, might have and can’t have
- talked about regrets with should have
- apologised properly and made up with a friend
- learned about St David’s Day and Wales

[image: Photo idea: two mugs of tea and a daffodil]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

For each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 9: Paperwork

**Objective:** Students can report what other people said, make a formal phone call to change a booking, and choose the right style for an email (CEFR B1: pass on messages; use the telephone for less routine matters).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 3 · Getting closer · Lesson 9

**Paperwork**

Today’s goal: say what other people told you, make a formal phone call and choose the right style for an email.

[image: Photo idea: a passport and forms on a desk]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

March. Haruka’s company offers to sponsor her visa, so now there’s paperwork, a phone call… and an English test. It’s also Mothering Sunday. Last lesson of Unit 3, with a progress check. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Yuki isn’t the Milk Second artist. She’s applied for a table at MCM Comic Con, and she and Haruka have made up (Lesson 8). Haruka’s Youth Mobility visa ends this summer.

Main point: reported speech. Practical topics: visa paperwork (simply), formal phone calls and emails. Mystery: the decisive clue.

Timing: 27 slides, about 46 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip What did they say?, True or false, the first Did you understand? and British phrases (saves about 5.5 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Calls and emails

Talk with your teacher.

- Do you prefer phoning or emailing? Why?
- Have you ever taken an English test? How did it go?
- What do people in Japan do on Mother’s Day?

[image: Photo idea: a phone and a laptop]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. Push for reasons. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: The office. Haruka at her desk with lots of forms. Priya brings her tea. Haruka sighs and puts her head on the forms. Priya puts the tea down, pats her back and laughs.]

- What is Haruka doing? How does she feel?
- What does Priya do? Why, do you think?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Good news

Wednesday at the office. Priya and Haruka.

**Priya:** Haruka, have you got a minute? The company wants to sponsor you for a Skilled Worker visa!  
**Haruka:** Really? So I can stay? What do I need to do?  
**Priya:** We’ll give you a certificate of sponsorship. Then you apply online.  
**Haruka:** Is that all?  
**Priya:** Not quite. You’ll also need to pass an English test, at B2 level.  
**Haruka:** A test? I’ve been living here for a year and a half!  
**Priya:** I know. It’s the rules. But you’ll be fine.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Haruka), then swap. (~2 min)
Real rules, kept simple: Youth Mobility visas can’t be extended for Japanese nationals, but you can switch to a Skilled Worker visa inside the UK if a licensed employer sponsors you. Since January 2026, first-time Skilled Worker applicants must prove English at B2 level (for example with an approved test like IELTS for UKVI) unless they have an English-taught degree.
The student is studying B1 to B2 too, so ask: Could you pass a B2 test now?

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- What’s Priya’s good news?
- What does Haruka need to do first?
- What else does she need to do?
- How does Haruka feel about the test?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. The company wants to sponsor her visa.  2. Get a certificate of sponsorship and apply online.  3. Pass an English test at B2 level.  4. Surprised, a bit annoyed: she’s lived here for a year and a half.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · Paperwork (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to sponsor | verb | （ビザの）スポンサーになる | to officially support someone (e.g. for a visa) | The company will **sponsor** my visa. |
| a certificate | noun | 証明書 | an official document that shows something is true | You’ll get a **certificate** of sponsorship. |
| a document | noun | 書類 | an official paper, like a passport or a letter | Send all your **documents** online. |
| a deadline | noun | 締め切り | the last day you can do something | The **deadline** is the end of July. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the dialogues and the reading. (~1.5 min)
A certificate of sponsorship is a reference number from the employer, not a paper certificate.

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · On the phone (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to hold | verb | （電話を切らずに）待つ | to wait on the phone | Could you **hold**, please? |
| to confirm | verb | 確認する | to say that something is definitely true or arranged | I just need to **confirm** your date. |
| available | adjective | 空いている | free to use or book | Two o’clock is **available**. |
| to book | verb | 予約する | to arrange a time or place in advance | I’ve **booked** my test. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come in the phone call later in the lesson. (~1.5 min)
“Hold on” is the informal version: Hold on a sec!

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · A good day

That evening at home. Gwen, Tom and Haruka.

**Gwen:** You look happy, love. Good day?  
**Haruka:** Great day! Priya said the company would sponsor my visa.  
**Tom:** So you’re staying? Brilliant!  
**Haruka:** Maybe. She told me I needed to pass an English test first.  
**Tom:** You? You speak better English than me!  
**Gwen:** That’s not hard, Tom. Oh, it’s Mothering Sunday this weekend. Sian’s taking me out for lunch.  
**Haruka:** Mothering Sunday? In Japan, Mother’s Day is in May!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Tom’s “Brilliant!” is sincere this time. Ask: How do you know? (Lesson 6 intonation.)
Gwen teases Tom: banter again.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Story · A card for Mum

A moment later. Gwen, Tom and Haruka.

**Gwen:** You should send your mum a card. It’d be a nice surprise.  
**Haruka:** Good idea. Mum said she wanted a British card for her fridge.  
**Tom:** Get one with a corgi on it. Mums love a corgi.  
**Gwen:** Says the man who forgot his own mum’s birthday last year!  
**Tom:** I didn’t forget! It was just… late.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Haruka will post the card to Osaka. Remember the card: it matters at the end of the lesson.
Tom is always late, even with birthday cards.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Culture · Mothering Sunday

> **Not in May!**
>
> In the UK, Mother’s Day is called **Mothering Sunday**. It’s on the fourth Sunday of Lent, three weeks before Easter, so it’s usually in March.
>
> Long ago, it was the day people visited their “mother church”, and young servants got a day off to see their mothers.
>
> Today people give their mums cards and flowers, or take them out for lunch. In Japan, the USA and many other countries, Mother’s Day is in May.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

This explains the dialogues. (~1.5 min)
Some families also eat simnel cake (a fruit cake with marzipan) around this time.
Sources: British Library, “The medieval origins of Mothering Sunday”; Wikipedia, Mothering Sunday and Simnel cake.
Ask: What do you usually give your mum?

</details>


#### Slide 11: Listening · A message for Haruka

The next day. Haruka gets a voice message. Listen and answer.

[audio: Test centre message]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Hello, this is a message for Haruka from the London English Test Centre. We need to confirm your test booking. Please call us back before Friday. Thank you.

- Who is calling?
- What do they need to do?
- When should Haruka call back?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. The London English Test Centre.
> 2. Confirm her test booking.
> 3. Before Friday.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Hello, this is a message for Haruka from the London English Test Centre. We need to confirm your test booking. Please call us back before Friday. Thank you.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~2 min)
Haruka calls back in the next dialogue.

</details>


#### Slide 12: Language focus · She said…, she told me…

> **Reporting**
>
> *say and tell*
>
> Priya **said** (that)… · Priya **told me** (that)… (not “said me”)
>
> *The tense moves back*
>
> “The company **will** sponsor you.” → She said the company **would** sponsor me.
> “You **need** a test.” → She told me I **needed** a test.
>
> *More examples*
>
> “I**’ve applied** for a table.” → Yuki said she**’d applied** for a table.

**From the story**
- Haruka: Priya **said** the company **would** sponsor my visa.
- Haruka: She **told me** I **needed** to pass an English test.
- Haruka: Mum **said** she **wanted** a British card.

Use reported speech to tell people what someone said.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Reported speech: the verb usually moves one step back (will → would, need → needed, have done → had done). Say + what was said; tell + a person. Japanese learners often say “She said me”. In everyday speech, people sometimes keep the present if it’s still true: “Priya said the company will sponsor me” is also fine. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Practice · What did they say?

- Tom: “I’m late again.”
- Gwen: “I’ll put the kettle on.”
- Yuki: “I’ve applied for a table.”
- Priya: “You need a certificate.”
- Dan: “The landlord wants your share code.”
- Your turn: something someone told you this week.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Tom said he was late again.
> 2. Gwen said she’d put the kettle on.
> 3. Yuki told me she’d applied for a table.
> 4. Priya told Haruka she needed a certificate.
> 5. Dan said the landlord wanted her share code.
> 6. Student’s own answer

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reports each sentence. Accept said or told + person. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 14: Story · Calling back

The next morning. Haruka calls the test centre back.

**Receptionist:** Good morning, London English Test Centre. How can I help?  
**Haruka:** Hello. I’m calling about my test booking. You left me a message.  
**Receptionist:** Of course. Could I have your name, please?  
**Haruka:** It’s Haruka. Shall I spell it? H-A-R-U-K-A.  
**Receptionist:** Thank you. Could you **hold**, please? … Right. I just need to **confirm** your date: Friday the 19th at nine?  
**Haruka:** Would it be possible to change it to the afternoon? I’ve got a meeting.  
**Receptionist:** Let me check… Yes, two o’clock is **available**. I’ll send you an email to confirm.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
A typical formal phone call. Notice how polite and indirect Haruka is: “Would it be possible to…?”
Offering to spell your name is very useful for Japanese names on the phone.

</details>


#### Slide 15: Language focus · On the phone

> **Phone phrases**
>
> *Starting*
>
> I**’m calling about**… · **Could I speak to**…?
>
> *Being polite*
>
> **Would it be possible to**…? · **Could you hold**, please?
>
> *Checking*
>
> **Shall I spell it?** · **So that’s** Friday at two?

**From the story**
- Haruka: **I’m calling about** my test booking.
- Receptionist: **Could you hold**, please?
- Haruka: **Would it be possible to** change it?

Use these phrases for formal phone calls.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Formal phone English is mostly fixed phrases. Practise them as chunks. In British English, “Could you hold?” and “Bear with me” are very common. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 16: Pronunciation · Sounding polite

> **Friendly or flat?**
>
> Friendly: the voice starts high and moves → “Good **MOR**-ning! How can I **HELP**?”
> Flat: low and the same all the way → sounds bored or rude

**Say it with your teacher**
- Good **mor**ning! How can I **help**?
- I’m **call**ing about my **book**ing.
- Would it be **poss**ible to **change** it?
- **Thank** you so **much**!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

On the phone people can’t see your face, so your voice has to sound friendly. Start high and let your voice move. A flat, low voice can sound bored or rude. Say each line; the student repeats. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 17: Role play · Changing a booking

> **Student**
>
> You have an English test this Friday at 10 a.m., but you can’t go. Call the test centre.
>
> • Say why you’re calling and spell your name.
> • You work Monday to Thursday, 9 to 5.
> • Change the test politely.

**Useful phrases**
- I’m calling about…
- Shall I spell it?
- Would it be possible to…?
- Could you hold, please?
- So that’s… at…?
- Thanks so much.

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re the receptionist at the test centre.
>
> • Answer formally. Ask the caller to hold once.
> • First offer: Tuesday at 2 p.m.
> • Then offer Saturday at 11 a.m.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student’s work hours mean your first offer doesn’t work, so they have to say no politely. No swap needed. (~2.5 min)
Listen for phone phrases and polite requests.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Progress check · Unit 3 check

> **Student**
>
> Tell your teacher what happened with Tom and Yuki.
>
> • What did Tom think, and why?
> • What must or might have happened, in his mind?
> • What should he have done?

**Unit 3 phrases**
- She said…
- He told me…
- It must have been…
- It can’t have been…
- I should have…
- I’m really sorry about…

> **Teacher**
>
> Act as if you don’t know the story.
>
> • Ask: What happened? What did he say?
> • Guess: It might have been…
> • Ask: What would you have done?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Progress check for Unit 3. The student retells the misunderstanding from the story, so there’s nothing to invent. Let the student lead and don’t correct during the conversation. Note what they do well and one thing to practise. (~3 min)
Look for: must / might / can’t (have), should have, reported speech, and apologising properly.

</details>


#### Slide 19: Reading · Dear, Hi or Hiya?

[image: Photo idea: writing an email on a laptop]

British emails can be more or less formal. To someone you don’t know, start with **Dear** and the name (Dear Ms Jones), and end with **Kind regards** or **Best wishes**.

With colleagues, most people write **Hi** and the first name, and end with **Thanks** or **Best**.

**Hiya** and **Cheers** are friendly and informal: fine for friends, but not for your first email to a new client.

Many British people end messages to friends and family with an **x**, a kiss. Between friends, it usually isn’t romantic. Some people use it with colleagues, but at work it’s safer not to.

Not sure? Copy the style of the other person’s email.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: How do you start an email to someone you don’t know? What does “x” mean? Should you use it at work?
This describes common British habits, using soft language (many, most). There are no fixed rules, which is why the last tip matters.

</details>


#### Slide 20: Reading · True or false?

- Start an email to a stranger with “Hiya”.
- “Kind regards” is a formal ending.
- An “x” at the end is always romantic.
- Copying the other person’s style is a good idea.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: use “Dear”.  2. True.  3. False: it’s often just friendly.  4. True.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a video call with family]

- How formal are work emails in Japan? How do they start and end?
- Which is harder in English for you: phone calls or emails? Why?
- What do you usually do for your mum on Mother’s Day?
- Should Haruka stay in the UK? Why or why not?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Some may mention fixed opening phrases in Japanese work emails. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 22: Story · The card arrived

The next weekend. Haruka video-calls her mum. Gwen is with her.

**Mum:** Haruka! Thank you for the card. It arrived yesterday!  
**Haruka:** Mum, your English is getting really good!  
**Mum:** Thank you. I’ve been practising every day. Is Gwen there?  
**Gwen:** Hello! Nice to meet you at last. Your English is lovely!  
**Mum:** Thank you, Gwen. Haruka tells me everything about you.  
**Gwen:** Oh dear. Even my tea rules?  
**Mum:** Especially your tea rules!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Mum speaks English because Gwen is there, and because she’s been practising (she left Haruka an English voice message in Lesson 4).
Teacher only: Mum has heard all about Gwen and her tea rules on Haruka’s calls since the beginner course, so she knows the rule “milk second”, the comic’s name. Don’t point it out. Some students will notice.

</details>


#### Slide 23: Story · A trip in May

A moment later. Gwen, Mum and Haruka.

**Gwen:** Are you going anywhere nice this spring?  
**Mum:** My English class is planning a trip abroad. In May.  
**Haruka:** Really? Where to?  
**Mum:** Somewhere English-speaking. I’ll tell you later.  
**Gwen:** Ooh, how mysterious!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1 min)
Teacher only: another clue. Mum’s class trip in May is to London, for Comic Con. Don’t point it out.

</details>


#### Slide 24: Story · Wish me luck!

Friday the 19th at the office. Yuki, Priya and Haruka.

**Yuki:** Haruka, look! The new Milk Second. Hana sends her mum a card.  
**Priya:** And look at the last picture. The card is on someone’s fridge.  
**Haruka:** Sorry, I can’t look now. My English test is in an hour!  
**Yuki:** Haruka, didn’t you send your mum a card too?  
**Haruka:** Probably. Tell me later! Wish me luck!  
**Priya:** Good luck! … Yuki, whose fridge is that?  
**Yuki:** Hmm…  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
The decisive clue: the last picture shows Haruka’s card on a fridge. Haruka said Mum wanted a British card for her fridge. Haruka is too busy to notice.
Don’t solve it. The next slide shows the picture.

</details>


#### Slide 25: Mystery · Milk Second: the fridge

[image: Milk Second: Hana’s card on a fridge]

- What do you see on the fridge?
- Where do you think this kitchen is? How do you know?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe the picture in detail. Just plant the idea: don’t ask who drew it, and don’t confirm any guesses. (~1.5 min)
Details to look for: Haruka’s card (a cup of tea and a daffodil), an Osaka Castle magnet, a takoyaki magnet, a printed photo of Haruka with her huge navy suitcase, and a calendar with a tiny plane drawn on one day in May (this pays off in Lesson 11).
Teacher only: it’s Mum’s fridge in Osaka.

</details>


#### Slide 26: Vocabulary · British phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Have you got a minute? | phrase | ちょっといい？ | can I talk to you quickly? | **Have you got a minute?** I’ve got some news. |
| lovely | adjective | すてきな、いい | very nice (British people use it for almost anything good) | Your English is **lovely**! |
| to love a… | phrase | 〜が大好き | to really like a kind of thing | Mums **love a** corgi. |
| Oh dear. | phrase | あらまあ | a soft way to show you’re a bit worried or embarrassed | **Oh dear.** Even my tea rules? |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

All very typical of British English. They all came up in today’s dialogues. (~1.5 min)
“Have you got…?” is more British; Americans usually say “Do you have…?”. “Love a…” (I love a cup of tea, I love a bargain) is very common in Britain.
Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>


#### Slide 27: Wrap-up · Unit 3 check

**In Unit 3, I can…**
- make guesses about the present and the past
- talk about regrets and apologise properly
- tell people what someone said
- make a formal phone call and choose the right style for an email

[image: Photo idea: a card on a kitchen table]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Self-check for Unit 3: for each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1.5 min)

</details>


## Unit: Milk Second


### Lesson 10: Beside the seaside

**Objective:** Students can make a polite but firm complaint and talk about imaginary situations and an imaginary past with the second and third conditionals (CEFR B1: make a complaint; B1+/B2: speculate about hypothetical situations).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 4 · Milk Second · Lesson 10

**Beside the seaside**

Today’s goal: complain politely but firmly, and talk about situations that are imaginary or didn’t happen.

[image: Photo idea: Brighton beach and the pier]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The May bank holiday weekend. Haruka, Tom and Yuki go to Brighton: a hotel problem, a hungry seagull, and big news about Milk Second. First lesson of Unit 4. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Haruka’s company is sponsoring her visa, and she had her English test on Friday the 19th (Lesson 9). A Milk Second episode showed her Mothering Sunday card being opened, as if by the person who received it.

Main points: the second and third conditionals. Practical topic: complaining politely but firmly. Culture: the British seaside and bank holidays.

Timing: 23 slides, about 39 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip If only… and True or false (saves about 2.5 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Days out

Talk with your teacher.

- Do you prefer the sea or the mountains? Why?
- What do you do on a long weekend?
- Have you ever complained in a hotel or restaurant? What happened?

[image: Photo idea: an English seaside promenade]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. Push for reasons and details. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: Brighton pier. Haruka photographs her chips; Tom and Yuki are with her. A seagull is behind her. A seagull grabs some of Haruka’s chips while she takes a photo. Haruka jumps, Tom laughs, Yuki points at the bird.]

- What happens to Haruka’s chips?
- How do the three friends react?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.
Callback: Haruka photographs every meal. This time it costs her.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Off to the seaside

May bank holiday Sunday. Tom, Yuki and Haruka on the train.

**Tom:** Bank holiday weekend, the sun’s out, and we’re off to the seaside. Perfect.  
**Yuki:** And I’ve got news. I got a table at MCM Comic Con!  
**Haruka:** Yuki, that’s amazing! And I passed my English test. My visa application has been submitted.  
**Tom:** Two bits of good news! If I’d known, I’d have bought champagne.  
**Haruka:** You never buy champagne.  
**Yuki:** So what’s in Brighton?  
**Tom:** The pier, the beach, chips… and seagulls. If I were you, I’d hold on to your food.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together, then swap. (~2 min)
Haruka passed her B2 test, and her Skilled Worker application has been sent. The decision usually takes about eight weeks.
Tom teases himself (“If I’d known, I’d have bought champagne”) and Haruka teases him back. Banter again.
Ask: What do you think will happen with the seagulls?

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- Where are they going?
- What is Yuki’s news?
- What is Haruka’s news?
- What is Tom’s advice?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. To Brighton, for the bank holiday weekend.  2. She got a table at MCM Comic Con.  3. She passed her English test and sent her visa application.  4. Hold on to your food (because of the seagulls).

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · At the hotel (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a booking | noun | 予約 | an arrangement to have a room, table or ticket | We made the **booking** a month ago. |
| a confirmation | noun | 確認（書・メール） | an email or message that says your booking is OK | We’ve got the **confirmation** here. |
| straight away | phrase | すぐに | immediately | I’d give you a room **straight away**. |
| good enough | phrase | 十分な、満足できる | OK, acceptable | I’m afraid that isn’t **good enough**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue. (~1.5 min)
“Straight away” is the usual British phrase; Americans often say “right away”.

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · At the seaside (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a bank holiday | noun | （英国の）祝日 | a public holiday in the UK, usually on a Monday | It’s a **bank holiday** on Monday. |
| a pier | noun | 桟橋 | a long structure that goes out into the sea | Let’s walk to the end of the **pier**. |
| a seagull | noun | カモメ | a big white and grey bird that lives by the sea | A **seagull** took my chips! |
| to grab | verb | ひったくる、さっとつかむ | to take something quickly and roughly | It **grabbed** half my chips. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come later in the lesson. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · No room

The hotel in Brighton. The manager, Tom and Haruka.

**Manager:** I’m very sorry. Your **booking** came through a website, and your room has been given to someone else.  
**Tom:** But we booked it a month ago. We’ve got the **confirmation** here.  
**Manager:** I understand. If we had any free rooms, I’d give you one **straight away**.  
**Haruka:** I’m afraid that isn’t **good enough**. If you were us, would you be happy?  
**Manager:** No, I wouldn’t. I can book you into our sister hotel, five minutes away, and pay for the taxi.  
**Tom:** And breakfast? If we’d booked directly, we’d have had breakfast included!  
**Manager:** Breakfast will be included. I’m really sorry again.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Haruka complains politely but firmly: “I’m afraid…”, then a question that makes the manager think. She doesn’t shout, and it works.
Booking through a third-party website can sometimes cause problems like this. Booking directly with the hotel is often safer.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Culture · Complaining, the British way

> **Polite… but firm**
>
> Many British people don’t like complaining. In a YouGov survey, four in ten said they’d feel uncomfortable sending food back in a restaurant. Some people complain to their friends, then tell the waiter “It’s lovely, thanks!”
>
> When people in Britain do complain, many stay very polite. They often start with “Sorry, but…” or “I’m afraid…”, stay calm, and ask “What can you do?”
>
> Shouting rarely works. Calm and firm usually does.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

This explains how Haruka complains in the dialogue. (~1.5 min)
Source: YouGov (2018): 39% of Brits would feel uncomfortable sending food back.
Ask: How do people complain in Japan? Do they?

</details>


#### Slide 10: Listening · On the pier

That evening, on Brighton pier. An announcement plays.

[audio: Pier announcement]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Good evening, everyone. The pier will close at ten o’clock tonight. And please don’t feed the seagulls. They’re hungry enough already!

- When will the pier close?
- What shouldn’t people do?
- Why is the last sentence funny?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. At ten o’clock tonight.
> 2. Feed the seagulls.
> 3. The seagulls are already greedy (it’s a joke).

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Good evening, everyone. The pier will close at ten o’clock tonight. And please don’t feed the seagulls. They’re hungry enough already!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~2 min)
Recycles “enough” from Lesson 7.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Language focus · If I were you…

> **If + past simple, would + verb**
>
> *Imaginary now*
>
> If we **had** a free room, I**’d give** it to you.
>
> *Advice*
>
> **If I were you**, I**’d hold** on to your food.
>
> *Asking*
>
> If you **were** us, **would** you **be** happy?

**From the story**
- Tom: **If I were you**, I**’d hold** on to your food.
- Manager: If we **had** any free rooms, I**’d give** you one.
- Haruka: If you **were** us, **would** you **be** happy?

Use the second conditional for imaginary situations now or in the future.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Second conditional: an imaginary or unlikely situation now, and its result. If + past simple, would + verb. “If I were you” uses were (not was) in this phrase. Japanese learners often put would in both halves: “If I would have…”. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 12: Practice · What would you do?

- If a seagull took your food, what would you do?
- If you could live anywhere in the UK, where would you live?
- If a hotel gave your room away, what would you say?
- If you had a free bank holiday tomorrow, what would you do?
- If you were Haruka, would you stay in the UK?

> **Example answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. I’d chase it! / I’d buy more chips.
> 2. If I could, I’d live in Edinburgh.
> 3. I’d ask to speak to the manager.
> 4. I’d go to the seaside.
> 5. Student’s own answer

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student answers each question with a full second conditional sentence and a reason. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Story · The seagull

Later, on the pier. Tom, Yuki and Haruka have bought chips.

**Haruka:** These chips look amazing. Let me just take a photo first…  
**Tom:** Haruka, careful! There’s a **seagull** right behind you!  
**Yuki:** Watch out!  
**Haruka:** No! He **grabbed** my chips! If I hadn’t stopped for a photo, I wouldn’t have lost them!  
**Tom:** I did warn you. **If I were** you, I**’d eat** the rest very quickly.  
**Yuki:** Did you get the photo, at least?  
**Haruka:** Yes… a lovely photo of a seagull.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. Enjoy it. (~1.5 min)
Haruka’s habit of photographing every meal finally goes wrong.
Herring gulls really do steal food in seaside towns. Many places have signs asking people not to feed them.

</details>


#### Slide 14: Language focus · If I’d known…

> **If + had + past participle, would have + past participle**
>
> *It didn’t happen*
>
> If I**’d known**, I**’d have bought** champagne. (I didn’t know.)
>
> *A different result*
>
> If we**’d booked** directly, we**’d have had** breakfast.
>
> *Negative*
>
> If I **hadn’t stopped**, I **wouldn’t have lost** them.

**From the story**
- Tom: If I**’d known**, I**’d have bought** champagne.
- Tom: If we**’d booked** directly, we**’d have had** breakfast included!
- Haruka: If I **hadn’t stopped** for a photo, I **wouldn’t have lost** them!

Use the third conditional to imagine a different past.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Third conditional: an imaginary past. It didn’t happen, and we imagine a different result. If + past perfect, would have + past participle. Often used for regrets and jokes. (~2 min)
Mixed conditionals (If I’d booked directly, I’d be happy now) are for recognition only at this level.

</details>


#### Slide 15: Practice · If only…

- Haruka stopped for a photo. A seagull took her chips.
- They booked on a website. They lost their room.
- Gwen didn’t check the trains. They were two hours late.
- Haruka asked Yuki in front of Priya. Yuki was upset.
- Your turn: something in your life that could have been different.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. If she hadn’t stopped, the seagull wouldn’t have taken her chips.
> 2. If they’d booked directly, they wouldn’t have lost their room.
> 3. If Gwen had checked the trains, they wouldn’t have been late.
> 4. If Haruka hadn’t asked in front of Priya, Yuki wouldn’t have been so upset.
> 5. Student’s own answer

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student makes a third conditional for each. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 16: Pronunciation · I’d have…

> **How it sounds**
>
> If I’d known → if-ide-**NOHN**
> I’d have bought it → ide-uv-**BAWT** it
> wouldn’t have → **WUD**-nt-uv

**Say it with your teacher**
- If I’d **known**, I’d have **come**.
- I wouldn’t have **lost** them.
- If we’d **booked** it, we’d have been **fine**.
- If I **were** you, I’d **go**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

In the third conditional, had and have are both very weak and short: I’d’ve. Students often can’t hear them, so they confuse the second and third conditional. Say each line; the student repeats. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 17: Role play · Making a complaint

> **Student**
>
> You’re in a hotel in Brighton. You paid for a sea view.
>
> • Your room looks onto the car park, and the shower is cold.
> • Explain politely and say why it isn’t good enough.
> • Ask what they can do.

**Useful phrases**
- I’m afraid there’s a problem with…
- Sorry, but that isn’t good enough.
- If you were me, would you be happy?
- What can you do about it?
- I’d like to speak to the manager.
- That sounds fair. Thank you.

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re the hotel manager.
>
> • Apologise. First offer: free breakfast.
> • Excuse: “We’re fully booked tonight.”
> • If the student stays calm and firm: a sea-view room tomorrow and 20% off.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The problem and your offers are on the cards. The student complains; no swap needed. (~2.5 min)
Listen for “I’m afraid…”, second conditionals (If you were me, would you…?) and calm, firm language.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Reading · Beside the seaside

[image: Photo idea: deckchairs on a pebble beach]

In 1871, a law created the UK’s first **bank holidays**. For many working people, it meant a free day for the first time, and new railways could take them to the sea for a cheap day out.

Seaside towns like Brighton and Blackpool grew fast. They built long **piers** with theatres, cafés and games. Brighton Palace Pier opened in 1899, and it’s still there today.

A British seaside day often means fish and chips, ice cream, the arcades and a walk along the pier, even when it’s windy. And Brighton’s beach isn’t sand: it’s **pebbles**!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: Who created bank holidays? Why did seaside trips become popular? When did Brighton Palace Pier open?
Sources: Bank Holidays Act 1871 (Sir John Lubbock); History of Parliament on Victorian MPs and holidays; Brighton Palace Pier history (opened 20 May 1899).

</details>


#### Slide 19: Reading · True or false?

- The first bank holidays started in 1871.
- Railways made seaside trips cheaper and easier.
- Brighton Palace Pier opened in 1999.
- Brighton has a sandy beach.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. True.  2. True.  3. False: in 1899.  4. False: it’s pebbles.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 20: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a beach in Japan]

- How are public holidays in Japan different from UK bank holidays?
- Is it easy to complain in Japan? How do people do it?
- If you had a long weekend in the UK, where would you go?
- Where do people in Japan go for a day by the sea?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Some may compare bank holidays with Golden Week. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Story · Big news

Monday breakfast. Yuki, Haruka and Tom.

**Yuki:** Haruka, look at this! MCM Comic Con has announced its guests.  
**Haruka:** And?  
**Yuki:** “Special guest: the artist behind Milk Second. Live on stage!”  
**Tom:** No way! So we’ll finally find out who it is.  
**Haruka:** We have to go. Yuki, you’ll be there anyway with your table.  
**Yuki:** If you come, you can help me sell my prints!  
**Tom:** Count me in. I’ll even be on time.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
MCM Comic Con London is usually on the last weekend of May at ExCeL London.
Hook: next lesson is Comic Con. Ask: Who do you think will walk on stage?

</details>


#### Slide 22: Mystery · Who will it be?

> **Predict**
>
> Who will walk on stage?
>
> It’ll be…
> It’s going to be…
> It might be…
> It won’t be…, because…

**Still possible?**
- Tom
- Callum
- Someone in Japan?
- Someone else?

> **Remember**
>
> • Private photos
> • 3 a.m. UK time
> • American English
> • Doesn’t get banter
> • Saw the card opened

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Make predictions together with will, going to, might and won’t. Ask the student to give reasons from the clues. Write down their answer to check in Lesson 11. (~1.5 min)
Teacher only: it’s Haruka’s mum. Don’t say anything, even if the student guesses.

</details>


#### Slide 23: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- complained politely but firmly
- talked about imaginary situations with If I were you…
- imagined a different past with If I’d known…
- learned about the British seaside and bank holidays

[image: Photo idea: chips on a pier with a seagull watching]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

For each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 11: Comic Con

**Objective:** Students can say how long something had been happening using the past perfect continuous, express strong feelings, regrets and wishes, and deal with practical situations at a big event (CEFR B1+/B2: convey degrees of emotion; highlight the personal significance of events).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 4 · Milk Second · Lesson 11

**Comic Con**

Today’s goal: talk about how long something had been happening, and express strong feelings and wishes.

[image: Photo idea: a busy comic convention hall]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Late May. MCM Comic Con London: Yuki has a table, and the Milk Second artist is on the main stage. This is the reveal. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: in Brighton, MCM announced its special guest: the artist behind Milk Second (Lesson 10). The decisive clue was the Mothering Sunday card, drawn as if by the person who opened it (Lesson 9).

Main points: the past perfect continuous and wish. Practical topic: big events (tickets, bag checks, finding people). Culture: Comic Con and manga in the UK.

Timing: 36 slides, about 48 minutes with everything. This is the big story lesson, so it can take two sessions: split it after the flashbacks and the check. For one session, skip If only…, True or false, the first Did you understand? and the reading (saves about 6 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Big events

Talk with your teacher.

- What’s the biggest event you’ve ever been to?
- Have you ever had a big surprise? What happened?
- Do you read manga or comics? Which ones?

[image: Photo idea: a queue outside an exhibition centre]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal and lively. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: The queue outside ExCeL London on Comic Con morning. Haruka and Tom with cosplayers around them. The queue moves. A cosplayer waves; Haruka laughs and takes a photo; Tom checks the time and points at the doors.]

- Where are Haruka and Tom? What can you see?
- Why do you think so many people are here?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · At the entrance

Saturday morning at ExCeL. A steward checks Tom and Haruka in.
