**Steward:** Morning! Can I see your tickets, please?  
**Tom:** They’re on my phone. Here you go.  
**Steward:** Lovely. Any bags? I need to check them before you go in.  
**Haruka:** Just this one. Is it OK to bring a water bottle?  
**Steward:** Yes, that’s fine. Here are your badges. Wear them all weekend.  
**Tom:** What time is the Milk Second talk?  
**Steward:** Main stage, two o’clock. Get there early. It’s going to be packed.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Typical for big UK events: e-tickets on your phone, bag checks at the door, and a badge or wristband to show you’ve paid.
MCM Comic Con London takes place at ExCeL London, usually twice a year (late May and late October).

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- Where are Tom’s tickets?
- What does the steward need to check?
- What do they need to wear?
- When and where is the Milk Second talk?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. On his phone.  2. Their bags.  3. Their badges, all weekend.  4. On the main stage at two o’clock.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · At the event (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a steward | noun | （イベントの）係員 | a person who helps and checks people at an event | Ask the **steward** where the main stage is. |
| a badge | noun | 入場証、バッジ | a card you wear to show you’ve got a ticket | Wear your **badge** all weekend. |
| packed | adjective | ぎゅうぎゅう詰めの | very full of people | The main stage is going to be **packed**. |
| a print | noun | （イラストの）プリント | a copy of a picture, to buy and put on your wall | Yuki is selling **prints** of her drawings. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the dialogues. (~1.5 min)
“Packed” is very common in British English: The train was packed!

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Big feelings (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to recognise | verb | （見て・聞いて）わかる | to know someone or something because you’ve seen or heard them before | I **recognise** that voice! |
| to keep (something) secret | phrase | 秘密にする | not to tell anyone | She **kept** it **secret** for months. |
| proud | adjective | 誇りに思う | very happy about what someone has done | I’m really **proud** of you. |
| homework | noun | 宿題 | work a teacher gives you to do at home | It started as English **homework**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come in the next dialogues. (~1.5 min)
British spelling: recognise. American spelling: recognize.

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · Yuki’s table

Late morning, in the Artist Alley. Yuki, Haruka and Tom.

**Yuki:** Look! I’d been drawing these **prints** for months, and half of them have gone already!  
**Haruka:** That’s brilliant, Yuki! I’m going to call Mum. I wish she could see this.  
**Tom:** Is she answering?  
**Haruka:** No. That’s strange. Oh, maybe she’s on that class trip.  
**Tom:** She might be. Come on, the talk starts at two!  
**Yuki:** Go! Tell me everything. I wish I could come with you.  
**Haruka:** I’ll take lots of photos. Obviously.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Teacher only: Mum doesn’t answer because she’s in the same building. Her “class trip” (Lesson 9) is to London. Don’t say anything.
“Obviously” is Haruka teasing herself about her photo habit.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Listening · The main stage

Two o’clock, at the main stage. The host speaks to the crowd.

[audio: Host’s announcement]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Ladies and gentlemen, it’s been a mystery for months. Please welcome to the main stage… the artist behind Milk Second!

- Where is the announcement?
- How long has the artist been a mystery?
- Who is the host introducing?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. On the main stage.
> 2. For months.
> 3. The artist behind Milk Second.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Ladies and gentlemen, it’s been a mystery for months. Please welcome to the main stage… the artist behind Milk Second!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~2 min)
Pause after the clip. Ask: Who do you think will walk on stage? Check the prediction from Lesson 10.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Story · The masked artist

The main stage. The host talks to the artist. Haruka and Tom watch.

**Host:** Welcome! So, you’re wearing a mask today. Why?  
**Artist:** Because the person in my comic doesn’t know who I am. Not yet.  
**Tom:** Hear that accent? She’s Japanese.  
**Haruka:** Lots of people are Japanese, Tom.  
**Host:** So how did Milk Second start?  
**Artist:** My English teacher asked us to keep a diary in English. I’d been drawing manga since I was a girl, so… I drew it.  
**Haruka:** A diary in English? …My mum has to do that.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, slowly. Build the tension. (~2 min)
The artist wears a mask, so nobody can see who it is. Haruka starts to wonder: Mum has to keep an English diary (Lesson 1).
Ask after each line: Who do you think it is now?

</details>


#### Slide 11: Story · A question from the crowd

A few minutes later. The host, the artist, Tom and Haruka.

**Tom:** Lots of people take English classes, Haruka.  
**Host:** Let’s take a question from the audience. Yes, you at the front!  
**Tom:** Hi! Is Hana a real person?  
**Artist:** Yes. She moved to London on her own, with a very big suitcase.  
**Tom:** Haruka… she keeps looking at you.  
**Host:** One last question. Will you take off your mask for us?  
**Artist:** …OK. Hana, I hope you’re not angry.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Tom’s first line mirrors Haruka’s “Lots of people are Japanese”: now he’s the one saying it isn’t her mum.
Stop here and ask: Who will it be? Then play the video on the next slide.

</details>


#### Slide 12: Story · The reveal

[video clip: The main stage. The masked artist takes off the cat mask: it’s Haruka’s mum. Haruka and Tom in the crowd. Mum lifts off the mask and smiles nervously. Haruka freezes and covers her mouth with both hands. Tom turns to her, shocked.]

- Who is the woman behind the mask?
- Describe Haruka’s face. How does she feel?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Play the video. Don’t say anything first: let the student watch and react. Then ask the questions. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe it slowly, like a story.

</details>


#### Slide 13: Story · Mum?!

On stage and in the crowd. Haruka, Tom, Mum and the host.

**Haruka:** I **recognise** that smile… Mum?!  
**Tom:** Your MUM? You’re joking!  
**Mum:** Hello, Haruka. Surprise!  
**Host:** Wait, Hana is here? Come up, come up!  
**Haruka:** Mum, what… how… You’re in London?!  
**Mum:** My English class came on a trip. I wanted to tell you in person.  
**Host:** Ladies and gentlemen… the real Hana!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, with feeling. (~2 min)
Ask: Did you guess? When did you guess? Check the prediction from Lesson 10.
Mum’s “trip with friends” was her English class coming to London.

</details>


#### Slide 14: Story · I wish you’d told me

Backstage after the talk. Haruka, Mum and Tom.

**Haruka:** Mum! Why didn’t you tell me? I wish you’d told me!  
**Mum:** I wanted to. But it was my English **homework**, and I wanted to **keep** it **secret** until today.  
**Haruka:** We’d been looking for the artist since October!  
**Mum:** Sorry, Tom. I drew you as a mean person. Haruka says it’s just banter.  
**Tom:** It is banter. But I don't mind.  
**Haruka:** But I didn’t even know you could draw!  
**Mum:** I stopped when you were born. You never saw my old drawings.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
They speak English because Tom is there, and Mum wants to use her English.
Mum understands banter now. “I’ll take it” = I accept that (with a smile).
This explains why Haruka never suspected her mum, even though she knew about the English diary.
Next: five questions. Each answer is followed by a flashback to a scene the student has already seen. Ask each time: Did you notice this clue?

</details>


#### Slide 15: The answers · Your homework

Backstage. Haruka asks Mum.

**Haruka:** So Milk Second was your English homework?  
**Mum:** Yes! My diary for the whole year. Remember? You were my homework!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~0.5 min)
The flashback on the next slide shows where this clue was.

</details>


#### Slide 16: Flashback · Lesson 1: Mum’s news (flashback, black and white)

A moment later. Gwen, Tom and Haruka.

**Gwen:** And how’s your mum? Still worrying about you?  
**Haruka:** Always! But she’s busy now. She’s started an English class.  
**Tom:** Good for her!  
**Haruka:** Her teacher says she has to keep a diary in English all year. So she asks me a hundred questions about my day!  
**Gwen:** So you’re her homework now!  
**Haruka:** Basically, yes.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Flashback. Read it again quickly. (~1 min)
Mum’s English class, the diary, and Gwen’s joke: “So you’re her homework now!”

</details>


#### Slide 17: The answers · The name

Backstage. Tom asks Mum.

**Tom:** But why call it Milk Second?  
**Mum:** Haruka told me about Gwen’s tea rules in her very first week. I never forgot them.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~0.5 min)
The flashback on the next slide shows where this clue was.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Flashback · Beginner Lesson 2: Tea rules (flashback, black and white)

A moment later. Haruka and her mum.

**Haruka:** Oh, and Gwen has very serious tea rules. Tea first, milk second. Never milk first!  
**Mum:** Oh, really? Gwen sounds fun. So, do you miss home?  
**Haruka:** A bit. But I’m OK! On Friday I’m going to the pub with Tom.  
**Mum:** The pub? Hmm… OK. But call me every week, and tell me everything!  
**Haruka:** Every week. I promise.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Flashback. Read it again quickly. (~1 min)
Haruka’s first week in London: she tells Mum about Gwen’s tea rules.

</details>


#### Slide 19: The answers · 3 a.m.

Backstage. Haruka asks Mum.

**Haruka:** And the 3 a.m. posts? Everyone thought it was Yuki!  
**Mum:** 3 a.m. in London is lunchtime in Osaka. I posted every episode with my lunch!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~0.5 min)
The flashback on the next slide shows where this clue was.

</details>


#### Slide 20: Flashback · Lesson 4: Gone viral (flashback, black and white)

The last day before the Christmas break. Priya, Haruka and Yuki.

**Priya:** Have you seen? Milk Second has gone viral. Two hundred thousand followers!  
**Haruka:** I know. There’s even a landlady in it now, with all of Gwen’s tea rules.  
**Priya:** Does Gwen know?  
**Haruka:** Yes, and she loves it. She’s shown everyone at the corner shop.  
**Priya:** It’s funny: new episodes always go up at 3 a.m. Who’s awake then?  
**Yuki:** Me! I’m always up then. I work for clients in Tokyo.  
**Haruka:** …Really?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Flashback. Read it again quickly. (~1 min)
The 3 a.m. clue, and why Yuki looked guilty.

</details>


#### Slide 21: The answers · Pants!

Backstage. Tom and Mum.

**Tom:** And all the American English?  
**Mum:** That’s what we learned at school. Color, check, pants…  
**Tom:** You know “pants” means underwear here?  
**Mum:** Oh no! I wish someone had told me!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. Enjoy it. (~1 min)
Mum learned American English at school, like Yuki (Lesson 6).

</details>


#### Slide 22: The answers · The fridge

Backstage. Haruka asks Mum.

**Haruka:** And the card on the fridge?  
**Mum:** That’s my fridge! I drew it the day your card arrived.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~0.5 min)
The flashback on the next slide shows where this clue was.

</details>


#### Slide 23: Flashback · Lesson 9: Wish me luck! (flashback, black and white)

Friday the 19th at the office. Yuki, Priya and Haruka.

**Yuki:** Haruka, look! The new Milk Second. Hana sends her mum a card.  
**Priya:** And look at the last picture. The card is on someone’s fridge.  
**Haruka:** Sorry, I can’t look now. My English test is in an hour!  
**Yuki:** Haruka, didn’t you send your mum a card too?  
**Haruka:** Probably. Tell me later! Wish me luck!  
**Priya:** Good luck! … Yuki, whose fridge is that?  
**Yuki:** Hmm…  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Flashback. Read it again quickly. (~1 min)
The decisive clue: the card on Mum’s fridge. Haruka was too busy to look.

</details>


#### Slide 24: The answers · The trip

Backstage. Haruka asks Mum.

**Haruka:** And your class trip in May… it was London!  
**Mum:** Well, I did say somewhere English-speaking!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~0.5 min)
The flashback on the next slide shows where this clue was.

</details>


#### Slide 25: Flashback · Lesson 9: A trip in May (flashback, black and white)

A moment later. Gwen, Mum and Haruka.

**Gwen:** Are you going anywhere nice this spring?  
**Mum:** My English class is planning a trip abroad. In May.  
**Haruka:** Really? Where to?  
**Mum:** Somewhere English-speaking. I’ll tell you later.  
**Gwen:** Ooh, how mysterious!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Flashback. Read it again quickly. (~1 min)
Mum’s class trip. Remember the little plane on the calendar on the fridge?

</details>


#### Slide 26: Story · Did you understand?

- How did Milk Second start?
- Where does the name come from?
- Why did new episodes go up at 3 a.m.?
- Why was Mum in London?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. As Mum’s English homework: a diary for the whole year.  2. Gwen’s tea rules.  3. It’s lunchtime in Osaka.  4. Her English class trip, and the Comic Con talk.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check on the reveal dialogues. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 27: Language focus · She’d been drawing…

> **had been + -ing**
>
> *Up to a time in the past*
>
> She**’d been drawing** manga since she was a girl.
>
> *How long?*
>
> We**’d been looking** for the artist since October.
>
> *Compare*
>
> Now: I**’ve been living** here for two years. · Then: I**’d been living** here for a year when Yuki started.

**From the story**
- Yuki: I**’d been drawing** these prints for months.
- Mum: I**’d been drawing** manga since I was a girl.
- Haruka: We**’d been looking** for the artist since October!

Use the past perfect continuous for something that went on up to a time in the past.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Past perfect continuous: an action that continued for some time up to a point in the past. Had been + -ing. It’s the past version of “I’ve been drawing”. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 28: Practice · How long had they been…?

- Mum / draw / Milk Second / for months / when she went on stage
- Haruka / live / in London / for a year / when Yuki started
- Sian / wait / for ages / when Gwen and Haruka arrived in Cardiff
- Tom / sleep / when Haruka’s mum called
- Your turn: How long had you been learning English when you started this course?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Mum had been drawing Milk Second for months when she went on stage.
> 2. Haruka had been living in London for a year when Yuki started.
> 3. Sian had been waiting for ages when Gwen and Haruka arrived in Cardiff.
> 4. Tom had been sleeping when Haruka’s mum called.
> 5. Student’s own answer

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student makes a sentence with had been + -ing for each. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 29: Language focus · I wish…

> **Wishes**
>
> *Now (it isn’t true)*
>
> I **wish** she **could** see this. · I **wish** I **had** more time.
>
> *The past (it didn’t happen)*
>
> I **wish** you**’d told** me! · I **wish** someone **had told** me!
>
> *The future? Use hope*
>
> I **hope** you’ll come. (not “I wish you will come”)

**From the story**
- Haruka: I **wish** she **could** see this.
- Haruka: I **wish** you**’d told** me!
- Mum: I **wish** someone **had told** me!

Use wish for things you want to be different, now or in the past.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Wish + past simple / could = I want the present to be different. Wish + past perfect = a regret about the past. Japanese learners often say “I wish you will…”. Use “I hope” for real future wishes: I hope you’ll come. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 30: Practice · If only…

- Yuki can’t go to the talk. (She wants to.)
- Mum didn’t tell Haruka about the comic.
- Haruka didn’t notice the card clue.
- Tom isn’t good at drawing.
- Your turn: something you wish was different.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Yuki wishes she could go to the talk.
> 2. Haruka wishes Mum had told her.
> 3. Haruka wishes she’d noticed the clue.
> 4. Tom wishes he could draw / was good at drawing.
> 5. Student’s own answer

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student makes a wish for each situation. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 31: Pronunciation · It was YOU?

> **Stress the feeling**
>
> It was **YOU**? (surprised)
> Your **MUM**?! (shocked)
> I wish you’d **TOLD** me! (upset)
> I’m so **PROUD** of you. (happy)

**Say it with your teacher**
- It was **you**?
- Your **mum**?!
- I wish you’d **told** me!
- I’m so **proud** of you.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

When we feel strongly, we put extra stress on the most important word, and the voice goes up and down more. Say each line with feeling; the student copies. Then the student says one, and you guess the feeling. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 32: Reading · Comic Con

[image: Photo idea: cosplayers at Comic Con]

MCM Comic Con London happens twice a year at ExCeL, a huge exhibition centre in East London. Thousands of fans come for comics, games, films and anime.

Many people come in **cosplay**: costumes of their favourite characters. Some spend months making them.

In the **Artist Alley**, artists like Yuki sell their own prints and comics. They apply with a portfolio months before.

Manga is very popular in the UK now. In 2024, manga made up more than half of the money spent on graphic novels in UK bookshops.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: Where and how often is MCM Comic Con London? What is cosplay? How popular is manga in the UK?
Sources: MCM Comic Con London (ExCeL London, twice a year since 2002); The Bookseller on UK graphic novel sales (2024: manga was over half of graphic novel sales); MCM Artist Alley guidance (artists apply with a portfolio).

</details>


#### Slide 33: Reading · True or false?

- MCM Comic Con London happens once a year.
- Cosplay means wearing a character’s costume.
- Anyone can get a table in the Artist Alley on the day.
- Manga is very popular in the UK.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: twice a year.  2. True.  3. False: artists apply months before.  4. True.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 34: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a mother and daughter hugging at an event]

- Did you guess the artist? When? Which clue helped?
- How would you feel if your mum drew a comic about you?
- Are events like Comic Con popular in Japan? Have you been to one?
- Is it ever too late to start learning a language? Why?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Some may mention Comiket. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 35: Story · Proud

That evening, in a café near ExCeL. Tom, Mum and Haruka.

**Tom:** So how long are you in London?  
**Mum:** One week. My English class is here too.  
**Haruka:** Then you have to meet Gwen. She’ll put the kettle on.  
**Mum:** Tea first, milk second. I know.  
**Tom:** Wait till Gwen finds out. She talked to the artist on a video call and didn’t even know!  
**Haruka:** Mum… I’m really **proud** of you.  
**Mum:** And I’m proud of you. Now, smile! This photo is for the next episode.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Mum’s last line turns the joke around: this time Mum takes the photo, and Milk Second isn’t finished.
Tom means the video call in Lesson 9, when Gwen met Mum.
Hook: next lesson, Mum meets Gwen, and Haruka waits for her visa decision.

</details>


#### Slide 36: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- talked about how long something had been happening
- expressed strong feelings and wishes
- dealt with tickets, bag checks and finding people at an event
- learned about Comic Con and manga in the UK

[image: Photo idea: a badge, prints and tea]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

For each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 12: Milk first?

**Objective:** Students can describe people, places and things in detail with relative clauses, talk about what will have happened by a future time, and give a short speech (CEFR B1+/B2: sustained monologue; highlight the personal significance of events).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 4 · Milk Second · Lesson 12

**Milk first?**

Today’s goal: describe people, places and things in more detail, talk about the future, and give a short speech.

[image: Photo idea: tea in a London garden]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

June. Mum’s week in London: sightseeing, tea at Gwen’s, Haruka’s visa decision and a goodbye party. The last lesson of the course, with a final check. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: at Comic Con, the Milk Second artist took off her mask. It was Haruka’s mum (Lesson 11). She’s in London for a week with her English class.

Main points: relative clauses (who, which, that, where) and the future perfect. Practical topics: hosting a visitor in London, and giving a short speech.

Timing: 21 slides, about 38 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip By this time next year… and True or false (saves about 3 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Looking back

Talk with your teacher.

- If a friend visited your town for a week, where would you take them?
- What’s the most intersting thing you’ve learned in this course?
- Where do you think you’ll be in a year?

[image: Photo idea: a London bus on a bridge]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal and positive. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: Gwen’s kitchen. Mum, Gwen, Haruka and Tom at tea time. Mum moves the milk jug towards an empty mug. Gwen gasps and covers the mug with her hand. Everyone laughs.]

- What does Mum do? How does Gwen react?
- Why do you think everyone laughs?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe what happens, then ask the questions. Watch again to check. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · On the bus

Tuesday afternoon. Haruka and Tom take Mum to meet Gwen.

**Mum:** Two tickets, please. I have cash.  
**Tom:** Sorry, London buses don’t take cash. Just tap your card here.  
**Mum:** My ICOCA card?  
**Haruka:** No, that only works in Japan. Use a bank card that has the contactless symbol.  
**Mum:** OK! In Osaka I still pay with cash. I’m a bit nervous about meeting Gwen.  
**Tom:** Don’t be. She’s the kind of person who makes everyone feel at home.  
**Mum:** Then I’ll be on my best behaviour.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Mum), then swap. (~2 min)
True: London buses haven’t taken cash since 2014. You tap a contactless bank card or phone, and if you use the same card all day, there’s a daily cap on what you pay. Many foreign cards work, but some banks charge a fee.
In Osaka, buses take cash, IC cards like ICOCA, and (since November 2025 on Osaka City Bus) contactless bank cards too. Mum just likes cash. Japanese IC cards don’t work in London.
“I’ll be on my best behaviour” = I’ll be very polite. Watch what she does next…

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- Why can’t Mum pay with cash?
- Can she use her ICOCA card?
- What kind of card does she need?
- How does Mum feel about meeting Gwen? What does Tom say?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. London buses don’t take cash.  2. No, it only works in Japan.  3. A bank card with the contactless symbol.  4. A bit nervous. Tom says Gwen makes everyone feel at home.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · Getting around (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| contactless | adjective | 非接触決済の | you pay by touching your card or phone on a reader | Is your card **contactless**? |
| to tap | verb | タッチする | to touch something quickly and lightly | **Tap** your card here. |
| a souvenir | noun | お土産 | something you buy to remember a place | I’ll buy a **souvenir** in the shop. |
| free | adjective | 無料の | it doesn’t cost any money | The museum is **free**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words are useful for any visitor to London. (~1.5 min)
Katakana false friend: お土産 for friends is usually “a present” in English. A souvenir is often something you keep for yourself.

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Big news (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| approved | adjective | 承認された | officially accepted | Your visa’s been **approved**! |
| to lead | verb | 率いる | to be in charge of a group of people | You’ll **lead** the new team. |
| a publisher | noun | 出版社 | a company that makes and sells books | An email from a **publisher**! |
| to turn (something) into | phrase | 〜を…にする | to change something into something else | They want to **turn** the comic **into** a book. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come later in the lesson. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · Milk first?

Ten minutes later, at Gwen’s. Gwen, Mum, Tom and Haruka.

**Gwen:** So you’re the famous artist! Welcome. Tea?  
**Mum:** Yes, please. Shall I put the milk in first?  
**Gwen:** Don’t you dare!  
**Mum:** Only joking. Tea first, milk second. I named a whole comic after it!  
**Tom:** Careful, Gwen. She can do banter now.  
**Gwen:** I can see that! Haruka, your mum is the only person who’s ever made me laugh about my tea rules.  
**Haruka:** That’s a first!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
The pay-off of the whole course: Gwen’s tea rule, Mum’s comic, and Mum teasing Gwen like a British friend.
“Don’t you dare!” is a strong but playful way to say “Don’t do that!”

</details>


#### Slide 9: Listening · A message from Priya

Wednesday morning. Haruka gets a voice message.

[audio: Priya’s voice message]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Haruka, it’s Priya. Call me back as soon as you get this. It’s about your visa. Don’t worry, it’s good news!

- Who is calling?
- What is the message about?
- Should Haruka worry? Why not?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Priya.
> 2. Her visa.
> 3. No: Priya says it’s good news.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Haruka, it’s Priya. Call me back as soon as you get this. It’s about your visa. Don’t worry, it’s good news!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~2 min)
Skilled Worker decisions usually take about eight weeks. Haruka applied in the spring.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Language focus · The kind of person who…

> **who, which, that, where**
>
> *People: who / that*
>
> She’s the only person **who**’s ever made me laugh.
>
> *Things: which / that*
>
> It’s a card **that** has the contactless symbol.
>
> *Places: where* · *Extra information: commas*
>
> That’s the café **where** we met. · Gwen, **who** loves tea, lives downstairs.

**From the story**
- Haruka: Use a bank card **that** has the contactless symbol.
- Tom: She’s the kind of person **who** makes everyone feel at home.
- Gwen: She’s the only person **who**’s ever made me laugh.

Use relative clauses to say more about people, things and places.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Defining relative clauses tell us which one (no commas). Non-defining clauses add extra information (with commas, and never “that”). Japanese learners often repeat the subject: “the museum that it has…”. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 11: Practice · Who is it?

- Gwen
- Greggs
- Tom
- Brighton
- Yuki
- Your turn: a person or place from your life.

> **Example answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. She’s the landlady who has strict tea rules.
> 2. It’s the bakery that’s famous for sausage rolls.
> 3. He’s the flatmate who’s always late.
> 4. It’s the town where a seagull stole Haruka’s chips.
> 5. She’s the illustrator who everyone thought was the artist.
> 6. Student’s own answer

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student describes each person or place with a relative clause, without saying the name. The teacher guesses. Then swap. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 12: Story · Approved!

Wednesday at the office. Priya, Haruka and Yuki.

**Priya:** Haruka! Did you get my message? Your visa’s been **approved**!  
**Haruka:** Really? So I can stay? That’s amazing!  
**Yuki:** Congratulations! By next year, you’ll have forgotten all your Japanese!  
**Priya:** And there’s more. From September, you’ll **lead** the new design team.  
**Haruka:** Me? Lead a team?  
**Priya:** You. And your first new designer is coming from Sydney. By September, he’ll have moved here.  
**Haruka:** And by then, I’ll have been in London for two whole years!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Yuki’s line is friendly teasing: Haruka won’t really forget her Japanese.
Hook for the next course: Haruka gets a promotion, and a new colleague is coming from Australia.

</details>


#### Slide 13: Language focus · I’ll have been here…

> **will have + past participle**
>
> *Done by a future time*
>
> By September, he**’ll have moved** here.
>
> *How long, by then*
>
> By then, I**’ll have been** in London for two years.
>
> *Negative*
>
> I **won’t have finished** by Friday.

**From the story**
- Yuki: By next year, you**’ll have forgotten** all your Japanese!
- Priya: By September, he**’ll have moved** here.
- Haruka: By then, I**’ll have been** in London for two whole years!

Use the future perfect for things that will be complete by a future time.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Future perfect: will have + past participle, for something that will be finished or true by a time in the future. Often used with “by”. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 14: Practice · By this time next year…

- By the end of this year, I’ll have…
- By the time I’m 60, I’ll have…
- By next summer, I won’t have…
- By the end of next year, my English will have…

> **Example answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. …finished this course.
> 2. …visited the UK.
> 3. …forgotten all this English!
> 4. …got much better.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student answers with the future perfect. Push for true, personal answers. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Pronunciation · Giving a speech

> **Pause, then stress**
>
> Almost two years ago | I **arrived** | with a **huge** suitcase.
> Thank you, **everyone**, | for **everything**.

**Say it with your teacher**
- I’d like to say | a few **words**.
- Two years ago | I didn’t know | **anyone**.
- Now | I have **friends** | who **feel** like **family**.
- So please, | raise your **glasses**!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

In a short speech, pause between chunks of meaning and slow down a little. Stress the important words. The | marks a short pause. Read each line slowly; the student repeats, then reads the whole speech on the next dialogue slide. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 16: Role play · Your speech

> **Student**
>
> You’re Haruka at the pub. Give a one-minute thank-you speech.
>
> • Say why everyone is here.
> • Thank three people with who: “Gwen, who…”
> • End with a toast.

**Useful phrases**
- I’d like to say a few words.
- I want to thank…
- …, who…
- By this time next year, …
- So please, raise your glasses!
- Cheers!

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re a guest at the pub.
>
> • Listen and react (laugh, clap!).
> • Ask one question afterwards.
> • Say one thing you liked.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student gives Haruka’s speech in their own words, using people from the story (Gwen, Tom, Yuki, Priya, Mum), so there’s nothing to invent. Haruka’s speech in the dialogue was very short; the student makes it longer. Then give feedback on pausing and stress. (~3 min)

</details>


#### Slide 17: Final check · Course check

> **Student**
>
> Talk about your English this year.
>
> • What have you been doing to improve?
> • What do you wish you’d done differently?
> • Where will you be, and what will you have done, a year from now?

**Course phrases**
- I’ve been…ing
- I wish I’d…
- If I could…, I’d…
- They told me that…
- It must have been…
- By next year, I’ll have…

> **Teacher**
>
> Ask follow-up questions.
>
> • Ask what someone told them that helped.
> • Ask: If you could live abroad, where would you go?
> • Give one piece of advice.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Final check for the whole course. Let the student lead and don’t correct during the conversation. Note what they do well and one thing to practise. (~3 min)
Look for: present perfect continuous, deduction (must have / can’t have), reported speech, conditionals, wish, relative clauses and the future perfect.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Story · A few words

Mum’s last night, at the pub. Haruka, Gwen, Mum, Yuki and Tom.

**Tom:** Speech! Speech!  
**Haruka:** OK, OK. Almost two years ago, I arrived with a huge suitcase and no idea what “Alright?” meant. Thank you, everyone, and Mum, who drew it all.  
**Gwen:** Oh, love. You’ll make me cry.  
**Mum:** Yuki, I’m sorry I made you the main suspect.  
**Yuki:** Don’t be! Your comic was the best part of my first year.  
**Tom:** To Haruka, who’s staying… and to Milk Second!  
**Gwen:** Cheers!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. Let the student read Haruka’s speech slowly, with pauses. (~2 min)
Callbacks: the huge suitcase and “Alright?” from the beginner course, and Yuki as the suspect (Lesson 8).
Mum meets Yuki properly here. She knows about the suspect story from Haruka.

</details>


#### Slide 19: Story · One more thing

Saturday morning, before Mum’s flight. Haruka, Mum and Tom.

**Haruka:** Mum, look at this email. It’s from a publisher in London.  
**Mum:** “We’d love to turn Milk Second into a book.” A book?!  
**Tom:** You’re going to be famous. Again.  
**Mum:** Then I’ll need more episodes. Haruka, what are you doing next year?  
**Haruka:** Leading a team, meeting new people… and probably getting into trouble.  
**Mum:** Perfect. Keep sending me photos.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
The end of the course story, and a hook for the next course: Haruka’s new team, and more Milk Second.

</details>


#### Slide 20: What’s next · To be continued…

> **Haruka’s third year**
>
> Haruka is staying in London, and from September she’ll lead a new design team. There’s a new colleague from Sydney, business trips, meetings and presentations… and Mum is still drawing.
>
> Haruka’s story continues in the advanced course.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Tell the student the story continues at B2–C1, if they’d like to carry on. (~0.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Wrap-up · Course check

**In this course, I can…**
- deal with real life in the UK: work, flats, doctors, travel and paperwork
- make guesses, talk about regrets and imagine different situations
- tell stories and report what people said
- describe people, places and plans in detail

[image: Photo idea: a comic book and a mug of tea]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Self-check for the whole course: for each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Congratulate the student on finishing the course! (~1.5 min)

</details>



---

## Great Britain: Advanced (B2→C1)

Haruka’s third year in London. She leads a design team pitching for a famous British tea company. Learn advanced English for meetings, feedback, business trips, presentations and negotiation, plus life admin, with British culture, banter and a puzzle in the archive.

Characters: Haruka, Gwen, Tom, Priya, Yuki, Josh, Mum, Mrs Hartley, Kenta, Callum, Sian, Amy, Ms Kato, Ruth, Mr Bell, Daichi


## Unit: New team


### Lesson 1: The new guy

**Objective:** Students can chair a short meeting, interrupt and manage turns politely, and soften disagreement and feedback (CEFR B2: manage interaction in discussions; express disagreement diplomatically).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 1 · New team · Lesson 1

**The new guy**

Today’s goal: run a meeting, and disagree or give feedback politely.

[image: Photo idea: a London design studio meeting room]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

September, the start of Haruka’s third year in London. She’s now the design team lead, and a new designer, Josh, has just arrived from Sydney. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Level: B2, moving towards C1. Main points today: softening and hedging, and the language of meetings. Practical topic: running a meeting.

Haruka and Yuki speak more naturally now, with British phrases, but they’re the same people. Josh is direct because of who he is, not where he’s from: don’t let students generalise about Australians.

Timing: 24 slides, about 41 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip The story so far (if the student did the first two courses), True or false and Useful phrases (saves about 4.5 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Meetings

Talk with your teacher.

- What makes a meeting good, or terrible?
- Do you prefer people who say exactly what they think? Why?
- Have you ever had to lead a group? How did it go?

[image: Photo idea: colleagues in a meeting]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. Push for examples. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Story · The story so far

> **Two years in London**
>
> In her first year, Haruka came to London from Osaka. She lived in Gwen’s house with Tom, worked with Priya, and did everything on her UK list.
>
> In her second year, a web comic called Milk Second told the story of her life. The artist turned out to be her mum! Haruka’s company sponsored her visa, so she could stay.
>
> Now it’s September again. Haruka is the new design team lead… and someone new has just joined her team.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

For students who didn’t do the first two courses, this is all they need. For students who did, ask: What do you remember? (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Meet the characters

> **Haruka · Osaka**
>
> Year three, and now
> the design team lead.
> Careful, kind, and still
> photographs every meal.

> **Josh · Sydney (new!)**
>
> A talented designer
> who has just moved
> to London. He always
> says what he thinks.

> **Yuki · Tokyo**
>
> A year in London now.
> More confident, more
> direct, and still
> drawing every day.

> **Priya · Birmingham**
>
> Haruka’s manager now.
> Organised, supportive,
> and very good at
> office politics.

[image: Haruka]

[image: Josh]

[image: Yuki]

[image: Priya]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the profiles. Ask: Who do you think will clash? Why? (~1.5 min)
Gwen and Tom are back too: they appear later in the lesson.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: A meeting room. Josh criticises a design on the screen. Yuki is upset; Haruka, at the head of the table, calms things down. Josh points at the screen and shakes his head. Yuki crosses her arms. Haruka raises a hand calmly, and Josh sits down.]

- What do you think Josh is saying about the design?
- Look at Yuki and Haruka. How does each one feel? How do you know?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe the body language before anything else. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 6: Story · Big day

Monday morning at the office. Priya and Haruka.

**Priya:** Big day! Your first meeting as team lead. Nervous?  
**Haruka:** A bit. To be honest, I’m not sure I’m quite ready.  
**Priya:** You are. Keep it short, stick to the agenda, and make sure everyone gets a chance to speak.  
**Haruka:** Even Josh? He’s only been here a week, and he already has opinions about everything.  
**Priya:** Especially Josh. He tells you exactly what he thinks. That can be useful.  
**Haruka:** It can also be… a lot.  
**Priya:** Welcome to management!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together (student = Haruka), then swap. (~2 min)
“A lot” on its own (It can be a lot) means too much, hard to deal with. Very common in British English.
Notice how Haruka softens what she says (“I’m not sure I’m quite ready”). The language focus comes later.

</details>


#### Slide 7: Story · Did you understand?

- Why is today a big day for Haruka?
- What is Priya’s advice?
- What is Josh like?
- How does Haruka feel about that?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. It’s her first meeting as team lead.  2. Keep it short, stick to the agenda, let everyone speak.  3. He says exactly what he thinks.  4. It can be too much (“a lot”).

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 8: Vocabulary · Meetings (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| an agenda | noun | 議題、アジェンダ | a list of things to discuss in a meeting | Let’s stick to the **agenda**. |
| to chair (a meeting) | verb | （会議の）議長を務める | to lead a meeting | Haruka is **chairing** the meeting. |
| an action point | noun | 決定事項、やること | a task someone agrees to do after a meeting | Let’s finish with the **action points**. |
| an item | noun | 項目 | one thing on a list or agenda | Next **item**: the Hartley’s brief. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the dialogues. (~1.5 min)
British meetings often end with “AOB” (any other business), a chance to mention anything not on the agenda.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Vocabulary · People at work (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| blunt | adjective | 無遠慮な、率直すぎる | saying exactly what you think, without being polite | He’s a bit **blunt** sometimes. |
| tactful | adjective | 気配りのある | careful not to upset people | Try to be more **tactful** in meetings. |
| to come across (as) | phrasal verb | 〜という印象を与える | to seem to other people | He **comes across as** rude, but he’s kind. |
| to take (something) personally | phrase | 個人攻撃と受け取る | to feel that criticism is about you as a person | Don’t **take it personally**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come later in the lesson. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 10: Story · The meeting

Ten o’clock. Haruka chairs the meeting. Josh and Yuki.

**Haruka:** Right, first **item**: Yuki’s new design for the bakery client. Yuki?  
**Yuki:** So, this is the latest version. I’ve changed the colours since last week.  
**Josh:** Honestly? It doesn’t work. The logo’s too small, and those colours are boring.  
**Yuki:** Boring? I’d say they’re slightly more… calm.  
**Haruka:** Thanks, Josh. I wonder if we could hear a bit more from Yuki first?  
**Josh:** Sure. But it still doesn’t work.  
**Haruka:** OK. Let’s put a pin in that and come back to it after lunch.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Josh is blunt. Yuki pushes back (a year ago she wouldn’t have). Haruka stays calm and uses very soft language.
“Let’s put a pin in that” = let’s stop discussing this for now and come back to it later. Common in British offices.
Ask: How would you feel if you were Yuki? Was Josh wrong to say it?

</details>


#### Slide 11: Culture · Direct or soft?

> **How people disagree at work**
>
> In many British workplaces, people **soften** criticism. Instead of “That’s wrong”, you might hear “I’m not sure that quite works” or “Could we look at that again?”.
>
> Other people, like Josh, prefer to say exactly what they think. Neither style is wrong, but when they meet, people can **come across** badly without meaning to.
>
> In a meeting, softening can help: people often listen more when they don’t feel attacked. Styles vary a lot between workplaces and people.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

This explains the clash in the meeting. (~1.5 min)
Styles differ between people and workplaces, not only countries. Don’t let the student generalise about any nationality.
Ask: In Japan, how do people disagree in a meeting?

</details>


#### Slide 12: Listening · A voicemail

Lunchtime. Priya listens to a voicemail on her phone.

[audio: Mrs Hartley’s voicemail]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Good morning, it’s Margaret Hartley from Hartley’s Tea. We’ve seen some of your work, and it’s… rather interesting. Would you be free to pitch for us? Lovely.

- Who is calling? From which company?
- What does she want?
- What do you think she means by “rather interesting”?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Margaret Hartley, from Hartley’s Tea.
> 2. She wants them to pitch for a project.
> 3. Open question. It could be praise, or polite doubt. That’s the puzzle!

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Good morning, it’s Margaret Hartley from Hartley’s Tea. We’ve seen some of your work, and it’s… rather interesting. Would you be free to pitch for us? Lovely.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~2 min)
Priya hears this alone; she tells the team after lunch.
The first “decode Mrs Hartley” moment. Don’t give an answer: in British English “rather interesting” can be positive or a polite warning. The course comes back to her messages.

</details>


#### Slide 13: Language focus · Softening

> **Softening and hedging**
>
> *Soft openers*
>
> **I’m not sure** that works. · **I wonder if** we could…
>
> *Small words*
>
> It’s **a bit** small. · It’s **slightly** dark. · It’s not **quite** right. · **I’d say** it’s calm.
>
> *Questions instead of statements*
>
> **Could we** try a bigger logo? · **Would it be worth** changing the colours?

**From the story**
- Haruka: **I’m not sure** I’m **quite** ready.
- Yuki: **I’d say** they’re **slightly** more… calm.
- Haruka: **I wonder if** we could hear a bit more from Yuki?

Use softening language to disagree or give feedback without sounding rude.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Softening and hedging make disagreement easier to hear. Main tools: modal verbs (might, could), “I wonder if…”, “I’m not sure…”, small words (a bit, slightly, quite), and “I’d say…”. Some learners have many politeness strategies in Japanese but fewer ready-made ones in English, so they can sound blunter than they mean to. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 14: Practice · Say it nicely

- That’s wrong.
- The logo is too small.
- Your idea won’t work.
- Those colours are boring.
- We need to finish today.
- I don’t agree.

> **Possible answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. I’m not sure that’s quite right.
> 2. The logo might be a bit small.
> 3. I wonder if that idea would work for this client.
> 4. Could we try slightly brighter colours?
> 5. Would it be possible to finish today?
> 6. I’m not sure I agree, to be honest.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student makes each blunt sentence softer. There are many good answers. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Story · After lunch

After lunch. Haruka, Yuki, Josh and Priya.

**Haruka:** OK, back to Yuki’s design. Yuki, would you like to start?  
**Yuki:** I’ve made the logo bigger, but I’d like to keep the colours.  
**Josh:** Can I just come in here? I still think—  
**Haruka:** Sorry, Josh. Let Yuki finish, and then it’s your turn.  
**Priya:** Sorry to interrupt, everyone, but I’ve got news. Hartley’s Tea wants us to pitch!  
**Josh:** Hartley’s? The tea company with the old tins?  
**Haruka:** That’s the one. Right, **action points**: Yuki finishes the bakery design, and we all read the Hartley’s brief by Friday.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Haruka is more confident now: she stops Josh politely but clearly, and ends with clear action points.
Hartley’s Tea is a fictional company. The project runs through the whole course.

</details>


#### Slide 16: Language focus · Can I just come in here?

> **Managing a meeting**
>
> *Interrupting politely*
>
> **Can I just come in here?** · **Sorry to interrupt, but…**
>
> *Managing turns*
>
> **Let** Yuki **finish**, then it’s your turn. · **Let’s put a pin in that.**
>
> *Finishing*
>
> **Right, action points:** … · **Any other business?**

**From the story**
- Josh: **Can I just come in here?**
- Haruka: Sorry, Josh. **Let** Yuki **finish**.
- Priya: **Sorry to interrupt**, everyone, but…

Use these phrases to interrupt, manage turns and finish a meeting.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Fixed phrases for managing a meeting. “Just” makes an interruption softer. These phrases are very common in British meetings. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 17: Pronunciation · Soft, not flat

> **Fall-rise for softening**
>
> I’m not **SURE** ↘↗ that works. (soft, open to discussion)
> That doesn’t **WORK**. ↘ (final)

**Say it with your teacher**
- I’m not **sure** ↘↗ that works.
- It’s **slightly** ↘↗ dark.
- **Could** we ↘↗ try again?
- I’d **say** ↘↗ it’s calm.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Softening works best with a fall-rise on the key word: the voice goes down and then up a little, as if to say “…but I could be wrong”. A flat or falling voice sounds more final. Say each line; the student repeats. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 18: Role play · Chairing a meeting

> **Student**
>
> You’re chairing the team meeting.
>
> Agenda: the party date, a new coffee machine, who goes to Hartley’s.
>
> • Keep to the agenda.
> • Deal with interruptions politely.
> • Finish with action points.

**Useful phrases**
- First item:…
- Let’s stick to the agenda.
- Let’s put a pin in that.
- Can I just come in here?
- Right, action points:…
- Any other business?

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re Sam, a colleague who talks a lot.
>
> • Interrupt twice and go off topic.
> • Disagree once, bluntly: “That won’t work.”
> • Then swap: you chair, the student interrupts.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Sam’s views: the party on the last Friday of the month, yes to a coffee machine, and he wants to go to Hartley’s himself. The student chairs and keeps Sam on track. Then swap: you chair, and the student uses “Can I just come in here?” and softening to disagree. (~3 min)

</details>


#### Slide 19: Reading · How to run a good meeting

[image: Photo idea: a meeting table with an agenda and sticky notes]

A good meeting starts before anyone walks into the room. Send the **agenda** a day or two before, so people can prepare.

Start on time and finish on time. If the chair waits for latecomers, everyone learns that being late is fine.

A good chair makes sure quiet people get a turn and stops one person taking over. Ideas that are off-topic get “parked” for later.

Finish with **action points**: who will do what, and by when. Then send short notes the same day.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: What should people get before the meeting? What happens to off-topic ideas? What should every action point have?
This is general good practice; workplaces vary.

</details>


#### Slide 20: Reading · True or false?

- Send the agenda just before the meeting starts.
- A good chair waits for people who are late.
- Off-topic ideas can be “parked” for later.
- Action points should say who, what and when.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: a day or two before.  2. False: start on time.  3. True.  4. True.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a meeting in a Japanese office]

- How are meetings in Japan different from Haruka’s meeting?
- Is it easy to disagree with your boss in Japan? How do people do it?
- Which style do you prefer: Josh’s or Haruka’s? Why?
- How would you have handled Josh in the meeting?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Some may mention nemawashi (agreeing things before the meeting). (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 22: Story · The boss

That evening at home. Gwen, Tom and Haruka.

**Gwen:** So, how was your first meeting as the boss, love?  
**Haruka:** Interesting. The new designer, Josh, is very… direct.  
**Tom:** Direct how? Like me?  
**Haruka:** No, you just like to banter. He’s **blunt** with everyone, and not very **tactful**.  
**Gwen:** Try not to **take it personally**. Some people just need time. Anything else happen?  
**Haruka:** We’re pitching for Hartley’s Tea!  
**Gwen:** Hartley’s? My nan had a Hartley’s tin with a funny white bird on it. I kept my buttons in it!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Haruka teases Tom (banter from the intermediate course) and uses “blunt” and “tactful” from today’s vocabulary. Gwen uses “take it personally”.
Teacher only: remember Gwen’s tin with the white bird. It’s the first clue in the archive puzzle later in the course.

</details>


#### Slide 23: Vocabulary · Useful phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to put a pin in it | phrase | いったん保留にする | to stop discussing something and come back to it later | Let’s **put a pin in that** and come back to it after lunch. |
| to stick to the agenda | phrase | 議題から外れない | to only discuss the planned items | Keep it short and **stick to the agenda**. |
| AOB | abbreviation | その他の議題 | any other business: the last item in a meeting | Right, any **AOB** before we finish? |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Common meeting phrases. “Put a pin in it” and “stick to the agenda” came up in today’s dialogues and are used in many English-speaking offices; AOB is common on UK meeting agendas, especially formal ones. (~1.5 min)
Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>


#### Slide 24: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- chaired a meeting and managed turns
- interrupted and disagreed politely
- softened feedback so it’s easier to hear
- learned how British workplaces often soften criticism

[image: Photo idea: an old tea tin with a white bird]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

For each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 2: What she really means

**Objective:** Students can work out the intended meaning of indirect feedback, check politely until they understand, report what people said with a range of reporting verbs, and understand the key phrases of a formal follow-up email (CEFR B2: understand implied meaning; check understanding; understand formal correspondence).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 1 · New team · Lesson 2

**What she really means**

Today’s goal: work out what a polite client really means, report what people said, and understand a formal follow-up email.

[image: Photo idea: vintage tea tins in a London office]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

October. The team’s first meeting with the client, Mrs Hartley of Hartley’s Tea. Josh loves his bold idea. Mrs Hartley calls it “very brave”. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Haruka chaired her first meeting as team lead, and clashed politely with Josh’s direct style (Lesson 1). Hartley’s Tea asked the team to pitch.

Main points: reporting verbs (suggest, warn, admit…), checking what someone really means, and formal follow-up emails.

Timing: 22 slides, about 39 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip Make it formal, the pronunciation slide and British phrases (saves about 4.5 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Saying no

Talk with your teacher.

- How do you say no to a client or a boss without saying “no”?
- Have you ever misunderstood someone’s feedback?
- Is it better to be honest or polite with a client?

[image: Photo idea: a polite business handshake]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. Push for examples. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: The Hartley’s Tea meeting room. Josh shows a neon-pink design; Mrs Hartley and Haruka react. Josh proudly holds up the design. Mrs Hartley raises her eyebrows and smiles politely. Haruka bites her lip.]

- What does Josh show Mrs Hartley? How does he feel?
- Look at Mrs Hartley and Haruka. What do you think they’re thinking?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Ask the student to describe the body language. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · On the way

Monday morning, walking to Hartley’s Tea. Josh and Haruka.

**Josh:** I’m telling you, this is going to blow her away. Neon pink, a cartoon teapot… it’s fresh.  
**Haruka:** It’s definitely fresh. I just wonder if Mrs Hartley might prefer something a bit more traditional.  
**Josh:** Traditional is the problem. They’ve looked the same for a hundred years.  
**Haruka:** That might be why people love them.  
**Josh:** Trust me. Bold sells.  
**Haruka:** OK. But if she doesn’t like it, let me do the talking.  
**Josh:** Deal. But she’ll love it.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together, then swap. (~2 min)
Haruka uses softening from Lesson 1 (“I just wonder if…”, “a bit more”). Josh doesn’t hear the warning.
Ask: Who do you agree with? Why?

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- What is Josh’s idea?
- What does Haruka think Mrs Hartley might prefer?
- Why does Josh think traditional is a problem?
- What do they agree?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Neon pink and a cartoon teapot.  2. Something more traditional.  3. The brand has looked the same for a hundred years.  4. If Mrs Hartley doesn’t like it, Haruka will do the talking.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · Pitching (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a concept | noun | コンセプト、構想 | the main idea for a design or project | Josh presented his **concept**. |
| bold | adjective | 大胆な | strong and confident, not afraid to be different | It’s a very **bold** design. |
| to shake things up | phrasal verb | 大きく変える、刷新する | to make big changes to something that has been the same for a long time | We want to **shake things up**. |
| heritage | noun | 歴史的伝統、遺産 | history and traditions that are important to a company or country | Hartley’s has a hundred years of **heritage**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogues. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Feedback (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a compliment | noun | 褒め言葉 | something nice you say about someone | “Brave” isn’t always a **compliment**. |
| to admit | verb | （しぶしぶ）認める | to agree that something is true, often unwillingly | OK, I **admit** it was a bit bold. |
| to see (something) coming | phrase | 予想する | to expect something before it happens | I didn’t **see** that **coming**. |
| to look forward to | phrasal verb | 楽しみにする | to be happy about something that will happen | I **look forward to** seeing your new ideas. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come later in the lesson. (~1.5 min)
Remember: look forward to + -ing (not “to see”).

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · A very brave idea

Hartley’s Tea. Josh shows Mrs Hartley his concept. Haruka is there.

**Mrs Hartley:** Do come in. So, what have you got for me?  
**Josh:** Something **bold**. Neon pink tins, a cartoon teapot, and a new name: “HARTZ”.  
**Mrs Hartley:** Goodness. That’s… a very brave idea.  
**Josh:** Thanks! We really wanted to **shake things up**.  
**Mrs Hartley:** Yes, I can see that. I’ll certainly bear it in mind.  
**Haruka:** We’d also love to bring in more of Hartley’s **heritage**. Leave it with us.  
**Mrs Hartley:** Lovely. I **look forward to** it.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Josh hears praise because he loves his idea. Haruka hears a polite no. The next slides decode what Mrs Hartley meant.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Listening · Her message

That afternoon, Priya plays the team a voicemail from Mrs Hartley.

[audio: Mrs Hartley’s voicemail]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Hello Priya, Margaret Hartley here. Thank you for this morning. Very… brave. You might want to look at the traditional elements again. We mustn’t lose a hundred years of history. Lovely.

- What does Mrs Hartley want the team to do?
- What does she say they mustn’t do?
- Is she happy with the meeting? How do you know?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Look at the traditional elements again.
> 2. Lose a hundred years of history.
> 3. Not really: “brave”, the pause, and the instruction all show she wants changes.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Hello Priya, Margaret Hartley here. Thank you for this morning. Very… brave. You might want to look at the traditional elements again. We mustn’t lose a hundred years of history. Lovely.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~2 min)
“You might want to…” sounds like a suggestion, but in British business English it often means “you should”.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Puzzle · Decode Mrs Hartley

- “It’s… rather interesting.” (Lesson 1)
- “That’s… a very brave idea.”
- “I’ll certainly bear it in mind.”
- “You might want to look at the traditional elements again.”
- “Lovely. I look forward to it.”

> **What she means** (teacher only)
>
> 1. I’m not sure about your work, but let’s see.
> 2. It’s risky. I don’t like it.
> 3. Probably not.
> 4. Change it.
> 5. This one is real: she’s happy to see new ideas.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The first “decode the client” slide. For each phrase, the student says what Mrs Hartley really means. Accept good guesses; discuss the clues (pauses, tone, the next sentence). (~2 min)
Don’t re-teach the basics from the intermediate course: focus on reading a real client.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Story · Brave?

After the voicemail. Josh, Haruka and Priya.

**Josh:** That went brilliantly! She said it was brave!  
**Haruka:** Josh… when a client says “brave”, it isn’t always a **compliment**.  
**Priya:** You heard her message. She suggested looking at the traditional elements again.  
**Josh:** So? That’s just a suggestion.  
**Priya:** She also warned us not to lose a hundred years of history. That isn’t a suggestion.  
**Josh:** So she hated it? OK… I admit it was a bit bold. I didn’t **see** that **coming**.  
**Haruka:** A bit? Come on, let’s fix it together.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Priya reports the voicemail with reporting verbs: the language focus on the next slide.
Haruka’s “A bit?” is friendly teasing. Josh is starting to trust her.
Josh isn’t confused because he’s Australian: Australians use the same polite code. He’s so excited about his idea that he hears what he wants to hear.

</details>


#### Slide 12: Language focus · She suggested…

> **Reporting verbs**
>
> *verb + -ing*
>
> She **suggested looking** at it again. (not “suggested us to”) · He **admitted making** a mistake.
>
> *verb + (someone) + (not) to*
>
> She **warned us not to lose** the history. · Haruka **promised to send** them by Friday. · Yuki **agreed to help**.
>
> *verb + that*
>
> He **admitted that** it was a bit bold. · She **insisted that** we keep the logo.

**From the story**
- Priya: She **suggested looking** at the traditional elements again.
- Priya: She **warned us not to lose** a hundred years of history.
- Josh: OK… I **admit** it was a bit bold.

Use reporting verbs to show what someone meant, not just what they said.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Reporting verbs say more than “said”: they show what the speaker was doing (suggesting, warning, admitting). Each verb has its own pattern: suggest/admit + -ing; warn/ask + someone + to; promise/agree/refuse + to; insist/admit + that. Many Japanese learners use “suggest someone to…”, which is wrong. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Practice · What did they say?

> *Pattern*
>
> suggest + -ing · warn + someone + to
> promise / agree + to · insist + that

- Mrs Hartley: “Why don’t you look at the old tins?” (suggest)
- Priya: “Don’t be late for the client.” (warn)
- Josh: “I was wrong about the colours.” (admit)
- Haruka: “I’ll send the new designs by Friday.” (promise)
- Mrs Hartley: “You must keep the original logo.” (insist)

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. She suggested looking at the old tins.
> 2. Priya warned Josh not to be late.
> 3. Josh admitted (that) he was wrong / admitted being wrong.
> 4. Haruka promised to send the new designs by Friday.
> 5. She insisted that they keep the original logo.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reports each sentence with the verb in brackets. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 14: Pronunciation · tradition → traditional

> **The stress moves**
>
> tra**DI**tion → tra**DI**tional (same)
> **HIS**tory → his**TOR**ical
> **PHO**tograph → pho**TOG**raphy
> **E**conomy → eco**NOM**ic → e**CON**omy

**Say it with your teacher**
- We mustn’t lose the **his**tory.
- It’s a his**tor**ical brand.
- She loves pho**tog**raphy.
- It’s an eco**nom**ic problem.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

In word families, the stress often moves. Learners often keep the stress of the first word. Say each pair; the student repeats, then uses one in a sentence. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Reading · The follow-up email

[image: Photo idea: writing an email in the evening]

**Subject: Next steps**

Dear Mrs Hartley,

Thank you for meeting us this morning. We really enjoyed hearing about Hartley’s history.

**As discussed**, we will look again at the traditional elements and send you two new options by Friday.

**In the meantime**, would it be possible to visit your archive? We’d love to see some of the original tins.

**Please let me know if** you have any questions.

Kind regards,
Haruka

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Haruka’s email to Mrs Hartley that evening. The student reads it aloud, then finds the phrases that make it polite and clear. (~2.5 min)
Notice the request at the end: Haruka asks to visit the archive. It matters later in the course.

</details>


#### Slide 16: Language focus · As discussed…

> **Follow-up emails**
>
> *Opening*
>
> **Thank you for meeting us.** · **Following our meeting**, …
>
> *Next steps*
>
> **As discussed**, we will… · **In the meantime**, …
>
> *Requests and closing*
>
> **Would it be possible to**…? · **Please let me know if**…

**From the story**
- Haruka: **As discussed**, we will look again at the traditional elements.
- Haruka: **In the meantime**, **would it be possible to** visit your archive?
- Haruka: **Please let me know if** you have any questions.

Use these phrases in formal follow-up emails.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Fixed phrases for formal follow-up emails. They make an email clear and polite at the same time. Compare the casual style in the intermediate course (Lesson 9). (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 17: Practice · Make it formal

- Thanks for the meeting!
- Like we said, we’ll send new ideas.
- Can we come and see the old tins?
- While we wait, here’s a quick sketch.
- Any questions? Just ask.

> **Possible answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Thank you for meeting us.
> 2. As discussed, we will send you some new ideas.
> 3. Would it be possible to visit your archive?
> 4. In the meantime, please find a quick sketch attached.
> 5. Please let me know if you have any questions.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student rewrites each sentence for a formal email to a client. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 18: Role play · Decode the client

> **Student**
>
> You designed a new logo for a London café. The owner gives you feedback.
>
> • Find out what they really think of the colour, the picture and the font.
> • Check: “So, just to be clear…?”
> • Agree the next step.

**Useful phrases**
- Just to be clear, …
- When you say “brave”, do you mean…?
- So would you prefer…?
- What would you change?
- Leave it with us.
- As discussed, we’ll…

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re the café owner. Very polite. What you really think:
>
> • Bright pink: you hate it.
> • The coffee cup picture: you love it.
> • The font: hard to read.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Say it the British way: “The pink is… brave”, “You might want to look at the font”, “The cup is rather nice.” Only be direct when the student checks clearly. Afterwards, the student reports what you said with reporting verbs (You suggested…, You warned me…). (~3 min)

</details>


#### Slide 19: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: colleagues talking over coffee]

- Is it easy for you to say no to a client in Japan? How do you do it?
- Are Japanese clients more like Mrs Hartley or more like Josh?
- Should Josh have listened to Haruka before the meeting?
- Which is worse: feedback that’s too direct, or too hard to understand?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Some may compare British indirectness with Japanese reading between the lines (kuuki wo yomu). (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 20: Story · Late at the office

That evening, at the office. Josh and Haruka.

**Josh:** Haruka, sorry about today. You tried to warn me.  
**Haruka:** To be fair, your colours were great. They just need some Hartley’s history in them.  
**Josh:** So “brave” means bad, and “you might want to” means “do it”. Why didn’t she just say so?  
**Haruka:** And “lovely” usually means lovely. Usually.  
**Josh:** Great. And can you explain my payslip? It says “NI” took a big chunk of my money. Who’s NI?  
**Haruka:** It’s not a person. NI means National Insurance. Bring your payslip in tomorrow and I’ll explain.  
**Josh:** Deal.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Josh admits he was wrong, and Haruka is generous. Their relationship starts to change.
Hook: next lesson is about payslips. NI = National Insurance, a deduction on every UK payslip. Josh thinks it sounds like a person’s name. Ask: Do you understand your payslip?

</details>


#### Slide 21: Vocabulary · Useful British phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| You might want to… | phrase | 〜した方がいいかもしれません（＝してください） | a polite suggestion; from a client or boss, it often means “you should” | **You might want to** look at the colours again. |
| Leave it with us. | phrase | こちらで対応します | we’ll deal with it | Don’t worry. **Leave it with us.** |
| To be fair, … | phrase | 公平に言えば | said before a fair point, often to be kind | **To be fair**, your colours were great. |
| I’ll bear it in mind. | phrase | 検討します（＝たぶん採用しない） | polite; depending on the context, it can mean “probably not” | Interesting. **I’ll bear it in mind.** |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Common British business phrases. They all came up in today’s dialogues. (~1.5 min)
“I’ll bear it in mind” came up in the intermediate course; here students hear it from a real client.
Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>


#### Slide 22: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- worked out what a polite client really meant
- reported what people said with suggest, warn, admit…
- checked politely until you understood
- understood the phrases in a formal follow-up email

[image: Photo idea: an old tea tin next to a new design sketch]

For each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1 min)
