### Lesson 3: Payday

**Objective:** Students can understand and explain the main parts of a UK payslip, ask for a problem with their pay to be sorted out, and talk about services and arranged actions with the causative (have / get something done, get someone to do) (CEFR B2: understand detailed information; explain a problem and make clear that the provider must act).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 1 · New team · Lesson 3

**Payday**

Today’s goal: understand a UK payslip, get problems sorted out, and talk about things you have done for you.

[image: Photo idea: a payslip on a desk]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Late October into November. Josh can’t understand his first UK payslip, so Haruka and Priya help him sort it out. On Sunday, Tom brings his girlfriend, Amy, to Gwen’s for lunch for the first time. Last lesson of Unit 1. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Mrs Hartley called Josh’s neon-pink idea “very brave”, which meant no. Haruka sent a follow-up email promising two new options and asking to visit the Hartley’s archive. Josh asked Haruka to explain his payslip (Lesson 2).

Main point: the causative (have / get something done, have someone do, get someone to do). Practical topic: UK payslips, tax codes, National Insurance and workplace pensions.

Puzzle: Gwen describes her nan’s Hartley’s tin (Lesson 1) in more detail. The white bird with long legs and a red cap is a clue for later units. Don’t point it out; if a student says “a crane?”, just say “Interesting…”.

Timing: 24 slides, about 44 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip the pronunciation slide, True or false and British phrases (saves about 3.5 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Money admin

Talk with your teacher.

- Do you read your payslip every month? What do you check?
- Who helps you with forms and paperwork?
- What would you do if your pay was wrong?

[image: Photo idea: sorting out paperwork at home]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. Students don’t need to share real numbers. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: Josh’s desk at the office. Josh is shocked by his payslip; Haruka explains one line. Josh shakes his head at the payslip. Haruka points at a line with her pen. Josh frowns, then nods.]

- What is Josh looking at? How does he feel?
- What do you think Haruka is explaining?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Let the student describe Josh’s reaction first. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Where’s my money gone?

The next morning at the office. Josh and Haruka.

**Josh:** Right, here’s my payslip. Look at this. Where’s all my money gone?  
**Haruka:** Let me see… income tax, National Insurance, pension. That’s all normal.  
**Josh:** And what exactly is National Insurance? Is it health insurance?  
**Haruka:** Not really. It pays for things like the State Pension. But look at your tax code: 0T.  
**Josh:** Zero T. Is that bad?  
**Haruka:** It means you’re paying too much tax. You need to get your code changed.  
**Josh:** How? I signed about fifty forms in my first week!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together, then swap. (~2 min)
Callback: in Lesson 2 Josh asked “Who’s NI?”, and Haruka told him it’s National Insurance. Haruka understands because she went through the same thing in her first year.
“Get your code changed” is the causative: don’t explain it yet. The language focus comes later.
Say 0T as “zero T” or “oh T”.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- What is Josh worried about?
- What does National Insurance pay for?
- What’s wrong with Josh’s tax code?
- Why doesn’t Josh know how to fix it?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. A lot of money has been taken from his pay.  2. Things like the State Pension.  3. It’s 0T, so he’s paying too much tax.  4. He signed so many forms in his first week.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Culture · What’s on a UK payslip?

> **Your payslip, line by line**
>
> **Gross pay:** your pay before anything is taken off.
>
> **Income tax:** most people pay no tax on their first £12,570 a year. The tax code **1257L** shows this. After that, the basic rate is 20%.
>
> **National Insurance (NI):** 8% of most of what you earn over £12,570. It helps pay for the State Pension and some benefits.
>
> **Pension:** if you’re eligible, you’re put into a workplace pension automatically.
>
> **Net pay:** what you take home.
>
> **0T** means no tax-free pay at all. Employers often use it when they don’t have a new worker’s details yet.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Explains the system straight after the scene. Figures are for England, Wales and Northern Ireland, checked for 2026/27 (Scotland has its own income tax rates; NI is the same across the UK). The story is in 2027/28: the £12,570 allowance and NI thresholds are frozen until April 2031, but check the rates before teaching. (~2 min)
New starters give their employer a P45 from their last UK job or fill in HMRC’s starter checklist. With no information, the employer uses 0T.
Ask: What’s taken off your pay in Japan? Who works out your tax?
Sources: GOV.UK income tax rates and Personal Allowance (£12,570, frozen); GOV.UK National Insurance rates 2026/27 (8% between £12,570 and £50,270, 2% above); Autumn Budget 2025 (thresholds frozen until April 2031); HMRC starter checklist guidance (0T when no information is given).

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Pay (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| payroll | noun | 給与担当（部署） | the department that pays a company’s staff | Ask **payroll** about your tax code. |
| a refund | noun | 払い戻し、還付 | money that is paid back to you | You’ll get a **refund** next month. |
| to opt out (of) | phrasal verb | （制度から）脱退する | to choose not to take part in something | You can **opt out** of the pension. |
| generous | adjective | 気前のいい、手厚い | giving more than people expect | Our pension isn’t very **generous**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 8: Vocabulary · Sunday lunch (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a good influence | phrase | 良い影響を与える人 | someone who helps another person behave better | Amy’s a **good influence** on Tom. |
| to ring a bell | idiom | 聞き覚え（見覚え）がある | to sound or look familiar | Hartley’s? That **rings a bell**. |
| the loft | noun | 屋根裏（収納） | the space under the roof of a house, often used for storage | The old boxes are in the **loft**. |
| to dig (something) out | phrasal verb | （しまってある物を）探し出す | to find something you haven’t used for a long time | I’ll **dig out** my old photos. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come later in the lesson. (~1.5 min)
Loft: British English. Americans usually say “attic”.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Story · Free money

Later that morning. Priya, Haruka and Josh.

**Priya:** Payslip trouble? I heard “Where’s all my money gone?” from across the room.  
**Haruka:** His tax code’s 0T. I don’t think he did the starter checklist.  
**Priya:** Easy. Fill it in today, and I’ll get Mark in **payroll** to update your code. You’ll get a **refund** next month.  
**Josh:** Legend. And can I have this pension thing cancelled? I’m not planning to retire in London.  
**Priya:** You can **opt out**, but the company pays in 3% too. If you opt out, you lose that.  
**Josh:** Only 3%? Back in Sydney, my employer paid 12% into my pension, and I didn’t have to put anything in.  
**Haruka:** OK, ours isn’t as **generous**. But that 3% is still free money.   

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
“Legend!” is informal for “You’re brilliant!” (common in Australia and the UK).
In Australia, the workplace pension is called “super” (superannuation). Employers there must pay 12% of ordinary earnings (from July 2025). In the UK the minimum is 8% in total, with at least 3% from the employer.
Ask: Would you opt out? Why or why not?
Sources: Australian Taxation Office / SuperSA (super guarantee 12% from 1 July 2025); GOV.UK workplace pensions (minimum contributions).
“Free money” is how people in the UK often describe the employer’s contribution: you only get it if you stay in. The catch: you can’t take money out of a UK pension until 57 (the minimum age rises from 55 to 57 in April 2028), even if you move abroad. Source: GOV.UK, “Increasing normal minimum pension age”.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Listening · A message from payroll

That afternoon, Josh gets a voicemail from Mark in payroll.

[audio: Mark’s voicemail]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Hi Josh, Mark from payroll. Your tax code’s all sorted, so you’ll get the extra tax back in November’s pay. Any questions, just pop down.

- What has happened to Josh’s tax code?
- When will Josh get his money back?
- What does Mark mean by “just pop down”?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. It’s been sorted (updated).
> 2. In November’s pay.
> 3. Come down to see him (informal).

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Hi Josh, Mark from payroll. Your tax code’s all sorted, so you’ll get the extra tax back in November’s pay. Any questions, just pop down.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~1.5 min)
“Sorted” and “pop down” are on the British phrases slide at the end of the lesson.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Language focus · Get it changed

> **The causative**
>
> *have / get + thing + past participle*
>
> I need to **get my tax code changed**. · We’re **having the office painted**.
>
> *have someone do · get someone to do*
>
> I’ll **have payroll check** it. · I’ll **get Mark to update** it.
>
> *Something bad happens*
>
> He **had his bike stolen**.

**From the story**
- Haruka: You need to **get your code changed**.
- Priya: I’ll **get Mark** in payroll **to update** your code.
- Josh: Can I **have** this pension thing **cancelled**?

Use the causative when someone else does something for you.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The causative: someone else does the action, usually because we ask or pay them. “Get” is more informal than “have”. “Get someone to do” often means asking or persuading; “have someone do” sounds like an instruction, often from a boss or a customer. For bad experiences, use have + past participle: “He had his bike stolen.” (~2.5 min)
Many Japanese learners say “I cut my hair” when a hairdresser did it. In English this means you cut it yourself. Practise “I had / got my hair cut.”

</details>


#### Slide 12: Practice · Have it done

> *Pattern*
>
> have / get + thing + past participle
> get + someone + to + verb

- Someone checked Josh’s payslip. (have)
- Priya asked Mark to update the code. (get)
- A mechanic is going to fix my car tomorrow. (have)
- I asked my brother to help me move. (get)
- Someone stole my bike last week. (have)
- The company is going to paint the office. (have)

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Josh had his payslip checked.
> 2. Priya got Mark to update the code.
> 3. I’m having my car fixed tomorrow.
> 4. I got my brother to help me move.
> 5. I had my bike stolen last week.
> 6. They’re having the office painted.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student rewrites each sentence with the word in brackets. (~2 min)
Then ask: What do you have done rather than doing it yourself?

</details>


#### Slide 13: Pronunciation · Saying large numbers

> **The weak “and”**
>
> £12,570 → twelve **THOU**sand, five hundred n **SEV**enty pounds
> 8% → eight per **CENT**
> £1,450.75 → one thousand, four hundred n **FIF**ty pounds seventy-**FIVE**
> 0T → **ZE**ro **T** (or **OH T**)

**Say it with your teacher**
- My take-home pay is £2,350.
- The company pays in 3 per cent.
- The tax-free amount is £12,570.
- I got a refund of £185.40.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

In British English, “and” comes after hundred, and it’s weak: /ən/. Say “per cent” as two words with the stress on CENT. Money: say “pounds” before the pence, and you can drop “pence”. (~1.5 min)
Many Japanese learners say every number word with equal stress. Model the rhythm, then the student repeats.

</details>


#### Slide 14: Story · Meet Amy

Sunday lunch at Gwen’s. Tom, Amy, Gwen and Haruka.

**Tom:** Gwen, Haruka, this is Amy.  
**Gwen:** Amy! Come in, love. I’ve heard so much about you.  
**Amy:** All good, I hope! Thanks for having me. These are for you.  
**Gwen:** Flowers! Tom’s never brought me flowers. And look, he’s even **had his hair cut**.  
**Tom:** I always look this smart.  
**Haruka:** Amy **got you to book** it. I heard the whole phone call.  
**Amy:** Guilty. I like to think I’m a **good influence**.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
The student meets Amy, Tom’s girlfriend, for the first time. Gwen is delighted. Haruka teases Tom, and Amy joins in: friendly banter, as in the intermediate course.
Find the two causatives: had his hair cut, got you to book it.

</details>


#### Slide 15: Story · The bird on the tin

After lunch. Haruka, Gwen, Tom and Amy.

**Haruka:** Gwen, look. These are the old Hartley’s tins from the brief. Do any of them **ring a bell**?  
**Gwen:** They’re lovely, but none of them is my nan’s. Hers had a white bird on the lid, with long legs and a little red cap.  
**Haruka:** A bird? There isn’t a bird on any of these.  
**Gwen:** Well, I didn’t imagine it. I kept my buttons in that tin for years. It’s in the **loft** somewhere.  
**Tom:** The loft? It’s full of boxes, Gwen. That could take all day.  
**Gwen:** Then I’ll get you to **dig** it **out** after pudding.  
**Amy:** I’ll help. I love a bit of history.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Callback: in Lesson 1 Gwen said her nan’s Hartley’s tin had “a funny white bird” and she kept her buttons in it. Now she gives more detail.
Puzzle (teacher only): the bird matches a red-crowned crane, a symbol of Japan. It’s a clue to the Hartley’s archive story later in the course. Don’t point it out. Just ask: Why do you think the bird isn’t on the tins in the brief?

</details>


#### Slide 16: Role play · At payroll

> **Student**
>
> New job in London. Your first payslip says tax code 0T, and 5% has gone into a pension.
>
> • Ask what 0T means. Get it changed.
> • Should you opt out of the pension?
> • When will you get your money back?

**Useful phrases**
- Could you explain…?
- How do I get it changed?
- Could you have it updated?
- So if I opt out, …?
- When will I get it back?
- That’s sorted, then.

> **Teacher**
>
> You work in payroll.
>
> • 0T: no starter checklist. Say: “I’ll have it updated. You’ll get a refund next month.”
> • Pension: the company adds 3%. Opt out within a month: they get their 5% back, but lose the 3%.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student is a new employee; you work in payroll. Everything you need is on your card. No swap needed. (~3 min)
Listen for the causative (get it changed, have it updated) and checking questions.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Progress check · Unit 1 check

> **Student**
>
> You’re Haruka. A new designer, Alex, joins your team today.
>
> • Explain how your team meetings work.
> • Tell Alex what Mrs Hartley said about Josh’s idea.
> • Tell Alex what to get sorted in their first week.

**Useful phrases**
- Can I just come in here?
- I wonder if…
- She suggested…
- She warned us not to…
- You’ll need to get… changed.
- Let’s put a pin in that.

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re Alex, the new designer.
>
> • Interrupt once.
> • Say one thing too directly: “Neon pink sounds great. She’s wrong.”
> • Ask: What do I need to do about my payslip?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Unit 1 progress check. The student plays Haruka and uses the story, so there’s nothing to invent. Let the student lead and don’t correct during the conversation. Note what they do well and one thing to practise. (~3 min)
When you say something too directly, the student should respond politely, as Haruka did with Josh.
Look for: softening (I wonder if…, slightly), polite interrupting, reporting verbs (suggested, warned), and the causative.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Reading · Signed up automatically

[image: Photo idea: checking a pension on a phone]

Since 2012, most employers in the UK have had to put their workers into a workplace pension automatically. It’s called **automatic enrolment**.

You’re enrolled if you work in the UK, you’re aged 22 or over, you’re under State Pension age, and you earn more than £10,000 a year.

At least 8% of most of your pay goes in: 3% or more from your employer, and the rest from you, including tax relief from the government.

You can **opt out**. If you do it within a month, you get your money back. But every three years or so, your employer has to enrol you again.

The idea behind it is simple. Most people stay with whatever is chosen for them.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: Who is enrolled? How much goes in? What happens if you opt out?
Sources: GOV.UK “Workplace pensions” (automatic enrolment from 2012; eligibility: working in the UK, 22 to State Pension age, earning over £10,000 a year; minimum 8% of qualifying earnings, at least 3% from the employer; opting out within one month gets a refund; re-enrolment about every three years). The Pensions Regulator, automatic enrolment duties.

</details>


#### Slide 19: Reading · True or false?

- Automatic enrolment started in 2012.
- Everyone who works in the UK is enrolled.
- Employers pay at least 3%.
- If you opt out after a year, you get all your money back.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. True.  2. False: you must be 22 or over, under State Pension age and earn over £10,000.  3. True.  4. False: only if you opt out within a month.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 20: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a desk with a calculator and envelopes]

- In Japan, who sorts out your tax: you or your company?
- Would you opt out of a workplace pension, like Josh wanted to? Why?
- Is it OK to talk about your pay with friends or colleagues in Japan?
- Which is easier to understand, a Japanese payslip or a UK one?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Many will mention year-end tax adjustment (nenmatsu chōsei), where the company sorts out most employees’ tax. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Story · Going east

Monday at the office. Priya, Haruka and Josh.

**Priya:** Mrs Hartley’s replied about the new options. “Both are much closer. Before I decide, I’d like to hear what tea drinkers in Japan think.”  
**Josh:** “Much closer.” Is that good or bad?  
**Haruka:** Good. Not perfect, but good. Did she say anything about Gwen’s bird? Gwen still can’t find her tin.  
**Priya:** Only this: “I don’t remember a bird. The archive is being catalogued. Perhaps in the spring.” I’ll keep chasing her.  
**Josh:** So, Japan. Who’s going?  
**Priya:** You two. I’ll **have the travel team book** flights to Tokyo and Osaka for December.  
**Josh:** I’ve never been to Japan! Haruka, you’ll have to teach me some Japanese.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~2 min)
Haruka asked Mrs Hartley about the bird after Sunday lunch. Tom and Amy didn’t find Gwen’s tin in the loft.
Hook: Unit 2 is the business trip to Japan, and Haruka will be in Osaka, her home town. Ask: What should Josh know before his first trip to Japan?

</details>


#### Slide 22: Puzzle · Decode the email

- “Both are much closer.”
- “Before I decide, I’d like to hear what tea drinkers in Japan think.”
- “The archive is being catalogued. Perhaps in the spring.”

> **What she means** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Better, but not right yet. Keep going.
> 2. Show me evidence before I choose: do some research in Japan.
> 3. Not now. Don’t ask again for a while.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

For each phrase, the student says what Mrs Hartley really means. Accept good guesses and ask for the clues. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 23: Vocabulary · British phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| It’s/all sorted. | phrase | 解決済みです、手配済みです | the problem has been dealt with | Your tax code’s **all sorted**. |
| to pop down / over | phrasal verb | ちょっと立ち寄る | to go somewhere quickly or for a short time (informal) | Any questions, just **pop down**. |
| to chase (someone) | verb | （返事などを）催促する | to keep reminding someone to do something | She hasn’t replied. I’ll **chase** her. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Common in British offices. They all came up in today’s dialogues and voicemail. (~1 min)
Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>

## Unit: Going east


### Lesson 4: Planning the trip

**Objective:** Students can plan a business trip, describe arrangements and activities in progress at a future time with the future continuous, ask politely about other people’s plans, and say how long something will have been happening with the future perfect continuous (CEFR B2: describe plans and arrangements in detail; Core Inventory B2: future perfect and continuous).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 2 · Going east · Lesson 4

**Planning the trip**

Today’s goal: plan a business trip, talk about what you’ll be doing, and ask politely about other people’s plans.

[image: Photo idea: a passport and boarding pass on a suitcase]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Late November into December. Hartley’s wants to know what tea drinkers in Japan think, so Haruka and Josh plan a week in Tokyo and Osaka. Josh meets Haruka’s mum on a video call. First lesson of Unit 2. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Josh sorted out his payslip with help from Haruka and Priya. At Sunday lunch, Tom introduced Amy, and Gwen described the white bird on her nan’s Hartley’s tin, which is lost in the loft. Mrs Hartley said the new options were “much closer” and asked for research in Japan (Lesson 3).

Main points: the future continuous (plans, actions in progress at a future time, polite questions) and the future perfect continuous (how long by a future time). Practical topic: planning a business trip, expenses and jet lag.

The future perfect simple (By next year I’ll have finished…) was taught in the intermediate course. Don’t re-teach it: use it only as a contrast.

Timing: 23 slides, about 40 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip How long by then?, True or false and British phrases (saves about 4 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Business travel

Talk with your teacher.

- Have you ever travelled for work? Where did you go?
- What’s the hardest part of a long flight for you?
- What do you always pack?

[image: Photo idea: waiting for a flight at sunrise]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. If the student hasn’t travelled for work, ask about any long trip. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: The office in winter. Josh tries on a big winter coat; Haruka looks at a map of Japan. Josh zips up the coat and pulls a face. Haruka laughs and points at the map.]

- What is Josh doing? Why do you think he needs it?
- What do you think Haruka is planning?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Let the student describe what happens first. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Off to Japan

Monday morning at the office. Priya, Haruka and Josh.

**Priya:** Good news: the Japan trip’s confirmed. You fly out on Sunday the 5th and come back the following Saturday.  
**Josh:** A week in Japan! What’s the plan?  
**Priya:** Tokyo first: a day of focus groups with tea drinkers, then a meeting with a department store. It’s a whistle-stop tour.  
**Haruka:** Then Osaka on Thursday. That’s my home town, Josh.  
**Josh:** Brilliant. By Friday, I’ll have been eating sushi for five days straight.  
**Priya:** You’ll be too busy to eat. Next Tuesday, you’ll be running focus groups all day.  
**Josh:** Fine by me. Mind you, it’s December. Back home, everyone’s at the beach.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together, then swap. (~2 min)
A focus group = a small group of people who discuss a product so a company can learn what they think.
Josh’s joke: December is summer in Sydney.
Both future forms appear here (you’ll be running, I’ll have been eating). Don’t explain them yet.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- When do they fly out, and when do they come back?
- What will they do in Tokyo?
- Why is Osaka special for Haruka?
- Why does December feel strange to Josh?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Sunday the 5th; the following Saturday.  2. A day of focus groups with tea drinkers, then a meeting with a department store.  3. It’s her home town.  4. It’s summer in Sydney.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · Business trips (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| an itinerary | noun | 旅程表 | a plan of a journey: times, places, bookings | I’ll send you the **itinerary** tonight. |
| expenses | plural noun | 経費 | money you spend for work that the company pays back | Keep a record of your **expenses**. |
| to claim (something) back | phrasal verb | （経費などを）請求する、払い戻してもらう | to ask for money you spent to be paid back | You can **claim** taxis **back**. |
| a daily allowance | noun | 日当、1日の上限額 | the most you can spend each day, for example on meals | There’s a **daily allowance** for meals. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue. (~1.5 min)
Itinerary: stress on the second syllable (i-TIN-er-ary).

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Getting ready (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| jet lag | noun | 時差ぼけ | feeling tired and confused after flying to a different time zone | I always get **jet lag** flying east. |
| to tag along | phrasal verb | ついて行く | to go somewhere with other people, often without being formally invited | Can I **tag along** to dinner? |
| to wrap up (warm) | phrasal verb | 暖かい格好をする | to wear warm clothes | **Wrap up warm**. It’s cold in Tokyo. |
| to be in good hands | idiom | 安心して任せられる | to be looked after by someone who will take good care of you | Don’t worry. You’re **in good hands**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come later in the lesson. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · Expenses

That afternoon. Priya, Josh and Haruka.

**Priya:** One more thing. I’ve signed off the trip, and Hartley’s is paying, so keep every receipt. No receipt, no refund.  
**Josh:** Can we **claim** our meals **back** as well?  
**Priya:** Up to a **daily allowance**, yes. Oh, and Mrs Hartley has added a note about **expenses**.  
**Josh:** Go on.  
**Priya:** “I’m sure your team will be sensible. Economy is perfectly comfortable, I find.”  
**Josh:** In economy? By the time we land, we’ll have been sitting there for fourteen hours!  
**Haruka:** Bring a good book. I’ll ping you the **itinerary** tonight.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Direct flights from London to Tokyo take about 14 hours at the moment, because most airlines avoid Russian airspace.
Mrs Hartley’s note is decoded on the next slide.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Puzzle · Decode Mrs Hartley

**Mrs Hartley’s note**
- “I’m sure your team will be sensible.”
- “Economy is perfectly comfortable, I find.”
- “I do hope the research will be… thorough.”

> **What she means** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Don’t spend too much of my money.
> 2. Don’t book business class.
> 3. I expect a lot. Do it properly.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

For each phrase, the student says what Mrs Hartley really means. Accept good guesses and ask for the clues. (~1.5 min)
The third line isn’t in the dialogue: it’s the end of her note, which Priya didn’t read out.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Listening · Your trip is booked

That evening, Haruka gets a voicemail from Chloe in the travel team.

[audio: Chloe’s voicemail]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Hi Haruka, Chloe from travel. Your flights are booked: you’ll be flying out on Sunday morning. Will you be needing a hotel in Osaka? Let me know.

- When will they be flying out?
- What does Chloe want to know?
- Why might Haruka not need a hotel in Osaka?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. On Sunday morning.
> 2. If Haruka needs a hotel in Osaka.
> 3. Her family lives there. She can stay at home.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Hi Haruka, Chloe from travel. Your flights are booked: you’ll be flying out on Sunday morning. Will you be needing a hotel in Osaka? Let me know.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~1.5 min)
Notice “Will you be needing…?”: a polite way to ask about someone’s plans. It’s on the next slide.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Language focus · You’ll be flying out…

> **The future continuous**
>
> *In progress at a future time*
>
> Next Tuesday, you**’ll be running** focus groups all day.
>
> *Plans and arrangements*
>
> We**’ll be flying out** on Sunday. · I**’ll be staying** with my family.
>
> *Polite questions about plans*
>
> **Will you be needing** a hotel? · **Will you be staying** for dinner?

**From the story**
- Priya: Next Tuesday, you**’ll be running** focus groups all day.
- Chloe: You**’ll be flying out** on Sunday morning.
- Chloe: **Will you be needing** a hotel in Osaka?

Use will be + -ing for the future when something is in progress, planned, or asked about politely.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The future continuous (will be + -ing). Three uses: an action in progress at a time in the future; a plan or arrangement that sounds neutral (not a new decision); and polite questions about someone’s plans. “Will you be needing…?” is very common in service English, even though “need” isn’t usually continuous. (~2.5 min)
Many Japanese learners use “will + verb” for plans (I will go to Osaka), which can sound like a decision made now. Recycles softening from Lesson 1: “Will you be…?” is softer than “Are you going to…?”.

</details>


#### Slide 12: Practice · This time tomorrow

> *Pattern*
>
> will be + -ing

- The focus group runs from 9 to 12. At 10, they… (run)
- Ask Josh politely: stay at a hotel in Osaka?
- Ask a client politely: need a parking space?
- Tell your manager your plan: work from home on Friday.
- Sunday, 3 p.m. London time: Haruka and Josh… (fly)

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. At 10, they’ll be running a focus group.
> 2. Will you be staying at a hotel in Osaka?
> 3. Will you be needing a parking space?
> 4. I’ll be working from home on Friday.
> 5. They’ll be flying to Tokyo.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student makes each sentence with will be + -ing. Then ask: What will you be doing this time tomorrow? (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Story · Meet Mum

Friday morning. Haruka and Josh video-call Mum in Osaka.

**Haruka:** Mum, this is Josh. Josh, this is my mum.  
**Mum:** Hello, Josh! Haruka says you’re very… honest.  
**Josh:** I’ll take that as a compliment. Lovely to meet you!  
**Mum:** **Will you be staying** in Osaka for the weekend? You must come to us for okonomiyaki.  
**Josh:** I’d love to **tag along**. I’m clearly **in good hands**.  
**Haruka:** Mum, please don’t clean the whole house for him.  
**Mum:** Too late. I**’ll be waiting** at Shin-Osaka on Thursday. By then, I’ll have been cleaning for a week!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Mum speaks English because Josh is there. She has kept up her English class since the intermediate course.
Mum’s “very… honest” is her own little decode joke: she means “direct”.
Okonomiyaki: a savoury pancake, famous in Osaka. Let the student explain it.
Time check: 9 a.m. in London is 6 p.m. in Osaka in December (9 hours ahead).

</details>


#### Slide 14: Language focus · By Friday, I’ll have been…

> **The future perfect continuous**
>
> *by / by the time + will have been + -ing + for*
>
> By Friday, I**’ll have been eating** sushi **for** five days.
>
> *How long? (continuous) or how many? (simple)*
>
> I**’ll have been working** here **for** a year. · I**’ll have finished** three projects.

**From the story**
- Josh: By Friday, I**’ll have been eating** sushi for five days straight.
- Josh: By the time we land, we**’ll have been sitting** there for fourteen hours!
- Mum: By then, I**’ll have been cleaning** for a week!

Use will have been + -ing to say how long something will have been happening by a future time.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The future perfect continuous (will have been + -ing) looks back from a point in the future and says how long something will have been happening. It usually comes with by / by the time and for. Contrast with the future perfect simple from the intermediate course, which counts finished results: By Friday, I’ll have eaten sushi five times. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Practice · How long by then?

> *Pattern*
>
> will have been + -ing + for…

- Josh started at the company in September. By Christmas, he…
- The flight leaves at 11:30. By 9:30 p.m., they…
- Haruka came to London two years ago in September. By next September, she…
- The focus group started at 9. By 12, they…
- Gwen started looking for the tin in November. By January, she…

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. …he’ll have been working there for about four months.
> 2. …they’ll have been flying for ten hours.
> 3. …she’ll have been living in London for three years.
> 4. …they’ll have been talking for three hours.
> 5. …she’ll have been looking for it for about two months.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student finishes each sentence with will have been + -ing and for. Then ask: In a year’s time, how long will you have been learning English? (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 16: Pronunciation · I’ll’ve been…

> **Weak forms**
>
> we’ll be flying → weel-bee **FLY**-ing
> will you be needing → wil-yuh-bee **NEED**-ing
> I’ll have been eating → ail-uv-bin **EAT**-ing
> we’ll have been sitting → weel-uv-bin **SIT**-ing

**Say it with your teacher**
- We’ll be **fly**ing out on **Sun**day.
- Will you be **need**ing a **ho**tel?
- By **Fri**day, I’ll have been **eat**ing sushi for **five days**.
- We’ll have been **sit**ting there for **four**teen **hours**!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

In fast speech, “will” and “have” become weak: I’ll be → /aɪl bi/, I’ll have been → /aɪl əv bɪn/. “Been” is short: /bɪn/, not “bean”. The stress goes on the main verb and the time. (~1.5 min)
Many Japanese learners say every word fully (I will have been), which sounds slow and formal. Both are correct, but practise the natural version.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Role play · Booking the trip

> **Student**
>
> Call Chloe in the travel team about your trip to Japan.
>
> • You’ll be flying out on Sunday and back on Saturday.
> • Tokyo Monday to Wednesday. In Osaka you’ll be staying with family.
> • Ask: can you claim taxis back?

**Useful phrases**
- I’ll be flying out on…
- I’ll be staying with…
- Will I be needing…?
- Could I claim… back?
- Could you check with Priya?
- That works for me.

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re Chloe in the travel team.
>
> • Ask: Will you be needing…? (a hotel, a taxi, train tickets)
> • Sunday economy is full. Offer Saturday, or premium economy if Priya agrees.
> • Taxis: yes, with a receipt.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student calls the travel team. You’re Chloe. Everything you need is on your card. No swap needed. (~3 min)
Listen for the future continuous (I’ll be flying, I’ll be staying) and polite questions.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Reading · Beating jet lag

[image: Photo idea: morning light after a long flight]

**Jet lag** happens when your body clock doesn’t match the time where you are. You might feel tired in the day, wide awake at night, or hungry at strange times.

Flying east, from London to Tokyo for example, is usually harder than flying west. Your day gets shorter, and most body clocks find it easier to stay up late than to go to bed early.

Before you go, change your sleep times a little. On the plane, drink plenty of water and go easy on alcohol and coffee. When you arrive, get outside in the morning light and eat at local times.

Most people feel normal again within a few days.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: Why is flying east harder? What helps when you arrive?
Sources: NHS “Jet lag” (symptoms, tips, usually improves within a few days); research summarised by Which? (body clocks adjust about an hour a day going east, more slowly than going west).

</details>


#### Slide 19: Reading · True or false?

- Flying from London to Tokyo is usually easier than flying west.
- Morning light can help after flying east.
- Coffee and alcohol help with jet lag.
- Most people feel normal again within a few days.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: flying east is usually harder.  2. True.  3. False: go easy on them.  4. True.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 20: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a bullet train at a platform]

- What should Josh know before his first business trip to Japan?
- If a colleague visited your home town, what would you show them?
- How do companies in Japan handle business trip expenses?
- What’s the longest journey you’ve ever made? How did you deal with jet lag?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Next lesson is about meetings in Japan, so let them give Josh advice now and check it later. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Story · Heathrow

Sunday morning at Heathrow. Josh and Haruka.

**Josh:** Right. Passport, boarding pass, coat. Wait… do I need a visa? I didn’t even check!  
**Haruka:** Josh! …No, you’re fine. Australians can visit Japan for up to 90 days without one.  
**Josh:** Phew. So this time tomorrow, we’ll be having dinner in Tokyo.  
**Haruka:** Sleep on the plane, and **wrap up warm**. Tokyo’s cold in December.  
**Josh:** In economy? “Perfectly comfortable,” remember? I’ll have terrible **jet lag**.  
**Haruka:** Oh, a message from Mum: “Safe flight! I’ll tell you my big news on Thursday.”  
**Josh:** Big news? Now I can’t wait for Thursday.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Fact check: Australians, like British citizens, can visit Japan without a visa for up to 90 days for short business trips (meetings, research) or holidays, but not for paid work. Japan plans an online pre-travel check (JESTA) from around 2028; check the rules before real travel.
Time check: they land in Tokyo on Monday morning (local time), so 9 a.m. on Monday in London is dinner time in Tokyo.
Hook: Mum’s big news comes in the next lesson. Ask: What do you think it is?
Sources: Ministry of Foreign Affairs of Japan, visa exemption list; Executive Traveller, “New travel rules for Aussies visiting Japan” (JESTA).

</details>


#### Slide 22: Vocabulary · British phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a whistle-stop tour | noun | 駆け足の視察（旅行） | a fast trip that visits a lot of places in a short time | It’s a **whistle-stop tour**: Tokyo, then Osaka. |
| to sign (something) off | phrasal verb | （上司が）承認する | to give official approval for something | I’ve **signed off** the trip. |
| to ping (someone) something | verb | （メールなどで）さっと送る | to send something quickly by email or message (informal) | I’ll **ping** you the itinerary tonight. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Common in British offices. They all came up in today’s dialogues. (~1 min)
Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>
