### Lesson 5: Tokyo meetings

**Objective:** Students can explain what someone means in a cross-cultural meeting, make their main point clear and correct misunderstandings with cleft sentences, and report the outcome of a meeting (CEFR B2–C1: mediating communication; clarifying and emphasising).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 2 · Going east · Lesson 5

**Tokyo meetings**

Today’s goal: explain what someone really means, and make your main point clear in a meeting.

[image: Photo idea: a Tokyo street at dusk]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

December, the week in Japan. Focus groups in Tokyo, a meeting with a department store buyer, and dinner at Mum’s in Osaka, where Mum shares her news and Haruka finally recognises Gwen’s bird. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Haruka and Josh planned a week in Tokyo and Osaka. Hartley’s is paying (“Economy is perfectly comfortable, I find”). Josh met Mum on a video call, and Mum promised big news on Thursday (Lesson 4).

Main point: cleft sentences for emphasis and clarifying (What they loved was…, It’s the price that…). Function: explaining what someone means in a cross-cultural meeting.

Let the student explain Japanese business culture; don’t tell them. Present every business style (Josh’s, Ms Kato’s, Mrs Hartley’s) as a person’s style, not a national one.

Timing: 22 slides, about 39 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip the pronunciation slide, True or false and Useful phrases (saves about 4 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Working across cultures

Talk with your teacher.

- Have you ever worked with people from another country? What surprised you?
- Have you ever been misunderstood at work? What happened?
- Is it better to get to the point quickly or take your time in a first meeting?

[image: Photo idea: an international meeting]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. Push for a real example. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: A department store meeting room in Tokyo. Ms Kato offers her business card; Josh takes it with one hand. Ms Kato offers her card with both hands. Josh takes it with one hand and pockets it. Haruka reacts.]

- What are they doing? What does Josh do differently?
- Look at Haruka. What do you think she’s thinking?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Let the student describe what happens and explain it; don’t tell them. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · The focus groups

Tuesday evening in Tokyo. Josh and Haruka.

**Josh:** Well? I didn’t understand a word. Did they like it?  
**Haruka:** They liked the new options. But it was the old tins they kept talking about.  
**Josh:** So my neon pink idea would have been a disaster.  
**Haruka:** Probably. What they really loved was the history: a British tea company that’s a hundred years old.  
**Josh:** So Mrs Hartley was right. Don’t tell her I said that.  
**Haruka:** Your secret’s safe. Tomorrow it’s the department store. The buyer, Ms Kato, used to live in London, so the meeting will be in English.  
**Josh:** Perfect. Anything I should know before we go?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together, then swap. (~2 min)
The focus groups were in Japanese, so Haruka sums them up for Josh.
The cleft sentences (it was the old tins they…, What they really loved was…) are taught later. Don’t explain them yet.
Josh’s last question goes on the next slide: let the student answer it.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- Did the focus groups like the new options?
- What did people talk about most?
- Why will the meeting be in English?
- What would you tell Josh before the meeting?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Yes.  2. The old tins and the history.  3. The buyer, Ms Kato, used to live in London.  4. Student’s own answer.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. The last question is open: let the student advise Josh (business cards, letting people finish, not rushing a decision…). Don’t correct their view of Japan. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · The meeting (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to stock (something) | verb | （商品を）取り扱う、仕入れる | to keep goods in a shop to sell | Would you **stock** our tea? |
| a sticking point | noun | 合意の妨げになる点 | the main problem that stops people agreeing | Price is the **sticking point**. |
| to commit (to something) | verb | 確約する | to promise to do something definitely | We can’t **commit** to that yet. |
| a trial | noun | 試験販売、トライアル | a short test period to see if something works | Let’s start with a three-month **trial**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Understanding people (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to get the wrong end of the stick | idiom | 勘違いする、誤解する | to understand something in the wrong way | I think you **got the wrong end of the stick**. |
| to read between the lines | idiom | 行間を読む | to understand what someone means but doesn’t say | You have to **read between the lines**. |
| to come out | phrasal verb | 発売される、公開される | (of a book or film) to be published or shown for the first time | Her book **comes out** in July. |
| to be over the moon | idiom | 大喜びする | to be extremely happy | She was **over the moon**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come later in the lesson. (~1.5 min)
“Get the wrong end of the stick” and “over the moon” are especially British.

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · The department store

Wednesday morning. Ms Kato, Josh and Haruka.

**Ms Kato:** Thank you for coming all this way. Please, have some tea first.  
**Josh:** Thanks. So, let’s get straight to it. Would you **stock** Hartley’s?  
**Ms Kato:** The tea is lovely. The price might be a little… challenging for our customers.  
**Josh:** It’s premium tea. If people want quality, they pay for it.  
**Haruka:** What Josh means is that your customers might value the history. Would a smaller tin at a lower price work?  
**Ms Kato:** It might. To be honest, it’s the price that’s the **sticking point**, not the tea.  
**Josh:** Great. So can you **commit** to a **trial** in the spring?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Ms Kato is polite but clear about the price: she isn’t “vague”. Josh skips the tea and goes straight to business. Haruka steps in and explains what Josh means, in a softer way.
Ask: Was Haruka right to speak for Josh?

</details>


#### Slide 9: Story · Not a no

After the meeting, in a taxi. Josh and Haruka.

**Josh:** She said, “We’ll consider it carefully.” That’s a no, isn’t it?  
**Haruka:** Not necessarily. I think you’ve **got the wrong end of the stick**.  
**Josh:** She didn’t say yes.  
**Haruka:** She can’t yet. She’ll need to talk to her team first. What she said was “not today”, not “no”.  
**Josh:** So Ms Kato and Mrs Hartley speak the same language.  
**Haruka:** In a way. You have to **read between the lines** with both of them.  
**Josh:** OK. Next time, I’ll try reading between the lines before I ask for a decision.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
“We’ll consider it carefully” was Ms Kato’s last line in the meeting.
Many companies, in Japan and in the UK, make decisions as a team. Ms Kato is careful, like Mrs Hartley: it’s about the person and the company, not a whole country.
“Speak the same language” = understand each other well (it’s on the phrases slide at the end).

</details>


#### Slide 10: Listening · Ms Kato’s message

Thursday morning, on the bullet train to Osaka. Josh plays Haruka a voicemail.

[audio: Ms Kato’s voicemail]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Hello Josh, Kato here. I’ve spoken to my team. What we’d like is a small trial in April, with the smaller tin. Let’s talk next week.

- Who has Ms Kato spoken to?
- What would she like?
- So was “We’ll consider it carefully” a no?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Her team.
> 2. A small trial in April, with the smaller tin.
> 3. No. It meant “not yet”: she needed to check with her team.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Hello Josh, Kato here. I’ve spoken to my team. What we’d like is a small trial in April, with the smaller tin. Let’s talk next week.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~1.5 min)
Notice “What we’d like is…”: a cleft sentence. It’s on the next slide.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Language focus · What they loved was…

> **Cleft sentences**
>
> *What + clause + is / was…*
>
> **What they loved was** the history. · **What Josh means is** (that)…
>
> *It + is / was + focus + that / who…*
>
> **It’s the price that’s** the problem. · **It was** Haruka **who** found the answer.
>
> *To correct someone*
>
> **It isn’t** the tea, **it’s** the price.

**From the story**
- Haruka: **What they really loved was** the history.
- Haruka: **What Josh means is** that your customers might value the history.
- Ms Kato: **It’s the price that’s** the sticking point, not the tea.

Use cleft sentences to focus on the most important part, or to clarify and correct.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Cleft sentences split one idea into two parts to put the focus on the important part. What-clefts (What I mean is…) are great for clarifying; it-clefts (It’s the price that…) are great for correcting (It’s the price, not the tea). They’re C1-level tools for meetings and mediation. (~2.5 min)
Many Japanese learners show emphasis with word order or particles (は / が); in English, clefts and stress do the same job.

</details>


#### Slide 12: Practice · Make it clear

> *Pattern*
>
> What + … + is / was …
> It’s / It was + focus + that / who …

- They loved the old tins. (What…)
- The price worries Ms Kato. (It’s…)
- Josh needs more patience. (What…)
- Haruka booked the hotel, not Josh. (It was…)
- I mean we need more time. (What I mean…)
- We didn’t like the colour. The design was fine. (It was…)

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. What they loved was the old tins.
> 2. It’s the price that worries Ms Kato.
> 3. What Josh needs is more patience.
> 4. It was Haruka who booked the hotel, not Josh.
> 5. What I mean is (that) we need more time.
> 6. It was the colour (that) we didn’t like, not the design.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student rewrites each sentence with the words in brackets. Then ask: What do you like most about your job (or studies)? Answer with “What I like most is…”. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Pronunciation · It’s the PRICE…

> **Stress the focus**
>
> It’s the **PRICE** that’s the problem. (not the tea)
> What they **LOVED** | was the **HIS**tory.
> It was **HA**ruka who found it. (not Josh)

**Say it with your teacher**
- It’s the **price** that worries her.
- What we **need** | is more **time**.
- It was **Josh** who asked for a decision.
- It isn’t the **tea**, it’s the **price**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

In a cleft sentence, the focus gets the strongest stress. In what-clefts there’s often a small pause before “is / was”. (~1.5 min)
Many Japanese learners stress every word the same, so the focus is lost. Say each line; the student repeats, then changes the focus (It’s the TEA that…).

</details>


#### Slide 14: Story · Big news

Thursday evening at Mum’s in Osaka. Mum, Josh and Haruka.

**Mum:** Josh, welcome! Sit down, sit down. The okonomiyaki is ready.  
**Josh:** This smells incredible. What’s in it?  
**Mum:** Cabbage, pork and my secret sauce. Now, my news!  
**Haruka:** Finally. What is it?  
**Mum:** The publisher has a date. Milk Second **comes out** in London in July!  
**Haruka:** Mum, that’s amazing! I’m **over the moon** for you.  
**Josh:** Sorry, what’s Milk Second?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~1.5 min)
Mum, Haruka and Josh speak English because Josh is there.
Callback: in the intermediate course, a London publisher asked to turn Milk Second into a book. Now it has a date.
Okonomiyaki: let the student explain it.

</details>


#### Slide 15: Story · The bird

After dinner, Mum pours the tea. Josh and Haruka.

**Mum:** It’s a comic about Haruka’s life in London. **What it’s really about is** finding a new home.  
**Josh:** I need a copy. These cups are beautiful, by the way.  
**Haruka:** They were Grandma’s. Wait… a white bird, with long legs and a red cap.  
**Mum:** A crane. In Japan, cranes are a symbol of long life and good luck.  
**Haruka:** Josh, that’s Gwen’s bird! The one on her nan’s Hartley’s tin.  
**Josh:** A Japanese bird on a British tea tin? A hundred years ago?  
**Haruka:** **It’s the bird that’s** missing from every tin in the brief. I want to know why.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~2 min)
Puzzle: Gwen described the bird in Lesson 1 (“a funny white bird”) and Lesson 3 (“long legs and a little red cap”). It’s a red-crowned crane. Some students will have guessed already: praise them.
Don’t solve it. Ask: Why might a London tea company use a Japanese bird in the 1920s? Write the student’s ideas down for later.

</details>


#### Slide 16: Role play · Calling Priya

> **Student**
>
> You’re Haruka. Call Priya in London and update her on the trip.
>
> • The focus groups: what did people love?
> • Ms Kato: what was the problem?
> • What happens next?

**Useful phrases**
- What they loved was…
- It was the price that…
- It wasn’t Josh, it was…
- What she’d like is…
- She suggested…
- The next step is…

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re Priya.
>
> • Ask: So, how did it go?
> • Ask: Was it Josh who upset Ms Kato?
> • Ask: What exactly does she want?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student plays Haruka and uses the story, so there’s nothing to invent. You’re Priya in London. No swap needed. (~3 min)
Listen for cleft sentences and reporting verbs from Lesson 2 (She suggested…).

</details>


#### Slide 17: Reading · Is royal milk tea royal?

[image: Photo idea: milk tea in a Tokyo café]

Ask for a “royal milk tea” in London, and you’ll probably get a confused look. The name comes from Japan. The Lipton tea house in Kyoto says it was the first to serve it, in 1965.

Royal milk tea is made by brewing black tea in milk. In Britain, people usually make tea with water and add a little milk.

Black tea is popular in Japan in other ways too. Kirin’s Gogo no Kocha (“Afternoon Tea”), launched in 1986, is one of the country’s best-known bottled teas.

So a British tea company in Japan isn’t starting from zero.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. Japanese students often know royal milk tea well: let them explain how it tastes. (~2.5 min)
Check: Where does the name come from? How is it made? Why is this good news for Hartley’s?
Sources: Sir Thomas Lipton Tea House, Kyoto (Sanjo branch), says it first served royal milk tea in 1965; Kirin, company history (Gogo no Kocha launched 1986, Japan’s best-known bottled black tea brand).

</details>


#### Slide 18: Reading · True or false?

- Royal milk tea comes from the UK.
- Royal milk tea is made by brewing tea in milk.
- In Britain, people usually add a little milk to tea made with water.
- Gogo no Kocha was launched in 1965.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: the name comes from Japan.  2. True.  3. True.  4. False: 1986.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 19: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: business cards and tea on a table]

- What should Josh have done differently with Ms Kato?
- How is a first meeting with a new partner usually done at your company?
- Have you ever had to explain what someone “really meant”?
- Is it OK for a colleague to explain what you mean in front of a client?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. There’s no single “Japanese” or “British” way to do business: ask about the student’s own experience. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 20: Story · Going home

Saturday, on the train to the airport. Josh and Haruka.

**Josh:** Can I say something? In the meeting, when you said, “What Josh means is…”  
**Haruka:** Yes?  
**Josh:** That wasn’t what I meant. It felt like you were apologising for me.  
**Haruka:** I was trying to help. Ms Kato looked uncomfortable.  
**Josh:** Then tell me before the meeting, not in front of the client.  
**Haruka:** …Fair enough. And maybe let people finish their tea before you ask for a decision.  
**Josh:** Also fair. Let’s talk properly when we’re back in London.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
The meeting went well in the end, but Josh felt Haruka spoke for him. Both have a point.
Hook: Lesson 6 is about giving and receiving feedback. Ask: Who’s right, Josh or Haruka?

</details>


#### Slide 21: Vocabulary · Useful phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Let’s get straight to it. | phrase | 早速本題に入りましょう | let’s start the main business now | Thanks for coming. **Let’s get straight to it.** |
| to speak the same language | idiom | 話が通じる、考え方が近い | to understand each other well because you think in a similar way | Ms Kato and Mrs Hartley **speak the same language**. |
| Your secret’s safe with me. | phrase | 誰にも言わないよ | I won’t tell anyone | Don’t worry. **Your secret’s safe with me.** |
| Fair enough. | phrase | なるほど、それもそうだね | that’s reasonable; I accept that (very common in British English) | “Tell me before the meeting.” “**Fair enough.**” |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Useful phrases from today’s dialogues. “Fair enough” is very British. (~1.5 min)
Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>


#### Slide 22: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- made the main point clear with What… is / It’s… that
- explained what someone else meant
- talked about a meeting across cultures
- reported what happened in a meeting

[image: Photo idea: teacups with cranes]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

For each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 6: Feedback

**Objective:** Students can give and receive feedback clearly, specifically and kindly, talk about how past events affect the present (and how present qualities affected the past) with mixed conditionals, and manage a difficult work conversation (CEFR B2: account for and sustain views; Core Inventory B2: mixed conditionals).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 2 · Going east · Lesson 6

**Feedback**

Today’s goal: give and receive feedback clearly and kindly, and talk about how the past affects the present.

[image: Photo idea: a one-to-one meeting]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

January into February. Back in London, Haruka is too polite about Josh’s new designs, Josh loses a week, and they finally have it out in a one-to-one. Tom and Amy get engaged, and Gwen sees the crane. Last lesson of Unit 2. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: In Japan, the focus groups loved Hartley’s history, and Ms Kato asked for a small trial in April. Haruka realised Gwen’s bird is a crane. On the way home, Josh said Haruka had spoken for him in front of the client (Lesson 5).

Main point: mixed conditionals (If you’d told me on Monday, I wouldn’t be behind now). Practical topic: giving and receiving feedback, one-to-ones and appraisals.

Haruka now does what Mrs Hartley does: her “interesting” means no. Let the student decode it.

Timing: 25 slides, about 46 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip the pronunciation slide, True or false and Useful phrases (saves about 4 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Feedback

Talk with your teacher.

- Have you ever had feedback that really helped you? What was it?
- How do you feel when someone criticises your work?
- Is it harder to give feedback or to receive it?

[image: Photo idea: reading feedback on a document]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. Push for a real example. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: A meeting room in London. Josh’s gold designs are on the screen; Josh is frustrated with Haruka; Priya steps in. Josh points at the designs and throws his hands up at Haruka. Priya calms things down. Haruka looks down.]

- What do you think is happening?
- How does each person feel? How do you know?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Let the student describe the body language first. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · Interesting

The first Monday in January. Josh and Haruka.

**Josh:** Happy New Year! I finished the designs for the small Japan tin over the break. What do you think?  
**Haruka:** Oh… they’re interesting. I just wonder if the gold might be a bit… strong?  
**Josh:** Strong’s good. It stands out on the shelf.  
**Haruka:** Mm. Maybe we could look at it again later.  
**Josh:** Sure. So I’ll carry on with these?  
**Haruka:** Let’s… see how they develop.  
**Josh:** Brilliant. I’ll have all six ready by Friday.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together, then swap. (~2 min)
Haruka has become Mrs Hartley: “interesting”, “I just wonder if…”, “Maybe we could look at it again later.” Josh hears a yes.
Don’t explain. The check slide asks the student to decode Haruka.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- What has Josh designed?
- What does Haruka really think of the gold?
- What does Josh think she means?
- What will Josh do this week?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Designs for the small Japan tin.  2. There’s too much. She doesn’t like it.  3. That she’s happy and he should carry on.  4. Finish all six designs by Friday.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. The student should be able to decode Haruka by now: praise them. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · At work (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to push (something) up | phrasal verb | （価格などを）押し上げる | to make a price or number higher | Gold will **push** the price **up**. |
| to be behind | phrase | （予定より）遅れている | to be late with work that should be done by now | I’m two days **behind**. |
| to take (something) offline | phrase | （会議の外で）別途話す | to discuss something later, in private, not in the meeting | Let’s **take this offline**. |
| a one-to-one | noun | 1対1の面談 | a private meeting between two people, often a manager and a team member | I’ve got a **one-to-one** with Priya. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Feedback (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| constructive | adjective | 建設的な | useful and meant to help, not just to criticise | Keep your feedback **constructive**. |
| to be taken aback | phrase | 面食らう、たじろぐ | to be very surprised and a little upset | I was **taken aback** by what he said. |
| to own up (to something) | phrasal verb | （非を）認める | to admit that you did something wrong | She **owned up** to her mistake. |
| to clear the air | idiom | わだかまりを解く | to talk about a problem so that people stop feeling angry | I’m glad we **cleared the air**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come later in the lesson. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · Friday

Friday morning. Priya, Josh and Haruka.

**Priya:** These are lovely, Josh. But Ms Kato wants a simple tin that’s cheap to print. All this gold will **push** the price **up**.  
**Josh:** Gold? Haruka saw these on Monday. She didn’t say anything.  
**Haruka:** I did say the gold might be a bit strong…  
**Josh:** You said they were “interesting”! If you’d told me on Monday, I wouldn’t be this far **behind** now.  
**Priya:** OK. Let’s **take this offline**.  
**Josh:** Sorry, but it’s true. You never say what you actually think.  
**Priya:** Right. You two, book a **one-to-one**. Today.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Josh is right about the problem, but wrong to say it in front of Priya. Haruka is hurt.
In Lesson 5 they agreed to “talk properly” back in London. With Christmas in between, they never did.
Josh’s “If you’d told me on Monday, I wouldn’t be…” is a mixed conditional. Don’t explain it yet.
Ask: Who’s right?

</details>


#### Slide 9: Culture · One-to-ones

> **Feedback at work in the UK**
>
> In many UK workplaces, people have regular **one-to-ones**: short private meetings, often with their manager, to talk about work, problems and goals.
>
> Many companies also have a yearly **appraisal** (or performance review). You look back at the year and agree goals for the next one.
>
> Feedback, especially criticism, is usually given in private, not in front of the team. That’s why Priya stops the conversation.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Explains Priya’s reaction straight after the scene. Workplaces vary a lot; this is common practice, not a rule. (~1.5 min)
Ask: Does your company have one-to-ones or appraisals? How do they work?

</details>


#### Slide 10: Listening · Priya’s tip

Before the one-to-one, Priya sends Haruka a voice message.

[audio: Priya’s voice message]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Haruka, quick tip for your one-to-one. Be specific: say what happened and what it caused. Keep it constructive. And if I were you, I’d listen first.

- What two things should Haruka say?
- What kind of feedback should it be?
- What does Priya advise Haruka to do first?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. What happened, and what it caused.
> 2. Constructive.
> 3. Listen.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Haruka, quick tip for your one-to-one. Be specific: say what happened and what it caused. Keep it constructive. And if I were you, I’d listen first.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~1.5 min)
Priya is describing a common feedback model (situation, behaviour, impact). It’s in the reading later.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Story · The one-to-one

Friday afternoon, a meeting room. Haruka and Josh.

**Haruka:** Thanks for doing this. Can I start? It’s about the meeting with Ms Kato.  
**Josh:** Go on.  
**Haruka:** When you asked for a decision before she’d finished her tea, she looked uncomfortable. That’s why I jumped in.  
**Josh:** OK. I can see that now. If I were more patient, I’d have waited.  
**Haruka:** But you had a point. I should have talked to you before the meeting, not in front of her.  
**Josh:** Thanks. That means a lot.  
**Haruka:** So… your turn. Be honest.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Haruka follows Priya’s tip: what happened, and what it caused. Then she owns her part.
Josh’s “If I were more patient, I’d have waited” is another mixed conditional.

</details>


#### Slide 12: Story · Your turn

The one-to-one continues. Josh and Haruka.

**Josh:** OK. On Monday, you said my designs were “interesting”. I thought you liked them.  
**Haruka:** I was trying to be kind.  
**Josh:** I know. But I spent a whole week on the wrong thing. If you were more direct, we’d have saved a week.  
**Haruka:** …You’re right. I was **taken aback** this morning, but I’ll **own up** to it.  
**Josh:** And I’m sorry I said it in front of Priya. That wasn’t fair.  
**Haruka:** So, going forward, I’ll tell you what I think. Clearly.  
**Josh:** And I’ll let people finish their tea. Glad we **cleared the air**.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
The turning point for Haruka and Josh: both give feedback and both accept it. Haruka learns to be clearer; Josh learns to soften.
Ask: Who gave better feedback, Haruka or Josh?

</details>


#### Slide 13: Language focus · If you’d told me…

> **Mixed conditionals**
>
> *Past → now* (If + had + past participle, would + verb)
>
> **If you’d told** me on Monday, I **wouldn’t be** behind now.
>
> *Always / now → past* (If + past simple, would have + past participle)
>
> **If I were** more patient, I**’d have waited**.

**From the story**
- Josh: **If you’d told** me on Monday, I **wouldn’t be** this far behind now.
- Josh: **If I were** more patient, I**’d have waited**.
- Josh: **If you were** more direct, we**’d have saved** a week.

Use mixed conditionals when the condition and the result are at different times.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Mixed conditionals mix time. Past condition → present result: If you’d told me on Monday (past), I wouldn’t be behind now (present). Present or general condition → past result: If I were more patient (always), I’d have waited (that day). The second and third conditionals were taught in the intermediate course; this lesson only mixes them. (~2.5 min)
Many Japanese learners keep both halves in the same time. Ask: Is this part about the past, or now?

</details>


#### Slide 14: Practice · Past and present

> *Pattern*
>
> If + had done…, would + verb (now)
> If + past simple…, would have done

- You didn’t tell me on Monday. I’m behind now.
- I didn’t sleep on the plane. I’m tired now.
- Haruka went to Osaka. Now she knows about the crane.
- Josh doesn’t speak Japanese. He didn’t understand the focus groups.
- Haruka is kind. She didn’t say no.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. If you’d told me on Monday, I wouldn’t be behind now.
> 2. If I’d slept on the plane, I wouldn’t be tired now.
> 3. If Haruka hadn’t gone to Osaka, she wouldn’t know about the crane.
> 4. If Josh spoke Japanese, he’d have understood the focus groups.
> 5. If Haruka weren’t so kind, she’d have said no.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student joins each pair into one mixed conditional. Then ask: Finish the sentence about your life: If I hadn’t…, I wouldn’t be… now. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Pronunciation · Sounding sincere

> **Wider range = more sincere**
>
> That’s really **HELP**ful. ↗↘ (sincere)
> That’s really helpful. → (flat: bored or sarcastic)
> Thanks for being **HON**est. ↗↘

**Say it with your teacher**
- That means a **lot**.
- Thanks for being **hon**est.
- I really ap**pre**ciate that.
- **Fair** enough. You’re **right**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

When we thank someone for feedback, a wide voice range (high on the key word, then down) often sounds more sincere and engaged. A flat, low voice can sound less interested, or even sarcastic, depending on the context. (~1.5 min)
Many Japanese learners keep a narrow pitch range in English, which can sound cold. Say each line twice, flat then sincere, and ask the student which sounds kind.

</details>


#### Slide 16: Story · Engaged!

Saturday evening at Gwen’s. Tom, Amy, Gwen and Haruka.

**Tom:** Everyone, Amy and I have some news.  
**Amy:** We’re engaged! He asked me on Christmas Day, at my parents’.  
**Gwen:** Oh, I knew it! I’m over the moon for you both. Let me see that ring.  
**Haruka:** Congratulations! When’s the wedding?  
**Tom:** July. And Haruka, we’d love you to give a speech. You were brilliant at the pub.  
**Haruka:** Me? I’d be honoured… and terrified.  
**Gwen:** **If** Tom **hadn’t met** Amy, he**’d still be** eating cereal for dinner.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Callback: Haruka gave a short speech at the pub at the end of the intermediate course.
“Over the moon” comes back from Lesson 5. Gwen’s teasing is affectionate banter.
The wedding is the finale of the course (Lesson 12).

</details>


#### Slide 17: Story · That’s my bird!

Later that evening. Haruka, Gwen, Tom and Amy.

**Haruka:** Gwen, before I forget. Look at this photo from my mum’s house.  
**Gwen:** Those cups… That’s it! That’s the bird on my nan’s tin!  
**Haruka:** It’s a crane. In Japan, cranes mean long life and good luck.  
**Tom:** So a London tea company put a Japanese bird on its tins? Why?  
**Amy:** Maybe someone at Hartley’s had been to Japan.  
**Gwen:** Back then? That was a long way to go for a cup of tea.  
**Haruka:** I’m going to ask Mrs Hartley. Again.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Gwen confirms that the crane on Grandma’s cups (Lesson 5) is the bird she remembers.
Puzzle: Amy’s guess is a good one. Ask: Who at Hartley’s could have been to Japan? Write the student’s ideas down for Unit 3.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Role play · A one-to-one

> **Student**
>
> Yesterday your colleague Sam sent a client the wrong file. The client was confused, and now you’re fixing it.
>
> • Say what happened and what it caused.
> • Listen to Sam.
> • Agree what to do next time.

**Useful phrases**
- Have you got a minute?
- Yesterday, when you…
- The result was…
- If you’d checked, we wouldn’t be…
- How do you see it?
- What could we do next time?

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re Sam.
>
> • First, be a bit defensive: “Nobody told me which file!”
> • Then own up to it.
> • Ask for one piece of advice.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student gives feedback; you’re Sam. Everything you need is on the cards. No swap needed. (~3 min)
Listen for what happened + what it caused, a mixed conditional, and a constructive ending.

</details>


#### Slide 19: Progress check · Unit 2 check

> **Student**
>
> You’re Haruka. Gwen wants to hear about Japan and your week.
>
> • Tell her about the trip: what went well, and the meeting with Ms Kato.
> • Tell her what happened with Josh, and what you learned.
> • Tell her what you’ll be doing next month.

**Useful phrases**
- What I loved was…
- It was… that…
- If I’d…, I wouldn’t be…
- I’ll be… -ing
- I should have…
- Next time, I’ll…

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re Gwen.
>
> • Ask: So what was the best part of Japan?
> • Ask: Are you and Josh friends again?
> • Ask: What would you have done differently?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Unit 2 progress check. The student plays Haruka and uses the story, so there’s nothing to invent. Let the student lead and don’t correct during the conversation. Note what they do well and one thing to practise. (~3 min)
Look for: the future continuous (I’ll be…), cleft sentences (What they loved was…), mixed conditionals, and giving feedback kindly but clearly.

</details>


#### Slide 20: Reading · Feedback that helps

[image: Photo idea: two colleagues talking over coffee]

Good feedback is specific. “Great job!” feels nice but doesn’t help anyone improve, and “You’re always late!” starts an argument.

Many managers use a simple model called **SBI**: Situation, Behaviour, Impact. “In Monday’s meeting, you interrupted Yuki twice, and she stopped sharing ideas.”

Give feedback soon and in private. Then stop talking, and ask how the other person sees it.

When you receive feedback, try not to defend yourself straight away. Say thank you, ask questions, and take time to think. If you disagree, you can still say: “Thanks. I see it differently, and here’s why.”

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: What does SBI stand for? When and where should you give feedback? What should you do when you receive it?
Sources: Center for Creative Leadership, “Situation-Behavior-Impact (SBI) feedback model”. The other tips are common workplace practice.

</details>


#### Slide 21: Reading · True or false?

- “Great job!” is very useful feedback.
- SBI stands for Situation, Behaviour, Impact.
- It’s best to give feedback in front of the team.
- When you get feedback, defend yourself straight away.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: it isn’t specific.  2. True.  3. False: give it in private.  4. False: say thank you, ask questions and think first.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 22: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a notebook and green tea on a desk]

- How is feedback usually given where you work or study?
- Do you prefer direct feedback or gentle feedback? Why?
- Was Haruka wrong to say “interesting”? What would you have said?
- Is it easier to give feedback in English or in Japanese? Why?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan and their own workplace; don’t tell them. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 23: Story · Back on track

February, at the office. Josh, Haruka and Priya.

**Josh:** Right. One colour, no gold, the old Hartley’s lettering. Be honest.  
**Haruka:** Honestly? I love it. But the lettering is too small.  
**Josh:** See? That wasn’t so hard.  
**Priya:** Look at you two, being honest. Mrs Hartley wants to see it on the 1st of March. No pressure.  
**Haruka:** Did she reply about the crane?  
**Priya:** “How very curious. I shall speak to our archivist.”  
**Josh:** “Curious.” Is that good or bad?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Haruka is now clear and kind at the same time: praise first, then one specific problem.
Hook: Unit 3 starts with the meeting with Mrs Hartley, and the archive. Ask: What do you think “curious” means here?

</details>


#### Slide 24: Vocabulary · Useful phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Going forward, … | phrase | 今後は | from now on (common in business) | **Going forward**, I’ll tell you what I think. |
| That means a lot. | phrase | そう言ってもらえると嬉しい | thank you, that’s important to me | Thanks. **That means a lot.** |
| to jump in | phrasal verb | （話に）割って入る | to start speaking suddenly, often to help or interrupt | That’s why I **jumped in**. |
| No pressure. | phrase | プレッシャーかけるつもりはないけど（冗談） | said as a joke when there is a lot of pressure | The client’s coming tomorrow. **No pressure.** |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Useful phrases from today’s dialogues. “No pressure” is usually ironic in British English. (~1.5 min)
Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>


#### Slide 25: Wrap-up · Unit 2 check

**In Unit 2, I can…**
- plan a business trip and ask politely about plans
- explain what someone means and make my main point clear
- give and receive feedback clearly and kindly
- talk about how the past affects the present

[image: Photo idea: a new tea tin design]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Self-check for Unit 2: for each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1.5 min)

</details>


## Unit: Pressure


### Lesson 7: Back to the drawing board

**Objective:** Students can deal with a setback at work, postpone a deadline politely, and speculate about past events with degrees of certainty, including what wasn’t necessary (needn’t have, might well have, can’t possibly have) (CEFR B2: speculating; Core Inventory B2: modals of deduction and speculation, needn’t have).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 3 · Pressure · Lesson 7

**Back to the drawing board**

Today’s goal: deal with a setback, postpone a deadline politely, and speculate about what happened.

[image: Photo idea: starting again on a new sketch]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

March. Mrs Hartley calls the new design “interesting”: it doesn’t tell a story. The team works out what went wrong, Josh calls Ms Kato to postpone the trial, and the archive finally opens. First lesson of Unit 3. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Haruka and Josh cleared the air in a one-to-one. Tom and Amy got engaged. Gwen confirmed that the crane is the bird on her nan’s tin, and Mrs Hartley said she would speak to her archivist (Lesson 6).

Main point: modal perfects for speculating and for things that weren’t necessary (needn’t have, might well have, can’t possibly have). Must have / can’t have / should have were taught in the intermediate course: don’t re-teach them. Practical topic: handling a setback and postponing a deadline.

Timing: 21 slides, about 38 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip the pronunciation slide, True or false and Useful phrases (saves about 4 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Setbacks

Talk with your teacher.

- Tell me about a time a plan went wrong. What did you do?
- How do you feel when a client or boss says no?
- Is it better to ask for more time or to rush?

[image: Photo idea: a setback at work]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. Push for a real example. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: Mrs Hartley’s office. She looks at the new tin and puts it down politely. Mrs Hartley turns the tin, smiles and puts it down. Josh’s smile drops. Haruka nods slowly.]

- What does Mrs Hartley do with the tin?
- How do Josh and Haruka react? What do you think she said?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Let the student describe the body language and guess. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · A story

1 March, at Hartley’s. Josh, Mrs Hartley and Haruka.

**Josh:** So: one colour, the original lettering, and a smaller tin for Japan.  
**Mrs Hartley:** Well. It’s very tasteful. Interesting.  
**Haruka:** Is there anything you’d like us to change?  
**Mrs Hartley:** It’s lovely. It just doesn’t… tell me anything. A tin should tell a story.  
**Josh:** Right. A story.  
**Mrs Hartley:** Now, don’t look so worried. Ruth, our archivist, says you may visit the archive next Thursday.  
**Haruka:** Thank you. We’d love that.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together, then swap. (~2 min)
Callback: “interesting” was the first word the student decoded (Lesson 1). Josh and Haruka both know what it means now.
The archive visit was Haruka’s request in her follow-up email (Lesson 2).

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- What does Mrs Hartley really think of the design?
- What’s missing, in her opinion?
- What’s the good news?
- How do you think Josh feels?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. She doesn’t want it (“interesting”).  2. A story.  3. They can visit the archive next Thursday.  4. Disappointed, maybe a bit lost.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · Setbacks (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a quote | noun | 見積もり | a price a company says it will charge for a job | We got three printing **quotes**. |
| a setback | noun | （一時的な）つまずき、後退 | a problem that stops progress for a while | It’s a **setback**, not a disaster. |
| to make it | phrase | 間に合う | to manage to finish something or arrive in time | We won’t **make it** by April. |
| to postpone | verb | 延期する | to move an event to a later time | Could we **postpone** the trial? |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogues. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · In the archive (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| faded | adjective | 色あせた | less bright than before, because it’s old | The label is **faded**. |
| to date back to | phrasal verb | （ある時代に）さかのぼる | to have existed since a time in the past | These tins **date back to** the 1920s. |
| a blend | noun | ブレンド（茶葉の配合） | a mix of different teas | It’s a **blend** of three teas. |
| to piece (something) together | phrasal verb | （断片から）全体像をつかむ | to understand something by putting small facts together | We’re trying to **piece** the story **together**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come later in the lesson. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · What went wrong?

Back at the office. Priya, Josh, Yuki and Haruka.

**Priya:** So? How did it go?  
**Josh:** She said “interesting”. Twice. It’s back to the drawing board.  
**Yuki:** She can’t possibly have hated it. It’s beautiful.  
**Haruka:** She didn’t hate it. She might well have liked it. But it doesn’t tell a story.  
**Priya:** And I’ve already got three printing **quotes**. I needn’t have bothered.  
**Josh:** What about the trial with Ms Kato? We’ll never **make it** in April.  
**Priya:** Then someone needs to call her. Today.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Yuki is back: more confident and direct than a year ago.
The modal perfects (can’t possibly have, might well have, needn’t have) come in the language focus. Don’t explain them yet.
“Back to the drawing board” = start again with a new plan (it’s on the phrases slide).

</details>


#### Slide 9: Listening · A message from Mrs Hartley

That afternoon, Mrs Hartley leaves Priya a voicemail.

[audio: Mrs Hartley’s voicemail]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Priya, Margaret Hartley. Please don’t worry about the timeline. I’d rather wait for the right idea than rush the wrong one. Lovely.

- What does Mrs Hartley say about the timeline?
- What would she rather do?
- Is she ending the project?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Don’t worry about it.
> 2. Wait for the right idea, not rush the wrong one.
> 3. No. She’s giving them more time.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Priya, Margaret Hartley. Please don’t worry about the timeline. I’d rather wait for the right idea than rush the wrong one. Lovely.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~1.5 min)
This time Mrs Hartley is clear. Ask: Why do you think she’s being so direct now?

</details>


#### Slide 10: Language focus · She can’t possibly have…

> **Modal perfects**
>
> *It wasn’t necessary (but you did it)*
>
> I **needn’t have bothered**. · We **needn’t have rushed**.
>
> *Quite likely*
>
> She **might well have liked** it. · He **may well have forgotten**.
>
> *Definitely not*
>
> She **can’t possibly have hated** it.

**From the story**
- Yuki: She **can’t possibly have hated** it.
- Haruka: She **might well have liked** it.
- Priya: I **needn’t have bothered**.

Use modal perfects to say how sure you are about the past, or that something wasn’t necessary.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Modal perfects look back at the past. This lesson adds three forms to must / can’t / should have from the intermediate course. Needn’t have done = you did it, but it wasn’t necessary (compare didn’t need to, which usually means you didn’t do it). Might well have = quite likely. Can’t possibly have = definitely not. (~2.5 min)
Many Japanese learners confuse needn’t have and didn’t need to. Ask: Did Priya get the quotes? (Yes, but it wasn’t necessary.)

</details>


#### Slide 11: Practice · How sure are you?

> *Pattern*
>
> needn’t have / might well have / can’t possibly have
> + past participle

- Priya got three quotes, but they weren’t needed. (needn’t)
- I’m fairly sure Mrs Hartley liked the colours. (might well)
- It’s impossible that Josh forgot the meeting. (can’t possibly)
- We took umbrellas, but it didn’t rain. (needn’t)
- It’s quite likely Ms Kato expected this. (might well)

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Priya needn’t have got the quotes.
> 2. She might well have liked the colours.
> 3. Josh can’t possibly have forgotten the meeting.
> 4. We needn’t have taken umbrellas.
> 5. Ms Kato might well have expected this.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student rewrites each sentence with the words in brackets. Then ask: Tell me about something you needn’t have done recently. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 12: Pronunciation · Stress and certainty

> **Stress the certainty word**
>
> She might have liked it. (maybe)
> She might **WELL** have liked it. (quite likely)
> She can’t **POS**sibly have hated it. (definitely not)

**Say it with your teacher**
- She might **well** have liked it.
- He can’t **pos**sibly have forgotten.
- I **needn’t** have bothered.
- They may **well** have stopped in the war.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The stressed word shows how sure you are. In “might WELL have”, stress on WELL makes it more likely. In “can’t POSsibly”, the stress makes it stronger. “Have” stays weak: /əv/. (~1.5 min)
Say each pair; the student says how sure the speaker sounds.

</details>


#### Slide 13: Story · The call

That afternoon, Josh calls Ms Kato. Haruka listens.

**Ms Kato:** Hello, Josh. Is everything all right?  
**Josh:** Yes, thanks. Well, mostly. I’m afraid we’ve had a bit of a **setback** with the design.  
**Ms Kato:** I see. Will it affect the trial?  
**Josh:** I’m sorry, but I don’t think we can **make it** in April. Would it be possible to **postpone** it until June?  
**Ms Kato:** Thank you for telling me early. June might be possible. I’ll talk to my team.  
**Josh:** Of course. Take your time. There’s no rush.  
**Ms Kato:** How patient! Has Haruka been giving you lessons?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Josh has changed since Lesson 5: he softens (I’m afraid, a bit of a setback, Would it be possible…?) and doesn’t push for a decision.
Ask: What does Josh do differently from the Tokyo meeting?

</details>


#### Slide 14: Story · The archive

Next Thursday, in the archive. Ruth, Josh and Haruka.

**Ruth:** Mind the dust. I found these while I was cataloguing. They **date back to** the 1920s.  
**Josh:** Haruka, look. A white bird, long legs, a red cap.  
**Haruka:** Gwen’s crane! “Hartley’s Crane **Blend**.” And there’s Japanese writing in the corner.  
**Ruth:** We’ve never known what it says. Can you read it?  
**Haruka:** It’s **faded**, but… “Uji”. That’s a famous tea area near Kyoto. And a name: Morita.  
**Ruth:** How odd. The orders stop in 1939. And there are boxes of old letters I haven’t opened yet.  
**Josh:** So why did they stop?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~2 min)
Puzzle, clue 1: the crane tin is real, it was a blend called Crane Blend, and it has a Japanese place (Uji, near Kyoto, famous for tea) and a name (Morita). The orders stop in 1939, and there are unopened boxes of letters.
Teacher only: Morita is a tea grower who worked with Edith Hartley, the founder. The letters come in Lesson 8. Don’t say so.

</details>


#### Slide 15: Story · Why 1939?

Back at the office. Yuki, Priya, Josh and Haruka.

**Yuki:** So the crane tins stopped in 1939. Why?  
**Priya:** The start of the war **might well have stopped** it. Things were getting difficult between Britain and Japan too.  
**Josh:** Or after that, nobody wanted a Japanese bird on a British tin.  
**Haruka:** Maybe. But someone at Hartley’s **can’t possibly have chosen** a crane by accident. Who was Morita?  
**Yuki:** There must be letters. Companies kept everything back then.  
**Josh:** Ruth has boxes of old letters she hasn’t opened yet.  
**Haruka:** Then let’s **piece** it **together**. Mrs Hartley wanted a tin that tells a story…  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
The team speculates with today’s modal perfects. Priya’s idea is reasonable: relations between Britain and Japan got worse in the late 1930s, and they were at war from December 1941.
Hook: Haruka connects the crane to Mrs Hartley’s “story”. Ask: What do you think the story is?

</details>


#### Slide 16: Role play · What went wrong?

> **Student**
>
> A client, Bloom Bakery, suddenly stopped answering your emails. You know:
>
> • Their manager left last month.
> • They asked for your price twice.
>
> Work out what happened, and decide what to do.

**Useful phrases**
- They might well have…
- They can’t possibly have…
- We needn’t have…
- That would explain it.
- What if they…?
- Let’s call them.

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re a colleague. You know:
>
> • Another agency is much cheaper.
> • Bloom posted a new logo online yesterday.
>
> Share one clue at a time. Agree what to do next.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

You and the student are colleagues. Each card has different clues: share them one at a time and work out together what happened. No swap needed. (~3 min)
Teacher only: Bloom Bakery found a cheaper agency and used them for the new logo. Let the student get there.
Listen for might well have, can’t possibly have and needn’t have.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Reading · Tea in wartime

[image: Photo idea: an old teapot and a ration book]

During the Second World War, the British government controlled the country’s tea, because almost all of it came from abroad by ship.

In July 1940, tea was **rationed**. Each adult could have about 2 ounces (57 grams) a week, enough for around three weak cups a day.

Tea was seen as important for people’s spirits, and the government made sure there was always some.

Rationing continued long after the war. Tea was finally taken off the ration in October 1952.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: When did tea rationing start and end? How much tea could an adult have?
Sources: History.co.uk, “Tea rationing ends” (October 1952); CooksInfo and Teas.co.uk, UK tea rationing 1940–1952 (2 oz per adult per week from July 1940).

</details>


#### Slide 18: Reading · True or false?

- Most of Britain’s tea was grown in Britain.
- Tea was rationed from July 1940.
- Each adult could have about 2 ounces a week.
- Tea rationing ended when the war ended.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: almost all of it came from abroad.  2. True.  3. True.  4. False: it ended in October 1952, seven years later.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 19: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a calendar with changed dates]

- When a project goes wrong in Japan, what usually happens?
- Is it easy to ask a client to postpone something? How would you do it?
- Why do you think Hartley’s used a Japanese crane on its tins?
- What do you think Mrs Hartley means by “a story”?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 20: Vocabulary · Useful phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| back to the drawing board | idiom | 振り出しに戻る | we have to start again with a new plan | It didn’t work. **Back to the drawing board.** |
| I needn’t have bothered. | phrase | （やったけど）無駄だった | what I did wasn’t necessary | They cancelled. **I needn’t have bothered.** |
| Mind the… | phrase | 〜に気をつけて | be careful of… | **Mind the** dust. · **Mind the** step. |
| There’s no rush. | phrase | 急がなくていいですよ | you don’t need to hurry | Take your time. **There’s no rush.** |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Useful phrases from today’s dialogues. (~1.5 min)
Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>


#### Slide 21: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- talked about a setback and what went wrong
- postponed a deadline politely
- speculated with might well have, can’t possibly have
- said what wasn’t necessary with needn’t have

[image: Photo idea: an old crane tin in an archive]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

For each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 8: The archive

**Objective:** Students can summarise information from several sources, use participle clauses to make summaries shorter and more formal, and report what people say or believe about the past with reporting passives (CEFR B2–C1: processing text; summarising; Core Inventory B2: passives).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 3 · Pressure · Lesson 8

**The archive**

Today’s goal: summarise what you’ve found out, and talk about what people say or believe about the past.

[image: Photo idea: old letters in an archive]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

April. In the Hartley’s archive, the team finds letters between Edith Hartley, the founder, and Mr Morita, a tea grower in Uji. Gwen remembers the missing piece, and the idea for the pitch is born. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Mrs Hartley said the new design didn’t “tell a story”. Josh postponed the Japan trial to June. In the archive, Ruth showed them a “Crane Blend” tin with the words “Uji” and “Morita”. The orders stop in 1939, and there are unopened boxes of letters (Lesson 7).

Main points: participle clauses (Written in 1923, the letter…; Having read that, …) and reporting passives (She’s said to have…; It’s thought to be…). Practical topic: researching and summarising what you found.

Puzzle: every clue is on a slide. Students can piece the story together before the team does.

Timing: 24 slides, about 44 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip the pronunciation slide, True or false and Useful phrases (saves about 4 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Looking back

Talk with your teacher.

- What do you know about your grandparents’ or great-grandparents’ lives?
- Does your family keep old letters or photos?
- Is there an old company or shop near you with an interesting history?

[image: Photo idea: an old family photo album]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. Students don’t need to share private family details. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: The Hartley’s archive. Ruth opens a box of old letters; Yuki, Haruka and Josh look inside. Ruth opens the box. Yuki points at the stamps. Haruka unfolds a letter and her eyes widen.]

- Where are they? What are they looking at?
- How does Haruka react? What do you think she has found?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Let the student describe and guess. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · The boxes

April, in the archive. Ruth, Yuki, Josh and Haruka.

**Ruth:** Gloves on, please. Nobody has opened these boxes for seventy years.  
**Yuki:** Look at these stamps. They’re Japanese!  
**Ruth:** All addressed to E. Hartley. Edith started the company in 1925.  
**Josh:** Mrs Hartley’s great-grandmother?  
**Ruth:** That’s right. She’s said to have travelled a lot when she was young, but nobody knew where.  
**Haruka:** These are from Uji. And the sender is… K. Morita.  
**Josh:** Morita! The name on the tin.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together, then swap. (~2 min)
Yuki joins the archive visit: she’s an illustrator and loves old designs.
“She’s said to have travelled…” is a reporting passive. It’s taught later; don’t explain it yet.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- What’s special about the envelopes?
- Who was Edith Hartley?
- What does Ruth know about Edith?
- Who sent the letters?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. They have Japanese stamps.  2. The founder of Hartley’s (1925), Mrs Hartley’s great-grandmother.  3. People say she travelled a lot when she was young, but nobody knew where.  4. K. Morita, from Uji.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · Old letters (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a symbol | noun | 象徴、シンボル | a picture or thing that represents an idea or a group | The crane is a **symbol** of long life. |
| a generation | noun | 世代 | all the people in a family born at about the same time | Five **generations** have grown tea here. |
| to correspond (with someone) | verb | 文通する | to write letters to each other | They **corresponded** for years. |
| fragile | adjective | 壊れやすい | easily broken or damaged | Be careful. The paper is **fragile**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the letter and the next dialogue. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Pieces of the story (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to be moved | phrase | 感動する、心を動かされる | to feel strong emotion, often sadness or warmth | I was really **moved** by the letter. |
| to keep (something) under wraps | idiom | （当面）秘密にしておく | to keep something secret for now | Let’s **keep it under wraps** until June. |
| a breakthrough | noun | 大発見、突破口 | an important discovery that helps you move forward | The letters are a real **breakthrough**. |
| to fall into place | idiom | （全体が）つながる、腑に落ちる | to suddenly make sense | Now everything **falls into place**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come later in the lesson. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · Edith’s letter

[image: Photo idea: an old handwritten letter]

Uji, 14th May 1923

Dearest Mother,

Mr Morita has shown me every corner of his tea garden. His family has grown tea on these hills for five **generations**, and every chest that leaves the farm is painted with a white crane, the family’s **symbol**.

He says the crane means a long life and a long friendship. I asked whether we might sell his tea in London. He laughed, and said that cranes fly a long way.

I think I have found the beginning of something.

Your loving daughter,
Edith

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Clue 2. The student reads Edith’s letter aloud, then answers: Who is Mr Morita? What does the crane mean to his family? What does Edith want to do? (~2.5 min)
Edith and Mr Morita are fictional. Uji, near Kyoto, is a real and famous tea area.

</details>


#### Slide 9: Story · A long friendship

In the archive. Yuki, Haruka, Josh and Ruth.

**Yuki:** So the crane was the Morita family’s **symbol**.  
**Haruka:** Written in 1923, this letter is from two years before the company started.  
**Josh:** So Edith and Mr Morita **corresponded** for years?  
**Ruth:** Looking through this box, I found one more. It’s thought to be the last letter he sent. Careful, it’s **fragile**.  
**Haruka:** 1939. “It is no longer possible to send tea to London. I hope one day the cranes will fly again.”  
**Yuki:** That’s so sad. I’m actually quite **moved**.  
**Josh:** Having read that, I feel terrible about my neon pink.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Clue 3: Mr Morita’s last letter, 1939. Callback: Edith wrote that “cranes fly a long way”; Morita hopes they’ll “fly again”.
Josh’s joke: his first idea for Hartley’s was neon pink with a cartoon teapot (Lesson 2).

</details>


#### Slide 10: Listening · Not yet!

That evening, Haruka gets a voicemail from Mrs Hartley.

[audio: Mrs Hartley’s voicemail]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Haruka, Margaret Hartley. Ruth tells me you’ve found something. Don’t tell me yet. Do keep it under wraps until the pitch in June. Lovely.

- Who told Mrs Hartley about the discovery?
- What does Mrs Hartley ask Haruka to do?
- When is the pitch?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Ruth, the archivist.
> 2. Keep it secret until the pitch.
> 3. In June.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Haruka, Margaret Hartley. Ruth tells me you’ve found something. Don’t tell me yet. Do keep it under wraps until the pitch in June. Lovely.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~1.5 min)
Mrs Hartley wants a surprise, so the team can’t tell her the story yet.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Language focus · Written in 1923, …

> **Participle clauses**
>
> *Past participle (passive meaning)*
>
> **Written** in 1923, the letter describes the tea garden.
>
> *-ing (active, often at the same time)*
>
> **Looking** through the box, Ruth found one more letter.
>
> *Having + past participle (one thing, then another)*
>
> **Having read** the letter, Josh felt terrible.

**From the story**
- Haruka: **Written** in 1923, this letter is from two years before…
- Ruth: **Looking** through this box, I found one more.
- Josh: **Having read** that, I feel terrible about my neon pink.

Use participle clauses to make summaries shorter and more formal.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Participle clauses make writing and summaries shorter and more formal. -ed participle = passive meaning (Written in 1923 = The letter was written in 1923). -ing = active, often at the same time. Having + past participle = one action finished before the next. The subject of both parts must be the same: “Having read that, I feel…” (I read it, I feel). (~2.5 min)
Many Japanese learners join clauses with and / so. Participle clauses are a C1 tool for summaries and presentations.

</details>


#### Slide 12: Practice · Make it shorter

> *Pattern*
>
> Written / Made… , … · Looking… , …
> Having + past participle… , …

- The letter was written in Uji. It describes a tea garden.
- Ruth looked through the box. She found another letter.
- Edith visited Uji. Then she started a company.
- The tins were made in London. They had a crane on the lid.
- Haruka read the letter. Then she called Priya.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Written in Uji, the letter describes a tea garden.
> 2. Looking through the box, Ruth found another letter.
> 3. Having visited Uji, Edith started a company.
> 4. Made in London, the tins had a crane on the lid.
> 5. Having read the letter, Haruka called Priya.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student joins each pair of sentences with a participle clause. Check that both parts have the same subject. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Pronunciation · Pause and continue

> **Rise, pause, then fall**
>
> **WRIT**ten in 1923 ↗ | the letter describes a **GAR**den. ↘
> **HAV**ing read that ↗ | I feel **TER**rible. ↘

**Say it with your teacher**
- Looking through the **box** ↗ | Ruth found one **more**. ↘
- Having visited **U**ji ↗ | Edith started a **com**pany. ↘
- Made in **Lon**don ↗ | the tins had a **crane**. ↘

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

A participle clause at the start of a sentence ends with a small rise and a pause, like a comma you can hear. This tells the listener “there’s more coming”. The main clause then falls at the end. (~1.5 min)
Many Japanese learners run the clause into the rest of the sentence. Model the pause, then the student repeats.

</details>


#### Slide 14: Story · Gwen remembers

Sunday at Gwen’s. Haruka, Gwen and Tom.

**Haruka:** Gwen, we found letters. The crane was a tea family’s symbol, in Uji. The orders stopped in 1939.  
**Gwen:** 1939… **Thinking** about it, my nan worked at Hartley’s, you know. In the packing room.  
**Tom:** You never told us that!  
**Gwen:** You never asked. She was said to be the fastest packer in the building.  
**Haruka:** Did she ever talk about the crane tins?  
**Gwen:** Only once. On the last day, Edith gave every girl a crane tin and said, “One day, the cranes will come back.”  
**Haruka:** So that’s where your tin came from. It’s all **falling into place**.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Clue 4, Gwen’s missing piece: her nan packed tea at Hartley’s, and the crane tin was Edith’s goodbye gift when the crane tins stopped in 1939.
Callback: Gwen kept her buttons in that tin (Lesson 1). Edith’s words echo Mr Morita’s letter (“the cranes will fly again”).
“Thinking about it” is a fixed participle phrase: very common in speech.

</details>


#### Slide 15: Language focus · She’s said to have…

> **Reporting passives**
>
> *Now*
>
> The farm **is thought to be** in Uji. · **It’s believed that** the family still grows tea.
>
> *The past*
>
> Edith **is said to have travelled** a lot. · She **was said to be** the fastest packer.

**From the story**
- Ruth: She**’s said to have travelled** a lot when she was young.
- Ruth: It**’s thought to be** the last letter he sent.
- Gwen: She **was said to be** the fastest packer in the building.

Use reporting passives to say what people say, think or believe.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Reporting passives say what people generally say, think or believe, without saying who. They’re common in history, news and formal summaries. Present: is said to + verb. Past: is said to have + past participle. With it: It is thought that… / It’s believed that… (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 16: Practice · People say…

> *Pattern*
>
> is said / thought / believed + to + verb
> is said + to have + past participle (the past)

- People say Edith travelled to Japan in 1923. (Edith…)
- People think the farm is still in Uji. (The farm…)
- People believe the tins stopped in 1939. (The tins…)
- People say Gwen’s nan was very fast. (Gwen’s nan…)
- People think the letters are very valuable. (It…)

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Edith is said to have travelled to Japan in 1923.
> 2. The farm is thought to still be in Uji.
> 3. The tins are believed to have stopped in 1939.
> 4. Gwen’s nan is said to have been very fast.
> 5. It is thought that the letters are very valuable.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student rewrites each sentence with a reporting passive, starting with the words in brackets. (~2 min)
Then ask: Tell me something your town or company is said to be famous for.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Story · The idea

Monday at the office. Priya, Haruka, Yuki and Josh.

**Priya:** So, what’s the **breakthrough**?  
**Haruka:** A story. **Having met** Mr Morita in 1923, Edith sold his tea in London until 1939. The crane was his family’s symbol.  
**Yuki:** And the Second World War stopped it. The cranes never came back.  
**Josh:** Until now. We bring Crane Blend back, in Japan, a hundred years later.  
**Priya:** I love it. But is the Morita farm still there?  
**Haruka:** It**’s believed to be** run by his great-grandson now. I’ve emailed them.  
**Priya:** Brilliant. And not a word to Mrs Hartley until June.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
The pitch idea is born: bring back Crane Blend, a hundred-year friendship between a London tea company and a tea family in Uji.
Haruka wrote to the Morita family in Japanese, so we don’t see the email.
Ask: Would you buy this tea? Why?

</details>


#### Slide 18: Role play · Research update

> **Student**
>
> Tell a new colleague about your office building. Your notes:
>
> • Built in 1898 as a biscuit factory.
> • People say it was damaged in the war.
> • People believe there’s a tunnel to the river. Nobody has found it.

**Useful phrases**
- Built in…, it…
- It’s said to have…
- It’s believed that…
- Having been…, it…
- Nobody knows for sure.
- Apparently, …

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re a new colleague.
>
> • Ask: How old is this building?
> • Ask: What happened in the war?
> • Ask: Is there really a tunnel? Can we look for it?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student summarises their research about the office building; you’re a new colleague. All the facts are on the student’s card. No swap needed. (~3 min)
Listen for participle clauses and reporting passives.

</details>


#### Slide 19: Reading · Looking for your family’s past

[image: Photo idea: researching family history online]

Many people in Britain enjoy researching their family history, and a lot of old records are now online.

The census, taken every ten years, is one of the best sources. Census records are usually kept private for 100 years, so the 1921 census only came out in 2022.

The 1931 census for England and Wales was destroyed in a fire during the war, and there was no census in 1941.

That makes the **1939 Register** special. Taken on 29 September 1939, it lists about 41 million people, with their address, date of birth and job.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: Why is the 1939 Register so useful? Why can’t people see the 1931 census?
Teacher note: Gwen’s nan would have been in the 1939 Register, perhaps with her job written as “tea packer”.
Sources: The National Archives / Findmypast, 1939 Register (taken 29 September 1939, about 41 million people, England and Wales); the 1931 census for England and Wales was destroyed in a fire in 1942, and there was no census in 1941; the 1921 census was released on 6 January 2022 (census records are closed for 100 years).

</details>


#### Slide 20: Reading · True or false?

- A census is taken every five years.
- Census records are usually kept private for 100 years.
- The 1931 census was lost in a fire.
- The 1939 Register lists people’s jobs.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: every ten years.  2. True.  3. True (in England and Wales).  4. True.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: tea fields on a hillside]

- How do people in Japan find out about their family history?
- Is there a company in Japan with a famous long history?
- Why do you think stories like Edith’s are good for selling a product?
- Would you like to know what your great-grandparents did?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Some may mention the family register (koseki). Don’t ask for private family details. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 22: Story · Wet shoes

The next morning. Haruka and Josh.

**Haruka:** Josh, why are your shoes wet? It’s sunny outside.  
**Josh:** Don’t ask. My ceiling’s leaking. Water came through the light this morning.  
**Haruka:** Through the light? That’s dangerous! Have you told your landlord?  
**Josh:** Three times. He keeps saying he’ll “sort it out next week”.  
**Haruka:** Then it’s time for a formal letter. I helped Yuki with her flat last year. I know the rules.  
**Josh:** You’d do that?  
**Haruka:** Of course. Bring everything: photos, dates and all his messages.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Callback: Haruka helped Yuki find a flat and avoid a scam in the intermediate course (Lesson 2).
Hook: Lesson 9 is about tenants’ rights and formal complaints. Ask: What should Josh do?

</details>


#### Slide 23: Vocabulary · Useful phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Thinking about it, … | phrase | 考えてみると | said when you suddenly remember or realise something | **Thinking about it**, my nan worked there. |
| You never asked. | phrase | 聞かれなかったから | a friendly reply when someone is surprised you didn’t tell them | “You never told us!” “**You never asked.**” |
| Not a word (to…). | phrase | （〜には）内緒にして | don’t tell anyone (about this) | **Not a word to** Mrs Hartley. |
| Don’t ask. | phrase | 聞かないで | said when something bad or embarrassing happened and you don’t want to explain | “Why are you wet?” “**Don’t ask.**” |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Useful phrases from today’s dialogues. (~1.5 min)
Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>


#### Slide 24: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- summarised what you found out
- used participle clauses to make summaries shorter
- said what people say or believe about the past
- pieced together the story of the crane

[image: Photo idea: an old letter next to a new design]
