For each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 9: Life admin

**Objective:** Students can understand a firm, formal complaint and make one politely by phone, with evidence and a deadline, use inversion for emphasis in formal language, explain tenants’ basic repair rights in England, and understand how to exchange a foreign driving licence (CEFR B2–C1: understand formal correspondence; make clear that the provider must act).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 3 · Pressure · Lesson 9

**Life admin**

Today’s goal: make a firm, formal complaint, know your rights as a tenant, and sort out a driving licence.

[image: Photo idea: a bucket catching a leak]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Late April. Josh’s landlord ignores a dangerous leak, so Haruka helps him write a formal complaint. Josh also finds out how to swap his Australian driving licence. The Morita family replies from Uji. Last lesson of Unit 3. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: In the archive, the team found letters between Edith Hartley and Mr Morita, a tea grower in Uji. Gwen remembered that Edith gave every packer a crane tin in 1939. The pitch idea: bring Crane Blend back, in Japan. Josh’s ceiling started leaking (Lesson 8).

Main point: inversion for emphasis in formal writing (Not only has…, At no point have…, Should you…). Practical topics: tenants’ rights and repairs in England, formal complaints, and exchanging a foreign driving licence.

Renting rules here are for England. Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland have their own rules.

Timing: 23 slides, about 42 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip the pronunciation slide, True or false and Useful phrases (saves about 3.5 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Problems at home

Talk with your teacher.

- Have you ever had a problem with a landlord, a neighbour or a company?
- How did you complain? Did it work?
- Do you prefer to complain by phone, email or letter? Why?

[image: Photo idea: dealing with a problem at home]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: Josh’s flat. Water drips from the light fitting into a bucket; Haruka takes photos. Water drips into the bucket. Haruka photographs the ceiling. Josh wipes the floor.]

- What’s wrong with Josh’s flat?
- Why do you think Haruka is taking photos?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Let the student describe and guess. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · The ceiling

Saturday, at Josh’s flat. Haruka and Josh.

**Haruka:** Oh, Josh. That ceiling looks awful. Is the electricity off?  
**Josh:** Yes, I turned it off at the fuse box. I’m not completely useless.  
**Haruka:** Good. Right, photos first. When did it start?  
**Josh:** Three weeks ago. It was just a small brown mark then.  
**Haruka:** And what has your landlord said?  
**Josh:** First he said it was nothing. Now he just says he’ll “sort it out next week”.  
**Haruka:** Well, it’s not nothing. Water near electrics is dangerous, and it’s his job to fix it.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together, then swap. (~2 min)
Safety first: water near electrics is dangerous. Turning the power off at the fuse box (consumer unit) is the right thing to do.
Ask: Whose job is it to fix a leak in a rented flat?

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- What has Josh done to stay safe?
- When did the problem start?
- How has the landlord reacted?
- Why does Haruka take photos?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Turned off the electricity at the fuse box.  2. Three weeks ago.  3. He said it was nothing, and now he keeps putting it off.  4. As evidence.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Culture · Who fixes what?

> **Repairs when you rent in England**
>
> Your landlord must fix the **structure** of the building, leaks and pipes, heating and hot water, gas, and electrical wiring.
>
> It’s best to report problems **in writing** and keep photos. Report anything dangerous straight away.
>
> If your landlord doesn’t act, contact your local council. Its environmental health team can make the landlord do the repairs.
>
> Since May 2026, landlords in England can no longer use “no-fault” evictions (section 21). They need a legal reason, so tenants can complain with less worry.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Explains the situation straight after the scene. England only. (~2 min)
Advice from Shelter and GOV.UK: keep paying rent while you wait for repairs (stopping can put the tenancy at risk).
Sources: GOV.UK “Private renting: repairs”; Renters’ Rights Act 2025 (section 21 “no-fault” evictions ended on 1 May 2026); Shelter England.

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Complaints (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| evidence | noun | 証拠 | facts, photos or documents that show something is true | Keep photos as **evidence**. |
| to take the matter further | phrase | （さらに上の機関に）訴える | to complain to a higher authority if a problem isn’t solved | If he doesn’t reply, I’ll **take the matter further**. |
| to act | verb | 対処する、行動を起こす | to do something to deal with a problem | The council can make him **act**. |
| to deteriorate | verb | 悪化する | to get worse | The damage has **deteriorated**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue and Josh’s letter. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 8: Vocabulary · Licences (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| valid | adjective | 有効な | officially accepted and allowed to be used | My licence is **valid** for twelve months. |
| eligible (for) | adjective | 資格がある、対象となる | allowed to do or get something because you meet the rules | Australian licences are **eligible** for exchange. |
| red tape | noun | お役所仕事、面倒な手続き | official rules and paperwork that seem unnecessary | There’s barely any **red tape**. |
| a hassle | noun | 面倒なこと | something annoying that takes time and effort (informal) | It’s a **hassle**, but it’s not a test. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come later in the lesson. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 9: Story · A formal letter

Sunday, at Josh’s laptop. Josh and Haruka.

**Josh:** I’ll just write: “Fix my ceiling, mate, or I’m not paying the rent.”  
**Haruka:** Please don’t. Keep paying the rent. A formal letter works much better.  
**Josh:** Formal how?  
**Haruka:** Dates, facts, and your photos as **evidence**. A clear deadline. And say you’ll **take the matter further** if he doesn’t **act**.  
**Josh:** Further where?  
**Haruka:** The council. Their environmental health team can make him fix it.  
**Josh:** You really do know the rules. OK, you type. I’ll try to sound polite.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Josh wants to be blunt; Haruka wants to be firm. The letter on the next slide is formal, clear and firm.
Haruka learned the rules when she helped Yuki find a flat (intermediate course).

</details>


#### Slide 10: Story · Josh’s letter

[image: Photo idea: a formal letter and photo evidence]

Dear Mr Carter,

**Re: Leak at Flat 3, 18 Elm Road**

I am writing about the leak in my ceiling, which I first reported to you on 3 April.

Not only has the damage **deteriorated**, but water is now coming through the light fitting. At no point have you arranged for anyone to inspect it.

I attach photos and copies of our messages as **evidence**. Please arrange a repair within 7 days of this letter. Should the problem continue, I will contact the council’s environmental health team.

Yours sincerely,
Josh Bennett

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads the letter aloud, then finds: the date of the first report, the problem now, the evidence, the deadline, and what happens next. (~2.5 min)
Notice the formal phrases and the three inversions (Not only has…, At no point have…, Should the problem…). They’re on the next slide.

</details>


#### Slide 11: Language focus · Not only has…

> **Inversion for emphasis**
>
> *Not only + auxiliary + subject…, but…*
>
> **Not only has** the damage got worse, **but**…
>
> *Negative start + auxiliary + subject*
>
> **At no point have** you replied. · **Never have** I seen this.
>
> *Should + subject (= If)*
>
> **Should** the problem **continue**, I will…

**From the story**
- Letter: **Not only has** the damage deteriorated, **but**…
- Letter: **At no point have** you arranged for anyone to inspect it.
- Letter: **Should** the problem **continue**, I will contact the council.

Use inversion to sound formal and strong, especially in writing.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Inversion for emphasis: after a negative or limiting expression at the start (Not only, At no point, Never, Under no circumstances), the auxiliary comes before the subject, like a question. It sounds formal and strong, so it’s common in complaints, speeches and news. “Should + subject + verb” is a formal way to say “If”. (~2.5 min)
Many Japanese learners forget the auxiliary (Not only the damage has…). Point at the question order: has the damage → Not only has the damage…

</details>


#### Slide 12: Practice · Make it stronger

> *Pattern*
>
> Not only + is / has + subject…, but…
> At no point / Never + have + subject…
> Should + subject + verb, …

- The leak is dangerous, and it’s getting worse. (Not only…)
- You have never replied to my emails. (At no point…)
- If you have any questions, please contact me. (Should…)
- I have never seen such a slow response. (Never…)
- The heating is broken, and the windows don’t close. (Not only…)

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Not only is the leak dangerous, but it’s getting worse.
> 2. At no point have you replied to my emails.
> 3. Should you have any questions, please contact me.
> 4. Never have I seen such a slow response.
> 5. Not only is the heating broken, but the windows don’t close either.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student rewrites each sentence with inversion, starting with the words in brackets. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 13: Pronunciation · NOT only…

> **Stress the negative**
>
> **NOT** only has it got **WORSE** | but…
> At **NO** point have you **RE**plied.
> **NE**ver have I **SEEN** this.

**Say it with your teacher**
- **Not** only is it **dan**gerous, | but it’s getting **worse**.
- At **no** point have you **called** me.
- **Should** you have any **ques**tions, | please **call**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Inverted sentences are emphatic, so the negative word at the start gets strong stress, and the auxiliary is weak. Read slowly and firmly, with a clear pause before “but”. (~1.5 min)
Many Japanese learners read formal sentences very fast and flat. Practise firm, calm emphasis.

</details>


#### Slide 14: Listening · A reply at last

Tuesday morning. Josh gets a voicemail from his landlord.

[audio: Mr Carter’s voicemail]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Josh, it’s Mike Carter. Got your letter. I’ll have a plumber round on Thursday. No need to get the council involved, all right?

- When is the plumber coming?
- What does Mr Carter not want?
- Why do you think he replied so quickly this time?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. On Thursday.
> 2. He doesn’t want the council involved.
> 3. The formal letter, the deadline and the mention of the council.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Josh, it’s Mike Carter. Got your letter. I’ll have a plumber round on Thursday. No need to get the council involved, all right?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~1.5 min)
Callback: “I’ll have a plumber round” is the causative (Lesson 3).

</details>


#### Slide 15: Story · Driving

A week later, at the office. Josh, Haruka and Yuki.

**Josh:** Good news: the ceiling’s fixed. Now I want to escape London for a weekend. Can I hire a car on my Australian licence?  
**Haruka:** For now, yes. It’s **valid** for twelve months after you moved here.  
**Josh:** And after that? I haven’t taken a driving test since I was seventeen.  
**Haruka:** You won’t need one. Australian licences are **eligible** for exchange. You fill in a form and send your licence off.  
**Josh:** Send it off? In the post? No way.  
**Yuki:** I did the same with my Japanese licence. Japan’s on the list too. Barely any **red tape**.  
**Josh:** So it’s a **hassle**, but not a test. Fair enough.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Facts are on the reading slide later. Japan, like Australia, is a “designated country”, so Japanese licences can be exchanged without a test (a translation is usually needed).
Ask: Have you ever driven abroad?

</details>


#### Slide 16: Role play · Calling the letting agent

> **Student**
>
> Your boiler broke on Monday: no heating and no hot water. You emailed the letting agent on Monday. Nobody replied.
>
> • Explain the problem and the dates.
> • Say why it can’t wait.
> • Get a repair date.

**Useful phrases**
- I’m calling about…
- I first reported it on…
- Not only is there…, but…
- That isn’t acceptable.
- I’ll have to take the matter further.
- So that’s confirmed for…?

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re the letting agent.
>
> • First say: “The engineer is busy until next week.”
> • If the student stays calm and firm, offer Thursday.
> • If they mention the council, offer tomorrow.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student is the tenant; you’re the letting agent. Everything you need is on your card. No swap needed. (~3 min)
Listen for firm, polite complaining, evidence and dates. Strong students may use inversion (Not only…, At no point…).

</details>


#### Slide 17: Progress check · Unit 3 check

> **Student**
>
> You’re Haruka. Amy asks what’s been happening.
>
> • The crane tins: what you found in the archive.
> • Why you think they stopped.
> • How you helped Josh with his landlord.

**Useful phrases**
- Edith is said to have…
- Having met…, she…
- They might well have…
- Not only…, but…
- He needn’t have…
- Written in…, the letter…

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re Amy.
>
> • Ask: So who was Edith?
> • Ask: Why did the crane tins stop?
> • Ask: How did you get Josh’s landlord to act?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Unit 3 progress check. The student plays Haruka and uses the story, so there’s nothing to invent. You’re Amy. Let the student lead and don’t correct during the conversation. Note what they do well and one thing to practise. (~3 min)
Look for: modal perfects (might well have, needn’t have), participle clauses, reporting passives and inversion.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Reading · Driving on a foreign licence

[image: Photo idea: car keys on a country lane]

If you move to Great Britain, you can drive on your foreign licence for up to 12 months.

After that, you need a British licence. If you come from a **designated country**, you can simply exchange it. Send a form, your licence and a fee (£43 in 2026), and you don’t need to take a test. Australia and Japan are both on the list. You must do it within five years of becoming a resident.

If your licence can’t be exchanged, you’ll usually need to pass the theory and practical tests.

And remember: Britain drives on the left, just like Japan and Australia.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: How long can you drive on a foreign licence? What’s a designated country? How much does the exchange cost?
Sources: GOV.UK “Exchange a foreign driving licence” (designated countries include Australia and Japan; up to 12 months on a foreign licence after becoming resident; exchange within 5 years; form D1, £43). Rules are for Great Britain; Northern Ireland has its own process. Japanese licences usually need an official translation.

</details>


#### Slide 19: Reading · True or false?

- You can drive on a foreign licence for up to 12 months.
- Everyone from abroad must take a British driving test.
- Japanese licences can be exchanged.
- Britain drives on the right.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. True.  2. False: people from designated countries can exchange their licence.  3. True.  4. False: on the left, like Japan.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 20: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a quiet residential street]

- If something breaks in a rented flat in Japan, who fixes it?
- How do people in Japan usually complain: phone, email or letter?
- Was Josh right to want to be blunt?
- Is driving in Japan easier or harder than in the UK?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Some may mention key money (reikin) or management companies that handle repairs. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Story · A reply from Uji

Monday at the office. Haruka, Priya and Josh.

**Haruka:** Everyone, the Morita family replied!  
**Priya:** And?  
**Haruka:** Daichi Morita runs the farm now. **Not only did** they keep Mr Morita’s letters, **but** they kept Edith’s too!  
**Josh:** The other half of the story!  
**Haruka:** They still paint a white crane on every tea chest. And he’d love to join the pitch on a video call.  
**Priya:** Then we’d better start rehearsing. It’s six weeks away.  
**Josh:** **Never have I** been so excited about tea.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
The Moritas replied to Haruka in Japanese; she sums it up in English for the team.
Daichi Morita is Mr Morita’s great-grandson. He’ll appear in Lesson 11.
Hook: Unit 4 starts with rehearsing the pitch. Ask: What should the pitch include?

</details>


#### Slide 22: Vocabulary · Useful phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to send (something) off | phrasal verb | （書類などを）郵送する | to post something to an organisation | Fill in the form and **send** it **off**. |
| to have (someone) round | phrase | （業者などに）来てもらう | to get someone to come to your home, for example to fix something | I’ll **have** a plumber **round** on Thursday. |
| No way. | phrase | ありえない、絶対いや | definitely not (informal) | “Post your licence?” “**No way.**” |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Useful phrases from today’s dialogues and voicemail. (~1 min)
Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>


#### Slide 23: Wrap-up · Unit 3 check

**In Unit 3, I can…**
- deal with a setback and speculate about what happened
- summarise research and say what people believe about the past
- make a firm, formal complaint
- understand my rights as a tenant and sort out life admin

[image: Photo idea: a repaired ceiling]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Self-check for Unit 3: for each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1.5 min)

## Unit: The pitch


### Lesson 10: Rehearsal

**Objective:** Students can structure a short presentation, guide the audience with signposts and discourse markers, give and take feedback on a run-through, and manage nerves (CEFR B2–C1: sustained monologue, addressing audiences; Core Inventory B2: markers to structure formal speech).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 4 · The pitch · Lesson 10

**Rehearsal**

Today’s goal: structure a short presentation, guide your audience with signposts, and stay calm.

[image: Photo idea: a meeting room ready for a presentation]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

May into June. The team rehearses the pitch. Haruka’s first run-through is a mess; Josh is blunt, and this time Haruka takes it well. Amy invites Haruka to her hen do, the weekend before the pitch. First lesson of Unit 4. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: Haruka helped Josh write a formal complaint, and the landlord fixed the leak. The Morita family replied from Uji: they kept Edith’s letters, and Daichi Morita will join the pitch on a video call (Lesson 9).

Main point: signposting and discourse markers for presentations (First, I’ll…; Moving on to…; Having said that, …; To sum up, …). Practical topic: structuring a presentation and handling nerves. Culture: hen and stag dos.

Timing: 22 slides, about 38 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip the pronunciation slide, True or false and Useful phrases (saves about 3.5 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Presentations

Talk with your teacher.

- When did you last give a presentation? How did it go?
- What makes a presentation easy to follow?
- What do you do when you’re nervous?

[image: Photo idea: giving a presentation]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: A meeting room. Haruka rehearses the pitch; Josh, Yuki and Priya watch. Haruka loses her place. Josh raises his hand. Haruka laughs nervously and puts her notes down.]

- What is Haruka doing? How is it going?
- Why do you think Josh raises his hand?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Let the student describe and guess. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · First run-through

May, in the meeting room. Haruka, Josh, Priya and Yuki.

**Haruka:** …and that’s the market data. Um, then there’s the price, and the design, and also the letters…  
**Josh:** Can I stop you there? Honestly, I got lost after slide three.  
**Haruka:** Fair enough. What would help?  
**Josh:** Signposts. Tell us where you’re going: first this, then that.  
**Priya:** And start with Edith, not the numbers. People remember stories.  
**Yuki:** I could draw the crane for the opening slide.  
**Haruka:** Great idea. Let me try again tomorrow.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together, then swap. (~2 min)
Josh is still direct, but now Haruka takes it calmly (“Fair enough. What would help?”). Compare Lesson 6.
Ask: Was Josh’s feedback useful? Was it kind?

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- What’s wrong with Haruka’s first run-through?
- What does Josh suggest?
- How should the pitch start, in Priya’s opinion?
- How will Yuki help?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. It’s hard to follow: Josh got lost.  2. Signposts: say where you’re going.  3. With Edith’s story, not the numbers.  4. She’ll draw the crane for the opening slide.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · Presenting (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to grab someone’s attention | phrase | 人の注意を引く | to make someone suddenly interested | Start with a story to **grab** their **attention**. |
| to talk (someone) through (something) | phrasal verb | （順を追って）説明する | to explain something to someone step by step | I’ll **talk** you **through** the numbers. |
| a key message | noun | 伝えたい要点 | the most important idea you want people to remember | What’s your **key message**? |
| to speed up | phrasal verb | 速くなる | to go or speak faster | You **sped up** at the end. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Nerves (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to dread (something) | verb | （〜を）ひどく恐れる、憂うつに思う | to feel very worried about something that will happen | I’m **dreading** the pitch. |
| terrified | adjective | ひどく怖がって | very frightened | I was **terrified** before my first talk. |
| to calm your nerves | phrase | 緊張をほぐす | to make yourself feel less nervous | Breathing slowly helps **calm my nerves**. |
| to wing it | idiom | ぶっつけ本番でやる | to do something without preparing (informal) | Don’t **wing it**. Practise first. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come later in the lesson. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · Take two

The next day. Haruka, Josh and Priya.

**Haruka:** Good morning. Today I’d like to tell you a story about two families and a white bird.  
**Josh:** Ooh. That **grabs** my **attention**.  
**Haruka:** I’ll start in 1923, then move on to our design, and finally, I’ll **talk** you **through** the numbers.  
**Priya:** Lovely and clear. Having said that, keep the numbers short. What’s your **key message**?  
**Haruka:** To sum up: one friendship, one bird, and one new tea for Japan. Any questions?  
**Josh:** Just one. Can you say it all again, but slower? You **sped up** at the end.  
**Haruka:** I always do that when I’m nervous.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Haruka uses signposts now (I’ll start…, then move on to…, finally…, To sum up). They’re taught on the language focus slide.
Ask: What’s better about Haruka’s second try?

</details>


#### Slide 9: Listening · A message from Uji

That afternoon, Priya plays the team a voice message from Daichi Morita.

[audio: Daichi’s voice message]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Hello everyone, Daichi Morita here. I’m happy to join on the fourteenth. I’ll show you our tea garden, and the crane on our chests. See you then!

- When will Daichi join the pitch?
- What will he show?
- How does he feel about it?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. On the fourteenth (of June).
> 2. His tea garden and the crane on the tea chests.
> 3. Happy, excited.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Hello everyone, Daichi Morita here. I’m happy to join on the fourteenth. I’ll show you our tea garden, and the crane on our chests. See you then!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~1.5 min)
The pitch is on Wednesday 14 June.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Language focus · First, I’ll…

> **Signposting**
>
> *Structure*
>
> **First**, I’ll… · **Then** I’ll **move on to**… · **Finally**, …
>
> *Changing topic*
>
> **Moving on to**… · **That brings me to**…
>
> *Contrast and summary*
>
> **Having said that**, … · **To sum up**, …

**From the story**
- Haruka: I’ll **start** in 1923, **then move on to** our design…
- Priya: **Having said that**, keep the numbers short.
- Haruka: **To sum up**: one friendship, one bird…

Use signposts so your audience always knows where you are.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Signposts tell the audience where they are in a talk. In English presentations, speakers often say what they’re going to say, say it, then sum it up. “Having said that” and “That said” introduce a contrast, a bit like “but” with more weight. (~2.5 min)
Many Japanese learners go straight into detail without an overview. Practise the opening: Today… First… Then… Finally…

</details>


#### Slide 11: Practice · Signpost it

- ______, I’ll tell you where my town is. (start)
- ______ the food. (change topic)
- The food is amazing. ______, it can be expensive. (contrast)
- ______, I’ll tell you why you should visit. (last point)
- ______: great food, kind people, and beautiful hills. (summary)

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. First
> 2. Moving on to / That brings me to
> 3. Having said that / That said
> 4. Finally
> 5. To sum up / In short

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student fills each gap with a signpost, then gives a 1-minute talk about their home town with three points: where it is, the food, and why people should visit. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 12: Pronunciation · One idea, one breath

> **Pause after signposts**
>
> **FIRST** | I’ll take you back to **1923**. |
> **MOV**ing on | to our **DE**sign. |
> To sum **UP** | one **FRIEND**ship, | one **BIRD**.

**Say it with your teacher**
- Good **mor**ning. | Today | I’d like to tell you a **sto**ry.
- That brings me | to the **num**bers.
- Having said **that** | we need to keep the price **low**.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Good speakers say one idea per breath and pause after each signpost. Mark the pauses with | and read slowly. Speeding up is the most common sign of nerves. (~1.5 min)
Many Japanese learners pause in the middle of a phrase to think. Plan the pauses instead.

</details>


#### Slide 13: Story · The hen do

Sunday at Gwen’s. Amy, Haruka and Gwen.

**Amy:** Haruka, you’re coming to my hen do, aren’t you? It’s in Bath, the second weekend in June.  
**Haruka:** I’d love to! Um… what exactly is a hen do?  
**Gwen:** A party for the bride and her friends before the wedding. Mine was a night at the bingo.  
**Amy:** Ours is afternoon tea, a spa, and a lot of dancing. **First** the tea, **then** the spa, **finally** the dancing.  
**Haruka:** The second weekend in June… That’s just before our pitch!  
**Gwen:** Then you’ll come back relaxed. Or very tired.  
**Amy:** And you can practise your wedding speech on the girls!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Amy uses signposts as a joke (First…, then…, finally…).
The pitch is on Wednesday 14 June, and the hen do is the weekend before. Haruka now has two speeches to prepare.

</details>


#### Slide 14: Culture · Hen and stag dos

> **Before a British wedding**
>
> Before a wedding, many British couples have separate parties with their friends: a **hen do** (or hen party) for the bride and a **stag do** for the groom. “Do” is an informal word for a party.
>
> Some are a weekend away, a few weeks before the wedding, in another UK city or abroad. Some are wild; many are calm: afternoon tea, a spa, a cooking class or a walk.
>
> Often a close friend organises it, and it’s often a surprise for the bride or groom.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Explains the hen do straight after the scene. Traditions vary a lot; same-sex couples and many modern couples have joint parties. (~1.5 min)
Ask: Is there anything similar in Japan?

</details>


#### Slide 15: Story · Nerves

Friday, after the last rehearsal. Josh and Haruka.

**Josh:** You were brilliant today. Why do you still look worried?  
**Haruka:** Because I’m **dreading** Wednesday. Mrs Hartley, Daichi, and my English.  
**Josh:** Your English is better than my Japanese. Can I tell you a secret? I’m **terrified** too.  
**Haruka:** You? You’re never nervous.  
**Josh:** Every time. **Having said that**, I’ve got a system to **calm my nerves**: breathe, pause after each signpost, and find one friendly face.  
**Haruka:** Whose face?  
**Josh:** Priya’s. She always nods. And whatever you do, don’t **wing it**.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Josh and Haruka are friends now: he admits he’s nervous too.
Ask: What helps you calm your nerves?

</details>


#### Slide 16: Role play · A one-minute pitch

> **Student**
>
> Pitch a second coffee machine for your office. Your notes:
>
> • Problem: 10-minute queues every morning.
> • Idea: a second machine (£900).
> • Result: about 30 hours saved a month.
>
> Open, signpost each point, sum up.

**Useful phrases**
- Today I’d like to talk about…
- First, I’ll…
- Moving on to…
- Having said that, …
- To sum up, …
- Any questions?

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re the audience.
>
> • Listen for: First…, Moving on to…, To sum up…
> • Ask one question: “Who will clean it?”
> • Give one piece of feedback on pace.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student gives a one-minute presentation from the notes on their card; you’re the audience. Then give feedback on signposting and pace. No swap needed. (~3 min)

</details>


#### Slide 17: Reading · Less is more

[image: Photo idea: a simple presentation slide]

Many presentations fail for the same reason: too much. Too many slides, too many words, too many numbers.

The investor Guy Kawasaki suggests a simple **10/20/30 rule** for pitches: no more than 10 slides, 20 minutes, and nothing smaller than 30-point text.

Tell people where you’re going, then take them there. Give each slide one idea, and say it out loud rather than reading it.

And don’t be afraid of silence. A short pause sounds confident, and gives the audience time to think.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: What is the 10/20/30 rule? Why is it good to pause?
Sources: Guy Kawasaki’s 10/20/30 rule for pitches (no more than 10 slides, 20 minutes, 30-point font). The other tips are common presentation advice.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Reading · True or false?

- The 10/20/30 rule means 10 minutes and 20 slides.
- Each slide should have one idea.
- You should read your slides aloud.
- A short pause can sound confident.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. False: 10 slides, 20 minutes, 30-point text.  2. True.  3. False: say the idea, don’t read the slide.  4. True.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 19: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: presentation notes on a lectern]

- What makes a good presentation at your company or school?
- Do Japanese presentations usually start with a story or with the facts?
- Is there anything like a hen or stag do in Japan?
- What would you include in Haruka’s pitch?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 20: Story · Board members

Monday 12 June, at the office. Priya, Haruka and Josh.

**Priya:** How was the hen do?  
**Haruka:** Exhausting. I practised my wedding speech on Amy’s friends. Two of them cried.  
**Priya:** Good, because I’ve got news. Mrs Hartley is bringing two people from her board on Wednesday.  
**Josh:** Her board? So they’ll want to talk money.  
**Priya:** Exactly. Budget, timeline, everything. Be ready to negotiate.  
**Haruka:** Then let’s go through the numbers one more time.  
**Josh:** **To sum up**: no pressure.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
A board = the group of people who make the big decisions for a company.
Callback: “No pressure” (Lesson 6).
Hook: Lesson 11 is pitch day: presenting, questions and negotiating.

</details>


#### Slide 21: Vocabulary · Useful phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Can I stop you there? | phrase | ちょっといいですか（話を止めて） | a polite way to interrupt someone who is presenting | **Can I stop you there?** I’m a bit lost. |
| a run-through | noun | 通しリハーサル | a practice of a presentation or event from start to finish | Let’s do one more **run-through**. |
| to go through (something) | phrasal verb | （内容を）確認する | to check or look at something carefully | Let’s **go through** the numbers again. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Useful phrases from today’s dialogues. (~1 min)
Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>


#### Slide 22: Wrap-up · Well done!

**Today you:**
- structured a short presentation
- used signposts to guide the audience
- gave and took feedback on a run-through
- talked about nerves and how to calm them

[image: Photo idea: a clicker and a crane sketch]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

For each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Give positive feedback and one thing to practise. (~1 min)

</details>


### Lesson 11: The pitch

**Objective:** Students can present a proposal, answer tough questions, and negotiate using conditional offers, conditions (provided, as long as) and soft proposals (What if we…?) (CEFR B2–C1: negotiating; making clear that the other side must make a concession; hedging).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 4 · The pitch · Lesson 11

**The pitch**

Today’s goal: present a proposal, deal with tough questions, and negotiate a deal that works for both sides.

[image: Photo idea: a meeting room ready for the pitch]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Wednesday 14 June: pitch day. Haruka tells Edith’s story, Daichi Morita joins from Uji, and the team negotiates the budget with a board member. Mrs Hartley is lost for words, and her answer is finally easy to read. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: The team rehearsed the pitch and Haruka learned to signpost. Amy’s hen do was the weekend before. Mrs Hartley is bringing two board members, so they’ll need to negotiate (Lesson 10).

Main point: language for negotiating (If you could…, we’d be happy to…; provided / as long as; What if we…?). Practical topic: presenting, answering questions and negotiating. The archive puzzle pays off.

Timing: 21 slides, about 37 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip the pronunciation slide, True or false and Useful phrases (saves about 3.5 minutes).

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Negotiating

Talk with your teacher.

- Have you ever negotiated a price or a salary? How did it go?
- Do you like negotiating, or do you find it stressful?
- What makes a deal fair for both sides?

[image: Photo idea: a handshake after a deal]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: Hartley’s meeting room. Daichi Morita is on a video call from his tea garden; Mrs Hartley is moved. Daichi shows the hillside. Mrs Hartley’s eyes fill with tears. Mr Bell raises his eyebrows. Haruka smiles.]

- Who is on the screen? Where is he?
- How does Mrs Hartley react? Why do you think she feels like that?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Let the student describe and guess. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · The story

Pitch day. Haruka, Mrs Hartley, Mr Bell, Josh and Daichi.

**Haruka:** In 1923, a young woman called Edith Hartley visited a tea farm in Uji, near Kyoto.  
**Mrs Hartley:** My great-grandmother? In Japan?  
**Haruka:** Yes. The Morita family painted a white crane on every tea chest. Until 1939, Hartley’s sold their tea as Crane Blend.  
**Mr Bell:** Then why have I never heard of it?  
**Josh:** Because it stopped in 1939. Edith gave the last tins to her packers and said, “One day, the cranes will come back.”  
**Haruka:** We’d like to bring them back. And we’re not alone. Daichi?  
**Daichi:** Good morning from Uji. This is the same hillside Edith walked on, a hundred years ago.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together, then swap. (~2 min)
The puzzle pays off: everything here came from clues the student has already seen (the tin, Edith’s letter, Mr Morita’s last letter, Gwen’s nan).
Mr Bell is on Hartley’s board. Daichi joins on a video call from his tea garden.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Did you understand?

- Where did Edith go in 1923?
- What was Crane Blend?
- Why did it stop?
- Who is Daichi, and where is he?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. To a tea farm in Uji, near Kyoto.  2. Hartley’s tea from the Morita farm, sold with a crane on the tin.  3. The start of the Second World War stopped it in 1939.  4. Mr Morita’s great-grandson, in the tea garden in Uji.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. Ask the student to retell Edith’s story in three sentences. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 6: Vocabulary · The deal (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| upfront | adverb | 前払いで | paid before the work starts | They want half the money **upfront**. |
| to meet (someone) halfway | idiom | 歩み寄る | to agree to a compromise between two positions | Let’s **meet** you **halfway**. |
| to supply | verb | 供給する | to provide goods to a company | The farm will **supply** the tea. |
| a deal-breaker | noun | 交渉決裂の決め手、譲れない条件 | one thing that would make you say no to a whole deal | Price isn’t a **deal-breaker**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · After the pitch (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| to be lost for words | idiom | 言葉を失う | to be so surprised or moved that you can’t speak | I’m quite **lost for words**. |
| thrilled | adjective | 大喜びの | very happy and excited | We’re **thrilled** with the result. |
| to seal the deal | idiom | 契約をまとめる | to finally agree a deal | We’ve **sealed the deal**. |
| to come full circle | idiom | 一周回って元に戻る | to return to where you started, often with a new understanding | The story has **come full circle**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come later in the lesson. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 8: Story · Tough questions

Later. Mr Bell, Priya, Josh, Mrs Hartley and Daichi.

**Mr Bell:** It’s a lovely story. But your budget is twenty per cent higher than we planned.  
**Priya:** I understand. If you could approve the full budget, we’d be happy to include the Japan launch at no extra cost.  
**Mr Bell:** That’s still a lot of money **upfront**.  
**Josh:** Then what if we **meet** you **halfway**? Half now, and half after the trial in Tokyo.  
**Mrs Hartley:** And the Morita family? Would they **supply** the tea?  
**Daichi:** Yes, provided the crane stays on the tin. That’s our only **deal-breaker**.  
**Mrs Hartley:** Then I don’t think we have a problem. Do we, Robert?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Priya makes a conditional offer, Josh proposes a compromise (calmly, this time), and Daichi sets one condition.
Mrs Hartley is clear for once: she wants this. Robert is Mr Bell’s first name.
The trial in Tokyo is the one Josh postponed with Ms Kato (Lesson 7).

</details>


#### Slide 9: Language focus · If you could…, we’d…

> **Negotiating**
>
> *Conditional offers*
>
> **If you could** approve it, **we’d be happy to**…
>
> *Conditions*
>
> Yes, **provided** the crane stays. · OK, **as long as** you sign this week.
>
> *Soft proposals*
>
> **What if we** meet you halfway? · **Would you be open to**…?

**From the story**
- Priya: **If you could** approve the full budget, **we’d be happy to**…
- Josh: **What if we** meet you halfway?
- Daichi: Yes, **provided** the crane stays on the tin.

Use conditional offers and soft proposals to negotiate without sounding aggressive.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Negotiating language makes offers and conditions without sounding aggressive. Conditional offers trade one thing for another. Provided (that) and as long as are more formal and firmer than if. “What if we…?” and “Would you be open to…?” are softer ways to propose something (C1 hedging). (~2.5 min)
Many Japanese learners make direct requests (Please pay half) or say yes too quickly. Practise trading: If you…, we’ll…

</details>


#### Slide 10: Practice · Make an offer

> *Pattern*
>
> If you could…, we’d be happy to…
> … provided / as long as… · What if we…?

- They pay 10% more. You deliver faster. (If you could…)
- You lower the price. They order 1,000 tins. (provided)
- Suggest splitting the cost 50/50. (What if…?)
- You start in July. They sign this week. (as long as)
- They give you more time. You add a free design. (If you could…)

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. If you could pay 10% more, we’d be happy to deliver faster.
> 2. We’ll lower the price, provided you order 1,000 tins.
> 3. What if we split the cost 50/50?
> 4. We can start in July, as long as you sign this week.
> 5. If you could give us more time, we’d be happy to add a free design.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student turns each situation into a negotiating sentence with the words in brackets. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 11: Pronunciation · Open or firm?

> **Rise = open, fall = firm**
>
> What if we meet you **HALF**way? ↗ (open)
> …provided the crane **STAYS**. ↘ (firm)
> If you could approve it, | we’d be **HAP**py to… ↗

**Say it with your teacher**
- Would you be **o**pen to that? ↗
- As long as you **sign** this week. ↘
- What if we **split** it? ↗
- That’s our only **deal**-breaker. ↘

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Intonation tells the other side how flexible you are. A rising tone on a proposal sounds open (we can discuss it). A clear fall on a condition sounds firm (this isn’t negotiable). (~1.5 min)
Say each line both ways and ask the student which sounds open and which sounds firm.

</details>


#### Slide 12: Story · A word

After the pitch, in the corridor. Mrs Hartley and Haruka.

**Mrs Hartley:** Haruka, a word? I must admit, I’m rather **lost for words**.  
**Haruka:** I hope that’s a good thing.  
**Mrs Hartley:** My grandmother used to say Edith had “a friend in the East”. I always thought it was a family joke.  
**Haruka:** It wasn’t. They wrote to each other for sixteen years.  
**Mrs Hartley:** And you found them. Not my family, not even my archivist. You.  
**Haruka:** Well, Gwen’s button tin found them, really.  
**Mrs Hartley:** Then I should very much like to meet Gwen.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Mrs Hartley is moved, and for once she says exactly what she means.
Edith and Mr Morita wrote from 1923 to 1939. Ruth found the crane tins while cataloguing, but nobody had opened the letters.
Hook: Mrs Hartley wants to meet Gwen (Lesson 12).

</details>


#### Slide 13: Listening · The answer

That evening, Mrs Hartley leaves Priya a voicemail.

[audio: Mrs Hartley’s voicemail]

*Transcript (teacher only):* Priya, Margaret Hartley. I’ll be brief, for once. The answer is yes. Crane Blend is coming home. Please thank the whole team. Lovely.

- What’s Mrs Hartley’s answer?
- What does she ask Priya to do?
- What’s different about this message?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. Yes.
> 2. Thank the whole team.
> 3. It’s short and completely clear: no decoding needed. Even “Lovely” really means lovely.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> Priya, Margaret Hartley. I’ll be brief, for once. The answer is yes. Crane Blend is coming home. Please thank the whole team. Lovely.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~1.5 min)
The decoding thread ends here: the student’s first decode was “rather interesting” (Lesson 1).

</details>


#### Slide 14: Story · Full circle

That evening, at the pub. Priya, Yuki, Josh and Haruka.

**Priya:** Well, team, we’ve **sealed the deal**. To Crane Blend!  
**Yuki:** I’m so **thrilled**. To the cranes!  
**Josh:** Last October, I thought “brave” was a compliment.  
**Haruka:** And in January, I said “interesting” when I meant no.  
**Priya:** Look at you both now. It’s **come full circle**.  
**Josh:** Seriously, Haruka, thank you. I couldn’t have done it without you.  
**Haruka:** Same. If you hadn’t been so blunt, I’d still be saying “interesting”.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Callbacks: Josh and “brave” (Lesson 2); Haruka’s “interesting” (Lesson 6). Haruka’s last line is a mixed conditional (Lesson 6).
Ask: How have Josh and Haruka changed this year?

</details>


#### Slide 15: Role play · Agreeing a price

> **Student**
>
> You quoted £5,000 for a new café logo and menu. The client wants to pay less.
>
> • You can drop to £4,500 if they pay half upfront.
> • You can add a free poster if they sign this week.
> • Don’t go below £4,300.

**Useful phrases**
- If you could…, we’d be happy to…
- …, provided that…
- …, as long as…
- What if we…?
- Would you be open to…?
- I think we have a deal.

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re the café owner. Your budget is £4,000.
>
> • Start by offering £3,800.
> • You can go up to £4,500 if you get something extra.
> • Ask: “Could you finish by August?”

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student is a designer; you’re the client. Each card has a limit the other person can’t see. No swap needed. (~3 min)
Listen for conditional offers (If you could…, we’d…), provided / as long as, and soft proposals (What if…?).

</details>


#### Slide 16: Reading · Getting to yes

[image: Photo idea: two people working out a deal]

In 1981, Roger Fisher and William Ury wrote **Getting to Yes**, one of the most famous books on negotiating.

One of their ideas is your **BATNA**: your Best Alternative To a Negotiated Agreement. In other words, what will you do if there’s no deal? If you know your BATNA, you know when to walk away.

They also say: focus on **interests**, not positions. Mr Bell’s position was “too expensive”. His interest was not losing money. Paying half later solved that.

The best deals leave both sides feeling they’ve won.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: What does BATNA mean? Why should you know your BATNA? What’s the difference between a position and an interest?
Source: Roger Fisher and William Ury, Getting to Yes: Negotiating Agreement Without Giving In (1981), which introduced the term BATNA.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Reading · True or false?

- Getting to Yes was written in 1981.
- BATNA means your best price.
- You should focus on interests, not positions.
- In a good deal, only one side wins.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. True.  2. False: your Best Alternative To a Negotiated Agreement.  3. True.  4. False: both sides should feel they’ve won.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 18: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: a crane tea tin in a shop]

- How are deals usually agreed in Japan: in the meeting, or before it?
- Is it OK to negotiate prices in Japan? Where?
- Would you buy Crane Blend? Why?
- What do you think Mrs Hartley will say to Gwen?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Some may mention preparing agreement before a meeting (nemawashi). (~2.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 19: Story · July

Sunday at Gwen’s. Tom, Haruka and Gwen.

**Tom:** So you won the pitch. Now for the hard part: my wedding speech.  
**Haruka:** It’s nearly ready. Nearly.  
**Gwen:** And Mrs Hartley wants to meet me? Good thing Amy found my button tin in the loft.  
**Haruka:** She’s coming to Mum’s book launch. You can bring the tin.  
**Tom:** Your mum’s launch, then my wedding… July is going to be busy.  
**Gwen:** Busy? It’s going to be the best month this house has ever had.  
**Haruka:** **As long as** nobody puts the milk in first.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
Callback: Milk Second, Mum’s comic, was named after Gwen’s tea rule (intermediate course).
Hook: Lesson 12 is July: Mum’s book launch and Tom and Amy’s wedding.
Amy offered to help dig out Gwen’s tin back in Lesson 3. She finally found it.

</details>


#### Slide 20: Vocabulary · Useful phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A word? | phrase | ちょっといいですか（個人的に話したい） | can I speak to you privately for a moment? | Haruka, **a word?** |
| I’ll be brief. | phrase | 手短に話します | I’ll keep this short | **I’ll be brief.** The answer is yes. |
| for once | phrase | 今回ばかりは | this time, unlike usual | She was clear, **for once**. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Useful phrases from today’s dialogues and voicemail. (~1 min)
Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>

**Today you:**
- presented a proposal as a story
- made conditional offers and set conditions
- proposed a compromise politely
- understood a clear yes after a year of decoding

### Lesson 12: The wedding

**Objective:** Students can give a short, memorable speech or toast using emphasis (cleft sentences, inversion, emphatic do), take part in conversation at a celebration, and talk about British wedding customs (CEFR C1: addressing audiences; sustained monologue; Core Inventory: emphasis).


#### Slide 1: Cover

Unit 4 · The pitch · Lesson 12

**The wedding**

Today’s goal: give a short, memorable speech, and talk about British weddings.

[image: Photo idea: a summer wedding reception]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

July, the end of the series. Mum’s book launches in London, Gwen meets Mrs Hartley with the crane tin, and Haruka tells Mum that London is home. Then Tom and Amy’s wedding: Tom is late, of course, and Haruka gives her speech. The last lesson of the course. Read the goal together. (~1 min)

Story so far: The pitch was a success. Mrs Hartley said yes to Crane Blend, and she wants to meet Gwen. July brings Mum’s book launch and the wedding (Lesson 11).

Main point: review of emphasis for speeches (cleft sentences, inversion, emphatic do). Practical topic: British weddings and giving a speech or toast. Final check for the whole course.

This is a big lesson. If you need to, split it into two sessions after the speech practice.

Timing: 25 slides, about 48 minutes with everything. For a shorter lesson, skip the pronunciation slide, True or false and Useful phrases (saves about 3.5 minutes), or split it into two sessions.

</details>


#### Slide 2: Warm-up · Big days

Talk with your teacher.

- Have you been to a wedding? What was it like?
- Have you ever given a speech at a special event?
- What makes a speech memorable?

[image: Photo idea: confetti outside a church]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Keep it personal. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 3: Warm-up · Watch and describe

[video clip: A London bookshop at Mum’s book launch. Gwen shows Mrs Hartley the crane tin. Gwen shows the tin. Mrs Hartley takes it, moved. Mum looks up and smiles at Haruka.]

- Where are they? What’s happening?
- What is Gwen holding? Why is it special?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Press play and watch together. Let the student describe and explain. (~1.5 min)
If the video isn’t ready yet, describe the scene to the student.

</details>


#### Slide 4: Story · The launch

July, a London bookshop. Mum, Gwen, Mrs Hartley and Haruka.

**Mum:** Thank you all for coming. I never thought my little comic would be a real book.  
**Gwen:** Mrs Hartley, this is the tin. My nan’s.  
**Mrs Hartley:** Oh my. Edith’s crane. May I?  
**Gwen:** Mind the buttons.  
**Mrs Hartley:** Would you lend it to us for the launch in Tokyo? It belongs in the story.  
**Gwen:** Lend it? Keep it. It’s come home.  
**Mum:** Haruka, this is going in the next book.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read once together, then swap. (~2 min)
Callbacks: Gwen’s button tin (Lessons 1, 3 and 8), “Mind the…” (Lesson 7), Mum’s book (Lesson 5).
Everyone speaks English: Gwen and Mrs Hartley are there.

</details>


#### Slide 5: Story · Home

Later, outside the bookshop. Mum, Haruka and Gwen.

**Mum:** Haruka, your visa, your job… Will you ever come back to Osaka?  
**Haruka:** To visit, always. But I think London is home now.  
**Mum:** I thought you’d say that. I’m not sad. Well, a little.  
**Gwen:** There’ll always be a room for her here. And for you.  
**Mum:** Then I’ll visit every summer. For research.  
**Haruka:** For the comic?  
**Mum:** What else? Your life is my best material.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Haruka’s big decision: London is home. Gwen is there, so they speak English.
Ask: Would you stay, like Haruka, or go home?

</details>


#### Slide 6: Story · Did you understand?

- What does Gwen do with the crane tin?
- Where will the tin go next?
- What does Haruka decide?
- How does Mum feel about it?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. She gives it to Mrs Hartley.  2. To the Crane Blend launch in Tokyo.  3. London is home now.  4. Not sad… well, a little. She’ll visit every summer.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Quick check. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 7: Vocabulary · Weddings (1/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the best man | noun | 花婿の付添人（ベストマン） | the groom’s friend or brother who helps him and gives a speech | Callum is Tom’s **best man**. |
| the reception | noun | 披露宴 | the party after a wedding ceremony | The **reception** is in a garden. |
| a toast | noun | 乾杯（の言葉） | when everyone raises a glass and drinks to someone | Let’s have a **toast** to the happy couple. |
| to tie the knot | idiom | 結婚する | to get married (informal) | They’re **tying the knot** in July. |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These words come in the next dialogue and the speeches. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 8: Vocabulary · Endings (2/2)

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| heartfelt | adjective | 心のこもった | sincere and full of real feeling | It was a **heartfelt** speech. |
| to be in stitches | idiom | 大笑いする | to laugh a lot (British, informal) | You had everyone **in stitches**. |
| to look back (on) | phrasal verb | 振り返る | to think about something in the past | **Looking back**, it was a great year. |
| a new chapter | noun | 新しい章、人生の新たな段階 | a new period in someone’s life | To the next **chapter**! |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

These come later in the lesson. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 9: Story · Late again

The wedding day. Callum, Gwen, Haruka and Tom.

**Callum:** Has anyone seen the groom? He’s **tying the knot** in ten minutes.  
**Gwen:** Tom? Late? On his own wedding day? I’m shocked.  
**Haruka:** He texted. His bus broke down. He’s running from the station.  
**Callum:** In a morning suit? This I have to see. Some **best man** I am.  
**Gwen:** Never have I known a man so late for everything.  
**Haruka:** There he is! Tom, your tie!  
**Tom:** I made it! Did I miss anything?  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Callum (Tom’s friend from the intermediate course) is the best man. Gwen’s “I’m shocked” is sarcasm, and her line is an inversion for emphasis (Lesson 9).
A morning suit = a formal suit with a long jacket, worn at some British weddings.

</details>


#### Slide 10: Culture · British weddings

> **A wedding day in Britain**
>
> British weddings can take place in a church, a register office or a licensed venue such as a hotel or a barn.
>
> After the ceremony comes the **reception**: a meal, **speeches** and dancing. Traditionally, the father of the bride, the groom and the best man give speeches. Today, brides, mothers and friends often speak too.
>
> The best man’s speech is famous for gently embarrassing the groom. Speeches often end with a **toast**: guests raise their glasses and say the couple’s names.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Explains the wedding straight after the scene. Customs vary a lot between families and religions. (~1.5 min)
Ask: Who gives speeches at weddings in Japan?

</details>


#### Slide 11: Listening · The best man

At the reception, Callum stands up to speak.

[audio: Callum’s speech]

*Transcript (teacher only):* As best man, it’s my job to embarrass Tom. Sadly, he does that himself. He was late today. Raise your glasses… to Tom and Amy!

- What does Callum say is his job?
- Why is that job easy?
- How does he end?

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. To embarrass Tom.
> 2. Tom does it himself: he was late to his own wedding.
> 3. With a toast to Tom and Amy.

> **Transcript** (teacher only)
>
> As best man, it’s my job to embarrass Tom. Sadly, he does that himself. He was late today. Raise your glasses… to Tom and Amy!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read the questions first. Play the clip twice. (~1.5 min)
Callum is Scottish. “He does that himself” uses emphatic do.

</details>


#### Slide 12: Story · Haruka’s speech

[image: Photo idea: a speech at a wedding]

Good evening, everyone. I’m Haruka, and almost three years ago, I became Tom’s flatmate.

On my first day, he said, “Alright?”, and I had no idea what to say. What I didn’t know was that I’d just met my best friend in London.

Not only is Tom funny, but he’s also the kindest person I know. He is always late. He does, however, always turn up.

And Amy, it was you who got him to book a haircut. Never have I seen him look so smart.

So please, raise your glasses. To Tom and Amy!

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads Haruka’s speech aloud, as if giving it. Then find: one cleft sentence, one inversion and one emphatic do. They’re on the next slide. (~2.5 min)
Callbacks: “Alright?” (the beginner course), Amy and the haircut (Lesson 3).

</details>


#### Slide 13: Language focus · What I didn’t know was…

> **Emphasis in speeches**
>
> *Cleft sentences*
>
> **What I didn’t know was** that… · **It was** you **who**…
>
> *Inversion*
>
> **Never have I seen** him so smart. · **Not only is** he funny, **but**…
>
> *Emphatic do*
>
> He **does** always turn up. · I **do** hope you’ll visit.

**From the story**
- Haruka: **What I didn’t know was** that I’d just met my best friend.
- Haruka: **Never have I seen** him look so smart.
- Haruka: He **does**, however, always turn up.

Use these structures to make a speech memorable.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

A review of three ways to add emphasis, perfect for speeches. Cleft sentences (Lesson 5) put the focus on one idea. Inversion (Lesson 9) sounds dramatic. Emphatic do (new) stresses that something really is true, often after a contrast: He’s always late. He does, however, always turn up. Stress the do / does. (~2.5 min)
Many Japanese learners use “really” or “very” for emphasis. These structures make a speech sound more natural and more memorable.

</details>


#### Slide 14: Practice · Say it with feeling

> *Pattern*
>
> What I love about… is… · It was… who…
> Never have I… · He does / I do + verb

- I love Amy’s laugh. (What I love…)
- Gwen made the cake. (It was…)
- I have never seen a happier couple. (Never…)
- He tries hard. (emphatic do)
- Tom is kind, and he’s funny. (Not only…)

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. What I love about Amy is her laugh.
> 2. It was Gwen who made the cake.
> 3. Never have I seen a happier couple.
> 4. He does try hard.
> 5. Not only is Tom kind, but he’s also funny.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student rewrites each sentence with the words in brackets, then says it as if in a speech. (~2 min)

</details>


#### Slide 15: Pronunciation · Raise your glasses

> **Slow down for the toast**
>
> So please | **RAISE** your glasses. |
> To **TOM** ↗ | and **AM**y! ↘
> He **DOES** always turn up.

**Say it with your teacher**
- Please **raise** your **glass**es.
- To **Tom** | and **Am**y!
- I **do** hope you’ll be happy.
- **What** I love about them | is their **laugh**ter.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

A toast is slow and clear. Pause before the names, stress them, and let your voice rise on the first name and fall on the last. With emphatic do, the stress goes on DO / DOES. (~1.5 min)

</details>


#### Slide 16: Story · After the speeches

The reception. Tom, Amy, Haruka, Callum, Gwen and Mum.

**Tom:** Haruka, what a speech, and what a **toast**! You had Callum **in stitches**.  
**Amy:** And me in tears. **Heartfelt**, funny and short. Perfect.  
**Haruka:** I practised it at your hen do, remember?  
**Callum:** It was better than mine, and I’m the best man!  
**Gwen:** Better than yours wasn’t difficult, love.  
**Mum:** I was drawing the whole time. Next episode: “The speech”.  
**Haruka:** Mum! …Actually, I don’t mind. Draw it.  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together, then swap. (~2 min)
Gwen teases Callum: friendly British banter.
Callback: Mum has been drawing Haruka’s life since the intermediate course. This time, Haruka says yes.

</details>


#### Slide 17: Role play · Your toast

> **Student**
>
> Give a one-minute toast to a real friend, family member or colleague (a birthday, a wedding, a leaving party).
>
> • How do you know them?
> • One short story.
> • What you love about them.
> • Raise your glass!

**Useful phrases**
- I’ve known… since…
- What I love about… is…
- It was… who…
- Never have I…
- I do hope…
- Please raise your glasses. To…!

> **Teacher**
>
> You’re a guest.
>
> • Listen and react (laugh, clap!).
> • Ask one question afterwards.
> • Say which line you liked best.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student gives a one-minute toast to a real person in their life; you’re a guest. No swap needed. (~3 min)
Listen for a cleft sentence, an inversion or emphatic do, and a slow, clear toast at the end.

</details>


#### Slide 18: Reading · Something old, something new

[image: Photo idea: a wedding cake and confetti]

Many British brides follow an old rhyme: “Something old, something new, something borrowed, something blue.” The old item connects the bride to her family’s past, and the new one to her future.

After the ceremony, guests often throw **confetti**, small pieces of paper or dried petals, for good luck.

The cake is cut together by the couple. Traditionally it was a fruit cake, but sponge cakes, cheese towers and even piles of doughnuts are popular now.

Like Haruka’s story, a good wedding mixes something old with something new.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

The student reads aloud. (~2.5 min)
Check: What does the old rhyme say? Why do people throw confetti?
These are common traditions, not rules; many couples choose their own. The rhyme is Victorian (often linked to Lancashire).

</details>


#### Slide 19: Reading · True or false?

- The rhyme says “something old, something new”.
- Confetti is thrown for good luck.
- Wedding cakes must be fruit cakes.
- The couple cut the cake together.

> **Answers** (teacher only)
>
> 1. True.  2. True.  3. False: many couples choose other cakes now.  4. True.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Ask the student to correct the false sentences. Skip this slide if time is short. (~1 min)

</details>


#### Slide 20: Discussion · Japan and the UK

[image: Photo idea: London at sunset]

- How are weddings in Japan different from Tom and Amy’s?
- Who usually gives speeches at Japanese weddings?
- Looking back on the course, what was your favourite moment in the story?
- How has your English changed since you started?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Let the student describe Japan; don’t tell them. Many will mention speeches from bosses or colleagues and gifts of money in special envelopes. (~3 min)

</details>


#### Slide 21: Story · Milk second

The next morning, in Gwen’s kitchen. Gwen, Mum and Haruka.

**Gwen:** Tea, anyone? Milk second, of course.  
**Mum:** Of course.  
**Haruka:** **Looking back**, three years ago I didn’t even know there were rules.  
**Gwen:** And now you’re the one teaching them.  
**Mum:** So, what comes next for you?  
**Haruka:** A new tea in Japan, a new team in London… and more episodes for you.  
**Gwen:** To the next **chapter**, then. Cheers!  

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read together. (~1.5 min)
The final scene of the series: the beginner course started with Gwen’s tea rules, and Mum’s comic is named after them.
Ask the student how they think the story continues.

</details>


#### Slide 22: Story · What happened next

> **Epilogue**
>
> The next spring, Crane Blend went on sale in Tokyo. In the shop window, next to the new tin, sat an old one full of buttons.
>
> Daichi Morita visited London for the first time. Gwen made him tea. Milk second.
>
> Josh swapped his licence and finally escaped London for a weekend. Yuki’s crane became the new Hartley’s logo.
>
> Milk Second, Volume 2, came out the following summer. The last page shows a young woman in a London kitchen, holding a cup of tea, at home.

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Read it aloud together, slowly. (~1.5 min)
Thank the student for finishing the series. Ask: Which character will you miss most?

</details>


#### Slide 23: Vocabulary · Useful phrases

| Word | Type | Japanese | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the happy couple | phrase | 新郎新婦 | the two people getting married | A toast to **the happy couple**! |
| This I have to see. | phrase | それは見ものだ | said when something sounds funny or surprising and you want to watch it | Tom running in a suit? **This I have to see.** |
| I made it! | phrase | 間に合った！ | I arrived in time (often after a difficult journey) | **I made it!** Did I miss anything? |

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Useful phrases from today’s dialogues. (~1 min)
Skip this slide if time is short.

</details>


#### Slide 24: Final check · Course check

> **Student**
>
> Tell your teacher the story of Haruka’s third year.
>
> • Josh, Mrs Hartley and the first pitch.
> • Japan, the feedback and the archive.
> • The pitch and the wedding.
> • What would have happened if…?

**Useful phrases**
- First, …
- She suggested…
- What they loved was…
- If she hadn’t…, she wouldn’t…
- Edith is said to have…
- To sum up, …

> **Teacher**
>
> You missed the whole story.
>
> • Ask: Who was Edith?
> • Ask: How did Josh and Haruka change?
> • Ask: What was the best moment, in your opinion?

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Final check for the whole course. The student retells Haruka’s third year, so there’s nothing to invent. Let the student lead and don’t correct during the conversation. Note what they do well and one thing to practise. (~4 min)
Look for: softening, reporting verbs, the causative, future forms, cleft sentences, mixed conditionals, modal perfects, participle clauses, reporting passives, inversion and signposting.

</details>


#### Slide 25: Wrap-up · Course check

**In this course, I can…**
- run a meeting and disagree politely
- work out what people really mean, and say what I mean clearly
- plan, present and negotiate at work
- handle life admin, from payslips to formal complaints
- give a memorable speech

[image: Photo idea: tea on a London windowsill]

<details><summary>Teacher notes</summary>

Final self-check for the whole course: for each line the student says ✓ (I can), “with help”, or “not yet”. Celebrate how far they’ve come, and suggest what to practise next. (~2 min)

</details>
